# Coevolution analysis illuminates the evolutionary plasticity of the chaperonin system GroES/L

Mario A. Fares 

Instituto de Biolog´ıa Molecular y Celular de Plantas (C.S.I.C-U.P.V), Ingeniero Fausto Elio s/n, Valencia, Spain Department of Genetics, Smurfit Institute of Genetics, University of Dublin, Trinity College Dublin, Dublin, Ireland 

## 14.3.1 Introduction

Proteins are continuously produced, overwhelming the cytoplasm of the cell. If unprotected from the aqueous medium, synthesized proteins generally tend to aggregate in a nonspecific way through their exposed hydrophobic surfaces. Because of the nonspecific nature of such aggregations, most of these are toxic to the cell. The spontaneous folding of many proteins generally prevents this problem, but this is only true for small proteins. Large proteins are slow folding and require the folding assistance of other proteins (known as heat shock proteins (HSPs) or molecular chaperones) to acquire their native and active conformation. A special case of HSPs is that of GroES/L (for simplicity, I will call the complex GroEL), a ubiquitous HSP60 in prokaryotes, eukaryotic organelles, and archaea (Williams et al., 2010). GroES and GroEL are expressed at constitutive levels under normal physiological conditions, but their expression is enhanced under heat stress, allowing the survival of Escherichia coli cells under a wide range of temperatures (Fayet et al., 1989; VanBogelen et al., 1987a,b) (see Chapters 14.1 and 14.2). Both GroES and GroEL are encoded in a single operon (groE), which when expressed gives rise to a homotetradecamer organized in two heptameric back-to-back oriented rings (Figure 14.3.1), each of which comprise seven identical subunits that interact with GroES through their apical domains. Each of the GroEL subunits is subdivided into three main domains: the apical domain that binds unfolded proteins and GroES; the intermediate domain that provides structural mobility and conformational change to the apical domain; and the equatorial domain, which is the place where adenosine triphosphate (ATP) hydrolysis and protein folding happen (Braig et al., 1994; Hunt et al., 1996; Xu et al., 1997). 

The main function of GroEL is folding proteins in the cell (Ellis, 2005, 2007; Horwich et al., 2007; Thirumalai and Lorimer, 2001), preventing the aggregation of unfolded polypeptides (Hartl and Hayer-Hartl, 2009) (see Chapter 14.1). The list of proteins folded by GroEL is vast and includes many essential proteins that lead to lethal phenotypes when deleted (Fujiwara et al., 2010; Kerner et al., 2005). Proteins requiring GroEL for folding (also known as clients) are central to many housekeeping processes, and thus deletion or inactivation of GroEL leads to lethal phenotypes because of both the direct GroEL-related detrimental effects and indirect effects on essential proteins. Arguably, GroEL is a highly conserved protein at the sequence level. Indeed, even in highly drifting biological systems, such as that of endosymbiotic bacteria of insects, GroEL is less tolerant to deleterious mutations than all other proteins in the cell and evolves under strong evolutionary constraints despite the mutational dynamics of endosymbiotic bacterial populations (Fares et al., 2002, 2004, 2005). 

Moreover, owing to its interaction with a number of essential and highly connected proteins in the cell, GroEL is involved in a vast number of processes. Less understood, although more relevant, is the promiscuous functional nature of GroEL, as this protein has been shown to be involved in folding-unrelated functions such as immune response in humans (Ronaghy et al., 2011; Tuccinardi et al., 2011; Zonneveld-Huijssoon et al., 2011) and growth and biofilm formation in bacteria, among other functions (George et al., 2004). However, how have such functions originated? And how is the conflict among many functions coexisting in a single protein resolved? 

The gene groEL has undergone a number of duplication events in bacteria (Lund, 2009) (see Chapter 14.5). Since gene duplication is generally linked to evolutionary innovation through genetic redundancy (Ohno, 1999) and mutational robustness (Fares et al., 2013; Keane et al., 2014), groEL duplication has been thought to be responsible for the origination of novel functions in this protein. The high level of evolutionary conservation of the protein GroEL contrasts with its redundancy in some bacteria and with the coexistence of many different functions alternative to folding (Henderson et al., 2013). The trade-offs between GroEL’s conservation and functional evolution remain poorly characterized.

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/9bffbd003a1c6eacfeec3bd11b47869adf73ea6a7427a02453241b5085cbd51a.jpg)


Approaches to understanding the functional importance of amino acid sites in GroEL have been undertaken, with the main strategy relying on the mutagenesis of single amino acid sites. However, the multifunctional nature of GroEL suggests the existence of a reservoir of functions in this molecule, each of which is encoded in a distinct set of interacting amino acids. The different sets of interacting amino acids may overlap in a few highly constrained amino acids that drive the evolution of the remaining amino acids within the interacting set. I hypothesize that the functional plasticity of GroEL is encoded in the evolutionary plasticity of overlapping sets of amino acids. The overlapping nature of these sets implies that changes in key amino acid positions of GroEL in specific bacterial groups should impose reciprocal selective constraints on other amino acids with which they are functionally or structurally linked, leading to strong coevolutionary signatures. 

In a recent study, we performed a search for such coevolutionary signatures in an extensive bacterial phylogeny (Ruiz-Gonzalez and Fares, 2013). The uncovered coevolutionary net- ´ works in hundreds of bacteria reveal the complexity underlying the evolution of multifunctionality in GroEL and shed considerable light on the functional relevance of previously unnoticed amino acid sites. 

## 14.3.2 Material and methods

## 14.3.2.1 Sequences, alignments, and phylogenetic inference

All GroES and GroEL (also known as cpn10 and cpn60, respectively) sequences were downloaded from the OMA browser site (http://omabrowser.org). We used either cpn10 or cpn60 and Rhizobium as keywords. Then we chose the link to the page with the highest number of orthologues: RHIL300891 (Q1MKX3) with 903 orthologues (01/04/2011) for cpn10, and RHIL300890 (CH601 RHIL3) with 870 orthologues (23/03/2011). We removed all eukaryotic and archaeal sequences prior to the analysis. Then, we aligned all sequences using ClustalX2 (Larkin et al., 2007; Thompson et al., 1997). The output alignment was manually refined using GeneDoc 2.6 (Nicholas and Nicolas, 1997), and this new alignment was used to build a neighbour-joining tree with 1000 bootstrap replicates in ClustalX2. The trees were visualized with FigTree 


Table 14.3.1 GroES (Cpn10) and GroEL (Cpn60) sequences used in our analysis. For the individual intragroup analyses, we chose those bacterial groups with more than 10 sequences. For the overall Cpn10 and Cpn60 intragroup analyses, we took all sequences (519 and 505, respectively).

1.3.1 (http://tree.bio.ed.ac.uk/software/figtree/), and all redundant sequences (same amino acidic sequences) were detected and deleted but leaving a representative one. Then, we removed the sequences belonging to duplicated genes within all given species, ending with a final alignment that included 519 sequences for the cpn10 and 505 sequences for the cpn60 (see Table 14.3.1). We used CAPS (Fares and McNally, 2006) to analyse the intra-protein coevolution clustering of amino acids for both the cpn10 and cpn60 alignments. For both alignments, we used a threshold alpha value of 0.001, a random sampling of 100,000, and a bootstrap value of 100. In addition to these two alignments, we prepared new alignments for those taxonomic groups with at least 10 sequences for both cpn10 and cpn60 proteins (sample sizes in Table 14.3.1): Actinobacteria, Bacteroidetes/Chlorobi group, Cyanobacteria, Firmicutes, all Proteobacteria together, and Spirochaetes. In these analyses, the bootstrap values were adapted to the sample sizes (20, 80, 100, 20, 10, and 9, respectively). 

To conduct coevolution analysis between GroES and GroEL, we built multiple sequence alignments for both of the proteins, which comprised the sequences belonging to the same organismal source (a total of 381 sequences for GroES and GroEL) (Table 14.3.1). We downloaded the sequences for the crystallized cpn10 and cpn60 proteins of E. coli (PDB ID: 1AON; MMDB ID: 47936) from the US National Center for Biotechnology Information (NCBI) site (http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/structure) to map the coevolving aminoacidic sites detected using CAPS in the protein structure. Since the output aminoacidic sites detected by CAPS correspond to the position in the input alignment, which included gaps, we wrote a script in C++ (Microsoft Visual C++ Standard Edition 6.0, available from authors upon request) to identify the coevolving sites in the sequence of the published structure of the protein. The networks of coevolving amino acids were performed using Cytoscape 2.8.2 (Smoot et al., 2011). 

## 14.3.2.2 Coevolution analyses

Coevolution analyses, which is the correlated variation of two amino acid sites throughout the multiple sequence alignment, was performed using a previously published coevolution method (Fares and Travers, 2006) implemented in the CAPS programme (Fares and McNally, 2006). Briefly, this method estimates how correlated the evolutionary variability is at two sites of the same or different protein-coding multiple sequence alignments. To account for the strength of the amino acids’ transitions in a site, the BLOSUM score of amino acid transitions of a site between two sequences was corrected by the time since the divergence of the two sequences compared. Time of divergence was calculated using Li’s corrected synonymous nucleotide substitutions. Phylogenetic artefacts – phylogeny asymmetries, long-branch attractions, and unequal codon and base composition biases among the bacterial clades – were accounted for by conducting the same coevolution analyses in a set of neutrally evolving simulated alignments, which bear the same evolutionary features as the real sequence alignments. A pair of sites was considered to coevolve if the probability of their correlation coefficient was lower than 0.001 when compared to the null distribution of such coefficients drawn from the simulated sequence alignments. Moreover, to identify coevolving pairs of sites that may be functionally or structurally linked across the bacterial phylogeny, we conducted nonparametric bootstrap analyses of covariation (see Section 14.3.2.3). 

## 14.3.2.3 Bootstrapping the pairs of coevolving sites

In this study, we have devised a new method to determine the reliability of a coevolution pair of amino acid sites. This test is based upon the assumption that pairs of sites involved in important functional roles within a phylogenetic group should be inextricably linked to each other with regard to their evolutionary patterns, such that the two sites of the pair should be evolutionarily dependent on one another through their reciprocal natural selection. That is, a change in one amino acid should be accompanied by a compensatory (coadaptive) change in its coevolving amino acid partner. Making the inverse rationale, pairs of amino acid sites that are consistently detected as coevolving in a phylogenetic context should be functionally related. 

For each of the pairs of amino acid sites detected in our coevolutionary analyses, we performed a nonparametric bootstrapping; that is, we randomly sampled sequences from the phylogenetic tree, performed the coevolutionary analyses for those sampled sequences using CAPS, and then checked whether a particular pair of sites detected in the real coevolutionary analyses was also detected in this new sampled dataset. We replicated this procedure 1000 times and then asked how many times each of the pairs of sites detected as coevolving in the real multiple sequence alignments was detected as significantly supporting coevolution. Those pairs that were identified in more than 70% of the phylogenetic random samples were deemed as consistently coevolving amino acid sites. 

## 14.3.2.4 Measuring statistical independence of coevolutionary groups among bacteria

To measure the statistical independence between one group of coevolving sites and another, we first calculated the entropy of the group $( \mathrm { D } _ { \mathrm { S } } )$ : 

$$
D _ {S} = f _ {i, j, \dots , S} ^ {(a)} \ln \frac {f _ {i , j , \dots , S} ^ {(a)}}{q ^ {(a)}} + \left(1 - f _ {i, j, \dots , S} ^ {(a)}\right) \ln \frac {1 - f _ {i , j , \dots , S} ^ {(a)}}{1 - q ^ {(a)}} \tag {14.3.1}
$$

Here , f(a)i,j,…,S is the frequency of the most represented amino acid $\mathrm { f } _ { \mathrm { i , j , . . . , S } } ^ { \mathrm { ( a ) } }$ (a) in each of the sites under coevolution $( i , j , \dots , S )$ within the group. This frequency is compared to the frequency of the amino acid (a) in all the proteins $( q ^ { ( a ) } )$ ). 

We then measured the correlation entropy of the group $( I _ { S } )$ as: 

$$
\mathrm{I} _ {\mathrm{S}} = \mathrm{D} _ {\mathrm{S}} - \sum_ {\mathrm{i} \in \mathrm{S}} \mathrm{D} _ {\mathrm{i}} ^ {(\mathrm{a})} \tag {14.3.2}
$$

Here, $\mathrm { D _ { i } ^ { ( a ) } }$ is the frequency of the amino acid (a) at site i, and it is calculated as: 

$$
\mathrm{D} _ {\mathrm{i}} ^ {(\mathrm{a})} = \mathrm{f} _ {\mathrm{i}} ^ {(\mathrm{a})} \ln \frac {\mathrm{f} _ {\mathrm{i}} ^ {(\mathrm{a})}}{\mathrm{q} ^ {(\mathrm{a})}} + \left(1 - \mathrm{f} _ {\mathrm{i}} ^ {(\mathrm{a})}\right) \ln \frac {1 - \mathrm{f} _ {\mathrm{i}} ^ {(\mathrm{a})}}{1 - \mathrm{q} ^ {(\mathrm{a})}} \tag {14.3.3}
$$

Two groups $( \mathbf { g } _ { \mathrm { a } }$ and $\mathrm { g _ { b } ) }$ are independent of one another, if their correlation entropies follow: 

$$
I _ {S (g _ {1}, g _ {2})} \approx I _ {S (g _ {1})} + I _ {S (g _ {2})} \tag {14.3.4}
$$

To determine the significance of the difference between both sides of Equation (14.3.4), we built 1000 groups, each with the same size as the coevolution group; estimated $\mathrm { I } _ { \mathrm { S ( g 1 ) } }$ and $\mathrm { I } _ { \mathrm { S ( g 2 ) } } ;$ and compared this to IS(g1,g2). $\mathrm { I } _ { \mathrm { S ( g l , g 2 ) } }$ 

## 14.3.3 Results

## 14.3.3.1 Sequence data and coevolution analyses

To reveal and understand the interaction profiles between amino acids within both the GroEL and GroES proteins, we generated high-confidence multiple sequence alignments for both these proteins isolated from an extensive group of bacteria, and performed intraprotein coevolution analyses with these alignments. To perform intraprotein coevolution analyses in GroES and GroEL, we searched groE sequences amongst the major bacterial phyla in which the number of orthologous sequences would make it feasible to perform accurate coevolution analyses. We found that Actinobacteria, Cyanobacteria, Bacteroidetes and Chlorobi, Firmicutes, Proteobacteria, and Spirochaetes comprised a number of groE homologues that would allow accurate inference of coevolution. Taking these groups of bacteria, the number of sequences ranged between 11 and 252 for groES genes, and between 12 and 278 for groEL genes, belonging to the Spirochaetes and Proteobacteria groups, respectively (Table 14.3.1). The number of sequences was sufficient to ensure a minimum of 85% sensitivity and higher specificity according to previous reports (Codoner et al., 2008; Fares and Travers, 2006). The difference in the number of sequences for the different alignments could, nevertheless, influence our ability to detect coevolution. In spite of the differences in the number of sequences, the mean amino acid sequence divergence was of the same order in all bacteria groups ranging between 0.302 and 0.4029, and these divergence levels were not correlated with the number of sequences in the alignment. Interprotein coevolution analyses between groES and groEL were performed by building pairs of files for each group of bacteria, both of which included the same bacterial species. Accordingly, the size of the alignments used for the GroES–GroEL (GroESL) inter-coevolution analyses ranged between 11 in Cyanobacteria and 215 in Proteobacteria (Table 14.3.1). 

## 14.3.3.2 Evolutionary dependencies between functional sites within GroES and GroEL

Evolutionary dependencies between amino acid sites are diagnostic of the functional and structural links among them. One way to determine the plasticity of these links is by identifying the ubiquity of each amino acid position in the groups of coevolution. To this end, we performed coevolutionary analysis to determine the network of residue dependencies in all bacteria. We performed intraprotein coevolution analyses in a 519-sequences-based GroES alignment and 505-sequencesbased GroEL alignment, representing the six major bacterial groups (Figure 14.3.2). To integrate our analyses within a phylogenetic framework, we calculated the probability of observing a coevolutionary pair of amino acid sites by chance using a nonparametric bootstrap approach (see Section 14.3.2, “Material and methods,” for details). All amino acid sites numbering and composition are referred to throughout the text according to the numbering in the crystal structure of GroESL from E. coli (1AON.pdb). 

We identified a single connected network of 16 coevolving amino acid sites in GroES, with amino acid sites Leu27, Gly29, Thr36, Arg37, Glu39, Arg47, and Lys74 establishing most of the evolutionary dependencies (Figure 14.3.3a). How important are these links in the network? To determine the importance of each of the amino acid sites in the network (e.g., amino acids establishing most of the connections), we calculated the centrality of each of these amino acids in the network using degree centrality, betweenness, and closeness. Networks are a collection of points joined together in pairs by lines. In networks jargon, points are referred to as vertices or nodes, and the links are referred to as edges. Centrality measures of nodes, including degree, betweenness, and closeness, are typically used to determine the importance of these nodes in the network. Degree is the number of edges departing from a node in the network. A node presents high closeness when its shortest distances to all other nodes in the network are low compared to the average closeness. Betweenness of a node is high when the number of shortest paths between all pairs of nodes in a network that pass through it is high.

We used centrality measures to determine how many coevolution links that each of the amino acid sites detected using CAPS had with the other sites in the protein. We used three main centrality measures, including betweenness, closeness, and degree for the networks of GroES (a to c) and GroEL (d to f). In these networks, amino acid sites are represented using the three-letter amino acid codes followed by the position of the amino acid in the three-dimensional structure of the GroESL protein complex (PDB ID: 1AON; MMDB ID: 47936). Interestingly, Leu27 and Gly29 – two amino acids known to be involved in the interaction between GroES and GroEL (Buckle et al., 1997; Fenton et al., 1994) – are the most central in the coevolution network (Figure 14.3.2a–c). The dependency of these two essential amino acids on other functionally uncharacterized ones hints possible functional links between both sets of amino acid sites. Indeed, Thr36, Arg37, Gly39, Arg47, and Lys74 lack a characterized functional role; however, the fact that they form a structural cluster establishing important contacts among the GroES subunit sparks the idea that such sites are part of a functional or structural unit (Figure 14.3.3b). Amino acid sites within each of the structural clusters were in close proximity to each other (e.g., their proximal carbon atoms were less than 4 A distant). Coevolution between structurally proximal˚ amino acid sites is a general pattern (Gloor et al., 2005) and suggests compensatory relationships, hence functional or structural links, between amino acids (Codoner and Fares, 2008; Davis et al., 2009; Fares, 2006). 

In GroEL, we identified 21 coevolving amino acid residues (Figure 14.3.3c), of which Leu116, Ala127, Ser135, Arg231, Lys245, Gln319, Arg350, Ala443, and Asn487 were the most central residues to the network. Arg231, Val236, and Lys245 are involved or close to (less than 4 A distance in the structure) sites˚ mediating substrate and GroES binding. Other positions were either included or close to charged amino acid sites that were facing the central GroEL cavity (e.g., Gln290, Val300, Lys311, and Arg350). Finally, Asn487 is located in the ATP and Mg2+ binding site, whereas other amino acid sites, such as Ala443 and Ala466, are at the rings’ interface and are likely involved in protein folding within the GroESL ring complex. All 21 amino acids are distributed between two structural groups: one in the apical and another in the equatorial domains (Figure 14.3.3d). Remarkably, coevolving sites are very close to functionally characterized regions, including sites involved in protein folding, substrate and GroES binding, ATP binding and hydrolysis, and inter-subunit contacts. Therefore, substitutions at these sites are likely to have important functional implications (Figure 14.3.3d).

## 14.3.3.3 Coevolution of GroES with GroEL

The interaction of GroES and GroEL is essential to induce the conformational changes needed for the folding cycle. These conformational changes may force coadaptation dynamics between GroES and GroEL. 

We performed coevolutionary analyses using the protein sequences of GroEL and GroES from the same set of bacteria (381 sequences for GroES and GroEL). These sequences span all the different bacterial groups (Table 14.3.1), with all these groups being well represented. Analysis of coevolution identified a group of amino acids from GroES coevolving with GroEL (Figure 14.3.4a). The centrality measures of coevolving sites were also calculated (Figure 14.3.5a–c). Coevolution did not affect GroES sites involved in the GroESL interaction. Nonetheless, sites coevolving between both proteins had important functional roles and mapped to different functional domains of GroEL. For example, two of the GroEL sites, Ala260 and Arg268, are involved in the binding of substrates and overlap with sites involved in GroES binding as well (Buckle et al., 1997). In addition, Glu461, involved in the coevolution between Ala260 and Arg268, has a role in stabilizing interring contacts (Brocchieri and Karlin, 2000). Since GroES is heavily involved in determining the function of GroEL as a single or as a double ring (Liu et al., 2009), the coevolution of Glu461 from GroEL with GroES amino acid sites may have implications in the structural stability of the double ring and, thus, GroES–GroEL folding cycle. 

In agreement with the suggestion of structural and functional communication between the coevolving sites of GroES and

GroEL, coevolving amino acids were clustered in the structure of GroES and GroEL (Figure 14.3.4b), and they included some sites with functional roles or were close to sites with reported functional importance. Taken together, these results support the hypothesis that the coevolutionary relationships are the result of selective constraints on amino acids that are structurally or functionally linked in the GroESL complex. 

## 14.3.3.4 Shifts of GroES–GroEL coevolutionary relationships during bacterial evolution

GroES and GroEL work as parts of the same machine: binding of GroES to GroEL induces conformational changes required for the folding activity of GroEL. Owing to the importance of this binding for GroESL complex function, interacting amino acid sites between both these proteins should be under reciprocal selective constraints. One way to identify these coevolutionary links is by detecting instances of changes in GroEL that have been functionally important and that have been concomitant to changes in GroES. Functional shifts in GroEL have been previously documented and linked to events of GroEL gene duplication (McNally and Fares, 2007) and to changes in the organismal lifestyle (Henderson et al., 2013; McNally and Fares, 2007). In this study, we identified evolutionary dependencies between amino acid sites that were specific to a particular bacterial group but not to others. To avoid the problem of undersampling in our bacterial groups and its effects on detecting coevolution (Codoner et al., 2008), we performed bootstrap–coevolution analyses of the coevolving pairs of sites. Amino acid sites in the coevolutionary networks were identified as coevolving with high bootstrap values. Amino acid sites detected in coevolution analyses between GroES and GroEL (Figure 14.3.6) were not detected in intraprotein coevolution analyses in either of these two proteins, and thus were not the result of indirect evolutionary within-protein dependencies. 

Most amino acid sites involved in GroES–GroEL coevolutionary relationships were at the centre of the apical and equatorial domains (Figure 14.3.7). Analysis of the entire alignment for all bacterial groups only provides partial information on the main amino acid dependencies but not on the shifts on such dependencies. Identifying such shifts is paramount to understanding the link between these dependencies and the biological properties of organismal groups. The distribution of amino acid coevolutionary relationships varied significantly between bacterial clades. Figure 14.3.7 represents the distribution of coevolving sites in GroES and GroEL for each of the bacterial groups examined in this study. This graph allows identifying significant differences in the distribution of sites in the different domains of GroEL. For example, in Firmicutes, coevolving sites (yellow filled circles) concentrate mainly in the apical domain, in good agreement with the distribution of coevolving sites when taking the entire set of bacteria (red stars). Proteobacteria (purple filled circles) present one set of coevolving sites in the apical domain and another in the C-terminal equatorial domain. Finally, Actinobacteria (blue filled circles) presents all but one coevolving site in the C-terminal domain of GroEL.

The distribution of coevolving sites in GroEL secondary structures and domains also differed among bacterial groups. Figure 14.3.8 represents the distribution of the expected number and the number of coevolving sites observed in Figure 14.3.7 in the alpha helices, beta strands, and extended strands. The main differences between bacteria groups are in the beta strands, which are highly enriched for sites under coevolution in Proteobacteria, non-enriched in other bacterial groups, and significantly impoverished in Actinobacteria. These data are in good agreement with the main differences in GroEL structure and functions, which have been found between Proteobacteria and Actinobacteria (Henderson et al., 2013). 

Coevolving sites are three-dimensionally proximal in the structure of GroES and GroEL. For example, His7 and Asn68 from Actinobacteria that are strongly proximal in the structure (the mean Euclidean distance, $D _ { E } ,$ between their proximal atoms is less than 4 A) were coevolving with two sets of˚ amino acids from GroEL, one set including Tyr478, Ala481 and Cys519, all three being very proximal to one another in the equatorial domain of GroEL, and another including Cys138 and His401, which were proximal in the intermediate domain. 

To determine the functional meaning of the groupings of coevolving sites, we performed two sets of analyses. First, we followed a previously published approach to define functional sectors in GroEL and GroES (Halabi et al., 2009). In this study, sectors are characterized by statistical independence, structural continuity, biochemical independence, and divergence independence. Using this method, Halabi and colleagues showed a strong correlation between statistical protein sectors and functional sectors. These statistical sectors bear a number of properties, which are necessary and sufficient to determine their existence. We tested three of the sectors’ properties using computational means: statistical and divergence independences and structural continuity. Second, we mapped sites identified as coevolving in only specific bacterial groups to known sites with folding unrelated functions to determine the functional importance of shifts in the coevolutionary relationships among amino acid sites.

## 14.3.3.5 Groups of coevolution form protein sectors statistically independent among bacteria

Functionally linked sites with coadaptation dynamics are expected to be correlated in terms of their variation – that is, their entropy should be correlated across many sequences (Halabi et al., 2009). Entropy at an amino acid site is a measure inversely proportional to its conservation. To test the correlated entropy between sites, we measured the amount of conservation (D ) for the sites of each GroEL protein domain as a function of entropy (see Section 14.3.2, “Material and methods,” for details). Then, we calculated the correlation entropy (I ) for each group of coevolving sites (see Section 14.3.2). To determine if the group of coevolving sites within a bacterial clade is independent from that of another bacterial clade, we compared the correlation entropy of groups of different bacterial clades for each of the GroEL domains. We compared three protein domains (apical, equatorial, and intermediate domains) between bacterial groups. If changes in the sites’ composition of coevolutionary networks are the result of functional shifts between bacteria, the entropies (Ii) of coevolving sites should correlate to a significantly larger extent within coevolution groups for a bacterial group (g1) than with any of the sites of the network of a different bacterial group (g2). That is, the entropy correlation of one bacterial group should be independent of that of the other group $( I _ { g l - g 2 } \approx I _ { g l } + I _ { g 2 } )$ . 

A main difference between our approach and that of a previous study (Halabi et al., 2009) is that sectors in our approach are defined based on coevolution analyses derived from computational calculations as implemented in CAPS, whereas those of Halabi and colleagues were identified using statistical coupling analyses (SCA) to determine the contribution of correlations to conservation profiles.

Our analyses of correlation entropies showed that all groups of coevolving sites within the apical domain for a bacterial group were independent from those in the other bacterial group (e.g., comparison of $I _ { g { \imath } - g 2 } - ( I _ { g { \imath } } + I _ { g 2 } )$ from the real group with a set of 1000 pseudorandom replicates yields no significant difference between the two groups (g1 and g2)) (Figure 14.3.9). The same was inferred for the groups of coevolving sites from the intermediate domain of GroEL. Conversely, in the apical domain, we found independent groups of coevolution for all bacterial groups with the exception of Spirochetes, in which $( I _ { g l - g 2 } )$ was much smaller than $( I _ { g l } + I _ { g 2 } )$ (Figure 14.3.9). 

## 14.3.3.6 Structural continuity among coevolution groups

To determine if the sites within a coevolution group were linked structurally within a bacterial clade, we plotted them into the crystal structure of an E. coli GroESL proteins complex. Figure 14.3.10 presents evidence of the structural clustering of sites within each of the bacterial groups in the three protein domains. Importantly, structural mapping of the coevolutionary groups for the different bacterial clades supports structural and functional differences among the groups of coevolving residues. A remarkable observation is that amino acids that coevolved in one group of bacteria are located in a completely different face of the protein structure than those detected in another group of bacteria, while both keeping structural continuity – that is, amino acids within groups were structural proximal to one another. As a case in point, the helices populated with coevolving amino acids in Proteobacteria are independent from those in Actinobacteria. This rule applies to both the equatorial and the apical domains (Figure 14.3.10a and 14.3.10f). In addition to the difference in structural patterns, Proteobacteria present coevolving amino acids in regions involved in protein folding, whereas Actinobacteria are mostly affected in the surfaces of subunits mediating the interring contacts. This differential distribution supports functional shifts between both bacterial clades, with one having a larger effect on folding whereas the other has a greater effect on the stability of the GroEL double-ring complex. Another striking example of functional and structural differentiation is that of Spirochetes, with most of the coevolving amino acids mapping to the interring regions of the equatorial domain (Figure 14.3.10d). Such regions may well be involved in the stabilization of the homotetradecamer structure and the work in trans of both rings during the folding process. 

## 14.3.3.7 Coevolution of sites related to GroEL folding-independent functions

A number of GroEL regions have been identified as involved in functional differences among bacteria, as reported in Figure 4 of Henderson et al. (2013). We have compared the sites coevolving in one bacterial clade but not another to those known to perform functions, and plotted these sites in the different domains known to confer GroEL alternative nonfolding functions. Many of the sites identified belonging to a coevolutionary group or pair in a bacterial group have been previously reported to be involved in a GroEL function alternative to protein folding (Figure 14.3.7). For example, in the group of Actinobacteria, two of the amino acid sites establishing coevolution with one another and with other amino acids closely located in the structure are directly involved in monocyte modulation by the actinobacterium Mycobacterium tuberculosis (Figure 14.3.7) (Hu et al., 2008). Moreover, many of the amino acids identified as coevolving exclusively in Proteobacteria map to a region from GroEL previously found to bind to potato leafroll virus and to facilitate its movement in the plant (Figure 14.3.7) (Hogenhout et al., 1998, 2000). The extensive list of coevolving amino acid sites mapping within these folding-alternative functions (Figure 14.3.7) is testament to the important implications of groups of coevolution in the functional plasticity of GroEL.

## 14.3.4 Discussion

## 14.3.4.1 Coevolution networks define the functional boundaries of GroES and GroEL amino acids

The link between coevolution and coadaptation has been the subject of intense debates (Fares et al., 2011). Discussions and controversies have surrounded the concept of coevolution and have led to hesitation among researchers concerning the extent to which coevolution reflects functional links between amino acids within and between proteins. Our analyses of the coevolutionary dynamics within GroES and GroEL as well as between both these interacting proteins uncover a complex network of evolutionary dependencies among amino acid sites. These dependencies often involve sets of sites with known functional relevance and also comprise other sites with unknown importance. However, the functional importance of these untested sites is supported by a number of observations and tests made in this study. First, we show that most amino acids involved in coevolutionary dynamics are three-dimensionally clustered in the protein structure and closely located to functionally or structurally important sites. As a case in point, functionally important sites in GroES present the largest centrality values in the GroES coevolutionary network, indicating their greater evolutionary dependencies with other sites closely located in the protein structure. The coevolution of sites surrounding important functional regions may compensate for the effects of mutations at these functional sites or near functional and catalytic pockets, thereby maintaining an overall volume or shape for that pocket (Gloor, et al., 2005). The structural proximity of sites identified as coevolving in our study to functional domains supports previous claims that covarying groups of amino acid sites are often identified at critical protein regions (Buck and

Atchley, 2005; Codoner and Fares, 2008; Fares and McNally, 2006; Gloor et al., 2005 , 2010; Tillier and Charlebois, 2009; Travers and Fares, 2007; Travers et al., 2007). Second, covarying amino acid sites identified in this study also form structural clusters, that is, these sites fall close to each other in the protein structure. In conclusion, the low number of coevolving sites, their structural clustering, and proximity to functionally and structurally important regions support their functional or structural importance. Our results are in line with the prediction that sites coevolving with few others in the protein are likely to represent functional coevolutionary relationships, whereas those coevolving with most amino acids in the protein likely result from common evolutionary histories (Little and Chen, 2009; Tillier and Charlebois, 2009; Tillier and Lui, 2003). 

Most covarying amino acid sites in GroEL were identified in the equatorial and apical domains, and only a few amino acids were located in the intermediate domain. Apical and equatorial domains perform most functions in GroEL. It is remarkable that many of the amino acids from the equatorial domain involved in coevolutionary relationships belong to the most carboxyterminal GroEL tail, whose size and hydrophobicity largely condition the folding of substrates within the central GroEL cavity (Horwich et al., 2007; Lin and Rye, 2006). The C-terminal tail of GroEL defines the environment within the central cavity of 

GroEL with regard to its hydrophobicity, which would impact both the size and nature of the substrate proteins folded by the chaperonin (Tang et al., 2006). Collectively, our results uncover a list of amino acid sites that might have profound implications on the functions of GroES and GroEL. 

## 14.3.4.2 Coevolution between GroES and GroEL provides information on the structural consequences of their interaction

Our coevolutionary analyses in GroES and GroEL identified several sets of sites with apparently distinct roles. First, GroES amino acid regions coevolving with residues from GroEL are all located in the interface between the GroES subunits. Second, GroEL residues coevolving with GroES are distributed among the three domains, apical, intermediate, and equatorial. In the apical domain, two amino acid residues coevolving with GroES are involved in substrate binding. One site is located at the interface between the two GroEL heptameric rings and may be involved in the stabilization of these domains. Indeed, the folding reaction cycle requires the double ring of GroEL, in which the information passes between the rings to signal the ATP hydrolysis progress in one ring, which causes important conformational changes in the opposite ring (Horovitz et al., 2001; Yifrach and Horovitz, 1995). One such change involves the weakening of GroES–GroEL binding, which ends with the binding of an ATP to the opposite ring (Weissman et al., 1995). The interring amino acid contacts are, therefore, essential for the folding cycle completion and release of GroES from the cis ring once ATP has been bound to the opposite ring; thus, coevolution between the interface of the rings and GroES may be the result of the constraints to maintain the structural communication between the two GroEL rings upon the interaction with GroES. 

## 14.3.4.3 Coevolution analyses uncover the evolutionary plasticity of GroES and GroEL amino acids

Our results bring forward the controversial, although intuitive, suggestion that the function of a protein may change across an evolutionary scale, leading to a plastic fitness landscape in which constraints on amino acids can vary dramatically. Against the static view of “one protein, one function,” we propose that proteins have the potential to perform many alternative functions, of which one is predominant in a particular context. Leaping from one function to another requires the correlated evolution of key amino acids in the protein. GroEL and its cochaperonin GroES offer a unique system to test this hypothesis because, despite its essentiality to the cell, this protein has evolved many alternative functions in other bacteria (Bittner et al., 2007; George et al., 2004; Gould et al., 2007; Li et al., 2010; Ojha et al., 2005; Rodriguez-Quinones et al., 2005; Ronaghy et al., 2011; Tuccinardi et al., 2011; Wang et al., 2013; Zonneveld-Huijssoon et al., 2011). The performance of alternative functions depends on the fixation of mutations in genes. 

Since amino acids are constrained by their interactions with other amino acids, fixation of mutations at sites with functional relevance must be accompanied by mutations in other sites of the protein through a coadaptation dynamics—that is, amino acids that are structurally or functionally linked exercise reciprocal natural selection on one another (Fares et al., 2011). 

We investigated the evolutionary plasticity in the major bacterial groups and found that groups of amino acids coevolving within or between the two proteins differed between bacterial groups, in good agreement with the apparent difference in functions of GroEL in these bacteria. Groups of coevolving amino acids in one domain of a bacterial group showed statistical and structural independence of that in the same domain from another bacterial group. Many of the coevolution groups found in one bacterial group map to regions of groEL that are known to encode functions alternative to protein folding. Other coevolving amino acids could not be mapped to known alternative functions; the interesting link between those coevolving sites for which functions are reported hints, however, at potential roles for unknown or untested sites. Remarkably, the set of amino acid sites involved in an evolutionary dependency in one bacterial group was close in the protein structure to the set of amino acids detected for another bacterial group. In fact, in some cases, the same amino acid was detected as coevolving with different sets of amino acids in two bacterial groups. For example, in the intra-GroEL coevolution analysis, Met514 was detected in Actinobacteria and Bacteroidetes, but it was coevolving with different amino acids in these two groups. The general trend was that alternative sets of coevolving sites identified in different bacteria were closely located in the structure, supporting the plausible hypothesis that shifts in the selective constraints on amino acid sites of GroEL are subtle between bacteria and affect the same or overlapping structural regions, probably those regions undergoing conformational changes when GroEL interacts with GroES. 

To conclude, we provide evidence of the plasticity of the evolutionary relationships between the amino acid sites in an essential protein. We also list a set of coevolving sites that might be worth testing for addressing important questions regarding the functional promiscuity of GroEL and its evolvability under different conditions. Experimental studies aimed at determining the importance of the amino acid sites listed here may aid the development of mechanistic models of protein folding in the cell and the evolution of alternative functions to the highly conserved ones.

# ClpL ATPase: a novel chaperone in bacterial stress responses

Pratick Khara and Indranil Biswas 

Department of Microbiology, Molecular Genetics and Immunology, University of Kansas Medical Center, Kansas City, Kansas, USA 

## 14.4.1 Introduction

Bacteria continually encounter various stressful environmental conditions such as exposure to antibiotics (see Chapter 2.9), DNA-damaging agents (see Sections 8 and 9), oxidative toxic chemicals (see Chapter 10), and high osmolarity (see Section 11). Exposure to these stressors causes denaturation of the cellular proteins that ultimately accumulate within the cell as aggregates. To counteract the inimical effects of various stresses, bacteria encode a number of heat shock proteins (HSPs) that disaggregate, refold, and/or degrade misfolded proteins (Craig et al., 1993; Hendrick and Hartl, 1993). Thus, the HSPs are commonly referred to as molecular chaperones. Caseinolytic protease (Clp) or HSP100 is one such group of HSPs that plays a major role in maintaining cellular protein homeostasis. Clp ATPases belong to the AAA+ ATPase superfamily that is characterized by a conserved segment of around 220 amino acids, containing conserved motifs necessary for adenosine triphosphate (ATP) binding and hydrolysis (Frees et al., 2004). Based on the number of ATP-binding domains, members of the Clp ATPase family can be grouped into class I (two ATP-binding domains) and class II (one ATP-binding domain). ClpA, B, C, D, E, and L are the members of class I Clp ATPases, whereas ClpM, N, X, and Y belong to class II. Clp ATPases often form functional complexes with the peptidase subunit ClpP for the proteolytic activity necessary to remove damaged proteins and occasionally for protein-folding functions (Gottesman et al., 1997; Zolkiewski, 2006). Generally, ClpP alone degrades proteins inefficiently (Jennings et al., 2008). The function of Clp ATPases is to determine the substrate specificity and guide them to the proteolytic complex composed of ClpP where the proteins are degraded (Frees et al., 2007; Kirstein et al., 2009; Moliere and Turgay, 2009; Zolkiewski, 2006). Some Clp ATPases instead of interacting with ClpP peptidase cooperate with the HSP70 system to disaggregate and refold denatured proteins (Glover and Lindquist, 1998). For example, ClpB from E. coli forms a hexameric-ring structure and interacts with the DnaK chaperone system that includes DnaK, DnaJ, and GrpE (Glover and Lindquist, 1998; Weibezahn et al., 2004; see Chapter 14.2). ClpB, which belongs to HSP100, disaggregates and refolds denatured proteins with the concerted effort of the cognate DnaK– DnaJ–GrpE system (Mogk et al., 1999; Motohashi et al., 1999; Zolkiewski, 1999). The mechanistic aspects of this interesting bichaperone system still remain poorly understood in spite of a great deal of biochemical and structural knowledge. This may be due to the fact that ClpB and DnaK systems interact only transiently (Doyle et al., 2007; Schlee et al., 2004) and recognize substrate proteins via sequential binding (Goloubinoff et al., 1999). In many low-GC Gram-positive bacteria additionally, a homolog of ClpB called ClpL is present along with ClpB (Figure 14.4.1) (Frees et al., 2007; Kwon et al., 2003). 

Surprisingly, ClpL is absent in Gram-negative bacteria, indicating that ClpL might have a special role in Gram-positive organisms (Tao and Biswas, 2013). Indeed, in Gram-positive bacteria, ClpL has been shown to be involved in thermotolerance (see Section 13), acid tolerance (see Section 16), virulence regulation, and resistance to various antibiotics that target the cell wall; however, the detailed molecular mechanisms of ClpL’s function are still unclear (Kajfasz et al., 2009; Suokko et al., 2008; Tran et al., 2011; Tu le et al., 2007). Recently, in Streptococcus pneumoniae, ClpL has been shown to be a unique HSP100 family member that is $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ -dependent; and, unlike ClpB, ClpL can perform chaperone activity without the help of cooperative chaperones (Park et al., 2015). ClpL can stabilize unfolded proteins, prevent heat aggregation, and also restore enzymatic activity of certain heat-denatured proteins. 

## 14.4.2 Role in pathogenesis

So far, approximately 130 ClpL homologs have been identified in Gram-positive bacteria. The prevalence of ClpL in this group of bacteria suggests that ClpL plays an important role particularly in lactic acid bacteria (LAB) that include important pathogens such as S. pneumoniae, Streptococcus pyogenes, and Streptococcus mutans (Figure 14.4.1). Most probably, ClpL is required for survival during confrontation with harsh environments such as thermal stress during niche change and entrapment by phagocytes followed by exposure to respiratory oxygen species (Nguyen et al., 2014). Thus, it is involved in stress tolerance response, virulence, and antibiotic resistance. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/19d26ae3e108ff3ff1c2e3da7a0a7f99433c57955ea1b9db4eaa9a9199f76def.jpg)



Figure 14.4.1 Synteny of clpL in various groups of streptococci obtained using Absynte (Archaeal and Bacterial Synteny Explorer; archaea.upsud.fr/absynte/). Similar genes are shown in identical color. Scores indicate the percent identity of homologous clpL in comparison to that of S. mutans.


During pneumococcal infection, the host cells respond to pathogens by producing antibodies or activating immune cells to restrict proliferation of the pathogen. However, streptococci utilize virulence factors to inhibit the host immune system and evade the host defense system. Under stressed conditions, an increase in the expression of HSPs safeguards the quality of cellular proteins. During pneumococcal infection, the raised temperatures encountered within a mammalian host serve as a trigger for rapid and transient increase in the synthesis of HSPs (Borges and Ramos, 2005; Walter and Buchner, 2002). 

During infection, both host cells and pathogens are confronted by dramatic alterations in their surroundings, and induction of HSPs is important for pathogen survival. Inactivation of heat shock genes has resulted in virulence attenuation in many microorganisms, including streptococci (Chakrabarti et al., 1999; Charpentier et al., 2000; Nair et al., 1999; Takaya et al., 2004). One mechanism by which pathogens subvert the host cell is through modulating the actin cytoskeleton to migrate into the cytoplasm by inducing unidirectional actin polymerization (Gruenheid and Finlay, 2003). The actin polymerization process is required for the actin-generated motility of bacteria, which enables them to move into infected cells (Haglund and Welch, 2011). The clpL gene is reported to reduce pneumococcal adherence to A549 lung cells, likely via modulation of Rap1 and Rac1-mediated filopodium formation (Nguyen et al., 2014). In S. pneumoniae, a clpL mutant shows greater adherence to and invasion of epithelial cells, but it elicits lower tumor necrosis factor alpha (TNFα) secretion by macrophages during early stages of infection by a mechanism dependent on actin polymerization. They are more susceptible to macrophage killing and host clearance mechanisms during the later stages of infection after intranasal challenge (Tu le et al., 2007). 

Induction of general stress responses is one of the many mechanisms to adapt to high acidity (see Section 16). Growth under acidic conditions can induce clpL expression as demonstrated in lactobacilli (Wall et al., 2007). In S. mutans, the ClpL protein is produced in large amounts when cells are grown at pH 5.0 compared to the cells grown at pH 7.0 (Len et al., 2004). Furthermore, osmotic stress (see Section 11) induced by addition of 10% ethanol to the growth medium activates clpL expression in Oenococcus oeni, a member of Lactobacillus (Beltramo et al., 2004). Moreover, dual induction of clpL by cold and heat exposure of cells (see Sections 13 and 15) and reduced tolerance to both temperature shocks in a clpL mutant indicate that the two stress responses are correlated in Streptococcus thermophilus (Varcamonti et al., 2006). On the other hand, to increase the evolutionary fitness in response to environmental challenges, Lactobacillus rhamnosus has acquired an additional cplL (annotated as clpL2) as part of a mobile element from another Lactobacillus strain (Suokko et al., 2005). 

HSPs are reported to be induced by antibiotics and hence contribute to in vivo antibiotic resistance (Tran et al., 2011) (see Chapter 2.9). In pneumococci, ClpL is present at the cell wall and interacts with the cell wall penicillin-binding protein (PBP2x). A clpL mutant of S. pneumoniae is more susceptible to cell wall–active antibiotics and has thinner cell walls compared to the wild-type strain (Tran et al., 2011). It is observed that penicillin-triggered lysis can be partially prevented by heat shock pretreatment as heat shock–induced ClpL interacts with PBP2x and co-localizes with PBP2x at the cell wall (Tran et al., 2011). Thus, in vivo stresses, such as inflammation, respiratory burst in phagocytes, and temperature upshifts, induce higher ClpL levels and increase resistance to penicillin. The wild-type strain has longer chain length and extended generation time compared to the clpL mutant, and is more resistant to deoxycholate and Triton X-100 treatment (Nguyen et al., 2014). 

## 14.4.3 Regulation of clpL expression

Regulation of clpL expression appears to be complex and varies depending on the organism under study. In staphylococci, clpL is entirely under the control of a secondary sigma factor, $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { B } }$ (Frees et al., 2004). However in lactobacilli, such as in Lactobacillus gasseri ATCC 33323, the clpL gene is under the control of HrcA, a transcriptional repressor (Suokko et al., 2008). HrcA repressor binds to a specific motif called CIRCE element, an inverted repeat sequence (TTAGCACTC.N9.GAGTGCTAA) that is present in the promoter regions. A sequence highly similar to the conserved CIRCE box is present upstream of the putative starting codon of clpL (see Chapter 14.2). Interestingly, in other lactobacilli, such as L. bulgaricus, L. acidophilus, and L. johnsonii, a CIRCE box is also present in the upstream regions of clpP and clpE genes (van de Guchte et al., 2006). Furthermore, as mentioned in this chapter, two genes encoding ClpL ATPases (clpL1 and clpL2) are found in a probiotic L. rhamnosus strain E-97800. In addition to HrcA, another repressor called CtsR, which recognizes a tandemly repeated heptanucleotidic sequence known as the CtsR box (A/GGTCAA/T), also regulates clp genes. Though a consensus CtsR binding site in the promoter region of $c l p P$ is found in this strain, no conserved CtsR binding site is identified in the upstream regions of clpL1 or clpL2. However, an inverted repeat structure containing the CIRCE element (see Chapter 14.2) is present in the promoter region of clpL1. Since no consensus DNA binding sequences for HrcA, CtsR or any other known regulators are identified in the promoter region of clpL2, the gene seems to be under the regulation of some unknown regulator(s) (Suokko et al., 2005). In lactococci and some streptococci, CtsR primarily regulates clpL gene expression, and the CtsR box elements are present in the promoter region of clpL genes. The exact placement of the CtsR box varies depending on the organism. For S. thermophilus clpL, the CtsR box is located just upstream of the −35 region (Varcamonti et al., 2006). In S. mutans, a weak CtsR box spanning the −10 region of the clpL promoter is present and the sequence is similar to that of other known CtsR binding boxes in S. mutans (Tao et al., 2012). The measurement of the clpL mRNA levels in a ctsR deletion-containing strain further confirms the fact that CtsR indeed represses the clpL transcription in this organism by a feedback mechanism (Tao et al., 2012). ClpL is also reported to be part of the CtsR regulon in several other Gram-positive bacteria, such as S. thermophilus and O. oeni (Grandvalet et al., 2005; Varcamonti et al., 2006). In O. oeni, clpL is part of a dicistronic operon where clpP is located upstream of the clpL gene (Beltramo et al., 2004). The expression of clpL is under the regulation of two promoters: a CtsR-regulated promoter which is located upstream of $c l p P ,$ and a CtsR-independent promoter, located in the intergenic region between clpP and clpL. Thus, clpL expression varies significantly depending on the organism. 

## 14.4.4 Molecular role as chaperone

Clp/HSP proteins act as chaperones to refold denatured proteins in an ATP-dependent manner (Ogura and Wilkinson, 2001). In S. mutans, ClpL acts as a chaperone to properly fold the CtsR repressor, and thereby prevents its accumulation as protein aggregate (Tao and Biswas, 2013). Though ClpL can refold urea-denatured CtsR in vitro, it is unable to resolubilize aggregated CtsR in vivo. It is predicted that ClpL recognizes and primarily interacts with soluble but denatured substrates before the formation of protein aggregates and helps to refold the substrate (Tao and Biswas, 2013). Interestingly, the C-terminal D2-small domain is reported to be necessary for preventing CtsR aggregation in vivo. Furthermore, ClpL also plays an important role in the posttranslational degradation of CtsR in S. mutans (Tao et al., 2012). 

In S. pneumoniae, ClpL is induced upon heat stress functions as a chaperone, stabilizes unfolded proteins, and prevents aggregation of native proteins at high temperatures (Park et al., 2015). Park et al. (2015) have recently shown that ClpL not only exhibits NTPase activity but also functions as a molecular chaperone without the help of cooperative chaperones, including the DnaK system or other adaptor proteins. The nucleotidebinding domains (NBDs) provide the energy necessary for ClpL chaperone activity through ATP hydrolysis. Pneumococcal ClpL in addition to ATP can utilize guanosine, cytidine, and inosine triphosphate (GTP, CTP, and ITP, respectively) and display NTPase activity at low temperature $( 2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ , normal pH, and low salt concentrations. Furthermore, the highest ATPase activity is achieved with $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ while in the presence of $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ ; the ATPase activity of ClpL is only 25% of that for $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ +. The other divalent cations such as $\mathrm { C a } ^ { \dot { 2 } + }$ and $Z \mathrm { n } ^ { 2 + }$ do not support the ATPase activity. This $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ -dependent, nonspecific NTPase activity of pneumococcal ClpL is distinct from those of other HSP100 AAA+ members. Studies on pneumococcal ClpL also show that it is capable of refolding denatured rhodanese (Kwon et al., 2003). The $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ -supported ClpL-dependent refolding of denatured proteins (luciferase and green fluorescence protein (GFP)) is also more efficient than $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ -supported refolding. Moreover, the dose-dependent chaperone activity has also been confirmed, as increase in concentration of ClpL enhances the percentage of refolded luciferase by eightfold in the presence of ATP and $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ . ClpL is a unique member of the HSP100 family whose chaperone activity does not require any co-chaperone. 

In contrast to ClpB, the DnaK system of pneumococci does not enhance ClpL-mediated refolding of denatured luciferase. Moreover, there is a species specificity of the HSPs, as the pneumococcal DnaK system fails to enhance E. coli ClpB refolding activity (Park et al., 2015). The chaperones have foldase and unfoldase activity, and hence prevent protein aggregation and disaggregate denatured proteins (Sharma et al., 2009). ClpL disaggregation activity increases in the presence of both $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ and $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ more than in the presence of $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ alone. But in the presence of $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ alone, ClpL does not disaggregate heat-induced aggregation of malate dehydrogenase (MDH). Although the pneumococcal DnaK system inhibits ClpL disaggregation activity, the DnaK system from E. coli stimulates disaggregation of aggregated MDH by ClpB. Interestingly, ClpL is able to refold MDH in the presence of $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ , and the refolding activity decreases in the presence of Mn2+ and the DnaK system (Park et al., 2015). ClpL prevents heat-induced aggregation of luciferase in the presence of the $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ ion and ATP. It also prevents heat-induced aggregation of luciferase in the presence of Mg2+ and ADP (Park et al., 2015). In contrast, ClpB with the DnaK system prevents protein aggregation by heat in the presence of ATP but not in the presence of ADP (Weibezahn et al., 2003). 

To carry out its chaperone activities, oligomerization of ClpL protein is necessary. The NBDs, apart from controlling ATP hydrolysis, also help in oligomerization (see Figure 14.4.2 for ClpL sequence alignment). The multiple sequence alignment of ClpL ATPases from various well-studied Gram-positive bacteria shows that although ClpL has two NBDs similar to ClpB, its linker M-domain is much smaller compared to ClpB. 

In the presence of nucleotides, HSP100 proteins form hexamers (Doyle and Wickner, 2009). Unlike other Clp/HSP100 family members, in ClpL both NBDs (NBD1 and NBD2) are required for the hexamerization. The NBD-mutated ClpL proteins with NBD1, NBD2, or NBD1/2 mutated are not able to refold denatured luciferase or prevent heat-induced aggregation because of lack of oligomerization (Park et al., 2015). 

## 14.4.5 Structure information

As mentioned in this chapter, both ClpL and ClpB are members of the AAA+ ATPase superfamily and belong to a class of regulatory Clp proteins that have two NBDs responsible for binding ATP (Figure 14.4.3). 

However, from the sequence analysis, it is evident that some differences do exist between the two proteins (Tao and Biswas, 2013). ClpB possesses two domains, M- and N-domains, which are larger compared to those of ClpL. Interestingly, a UVresistant (UVR) domain, homologous to the interaction domain between the nucleotide excision repair proteins, also exists in ClpL of L. gasseri, which is in contrast to other ClpL proteins characterized to date (Suokko et al., 2008). It is reported that although the N-domain is not essential for oligomerization and in vivo chaperone activity of ClpB (Barnett et al., 2000; Beinker et al., 2002; Hoskins et al., 2009; Mogk et al., 2003), the presence of the N-domain increases interactions with protein aggregates (Barnett et al., 2005; Chow et al., 2005; Mizuno et al., 2012; Nagy et al., 2010). Furthermore, the N-domain mobility is important for the disaggregation activity of ClpB (Nagy et al., 2010). In ClpB, the flexible linker connecting the N-domain and NBD1 controls the mobility of the N-domain. This flexible linker is predicted to play an important role in binding to and disaggregating aggregated protein (Zhang et al., 2012). In spite of having a smaller N-domain than ClpB, ClpL shows disaggregation activity (Park et al., 2015). In ClpB, ClpC, and HSP104, the presence of the M-domain is essential as it helps to interact with auxiliary factors such as DnaK, Hsp70, and MecA (Carroni et al., 2014; Rosenzweig et al., 2013; Wang et al., 2011). Since it appears that ClpL does not require any auxiliary factors for its chaperone activity, the function of the M-domain is expected to be different in this type of protein (Park et al., 2015). The indispensability of the M-domain, consisting of four α helices, in ClpB appears to stem from the fact that it forms a large coiled-coil structure that protrudes from the ClpB hexameric ring (Lee et al., 2003). The conserved helix 3 of the M-domain is required for the DnaK-dependent shuffling of aggregated proteins and not for soluble denatured substrates (Haslberger et al., 2007). Mutational studies on this domain indicate a loss of DnaK/J-GrpEdependent disaggregation activity while retaining the DnaK/J-GrpE-independent functions of ClpB (Haslberger et al., 2007; Kedzierska et al., 2003; Mogk et al., 2003). Since ClpL has a much smaller M-domain, it is speculated that ClpL does not cooperate with the DnaK system and therefore is not able to disaggregate preformed protein aggregates. Tao and Biswas (2013) provided evidence in support of this notion as they observed that ClpL alone was not capable of resolubilizing preformed CtsR aggregates. Hence, they hypothesized that ClpL interacts primarily with the target substrates before the formation of protein aggregates and helps to refold the substrates. It has been further shown that, like ClpB, ClpL too is present as a hexamer in solution (Mogk et al., 2003; Sugimoto et al., 2006; Watanabe et al., 2002). However, unlike in ClpB, both the N- and C-terminal domains are necessary for oligomerization of ClpL. It has also been shown that the C-terminal D2-small domain is necessary for preventing CtsR aggregation in vivo (Tao and Biswas, 2013). Sequence alignment showed that the AAA+ motifs (Walker A, Walker B, Sensor 1, Sensor 2 arginine finger, and pore loop) of ClpL are the same as those of ClpB and Hsp104. The two

NBDs are reported to play a role in oligomerization and ATP hydrolysis activation of Clp/HSP100 families. ClpB and HSP104 use only one NBD for hexamerization, whereas ClpL requires both the NBDs for hexamerization (Park et al., 2015). Furthermore, the NBDs of the hexamer ClpB/Hsp100 are essential for ATPase activity and chaperone functions. Since hexameric HSP104-like proteins are known to regulate their ATPase activity via NBD1 and NBD2 networking, binding of ATP to NBD1 switches the marginally active NBD2 to a highly active state, and as a result ATPase activity is subsequently enhanced (Franzmann et al., 2011). Moreover, ATP binding to NBD2 enhances affinity of NBD1 for ATP in a cooperative manner (Franzmann et al., 2011). On the contrary, the NBDs in ClpL are involved in ATPase and chaperone activities through oligomerization. Mutation at either of the NBDs represses both ATPase and chaperone activities without hexamerization. In comparison to most AAA+ ATPase chaperones, which are $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ dependent, ClpL is a $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ -dependent, nonspecific nucleotide phosphohydrolase (Park et al., 2015). In contrast, ClpB of E. coli is enhanced only by $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ and not by $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ . Thus, the $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ -specific ATPase and chaperone activities of ClpL are speculated not to be associated with the ATP-binding motifs, but with other motifs or domains (Park et al., 2015). This exclusive $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ dependency of ClpL from Pneumococcus can be correlated to the importance of $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ in pneumococcal physiology and virulence. For example, PsaR is a regulator known to trigger the expression of PsaBCA complex protein, which is involved in virulence, various stresses, competence, and adhesion; and $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ is reported to stimulate the gene regulation of PsaR regulator protein (Kloosterman et al., 2008).

## 14.4.6 Conclusions



Chakrabarti S, Sengupta N, Chowdhury R. 1999. Role of DnaK in in vitro and in vivo expression of virulence factors of Vibrio cholerae. Infection and Immunity 67(3): 1025–1033. 





Ogura T, Wilkinson AJ. 2001. AAA+ superfamily ATPases: common structure – diverse function. Genes to Cells 6(7): 575–597. 



Clp ATPases are ubiquitously present in bacteria and play a critical role in maintaining integrity of the proteome. ClpL, a less studied member of the Clp ATPase family, often maintains cellular homeostasis by virtue of its chaperone activity. Surprisingly, the regulation of ClpL is very complex and varies depending on the organism (Tao et al., 2012). In streptococci, ClpL is vital for proper folding of CtsR repressor and prevents formation of detrimental protein aggregates under ambient growth conditions (Tao and Biswas, 2013). But studies on $c l p L$ expression show that, in addition to CtsR, other unknown regulators may modulate clpL expression in bacteria. Since homologs of ClpL are prevalent in Gram-positive pathogens, it is very likely that various environmental stresses may induce antibiotic resistance and tolerance. Indeed, after heat shock, ClpL is reported to play a major role in modulating cell wall biosynthesis and ultimately lead to antibiotic tolerance (Tran et al., 2011). Hence, as the continual increase in resistance and tolerance to antibiotics is a threat to global health, extensive studies on ClpL may equip us with information needed to combat antibiotic-resistant pathogens. Despite being a very important protein for cell viability and playing a pivotal role in long-term survival, very little is known about the molecular functions and regulation of ClpL. Detailed structural, biophysical, and biochemical characterizations of this novel chaperone are needed to decipher its molecular mechanisms that would eventually shed light on how bacteria respond to various environmental stresses and maintain proteostasis.
