# Duplicated groEL genes in Myxococcus xanthus DK1622

Yan Wang1,2, Xiao-jing Chen1, and Yue-zhong Li1 

1State Key Laboratory of Microbial Technology, School of Life Science, Shandong University, Jinan, People’s Republic of China 2College of Marine Life Sciences, Ocean University of China, Qingdao, People’s Republic of China 

## 14.5.1 Introduction

GroEL belongs to the type I chaperonin, and it is responsible for protein folding, assembly, and transport in different bacterial species (Goyal et al., 2006; Lund, 2001, 2009; see Chapter 14.1). For instance, in Escherichia coli, GroEL is required for in vivo correct folding of more than 10% of the total proteins (Kerner et al., 2005). Because of their essential role in various cell functions, the groEL genes are ubiquitously distributed in bacteria. Bacterial cells usually have one copy of the groEL genes within the genome. However, genome sequencing shows that approximately 30% of bacteria possess multiple groEL genes (Goyal et al., 2006). The products of duplicated groELs have evolved to play divergent functions in various bacteria. For example, Mycobacterium smegmatis has two groEL genes; while the groEL2 gene is essential for cell growth, inactivation of the groEL1 gene prevents the formation of mature biofilm (see Section 22) and the biosynthesis of mycolic acid (Ojha et al., 2005). Three copies of the groEL gene are present in Rhizobium leguminosarum: The cpn60.1 gene (groEL1) is indispensable for the growth of this organism, while the other two paralogous genes, cpn60.2 and cpn60.3 (groEL2 and groEL3), can be deleted without affecting cell viability (Rodr´ıguez et al., 2005). Furthermore, Sinorhizobium meliloti has five groEL copies in its genome, of which, however, only the groEL1 gene is required for viability (Bittner et al., 2007). 

Myxobacteria are Gram-negative microorganisms with complex multicellular behavior, such as swarming in groups, cooperatively feeding on macromolecules or other microbial cells, and developing multicellular fruiting bodies containing numerous myxospores under adverse conditions (Shimkets, 1990). It has been suggested that, to fit the complex multicellular social living pattern, myxobacteria have evolved big genomes containing a great number of duplicated genes (Goldman et al., 2006; Han et al., 2013). Many of the duplications are essential genes, including various chaperone genes. 

Myxococcus xanthus DK1622 is the model strain of myxobacteria. The strain has two copies of groEL: One (MXAN 4895, named groEL1) is arranged as a groESL gene cluster, whereas the other (MXAN 4467, named groEL2) is a solo type without its cofactor groES gene (Figure 14.5.1). The two groELs share 83% and 79% identities in their nucleotide and amino acid sequences, respectively. In this chapter, we summarize the characteristics and divergent functions of the duplicated groEL genes. Based on experimental and bioinformatics studies, we will discuss the evolution mechanisms of the duplicated groEL genes in functional divergence. 

## 14.5.2 Duplicated groEL genes are alternatively required for cell growth

The groEL genes have been duplicated in selected bacterial species along with divergence of their functions. In most cases, one copy of groEL is essential, whereas the other(s) is (are) specialized in roles to meet the requirements of different bacterial processes. Normally, these cellular processes are non-essential, and the additional groEL gene(s) can be deleted (Ojha et al., 2005). However, the groEL1 and groEL2 genes in Myxococcus are alternatively required for the survival of the cell (Li et al., 2010). The two groELs can thus be deleted individually (we named the mutants as YL0301 for the groEL1 deletion and YL0302 for groEL2 deletion). We attempted to produce a mutant with a double deletion of the groELs but failed. If groEL1 or groEL2 was complemented ectopically at the Mx8 attB site in the genome of YL0301 or YL0302, the remaining groEL gene could be deleted from its native site. When cultured in CTT medium, YL0301 and YL0302 have similar growth curve and generation time as the wild-type strain, and the three strains reach nearly the same cell density after the exponential growth stage. These results indicate that the two GroEL proteins are partially redundant in function for cell growth. 


Myxococcus xanthus DK1622


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/1b26e40aae09b80ddbf04dc8a24019923d6f1438dd235c07189639cbbecb3d55.jpg)



Figure 14.5.1 Construction of the duplicated groEL genes in M. xanthus DK1622.


In Myxococcus, GroEL1 are usually expressed at a higher level than GroEL2. Transcriptional analysis shows that expression of the groEL1 genes increases during the first day of incubation, then decreases sharply and reaches the lowest level at about 48 h of incubation (Wang et al., 2013). In the late stationary growth stage, the expression of groEL1 increases again. In contrast, the expression of groEL2 is relatively stable but lower than that of groEL1 throughout the cellular growth stage. Interestingly, deletion of one groEL has an effect on the expression of the other groEL genes at the native sites. For instance, quantitative PCR results demonstrate that, after deletion of groEL1 (mutant YL0301), the expression of the groEL2 gene increases approximately threefold at 24 h, compared to that in wild-type strain DK1622. However, the expression of groEL1 in the groEL2 mutant (YL0302) is similar to that in the wild-type strain DK1622. Higher expression of the groEL2 gene in the groEL1 deletion mutant possibly serves to compensate to support the normal growth of cells. Insufficient expressions of groEL genes will affect cell growth. For instance, in the groEL1-complemented double-deletion mutant (YL0308) and the groEL2-complemented mutant (YL0310), the expression levels of the ectopic groEL1 and groEL2 genes are approximately 30–50% and 75–90% of wild-type levels in strain DK1622 at 24 h and 28 h in CTT cultures, respectively. The decreased expression of the integrated complementary genes leads to an increase of the generation time of the complemented doubledeletion mutants. Thus, there is a balanced expression of the duplicated groEL genes in Myxococcus cells. The sequence and organization differences, as well as the expression differences, suggest divergent cellular roles of the duplicated groEL genes in M. xanthus cells. 

## 14.5.3 The total expression levels of groELs determine distinct phenotypes during heat shock conditions

Because of its ability to aid correct folding of denatured proteins, GroELs are among the major heat shock proteins and are normally overexpressed under nonpermissive conditions (Fayet et al., 1989; see Chapter 14.1). The groEL1 and groEL2 genes in 

Myxococcus have different phenotypes under heat shock conditions (Li et al., 2010). After a 30 min heat shock treatment at $4 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ as compared with the wild-type DK1622 cells, only 2.1% of the groEL2 deletion YL0302 cells survive, while the groEL1 deletion YL0301 cells are nonviable. Accordingly, groEL1 is essential for cell survival under heat shock conditions, whereas the deletion of groEL2 causes cells to be more sensitive to higher temperatures than the wild-type strain. Otani et al. found that, although GroEL1 and GroEL2 are among the major proteins induced by heat shock, the density of GroEL2 spots in twodimensional electrophoretic gels is much lower than that of GroEL1 (Otani et al., 2001). Interestingly, after heat shock at $4 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min, the survival rates of mutants containing two copies of the groEL2 genes but no groEL1 (or two groEL1 genes but no groEL2) increase similarly as compared to the strain containing both the groEL1 and groEL2 genes, paralleling the increase in the total groEL expression levels (Wang et al., 2013). These results suggest that the lethal effect of heat shock on the groEL1 deletion mutant and the increased sensitivity of the groEL2 deletion mutant to high temperatures result from a significant decrease in the total expression level of GroEL, leading to an insufficient level of GroEL proteins to facilitate the refolding of denatured proteins. This is consistent with a model where there is a threshold level of GroEL beneath which cells cannot survive, and it supports the hypothesis that both groEL1 and groEL2 retain fundamental functions by balancing their expression levels (Qian et al., 2010). 

## 14.5.4 Duplicated GroELs have subneofunctionalized for specialized cell processes

The two GroELs play divergent functions in the social life of Myxococcus (Li et al., 2010). When spotted on an E. coli mat, M. xanthus DK1622 and groEL1 deletion cells form visible degradation regions as early as 12 h after inoculation. In contrast, the degradation region produced by the groEL2 deletion cells is not visible until 48 h after inoculation. These results suggest that the GroEL2 protein plays an important role in predation feeding on macromolecule substrates and E. coli cells, but the GroEL1 protein appears not to exhibit the same function. Following the exhaustion of E. coli cells, the YL0302 cells are also able to develop into normal fruiting body structures, although the developmental process is significantly delayed due to the belated predation behavior. When cultured on the developmental medium TPM, the groEL2 deletion mutant YL0302 exhibits a developmental phenotype similar to that of fruiting body structures of the wild-type strain, whereas the groEL1 deletion strain YL0301 is also able to aggregate, but irregularly, and forms immature mounds. Thus, GroEL2, but not GroEL1, participates in the predation and feeding processes of the bacteria, while GroEL1, but not GroEL2, is strictly required for the fruiting body development and sporulation of Myxococcus. 

When the groEL1 deletion mutant (strain YL0901) is complemented with groEL1, the developmental defect is mostly reversed, with sporulation reaching 70–80% that of DK1622 (Wang et al., 2013). In contrast, although YL0902 (containing two copies of groEL2) has a total groEL expression level similar to that of YL0901 at different developmental stages, the mutant displays a developmental defect similar to that of YL0301, and the sporulation ability of YL0902 is approximately 20% that of the wild-type strain DK1622. Similarly, the introduction of groEL1 into YL0302 (strain YL0906) does not improve the predation-feeding ability of cells on an E. coli mat, and the swarming time of YL0906 to the edge of the E. coli colony is 60–65 h, which is similar to that of YL0302. When groEL2 is introduced into YL0302 (strain YL0907), the swarming time to the E. coli colony edge decreases to 40 h. The total expression levels of groEL1 and groEL2 are also similar in the YL0906 and YL0907 mutants, suggesting that the changes in expression are not the major contributors to functional divergence. The function divergences of the duplicate groELs thus not only are caused by the different expression levels but also are the results of their substrate specificity. It is also noted that the alternative deletions of the duplicated groEL genes do not affect the social motility (S-motility). Instead, the chaperone HSP70 proteins (DnaK and its homolog; see Chapter 14.2) are involved in the control of S-motility (Yang et al., 1998). 

M. xanthus is able to inhibit the growth of E. coli cells. The killing assays using a series of groEL mutants indicate that, compared with that of wild-type DK1622 and mutant YL0301, the killing ability of YL0302 has been markedly weakened (Wang et al., 2014). The mutants complemented with the groEL1 or the groEL2 gene in YL0302 (named YL0906 and YL0907, respectively) exhibit different bactericidal activities against E. coli cells. The bactericidal activity of YL0906, which contains two copies of the groEL1 gene, is similar to that of YL0302; but the bactericidal activity of YL0907, which contains an original groEL1 gene and a complemented groEL2 gene, is obviously higher than that of YL0302 and YL0906, but similar to that of wild-type DK1622. Thus, groEL2, but not groEL1, is indispensable for the bactericidal activity. Consistently, while extracts of the groEL2 deletion mutant YL0302 present a greatly decreased inhibition zone, there is no obvious difference between the wild-type strain DK1622 and the groEL1 deletion mutant YL0301. The total extracts of wild-type DK1622 and a series of mutants have been analyzed by high-pressure liquid chromatography. One major peak in DK1622 and YL0301 with a retention time of approximately 15.9 min is not visible in YL0302. Based on the mass spectrometry results, the putative molecular weight of the compound is 623.60 ([M+H] 624.60, [M+Na] 646.60), which corresponds well to myxovirescin (TA), one of the major secondary compounds produced in M. xanthus DK1622. Experiments with supplementation of exogenous TA indicate that the compound is one of the key reasons for the decreased bactericidal activity of YL0302 (Wang et al., 2014). These results suggest that the groEL2 gene is required for correct folding of some key enzymes in the biosynthesis of TA, which is also confirmed by the immunoprecipitation assays with the groEL1 and groEL2 deletion mutants (Wang et al., 2013). Thus, groEL2 is necessary for the production of TA. 

## 14.5.5 Substrates and their characteristics of GroEL1 and GroEL2

To identify the proteins that interact with GroEL1 and GroEL2 in M. xanthus DK1622, immunoprecipitation assays were performed using the groEL1 and groEL2 deletion mutants (YL0301 and YL0302), and the bound proteins were subjected to mass spectral identification. After removing the nonspecifically bound proteins, 151 and 114 proteins were found to bind to GroEL1 and GroEL2, respectively. Of the bound proteins, 68 are bound by both GroEL1 and GroEL2 (GroEL1/2), whereas 83 and 46 proteins bind exclusively to GroEL1 and GroEL2, respectively. Of the 58 functionally annotated GroEL1/2 co-substrates, many have functions or predicted functions related to fundamental physiological cellular processes; examples of such substrates include succinyl coenzyme A synthetase and isocitrate dehydrogenase, two key enzymes of the citric acid cycle. This result is consistent with the facts that either the groEL1 or the groEL2 gene can be deleted without affecting cellular growth, but the double deletion of groEL1 and groEL2 results in unviable cells. However, except for PilA, no proteins that have been determined to be involved in M. xanthus social behavior are found bound to both GroELs. In contrast, of those annotated proteins that are exclusively bound by GroEL1 or GroEL2, a considerable number are involved in the social behaviors of M. xanthus DK1622. For example, the frz signal transduction system is well known to play important roles in the development process of M. xanthus DK1622. The frizzy aggregation protein FrzCD is in the substrate list of GroEL1, whereas type IV pilus assembly ATPase PilB, gliding motility protein MglA, type IV pilus biogenesis protein PilM, and several proteins related to the biosynthesis of secondary metabolites are exclusively bound by GroEL2 (Wang et al., 2013). These results are consistent with the hypothesis that GroEL is an essential component and that the duplicated groEL genes have evolved to participate in various physiological processes in Myxococcus. 

Reliable secondary structures have been obtained for 36 of the 68 identified GroEL1/2 co-substrates, 38 of the 83 GroEL1- specific substrates, and 34 of the 46 GroEL2-specific substrates. It is known that proteins with β-sheets packed with α-helices have high-affinity interactions with the apical region of GroEL and are normally present as substrates of GroELs (Houry et al., 1999). As expected, most of the GroEL1/2 co-substrates contain at least one αβ domain: 31 of the 34 GroEL2-specific substrates possess at least one αβ domain, but only 27 of the 38 GroEL1- specific substrates contain an αβ domain. 

Another significant difference is the molecular sizes of the GroEL1 and GroEL2 substrates. The average molecular weights of GroEL1-specific substrates are obviously smaller than those of GroEL2-specific substrates. For example, while 51.8% of the GroEL1-specific substrate proteins are less than 40 kDa, the molecular weights of only 21.7% of the GroEL2-specific proteins are less than 40 kDa. 

It is also noted that there is no significant difference of the pI value between the GroEL1 and GroEL2 substrates (Wang et al., 2013). 

## 14.5.6 Molecular evolution of the duplicated groELs

To explore the evolution of groEL, the M. xanthus GroEL sequences have been compared with those of 10 genomesequenced bacteria, including three other myxobacteria, three actinobacteria, and three cyanobacteria. The GroEL protein sequences have been divided into five regions (i.e., an Nterminal equatorial region, an N-intermediate region, an apical region, a C-intermediate region, and a C-terminal equatorial region) (Brocchieri and Karlin 2000). Among them, the two intermediate regions possess the highest levels of conservation (97.7% and 97.2% identities) between M. xanthus DK1622 GroELs, while the identities for the other three regions are 81% for the N-terminal equatorial region, 75.4% for the apical region, and 62.6% for the C-terminal equatorial region, respectively. Similar sequence characteristics for the five GroEL1 and GroEL2 regions are in the four myxobacteria species (Table 14.5.1). For example, the Ka–Ks values of the N- and C-intermediate regions are very low, suggesting these two regions are highly conserved; 


Table 14.5.1 The Ka–Ks values of the five regions of the duplicated groEL genes from the four myxobacterial species.


<table><tr><td>Domains of GroEL</td><td>Ka-Ks</td></tr><tr><td>Equatorial-N region</td><td>0.28–0.39</td></tr><tr><td>Intermediate-N region</td><td>0.04–0.06</td></tr><tr><td>Apical region</td><td>0.26–0.45</td></tr><tr><td>Intermediate-C region</td><td>0.03–0.05</td></tr><tr><td>Equatorial-C region</td><td>0.59–0.77</td></tr></table>

in contrast, the other three regions had higher Ka–Ks values, suggesting they are most likely involved in the functional divergence of GroEL1 and GroEL2. 

Bioinformatics shows that this feature is common in different bacterial species that contain duplicated GroELs. We thus studied the gene evolution and gene functions of duplicated GroELs further. A sequence alignment shows that the high Ka–Ks values of the C-terminal equatorial regions are largely due to the variability of the C-terminal tail sequences (Figure 14.5.2). For example, the C-terminal tail of GroEL1 in M. xanthus is composed of six repeated GGM motifs, similar to that of E. coli GroEL, whereas the C-terminal tail of GroEL2 is greatly different. The C-terminal region sequences between the duplicated GroELs are also substantially different. 

To clarify the structure–function relationships, a series of single region-swapping assays have been performed between the groEL1 and groEL2 genes to determine the roles of the regions and their contributions to functional divergence. The swapped regions included the N-terminal equatorial, apical, and C-terminal equatorial regions between GroEL1 and GroEL2. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/66a4b3b179c7b6f3a321816a62babe4d2bf429d3928a0099969273949cb27727.jpg)



Figure 14.5.2 Schematic diagram of duplicated GroELs in different bacterial species. Five domains are contained in GroEL (from left to right): N-terminal equatorial, N-intermediate, apical, C-intermediate, and C-terminal equatorial domain.



Red >95% Blue 80%–90% Pink 70%–80% Yellow 60%–70% Green 50%–60% Black 40%–50%


The results show that the apical domain of GroEL1 is essential for the development process of M. xanthus DK1622, while the apical domain of GroEL2 is irreplaceable for the predation process (Wang et al., 2013). Besides, the C-terminal equatorial region also plays functions in social activities (i.e., the GroEL1 C-terminal region for the development process and the GroEL2 C-terminal region for the predation process). Accordingly, the apical region and the C-terminal equatorial region are the determinants of substrate preference, thus causing the functional divergence of the duplicated chaperonins with respect to development and predation in M. xanthus. Conversely, the Nterminal equatorial region has almost no effect. 

The repeated GGM region (GGMGGMGGMGGMG-GMGM) has also been deleted from GroEL1 to determine its functions in M. xanthus DK1622. Compared with the wild-type DK1622, the mutant (YL1001) is markedly defective in development, and the sporulation ability is only 32.6% that of DK1622. In addition, the terminal three-GGM repeat in GroEL1 has been swapped with YGGDDMDY (Figure 14.5.2), the corresponding sequence in GroEL2, producing the YL1002 mutant. Similar to YL1001, YL1002 is defective in development and exhibits decreased sporulation ability (38.1% that of DK1622). These results demonstrate that the GGM repeated region is also important for GroEL1 to perform its normal functions in development.

## 14.5.7 Discussion



Goyal K, Qamra R, Mande SC. 2006. Multiple gene duplication and rapid evolution in the groEL gene: functional implications. Journal of Molecular Evolution 63(6): 781–787. 



Myxococcus xanthus DK1622 possesses a huge genome, containing multiple duplicated genes that have been proposed to have evolved for its complex social lifecycle. The results described in this chapter show that the divergent functions of duplicated GroELs result from different mechanisms in different physiological processes. The groEL expression level is the key reason for the difference in the Myxococcus groEL1- and groEL2-deletion mutants in the heat shock responses. In contrast, the functional divergence of the duplicated GroELs with respect to their roles in development and predation processes is reflected in their substrate specificities, which have been suggested to relate to the complex social behavior of Myxococcus evolutionarily. The GroEL proteins have been divided into five regions based on structural characteristics and sequence conservation. Bioinformatics analysis indicates that the duplicated GroELs from different bacteria share similar characteristics, that is, the N- and C-intermediate regions are highly conserved, suggesting that these regions have essential functions in maintaining the functional structure of GroELs, and the apical, N-terminal, and C-terminal regions are much more flexible, suggesting their possible roles in functional divergence. The region-swapping experiments in M. xanthus indicate that the functional divergence of the duplicated GroELs is caused by the apical and C-terminal regions. Furthermore, the GGM repeat at the tail of the GroEL1 C-terminal region, which is similar to that of E. coli GroEL, is also important for the distinct functions of GroEL1 in development and sporulation. These results are consistent with the positions of these two regions in the GroEL oligomer complex. GroEL is characterized as a 14-mer complex of identical 58-kDa subunits, which are arranged as two 7+7 back-to-back rings (Horwich et al., 2006). In the evolutionary process of the groEL gene, some of the extremely conservative amino acids have evolved, for example the amino acid residues that are at the adenosine triphosphate– adenosine diphosphate (ATP–ADP) and $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ binding sites, the substrate binding positions, the central cavity, within the ring monomer–monomer interaction places, intramonomer domain contacts, or interactions between rings and positions contacting the GroES co-factor (Brocchieri and Karlin, 2000). In the classic model, GroEL is rigorously dependent on dome-shaped heptamer GroES to finish the task of folding in an ATP-dependent manner (Fenton et al., 1994). In the presence of ATP, the cochaperonin GroES binds to GroEL, forming a large central cavity that serves as a chamber that encapsulates substrate proteins for correct folding through multiple cycles of binding and release (Saibil and Ranson, 2002). The apical region of GroEL is at the opening, through which substrates enter the central cavity, and the C-terminal equatorial region is at the bottom of the cavity, which is important for the enzymatic reactions. The GGM repeat sequence, which is at the C-terminus of GroEL1, also plays an important role in functional divergence. Divergent functions of duplicated GroELs, which have similar patterns of variation in different bacterial species, have thus evolved mainly via alteration of the apical and the C-terminal equatorial regions.

## Cold shock responses

# Gene regulation by cold shock proteins via transcription antitermination

## Sangita Phadtare1 and Konstantin Severinov2–4

1Department of Biomedical Sciences, Cooper Medical School of Rowan University, Camden, New Jersey, USA 

2Waksman Institute, Rutgers, The State University of New Jersey, Piscataway, New Jersey, USA 

3Institutes of Molecular Genetics and Gene Biology, Russian Academy of Sciences, Moscow, Russia 

4Skolkovo Institute of Science and Technology, Skolkovo, Russia 

## 15.1.1 Introduction

Among all of the stresses that organisms encounter in nature, temperature change – specifically, a decrease from physiologically optimal temperature – is by far the most common. Organisms cope with this stress by eliciting a cold shock response. Escherichia coli normally grows inside the mammalian gastrointestinal tract. When it is excreted, it encounters a sudden drastic temperature downshift. Having an efficient cold shock response machinery helps the bacterium to efficiently adapt to and survive at suboptimal temperatures. E. coli exhibits a robust cold shock response after a downshift of temperature from 37 to $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . 

Although a cold shock response is observed in bacteria with different preferred temperatures of growth – psychrophiles, mesophiles, and thermophiles – it has been most extensively studied using model mesophiles such as E. coli and Bacillus subtilis (for reviews, see Ermolenko and Makhatadze, 2002; Inouye and Phadtare, 2007; Phadtare, 2004; Phadtare et al., 1999, 2000; Weber and Marahiel, 2003; Yamanaka et al., 1998). E. coli but not B. subtilis or Lactobacillus lactis (Chapot-Chartier et al., 1997; Willimsky et al., 1992) exhibits a lag period of growth (termed the acclimation phase) upon temperature downshift. During this phase, several changes occur, such as reduced translation of non-cold-shock proteins, increase in negative supercoiling of DNA, slow or inefficient protein folding, decrease in membrane fluidity, and stabilization of the secondary structures of RNA and DNA. Each of these changes hinders the growth of the bacterium. In order to overcome the various detrimental effects of temperature downshift, bacteria transiently produce very high levels of cold shock proteins. This induction occurs against a background of dramatic reduction in the total protein synthesis. This phase is followed by resumption of the cell growth with restoration of synthesis of normal cellular proteins and decrease in the rate of synthesis of cold shock proteins. Examples of cold shock proteins in E. coli include a family of nine homologous proteins called the CspA family, polynucleotide phosphorylase (PNPase), RNA helicase CsdA, initiation factor IF2, transcription factor NusA, RecA, histone-like protein H-NS, DNA gyrase, and ribosome-binding factor RbfA. Analysis of cells undergoing low-temperature adaptation has shown that various cold shock proteins are produced in a defined sequence, indicating that the cold shock response is hierarchical and highly orchestrated. Some cold shock proteins are needed at the earliest stages of cold shock response, whereas others are needed at later stages and may be regulated by proteins belonging to the first group. 

## 15.1.2 Counteracting the various detrimental effects of temperature downshift

## 15.1.2.1 Cold adaptation of ribosoms

Upon cold shock, ribosomes cease to translate most cellular messenger RNAs (mRNAs), except for mRNAs of genes encoding cold shock proteins. During the growth lag period after the temperature downshift, however, cold-unadapted ribosomes are converted to cold-adapted ribosomes by associating with cold shock ribosomal factors such as RbfA and CsdA. These cold-adapted ribosomes resume translation of non-cold-shock mRNAs after the acclimation period is over (Jones and Inouye, 1996). 

## 15.1.2.2 Changes in the supercoiling of DNA

Low temperatures lead to increase in negative supercoiling of DNA. This affects a plethora of DNA-related functions, such as replication, transcription, and recombination (Krispin and Allmansberger, 1995; Mizushima et al., 1997; Wang and Syvanen, 1992). A cold shock protein DNA gyrase plays an important role at low temperatures by influencing the supercoiling of DNA. 

Cold-induced protein chaperones to assist protein folding at low temperatures: Cold shock leads to changes in certain proteins, such as reversible methylation of the cytoplasmic signaling–adaptation domain of the aspartate chemoreceptor (Tar) of E. coli (Nishiyama et al., 1999). Temperature downshift also causes protein misfolding, although it is not as severe as in the case of heat shock. Proper folding of proteins as well as refolding of cold-damaged proteins are important after cold shock (Kandror and Goldberg, 1997). Global transcript profiling of E. coli cells experiencing cold shock has revealed induction of genes coding for molecular chaperones, such as caseinolytic proteases (Clps), trigger factor (TF), and GroEL and GroES (Phadtare and Inouye, 2004; see Chapter 14.1). TF, a peptidyl prolyl isomerase that catalyzes cis/trans isomerization of peptide bonds that are N-terminal to proline residues, is induced 2–3 h after cold shock (Kandror et al., 2002; Kandror and Goldberg, 1997). Its cellular level during storage at $4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ correlates with the viability of the cells. Presumably, TF is the first chaperone to interact with the nascent polypeptide chain as it emerges from the tunnel of the 70S ribosome and thus probably plays an important role in co-translational protein folding (Blaha et al., 2003). It has been suggested that TF helps protein synthesis and folding at low temperature by associating with ribosomes and influencing the folding of newly formed protein chains (Maier et al., 2003), and by accelerating proline-limited steps (Kandror and Goldberg, 1997). 

Counteracting decrease in membrane fluidity: The loss of membrane flexibility caused by low temperatures interferes with active transport and protein secretion. In E. coli, this is counteracted by increase in the proportion of unsaturated fatty acids (UFAs) such as cis-vaccenic acid, which has a low melting point and high flexibility (homeoviscous adaptation) (Sinensky, 1974). The enzyme β-ketoacyl-acyl carrier protein (ACP) synthase II converts palmitoleic acid to cis-vaccenic acid. While the synthesis of this enzyme is not induced upon cold shock, the enzyme is activated at low temperature (Garwin and Cronan, 1980; Garwin et al., 1980). In the case of B. subtilis and cyanobacteria, cold-inducible desaturases play an important role in alteration of the degree of saturation of fatty acids in membrane phospholipids to restore membrane-related functions (Aguilar et al., 1998; Murata and Wada, 1995). 

Resolving the nucleic acid secondary structures stabilized at low temperatures: Low temperature stabilizes secondary structures in nucleic acids. Such secondary structures can interfere with transcription and translation by affecting the movement of RNA polymerase or of ribosomes, respectively, along the DNA and RNA templates. Certain E. coli proteins such as CspA and its homologs act as RNA chaperones (Bae et al., 2000; Jiang et al., 1997), resolving the secondary structures in nucleic acids and thus facilitating transcription and translation at low temperature. Both the RNA chaperoning and transcription antiterminating activity of CspA and its homologs is critical for cold adaptation of cells (Phadtare and Inouye, 2004; Phadtare et al., 2002a). In this chapter, we will focus on the transcription antitermination activity of proteins of the CspA family and its role in cold adaption of cells. 

As mentioned here, in E. coli, there are nine Csp proteins, from CspA through CspI. Among these, CspA, CspB, CspG, and CspI are cold shock inducible, CspA being the major cold shock protein produced when cells are shifted from 37 to $1 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ CspC and CspE are constitutively produced at ${ } ^ { 3 7 } \ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ and CspD is induced upon nutritional deprivation. The induction patterns of CspF and CspH are not known (for a review, see Phadtare, 2004). None of the CspA homologs appear to be singularly responsible for cold shock adaptation, for individual, double, or triple deletions of cspA, cspB, cspG, and cspI genes do not lead to cold sensitivity (Xia et al., 2001). Accumulation of CspE at low temperature in a triple deletion strain (ΔcspAΔcspBΔcspG) suggests that CspA homologs may functionally substitute for each other during cold acclimation of cells. Deletion of all four coldinducible csp genes – cspA, cspB, cspG, and cspI – results in cold sensitivity, which can be compensated by overproduction of any one of the CspA homologs except CspD, which is toxic (Xia et al., 2001). CspA homologs are widely distributed in prokaryotes, and CspA is homologous to the cold shock domain in human Y-box protein YB-1 and the Y-box proteins from other eukaryotes (Graumann and Marahiel, 1998; Wolffe, 1994). 

## 15.1.3 Cold shock induction of CspA and its homologs

The regulation of the cold shock induction of CspA and its homologs occurs at the levels of transcription, mRNA stability, and translation. 

## 15.1.3.1 Regulation of transcription of cspA

Studies using reporter gene fusions (Goldenberg et al., 1997; Mitta et al., 1997), primer extension, Northern blot analysis, and DNA microarray analysis of the global transcript profile of cold-shocked cells (Phadtare and Inouye, 2004) have shown that the steady-state amount of the cspA transcript is increased 4– 5-fold upon cold shock. Certain elements such as an AT-rich UP element located immediately upstream of the −35 promoter consensus element (Goldenberg et al., 1997; Mitta et al., 1997) and the extended −10 promoter consensus element enhance the overall level of the cspA promoter activity, but they do not specifically contribute to the low-temperature activity of this promoter. There are long $5 ^ { \prime }$ untranslated regions (5′ -UTRs) in the cspA, cspB, cspG, and cspI genes transcripts. These contain a highly conserved, 11-base sequence termed the cold box. When the cellular levels of CspA are high, it regulates its own expression by binding to the cold box (Fang et al., 1998; Jiang et al., 1996). 

## 15.1.3.2 Stabilization of cspA mRNA

The cspA mRNA is transiently and significantly stabilized immediately following cold shock. This stabilization is mainly responsible for the observed induction of the cspA mRNA at low temperature (Phadtare and Severinov, 2005a). The 5′ -UTR has been shown to be responsible for the extreme instability of cspA mRNA at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (a half-life of 12 s), but it has a positive effect on its stabilization upon cold shock (a half-life of more than 20 min) (Mitta et al., 1997). The cspA mRNA undergoes a temperaturedependent structural rearrangement at low temperature, likely resulting from stabilization of a folding intermediate that is thermodynamically unstable at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ The low-temperature structure is more efficiently translated and somewhat less susceptible to degradation than the $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ structure (Giuliodori et al., 2010). 

## 15.1.3.3 Regulation of translation of CspA

The mRNAs of CspA, CspB, CspG, and CspI contain an ATrich sequence located 14 bases downstream of their initiation codons. This element, called translation-enhancing element (TEE), supposedly enhances translation initiation in cold shock mRNAs (Qing et al., 2004). It is also present in mRNAs of ribosome-associated factors like CsdA and RbfA. The exact mechanism of the stimulatory effect of TEE on translation initiation remains unknown at present (Mitta et al., 1997; Moll et al., 2001). 

## 15.1.4 CspA homologs as transcription antiterminators

Stabilization of the secondary structures of RNA at low temperatures hinders the elongation of nascent transcripts during DNA transcription and of nascent peptides during translation of mRNA. CspA and its homologs can resolve RNA secondary structures and thus facilitate both transcription and translation at low temperature. Because of their ability to melt nucleic acids’ secondary structures, these proteins are often referred to as RNA chaperones. Hairpin structures in nascent RNA can act as transcriptional terminators (rho-independent terminators) or induce transcribing RNA polymerase to pause. Decreasing stability of such structures is known to decrease the termination efficiency and stimulate transcript elongation by reducing pausing. RNA chaperones lead to similar effects (for a review, see Stulke, 2002). The function of CspA homologs as RNA chaperones promoting transcription antitermination has been studied in considerable detail (Bae et al., 2000; Jiang et al., 1997; Phadtare et al., 2002a). The transcription antitermination activity of these proteins is essential for cold acclimation of cells (Phadtare et al., 2002a). CspA homologs from higher organisms, such as wheat Csp protein WCSP1, also exhibit the RNA chaperone activity and may thus also cause transcription antitermination (Nakaminami et al., 2006). 

Available data suggest that CspA and its homologs are generic RNA chaperones with no specificity for particular target RNAs. 

CspA and its homologs do not exhibit a high degree of specificity for binding to their RNA or DNA substrates (Jiang et al., 1997; Lopez et al., 2001; Phadtare and Inouye, 1999). In vitro, CspA binds to RNA with a low sequence specificity and a low binding affinity (Jiang et al., 1997). CspB, CspC, and CspE are able to more selectively bind RNA and single-strand DNA; however, these proteins are not highly selective with respect to binding to RNA or DNA (Phadtare and Inouye, 1999). Such low specificity should be beneficial as it allows these proteins to act as general stimulators of transcription at low temperature. 

## 15.1.4.1 Structure of Csp proteins

The three-dimensional X-ray crystallographic and nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) structures of E. coli CspA and B. subtilis CspB have been resolved (Feng et al., 1998; Newkirk et al., 1994; Schindelin et al., 1993, 1994; Schnuchel et al., 1993). The structures are very similar and are ideal for the role of these proteins as RNA chaperones. The structure consists of five antiparallel β-strands (β1 to β5) that form a β-barrel with two β-sheets. The β2 and β3 strands contain the RNA-binding motifs, RNP1 and RNP2, which include a patch of surface-exposed aromatic residues (RNP1: W11, F18, F20; and RNP2: F31, H33, and F34). These residues contribute to nucleic acid binding by intercalating between DNA and RNA bases (Feng et al., 1998; Hillier et al., 1998). While the overall surface charge is negative, positively charged amino acids surround the surface-exposed aromatic patch. Apparently, Csp proteins initially bind to an RNA molecule through electrostatic attraction and subsequently stack the aromatic side chains between the RNA bases (Graumann and Marahiel, 1998). 

## 15.1.4.2 Mechanism of nucleic acid melting by the Csps

E. coli CspE was used to study the RNA chaperone activity in transcription antitermination and cold acclimation of cells by proteins of the CspA family. In vitro, the addition of CspE leads to transcription antitermination at every rho-independent terminator tested. Systematic substitutions of individual amino acids of the aromatic patch of CspE were generated to determine the ones critical for its nucleic acid melting activity. The analysis showed that only Phe17, Phe30, and His32 are essential for nucleic acid melting activity. Analysis of melting intermediates trapped by individual substitutions showed that Phe17 and Phe30 act at the earliest stages of melting, whereas His32 acts later and is necessary for the propagation of melting (Phadtare et al., 2004). The proteins carrying substitutions of these amino acids with Arg fully retained their nucleic acid binding activity but lost the ability to cause transcription antitermination and cold acclimation of cells (Phadtare et al., 2002a,b). The corresponding His residue has also been found to be important for the nucleic acid melting activity of a CspA homolog from a higher organism in the case of wheat Csp protein WCSP1 (Nakaminami et al., 2006). 

Initial studies of CspE-induced melting used a model singlestranded DNA substrate containing a 9 bp stem and a 61 nt loop upon folding. CspE-mediated melting of this DNA substrate exhibited dependence on the concentration of the protein. Melting initiation has been observed at the loop–stem junction site at the lowest CspE concentrations used. Further propagation of melting requires higher concentrations of CspE, and complete melting is only achieved in the presence of a large excess of CspE over the DNA substrate. This suggests that melting is initiated by the binding of a single Csp molecule at the Y-shaped stem– loop junction site, with the propagation of melted area occurring because of the strain accumulating at the junction site when the number of CspE molecules bound to the topologically constrained single-stranded loop increased, “pushing” into the stem. As one Csp molecule occupies ∼6 bases, a large number of Csp molecules are needed to cover the long loop of the DNA substrate used. This hypothesis has been tested by experiments that used DNA substrates of different topologies and lengths. As predicted, substrates with shorter single-stranded loop regions are melted at dramatically lower concentrations of CspE, and the melting is indeed initiated at the stem–loop junction. It was further shown that CspE melts the stem region in two directions and does not require the continuity of the substrate’s loop. The latter result suggests that the strain hypothesis is unlikely to be correct. Available data are consistent with preferential binding of CspE at the stem–loop junction site to initiate melting, followed by the binding of additional CspE molecules to fully cover the single-stranded region of a melting substrate leading to complete melting of the stem (Phadtare and Severinov, 2005b). Model substrates with short single-stranded overhangs (6–4 nt) are melted by the binding of a single CspE molecule. 

## 15.1.5 Detection of the transcription antitermination activity of proteins in vitro and in vivo

A number of methods have been developed for the detection of nucleic acid melting and transcription antitermination activity of E. coli Csps in vitro and in vivo. These methods have been successfully used for Csps belonging to higher organisms and also non-Csp proteins that have nucleic acid melting activity (Nakaminami et al., 2006; Phadtare et al., 2002a, 2004, 2007; Phadtare and Severinov, 2005b). 

## 15.1.5.1 In vitro transcription

Stalled E. coli RNA polymerase elongation complexes containing radioactively labeled nascent transcripts of defined length were prepared by initiating transcription on a linear DNA template containing the T7 A1 promoter with one of the nucleoside triphosphates (NTPs) missing. The transcribed part of the template contains a ρ-independent terminator tR2 between the promoter and the template end. Transcription was resumed by the addition of four unlabeled NTPs in the presence or in the absence of CspE proteins, and reaction products were separated by urea-PAGE (polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis) and revealed by autoradiography and phosphorimaging analysis. The mean read-through efficiency values (RE), defined as the fraction of the run-off transcripts of the total transcripts produced, were calculated. RE was less than 30% in the control reaction in the absence of CspE, while the addition of the CspE protein resulted in increase in the RE values to 55%. 

## 15.1.5.2 In vivo transcription

An E. coli strain RL211 designed by Landick et al. (1990) can be conveniently used to demonstrate in vivo transcription antitermination activity of Csp proteins. This strain contains the cat gene preceded by a strong ρ-independent trpL terminator and is therefore sensitive to chloramphenicol. When transcription termination at trpL is reduced, the cat gene is expressed, and the cells become resistant to chloramphenicol. The RL211 cells overproducing CspE form colonies on chloramphenicol-containing medium. 

Fluorescent molecular beacons: A molecular beacon is a single-stranded nucleic acid molecule with a stem–loop structure. A fluorescent moiety is attached to the end of one arm, and a nonfluorescent quenching moiety is attached to the end of the other arm. The stem keeps these two moieties in close proximity to each other, resulting in efficient quenching of the fluorophore fluorescence. Resolution of the stem–loop structure by a protein such as CspE results in a movement of the two arms away from each other, causing the separation of the fluorophore and the quencher. Because the fluorophore is no longer in close proximity to the quencher, its fluorescence strongly increases. 

$K M n O _ { 4 } \ p r o b i n g \colon \mathrm { K M n O } _ { 4 }$ probing can be used with DNA substrates and allows monitoring the extent of melting, as the thymine residues in the melted stem can be clearly visualized allowing analysis of different stages of the melting process. ${ \mathrm { K M n O } } _ { 4 }$ is specific for thymines in single-stranded form. $\mathrm { K M n O _ { 4 } }$ -modified thymines can be cleaved by piperidine, and the cleavage sites can be revealed, after electrophoretic separation, by autoradiography. In the absence of a RNA chaperone like CspE, the thymines in the loop region of a model substrate are accessible to modification, whereas thymines in the stem are protected. The addition of increasing concentrations of CspE leads to gradual melting, making thymine residues in the stem accessible to $\mathrm { K M n O } _ { 4 }$ modification. 

## 15.1.6 Regulation of genes by transcription antitermination activity of Csps

Global transcript analysis of profiles of cells carrying deletion of cspE and cspC as well as cells individually overexpressing the wild-type CspE–CspC proteins or a nucleic acid melting–defective CspE mutant revealed groups of genes whose expression (i) is regulated by CspC and CspE at physiological temperature or cold shock conditions and (ii) depends on the nucleic acid melting function of CspE. Bioinformatic analysis revealed that many genes from the second group contain promoter-proximal sequences that can block transcript elongation and may be targeted by the nucleic acid melting function of CspE (see the schematic model in Figure 15.1.1). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/aebe6640da4327006f8fe2e8ab1450e475a13087a82fbacd6ef466a6bb1da0bf.jpg)



Figure 15.1.1 Schematic representation of how the RNA melting function of CspA-family proteins relieves transcript elongation blocks at low temperature.


Genes whose products are associated with membrane-related functions such as malE and malK are induced 9–10-fold at low temperature and also in the cells, overproducing CspC and CspE at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Conversely, they are drastically downregulated in cells carrying a double deletion of CspC and CspE. The genes involved in stress response such as dps (DNA-binding protein Dps), katG (catalase hydroperoxidase I), rpoS (RNA polymerase sigma factor RpoS), uspA (universal stress protein), or encoding protein chaperones such as mopA (GroEL) and mopB (GroES) are also induced significantly at low temperature and by the overexpression of CspC or CspE at 37 ◦C. Except for rpoS and uspA, these genes also showed dependence on the nucleic acid melting activity of Csps. The rpoS and uspA mRNAs require CspE binding for stability and do not depend on its nucleic acid melting activity (Phadtare and Inouye, 2001). Certain genes involved in membrane-related functions such as hdeA, hdeB, and mmuP (S-methylmethionine permease), and genes involved in cell metabolism such as pyrB (aspartate carbamoyltransferase), show dependence on Csp proteins for their basal expression levels at low temperature but not at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Phadtare et al., 2006). 

Further analysis has been carried out to bioinformatically search for the presence of possible rho-independent terminators inside and/or in front of genes activated by CspE–CspC overproduction. This search was performed using a thermodynamic model of transcription elongation that reliably predicts known intrinsic terminators as well as sequences that may stall transcription elongation without causing transcript release (Tadigotla et al., 2006). In the model, the free energy (and, therefore, stability) of the RNA polymerase elongation complex located at any position along the genomic DNA is defined by three parameters: transcript length, the translocation state of the elongation complex, and the transcription bubble configuration. The advantage of this algorithm is that it is based only on the free energy criterion and does not make any assumptions regarding the terminator structure (i.e., a stem–loop followed by a poly(U) track). As mentioned in this chapter, it has been found that several Csp-responsive genes, such as malEFG, citX, hdeAB, mmuP, tnaCAB, fruBKA, dcu–fumB, pyrBI, treBC, rbsDACBK, motAB–cheAW, feoAB, appY, fes–entF– fepE, fhuF, and sfa, indeed show the presence of high-scoring sequences, although most differed from canonical intrinsic terminator structures. In vitro testing of one such sequence in a transcription assay confirmed its ability to stall transcription, and stalling was decreased in the presence of CspE (Phadtare et al., 2006). Additional studies will be necessary to define the exact nature of transcription blocks exerted by sequences revealed by this search algorithm. 

## 15.1.7 Transcription antitermination by other proteins of the cold shock domain family

The common protein fold of CspA homologs is named the cold shock domain (CSD). The structure of the CSD is very similar to the S1 domain structure. On the basis of this similarity, the CSD and S1 domains are grouped into the OB (oligomer binding) fold (Murzin, 1993). S1 is a ribosomal protein and is essential for initiation of translation of several mRNAs, especially those containing long 5′ UTRs likely to form secondary structures. It has been suggested that the S1 may melt nucleic acid secondary structures in UTRs (Kolb et al., 1977) and thus facilitates translation initiation (Sukhodolets et al., 2006; Tzareva et al., 1994). S1-like domains are also present in PNPase, translation initiation factors IF1 and IF2, NusA, RNase E, and other nucleic acid–binding proteins (Bycroft et al., 1997). The observations that the cold-sensitive phenotype of an E. coli strain harboring a quadruple deletion of csp genes is complemented by expression of the PNPase S1 domain (Xia et al., 2001) and that heterologous expression of E. coli IF1 in B. subtilis suppresses growth defects of a cspB cspC double deletion mutant suggested functional similarity between the CSD and S1 domain proteins (Weber et al., 2001).

Translational initiation factor IF1 is an essential 71 amino acid long S1 domain protein (Cummings and Hershey, 1994). The three-dimensional structure of IF1 closely resembles that of CspA (Sette et al., 1997). IF1 can also compensate for the absence of Csps, suggesting common biochemical functions. Indeed, IF1 has an RNA chaperone activity and acts as transcription antiterminator in vivo and in vitro (Phadtare et al., 2007). However, neither of these activities is essential and may not be required for the role of IF1 in translation.

# Metagenomic analysis of microbial cold stress proteins in polar lacustrine ecosystems

Hyunmin Koo, Joseph A. Hakim, and Asim K. Bej 

Department of Biology, University of Alabama at Birmingham, Alabama, USA 

## 15.2.1 Introduction

Polar ecosystems comprise the northernmost regions of the Americas, Russia, Greenland, Norway, Sweden, Finland, and the southernmost continent, Antarctica, and are only a fraction of the total cold and other extreme environments (Priscu et al., 2005). Yet, these environments have been probed for key insights into bacterial cold adaptation. Polar ecosystems consist of a variety of ecological niches, which impose unique survivorship challenges to the residing bacteria. Microbial communities have been documented in freshwater, saline, and hypersaline polar lake environments, often residing up to 4 m below ice sheets (Christner et al., 2014; Gilbert et al., 2004; Lanoil et al., 2009). Also, the sea ice environments impose survivorship challenges to the inhabiting microbial communities, requiring those communities to withstand temperatures that fall below $- 2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Thomas and Dieckmann, 2002). 

With polar ecosystems, there is a commonality between each niche with regard to bacterial stress – that the inhabiting bacteria must withstand extreme cold stress. Examples of cold stress challenges are (i) reductions in enzymatic reaction rates, (ii) decreased substrate transport, (iii) increased membrane rigidity, (iv) conformational changes in key biochemical molecules, and (v) ice damage to the intracellular components (D’Amico et al., 2006; Rodrigues and Tiedje, 2008; Shivaji and Prakash, 2010). 

It has been recognized that two general classes of cold-adapted extremophiles exist: psychrophilic, with an optimal growth preference of less than $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ and psychrotolerant, which prefer temperatures between 20 and $2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (De Maayer et al., 2014). In the event of rapid temperature decrease, certain mesophilic bacteria will express a cold-induced response, upregulating a suite of genes that retain homeostatic function to the bacteria (D’Amico et al., 2006). However, genetic expression differs in those extremophile bacteria that are persistently exposed to cold environments, as some cold-related genes may be expressed constitutively (Mazzon et al., 2012). In addition to the specialized cold-induced proteins, several general stress-responsive proteins have been reported to undergo up- or downregulation at cold temperatures. Importantly, there is a need to understand the processes that cold-adapted bacteria play in their respective environments, and specifically, those processes involved in response to the cold stress have garnered attention (Giudice et al., 2010). For this, a determination of a functional profile of bacteria inhabiting specific Antarctic niches would be advantageous to shed light on the contributions to their ecosystems. 

The field of environmental microbiology has undergone revolutionary advancements in methodology for understanding microbial biomass activity and diversity. Historically, established laboratory practice relied on the cultivability of microbes, which led to the allocation of metabolic profiles and environmental significance through biochemical testing (Vaz-Moreira et al., 2011). Therein lay crucial limitations to the culture-dependent means of characterization, as current estimates still affirm that as much as 99% of environmental microorganisms fail to grow on nutritionally defined culture media. Lack of cultivability of the “great majority” limits a comprehensive understanding of the microbial role in ecosystem functioning (Pham and Kim, 2012; Vartoukian et al., 2010). 

For a more comprehensive determination of the functional profiles of microbial communities, the metagenomics approach has been an instrumental development in the field of environmental microbiology, advancing rapidly with the accomplishments of NGS (next-generation sequencing) (Bragg and Tyson, 2014; Schuster, 2007). 

Metagenomics refers to the use of molecular technology to collectively sequence multiple microbial genomes, obtained from a particular sampling site (Ji and Nielsen, 2015). Shotgun metagenomic sequencing, or random genome fragmentation and parallel sequencing of the genomes of microbial populations, has largely eliminated many of the pitfalls to understanding microbial community structure and their function in various environments (De Maayer, 2014; Lewin et al., 2013; Poretsky et al., 2014). 

Of the many niches on this Earth that have been examined using the metagenomics approach, various datasets from multiple topographies of the Antarctic continent are publicly available on the Meta Genome Rapid Annotation using Subsystem Technology (MG-RAST) (http://metagenomics.anl.gov/) server, which is an open source system based on the SEED framework for comparative genomics (Meyer et al., 2008). Applying metagenomics to Antarctic environments allows us to identify bacterial taxa information, to achieve a more comprehensive assessment of microbial diversity, along with their potential functions in their ecosystems (Cavicchioli, 2007; Pearce et al., 2012, Wilkins et al., 2013; Yau and Cavicchioli, 2011). By accessing the publicly available Antarctic datasets in MG-RAST, it is possible to compare trends of bacterial gene expression. 

In this chapter, we investigate the occurrence and distribution of cold-induced and cold-associated stress responsive protein sequences in seasonally ice-free lakes (Ace Lake and Organic Lake) and open-ocean waters (S8 and S9) in Antarctica, as well as Arctic snow metagenomics data, by using the MG-RAST server. 

## 15.2.2 Description of Arctic and Antarctic samples

Of the seasonally ice-free lakes, Ace Lake (68.4731 S, 78.1891 E) is a meromictic, permanently stratified lake located in the Vestfold Hills of East Antarctica (Coolen et al., 2004; Cowan and Tow, 2004). After the invasion of seawater, the once freshwaterfilled Ace Lake is now a permanently cold sulfidic and sulfatedepleted saline lake that is methane-saturated at the bottom (Lauro et al., 2011; Ng et al., 2010). 

Organic Lake (68.4561 S, 78.1892 E) is a seasonally ice-free, hypersaline meromictic lake, also located in the Vestfold Hills of East Antarctica (Rodriguez-Valera et al., 1991; Yau and Cavicchioli, 2011; Yau et al., 2013). The bottom of this lake is unique due to the absence of hydrogen sulfide and the high concentration of the gas dimethylsulfide, dimethylsulfoniopropionate, and polysulfides (Yau and Cavicchioli, 2011; Yau et al., 2013). 

Of the open-ocean waters, the S8 marine coastal water samples were collected near Newcomb Bay, Antarctica (66.27 S, 110.533 E), and the S9 marine water samples collected from an open-ocean area of Antarctica (63.89 S, 112.07 E) (Logares et al., 2010). The two marine water samples (S8 and S9) were previously sequenced, analyzed, and uploaded into the MG-RAST server as public data; however, specific environmental features of sample locations have not been clearly reported (Logares et al., 2010). 

From the Arctic, snowpack is considered to be chemically dynamic, interacting with different environmental compartments and inhabiting microbial communities (Maccario et al., 2014). Snowpack stores nutrients, soluble inorganic or organic matter, and contaminants (Larose et al., 2013). Thus, snow samples were collected during a springtime field campaign in Ny-Alesund, Svalbard, Norway (78◦92 N, 11◦94 E), and then sequenced, analyzed, and uploaded into the MG-RAST server as public data (Larose et al., 2013). Filtering and the bioinformatics analyses of the cold-adaptive and cold-induced general stress-responsive proteins are described in the remainder of this chapter. 

## 15.2.3 Comparative analyses of the MG-RAST metagenomics database

Nine public Antarctic metagenomics datasets were used to filter the cold-induced and cold-associated general stress-responsive proteins (a list of proteins were elaborated in this chapter, under sections 15.2.4–15.2.5). These datasets included seven from seasonally ice-free lakes (Ace Lake and Organic Lake) and two from open-ocean waters (S8 and S9). The datasets were filtered by using MG-RAST (http://metagenomics.anl.gov/), and all analyzed metagenomics datasets were uploaded by Jeff Hoffman (J. Craig Venter Institute; jhoffman@jcvi.org). The MG-RAST IDs for the seasonally ice-free lakes of Antarctica are 4443683.3, 4443679.3, 4443681.3, 4443682.3, 4443680.3, 4443684.3, and 4443685.3. The MG-RAST IDs for the openocean waters are 4443686.3 and 4443687.3. To filter specific proteins from these datasets, the MG-RAST similarity search with the SEED database (http://www.theseed.org/wiki/Main Page) was used, through user-specified parameters (>15 amino acids, >65% similarity to a subsystem, and e-value $\leq 1 0 ^ { - 5 } )$ ). Following filtering, the total number of cold shock–related protein sequences were gathered in a table using Microsoft Excel software (Microsoft, USA). 

Nine public Arctic snow metagenomics datasets (MG-RAST IDs: 4451071.3, 4451072.3, 4451069.3, 4451076.3, 4451075.3, 4451065.3, 4451068.3, 4451070.3, and 4451073.3) were analyzed and uploaded by Catherine Larose (catherine.larose@eclyon.fr). The Arctic snow datasets were filtered and gathered using the aforementioned process and parameters. 

The bioinformatics workflow to analyze the cold-adaptive genes in nine Antarctic and nine Arctic snowmelt datasets is presented in Figure 15.2.1. Briefly, the raw metagenomic sequences are subjected to quality check, and the primer and barcode sequences are trimmed, assembled, and uploaded to the MG-RAST server (http://metagenomics.anl.gov). The processed sequence files are stored on the MG-RAST server for either public or private access. Currently, 26,560 metagenome files from 865 projects are available for public access, from which we have used the publicly available Arctic and Antarctic metagenomics sequences conducted on microbial communities in Antarctic and Arctic freshwater ecosystems. After implementing the “analyze metagenome” command on the metagenome overview page, the sequences were subjected to data analysis by selecting the “all annotations” option, available within the functional abundance menu. This step allows users to set up data selection in which a comparative analysis is conducted on all of the selected sequence files gathered through various annotation sources, unanimously selecting an e-value, percent minimum identity cutoff, and minimum alignment cutoff. Following the data selection step, data visualization allows for generating a table of targeted gene or protein sequences, followed by the exporting of the data for interpretation of the analysis. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/3f9414546e6f2c3b8d25b8c02efabf11731ac16dbef0f43265bdb0a3853540ea.jpg)



Figure 15.2.1 The bioinformatics workflow describing the stepwise analysis of the metagenomics sequence data for filtering and acquiring targeted genes or proteins from microbial communities.


## 15.2.4 Cold-induced proteins (CIPs) sequences in polar samples

The distribution of cold-induced proteins (CIPs) and other coldresponsive supportive general stress protein sequences from the selected and publicly available Antarctic and Arctic freshwater metagenomics samples is included in this chapter. Given that ice is widespread in polar ecosystems, antifreeze proteins are expected to be present in the inhabiting microorganisms. Besides permanently ice-covered lakes, the lacustrine environments in polar ecosystems may also experience seasonal freeze–thaw cycles, and during summer months, ice may melt into streams, ponds and lakes. Regardless, the temperature will remain relatively low, requiring microorganisms to protect themselves by activating cold-adaptive and other cold-induced stress proteins to maintain a steady-state cellular physiology and metabolism. The occurrence, distribution and description of these proteins in the metagenomics context are listed in Table 15.2.1 and described in the following section. 

## 15.2.4.1 Antifreeze proteins (AFPs)

Ice crystal formation is a common stress that affects microbial communities in the Arctic and Antarctic. Homogeneous ice crystal formation occurs in the absence of impurities, forming an ice nucleus $\mathrm { a t } - 4 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ from which water molecules arrange in lattice (Lorv et al., 2014). However, in nature, water impurities are inherent, and will seed ice formation in a process known as heterogeneous ice nucleation, which occurs at temperatures between −15 and $- 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Kawahara, 2002). Within the cell there is strong evidence implicating the cell membrane as the likely agent for heterogeneous ice formation (Prickett et al., 2015). Affecting the onset of intracellular ice formation (IIF) are several parameters, such as cell volume, membrane surface area, and extracellular and intracellular ice nucleation temperature (Prickett et al., 2015). In order to cope with IIF, certain polar bacterial species have evolved genetic proprieties that may stifle ice growth or modify the recrystallization process of water. These genetic mechanisms have been identified to be AFPs (Davies, 2014), the production of several intracellular cryoprotective agents (CPAs) (such as trehalose) that lower the freezingpoint depression of water to preserve the bacteria, and the alteration of membrane fatty acid profile (De Maayer et al., 2014; Pricket et al., 2015; Raymond et al., 2007). AFPs represent a class of proteins that demonstrate a diverse function in the evolutionarily guided strategy that offsets ice crystal formation. These proteins have been identified in many polar organisms, including bacteria and cyanobacteria (Gilbert et al., 2004; Singh et al., 2014), fungi (Duman and Olsen, 1993), plants (Urrutia et al., 1992), insects (Duman et al., 1998), and notably fish (DeVries et al., 1970; Fletcher et al., 2001; Knight et al., 1984; Sicheri and Yang, 1995). Depending on the organism, AFPs may act by two distinct mechanisms: either to depress the thermal hysteresis and lower the freezing temperature of the surrounding aqueous environment (Gaede-Koehler et al., 2012; Kristiansen and Zachariassen, 2005), or to inhibit the recrystallization of water (Singh et al., 2014). Bacteria in particular have been observed to utilize the latter approach – as demonstrated by Singh et al. (2014) who tested bacterial AFPs in a concentration-dependent manner on ice recrystallization inhibition (IRI). 

The metagenomics database on MG-RAST revealed that AFPs in Antarctic and Arctic freshwater ecosystems are widespread. Of the Antarctic lakes, Ace Lake had a positive detection of AFP sequences, whereas Organic Lake had none. This may imply that due to the high salt concentration in Organic Lake, which is conducive to an effective freezing-point depression, the AFPs are scarce. Rapid ice-crystal growth has a profound damaging effect on cellular macromolecules in bacteria, and the presence of a higher concentration of solutes helps to lower the freezingpoint depression, leading to the poor ice-crystal structure. The metagenomics of the S8 and S9 samples had AFPs, suggesting that microorganisms attached to floating ice surfaces in Antarctic open sea waters utilize the AFP to protect the membrane and cellular macromolecules from freeze damage.

All public metagenomics data were selected and analyzed by using the MG-RAST server (http://metagenomics.anl.gov/). The description of the samples is elaborated in the text.

In addition to the AFPs, other cellular mechanisms have been identified to function in the promotion of cell viability during cold stress. These mechanisms have been identified to counterbalance the detrimental impositions of cold stress in various physiological components of the cell. One of the majorly affected cell components is the membrane. As temperature decreases, the degree of rigidity in the membrane increases (Hunger et al., 2006; Iost and Dreyfus, 2006; Wang et al., 2013). To alleviate this stress, Escherichia coli and Bacillus subtilis have been shown to produce specific products that structurally modify the membrane. In E. coli, it was observed that the fatty acids desaturase enzyme is cold inducible, functioning to incorporate an unsaturated fatty acid (palmitoleic acid) into the membrane (Carty et al., 1999; Vorachek-Warren et al., 2002). In effect, this reduces the ratio of saturated fatty acids, increasing fluidity and lowering the temperature at which phase transitions occur (Barria´ et al., 2013). B. subtilis has been observed to cope with membrane rigidity through the desaturation of existing fatty acids embedded in membrane (Aguilar et al., 2001; Albanesi et al., 2004; Barria´ et al., 2013; Tsakalidou and Papadimitriou, 2011). In addition to the AFPs and fatty acids desaturase, trehalose synthase sequences have been identified in both Antarctic and Arctic samples, which have been reported to help freezing-point depression of the cellular aqueous environment to avoid freeze damage. 

## 15.2.4.2 Cold shock and DEAD-box proteins

Investigations of CIPs in bacteria have led to the distinguishing of two different classifications of the family of proteins: cold shock proteins (CSPs) and cold-adaptive proteins (CAPs) (D’Amico et al., 2006; Kuhn, 2012). First observed in E. coli (Goldstein et al., 1990), CSPs represent the majority of Protein Data Bank (PDB) installments of CIPs (Kuhn, 2012). For those bacteria that have evolutionarily adapted to cold environments, such as the psychrophilic and psychrotolerant bacteria, comparatively little has been identified regarding the role of CIPs in upholding cell physiology. These cold-adapted bacteria will express CSPs in response to temperature depression, but will also constitutively express an additional set of CIPs, the CAPs, now adapted as housekeeping genes or proteins at depressed temperatures (Berger et al., 1996; D’Amico et al., 2006; Graumann and Marahiel, 1996). Investigation of the roles of CIPs in the physiology of cold-adapted extremophiles has not received the same degree of attention as that of mesophilic bacteria, even though psychrophilic and psychrotolerant extremophiles constitute a high percentage of extremophile bacteria. The cold shock DEAD-box proteins are RNA helicases and rapidly expressed following temperature downshift of a bacterial culture (Iost and Dreyfus, 2006). The DEAD-box proteins have been described to function coordinately with CSPs (Wang et al., 2013). The DEAD-box proteins have been shown to co-localize with the CSPs, and deletion of the DEAD-box gene results in cell death (Hunger et al., 2006). 

Arguably less attention has been allotted to the presence of CSPs and CAPs in the cold-adapted psychrophilic and psychrotolerant bacteria. Of the cold-adapted bacteria, Pseudomonas fragi has been shown to express both CSPs as well as CAPs, and the difference in the two classifications of proteins resides in the kinetics of expression in the onset of downshifted temperature (Michel et al., 1997). In response to cold, both classes of proteins are expressed rapidly. However, after induction, CSPs will be temporarily overexpressed, whereas the CAPs will remain overexpressed longer after induction (Michel et al., 1997; Panicker et al., 2010). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/bebec1f49d2938de687404a1a712f688058be51845e3acd1a55862f565292433.jpg)



Figure 15.2.2 Heatmap plot showing the relative abundance of the cold-induced and other stress protein sequences in the Antarctic and Arctic ecosystems. Public metagenomes (Antarctica MG-RAST IDs: 4443683.3, 4443679.3, 4443681.3, 4443682.3, 4443680.3, 4443684.3, 4443685.3, 4443686.3, and 4443687.3; Arctic MG-RAST IDs: 4451071.3, 4451072.3, 4451069.3, 4451076.3, 4451075.3, 4451065.3, 4451068.3, 4451070.3, and 4451073.3) were downloaded and used to generate the heatmap plot through STAMP (http://kiwi.cs.dal.ca/Software/STAMP). Larger values are represented in red, and the smaller values in white.


As expected, the CSPs under the CIPs are present in most of the Antarctic and Arctic samples used in this investigation. However, the CAPs were not registered in our search process. This is due to the fact that both CSP and CAP proteins share conserved RNP1 and RNP2 RNA-binding domains along with high identity of the rest of the amino acids when sequences are aligned (Panicker et al., 2010). The expressional differences are due to the 5′ upstream regulatory sequences that allow CSPs to have a rapid response to a downshift of temperature, whereas the CAPs have a relatively slower but sustained expression to cope better with the perpetual cold temperatures in polar environments. In addition to the CSPs, we have identified the cold shock DEAD-box proteins sequences in all Antarctic and Arctic samples, as these two classes of proteins have been reported to function coordinately as RNA helicase at cold temperatures. 

## 15.2.5 Cold-associated general stress-responsive proteins

All samples from Antarctic and Arctic ecosystems revealed several cellular general stress-responsive proteins that have also been reported to respond to cold temperatures. A list of the most prevalent general stress-responsive proteins is presented in Table 15.2.1. Jones et al. (1992) observed that CspA will also enhance the production of GyrA, a component acting on single-stranded DNA and predicted to stabilize the DNA at the point of transcription initiation (Brandi et al., 1994; Weber et al., 2001). Other proteins such as bacterial chromosome replication initiator protein (DnaA), chaperone proteins DnaK and DnaJ, DNA topoisomerases (DNA GyrA and GyrB), and recombination factors (RecA) were all abundant in microorganisms from all Antarctic and Arctic samples used in this chapter. These proteins have been reported to undergo differential expression in bacteria upon exposure to cold temperatures, helping them maintain a steady-state cellular metabolism, growth, and division in perennially cold environments with numerous challenges such as nutrient deprivation, desiccation, and enzyme function (Varin et al., 2012).

Figure 15.2.3 Relative abundance profiles of cold-induced and other stress proteins from the Antarctic and Arctic public metagenomes (Antarctica MG-RAST IDs: 4443683.3, 4443679.3, 4443681.3, 4443682.3, 4443680.3, 4443684.3, 4443685.3, 4443686.3, and 4443687.3; Arctic MG-RAST IDs: 4451071.3, 4451072.3, 4451069.3, 4451076.3, 4451075.3, 4451065.3, 4451068.3, 4451070.3, and 4451073.3) were downloaded from MG-RAST (https://metagenomics.anl.gov). Total mean proportions in the different categories are shown in the left bar graph; the upper side (blue) represents the Antarctic, and the lower side (orange) represents the Arctic samples. The colored circles (blue and orange) show the 95% confidence intervals calculated using Welch’s t-test (Bluman, 2007). Corrected q-values were calculated using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR multiple test correction (q-value <0.05) (Benjamini and Hochberg, 1995).


## 15.2.6 Comparative analyses of cold-induced and other stress proteins using bioinformatics tools

All protein sequences were compared, and the relative abundance and distribution in Antarctic and Arctic ecosystem samples are shown in Figure 15.2.2. The results showed that the CIPs and other stress protein sequences filtered through MG-RAST were found to be predominant in Antarctic samples. This could be due to the relatively low total sequences used for the Arctic metagenomes as compared with the Antarctic metagenomes. 

The metagenomics sequences from the two polar ecosystem samples were further compared for distribution and abundance by using the STAMP (http://kiwi.cs.dal.ca/Software/ STAMP). Extended Error Bar Plot, which follows the two-sided Welch’s t-test (Bluman, 2007) with a Benjamini–Hochberg false discovery rate (FDR) multiple test correction (Benjamini and Hochberg, 1995), and the significance was determined based on a 95% confidence interval (Pan et al., 2014). The result showed the cold shock family of proteins (CSPs) – cold shock DEAD-box protein A, RecA protein, AFPs, DNA gyrase subunit B, trehalose synthase, chaperone protein DnaK, chaperone protein DnaJ, and DNA gyrase subunit A – were overrepresented in Antarctica, whereas the chromosomal replication initiator protein DnaA and fatty acid desaturase were highly represented in the Arctic samples (Figure 15.2.3). 

## 15.2.7 Conclusion

The genetic mechanisms of bacterial adaptation to cold and subzero temperature environments have been well addressed by numerous scientific investigations. Polar environments offer ecosystems suitable for the study of how bacterial genetic systems contribute to coping with cold stress. Advances in genomic technologies and the “omics” sciences have progressed our knowledge and understanding of cellular cold-adaptive traits at a global scale. In this chapter, we have analyzed publicly available selected metagenomic sequence datasets from samples collected from the Antarctic and the Arctic lacustrine ecosystems, to gain collective insight into CIPs and other stress protein sequences. Our analysis revealed that Antarctic ecosystems are rich in CIPs and general cold-responsive stress proteins in bacterial communities. The Arctic samples had an abundance of a few categories of cold-responsive proteins. Our observation suggests that perhaps the Antarctic cold ecosystems offer intense stress conditions that challenge bacterial communities to manifest an upregulation of diverse sets of cold-adaptive proteins, along with other general stress proteins. We believe that these manifestations allow the bacterial communities to implement well-orchestrated survival mechanisms as well as maintain cellular growth and division.
