# Role of two-component systems in cold tolerance of Clostridium botulinum

Yagmur Derman, Elias Dahlsten, and Hannu Korkeala˘ 

Department of Food Hygiene and Environmental Health, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, University of Helsinki, Helsinki, Finland 

## 15.3.1 Introduction

In order to execute effective responses to physicochemical deviations in the environment, organisms are equipped with specific signaling mechanisms (Eriksson et al., 2002). One of the key mechanisms in bacteria transforming environmental deviations into regulatory output is the two-component system (TCS) (Krell et al., 2010). Genes encoding TCSs are found in many sequenced genomes of bacteria and other single-celled domains of life (Wuichet et al., 2010) (see Chapter 3.1). In a typical TCS, with two major components of a membrane-associated sensor kinase (SK) and its cytoplasmic cognate response regulator (RR) (Cheung et al., 2005), the signaling cascade of the TCS is initiated as the SK encounters an environmental stimulus. The activated SK undergoes adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-dependent autophosphorylation at a conserved histidine residue, and subsequent transfer of the phosphoryl group is catalyzed by the RR to its aspartate residue (Stock et al., 2000). This phosphotransfer event triggers conformational changes in the RR, hence specific response is achieved via binding of the RR to DNA (Casino et al., 2010; Gao and Stock, 2009; West and Stock, 2001). Although for many TCSs the direct physiological signal that triggers their action remains unknown, TCSs play a central role in tolerance to different stresses (see Section 3). 

Strains of Clostridium botulinum display distinct genetic, physiologic, and metabolic properties (Collins and East, 1998); thus, they pose diverse hazards in foods (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2006b). Furthermore, the capability of certain C. botulinum strains to grow at refrigeration temperatures (Graham et al., 1997) and tolerate various environmental stresses is a severe safety hazard for the modern food industry (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2006b; Peck, 1997, 2006, 2009; Peck et al., 2008). 

The availability of genome sequences and genetic manipulation tools has enhanced the understanding of stress response mechanisms in C. botulinum. However, reports are scarce on the stress response machinery. It is essential to know the mechanisms that are involved in the stress response of C. botulinum to counter the safety hazards caused by this notorious pathogen. This chapter focuses on C. botulinum and TCSs that have been shown to be involved in the cold stress response. 

## 15.3.2 Clostridium botulinum

C. botulinum is a Gram-positive, strictly anaerobic rod that forms heat-resistant subterminal spores and, most importantly, produces botulinum neurotoxin (BoNT) during vegetative growth (Cato et al., 1986). BoNT is the causative agent of the life-threatening neuroparalytic disease botulism. Strains of C. botulinum reflect diverse genetic, metabolic, and physiologic characteristics. The species is categorized into four groups (I–IV) (Collins and East, 1998; Keto-Timonen et al., 2006; Smith and Sugiyama, 1988). However, to date, the name of the species is based on the common ability of these strains to produce BoNT. On the other hand, discovery of BoNT-producing Clostridium butyricum and Clostridium baratii strains has further complicated this classification (Suen et al., 1988). 

C. botulinum strains belonging to Groups I and II are associated with botulism in humans, and those in Group III with other animals, yet no reports are available on botulism due to Group IV strains (Popoff and Bouvet, 2013). To date, seven serologically distinct BoNTs have been identified and named A through G. A recent discovery indicates a novel toxin type named H (Barash and Arnon, 2014; Dover et al., 2014; Popoff, 2014). However, decisive data on the new toxin type are pending (Johnson, 2014). 

C. botulinum Group I strains produce either single-type toxin A, B, and F, or dual toxins Ab, Af, Ba, and Bf, in which the small letter represents a lesser amount of toxin produced relative to the toxin type indicated by the capital letter (Barash and Arnon, 2014; Dover et al., 2013; Hill and Smith, 2013). In addition, strains that can produce equal amounts of A and B toxin (AB) or carry both A and B toxin genes but produce only A toxin, marked as A(B) to note the “silent” B toxin gene in brackets, have been identified (Barash and Arnon, 2014; Franciosa et al., 1994). 

The wide genetic diversity among C. botulinum strains is reflected in their physiological properties; hence, strains of different groups exhibit diverse phenotypic characteristics (Lindstrom and Korkeala, 2006; Lynt¨ et al., 1982). 

Group I C. botulinum strains are mesophilic organisms with optimum growth temperatures of $3 7 { - } 4 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ that show a high proteolytic activity (Hatheway, 1993; Hinderink et al., 2009). They form spores of high heat resistance (Stumbo et al., 1975). The generally accepted growth range for Group I strains is 10– $4 8 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Hinderink et al., 2009; Lynt et al., 1982). Their growth is repressed by a water activity $( \mathsf { a } _ { \mathrm { w } } )$ value of 0.94, which corresponds to 10% NaCl w/v in brine (Cliver et al., 2011), and growth is suppressed at pH 4.6 (Smelt et al., 1982). Group II C. botulinum strains are psychrotrophic and can grow at temperatures as low as 3 ◦C (Eklund et al., 1967; Graham et al., 1997; Schmidt et al., 1961). The optimum growth temperature for Group II strains is between 26 and $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Derman et al., 2011; Smith and Sugiyama, 1988). Group II C. botulinum strains cannot utilize amino acids as their sole energy source, therefore they are named nonproteolytic C. botulinum. On the other hand, they are highly saccharolytic and ferment a number of sugars (Cato et al., 1986). The growth-restraining water-phase NaCl concentration and $\mathtt { a } _ { \mathrm { w } }$ for Group II strains are 5% and 0.97, respectively (Smith and Sugiyama, 1988). Growth is not reported below pH 5.0 (Segner et al., 1966). 

Foods contaminated by C. botulinum spores pose diverse patterns of risk in food processing and safety, due to the physiological differences between strains of Group I and II C. botulinum (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2006b). Traditionally, Group I strains are associated with home-canned or fermented foods. To overcome the safety hazard by Group I C. botulinum in the canning process, botulinum cook conditions $( 1 2 1 . 1 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 3 min) have been adopted to reduce the risk by a factor of $1 0 ^ { 1 2 }$ (Stumbo et al., 1975). Growth of proteolytic Group I strains has not been reported below $1 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , hence proper storage temperatures will prevent growth and toxin formation by these strains in foods. However, temperature abuse during storage has caused botulism related to Group I C. botulinum in commercially produced foods (Angulo et al., 1998; Chou et al., 1988; Jalava et al., 2011; Kalluri et al., 2003; Louis et al., 1988). Correct storage at refrigeration temperatures will eliminate the risk related to Group I C. botulinum. In contrast, these temperatures are not sufficient to remove the food safety hazard caused by Group II C. botulinum. A high prevalence of Group II C. botulinum has been reported in fish, especially in vacuum-packaged fish products (Hyytia¨ et al., 1998). The processing temperatures of mildly treated modern food products are often not enough to eliminate all Group II C. botulinum spores (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2003), thus allowing probable food safety hazards. Particularly, abuse in the storage temperature of vacuum-packaged hot-smoked fish products has been reported to cause foodborne botulism (King et al., 2009; Korkeala et al., 1998; Lindstrom¨ et al., 2006a). Therefore, such products should be stored at $3 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ or lower (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2006b). 

## 15.3.3 Cold tolerance of C. botulinum

When the changes in the ambient temperature become harmful, cells mediate defensive responses (Klinkert and Narberhaus, 2009). When an exponentially growing bacterial culture at its optimum growth temperature is exposed to a rapid temperature downshift, the growing cell culture generally undergoes a growth arrest. The sudden temperature decrease in the environment affects the membrane-associated transport and secretion mechanisms, reduces membrane fluidity, hampers efficient transcription and translation by stabilization of the secondary structures of nucleic acids, leads to a less competent ribosomal machinery than at optimum growth temperature, leads to improper protein folding, and perturbs enzymatic activity in the cell (Bae et al., 2000; Phadtare, 2004; Weber and Marahiel, 2003). 

The growth arrest allows cells to adapt and alter their protein synthesis accordingly to cold stress. At this repressed growth phase, general protein synthesis is dramatically inhibited, but synthesis of cold-induced proteins (CIPs) and cold acclimatization proteins (CAPs) is increased (Weber and Marahiel, 2003; see Chapter 15.2). Finally, production of CIPs decreases, and the acclimated cells resume growth. The CIPs are the prompt response to cold shock and are transiently induced, whereas CAPs are constantly present at high levels in the cold-shocked cells (Jones et al., 1987; Phadtare, 2004). 

Cold shock induces the expression of a set of highly conserved small proteins called cold shock proteins (CSPs; see Chapter 15.1). At suboptimal temperatures, RNA secondary structures become stable and decelerate transcription elongation and movement of ribosomes on RNA (Phadtare, 2004). These transiently induced CSPs appear to be RNA chaperones that bind single-stranded RNA, hence destabilizing the RNA secondary structures and regulating ribosomal translation, the rate of RNA degradation, and termination of transcription (Ermolenko and Makhatadze, 2002; Horn et al., 2007). In Group I C. botulinum, three CSPs (CspA–C) have been identified, and especially cspB has been proposed to influence the growth at suboptimal temperature (Soderholm ¨ et al., 2011). Furthermore, transcriptomic analysis of the cold shock response of C. botulinum indicates a significant induction of the CspA- and CspB-encoding genes (Dahlsten et al., 2014). 

During cold shock, the elastic liquid-crystalline state of the membrane changes to a rigid gel-like state, which results in reduced lipid molecular dynamics (Shivaji and Prakash, 2010). Bacteria confront this effect by changing the lipid composition from saturated fatty acid (FA) to an increased proportion of unsaturated FA in the membrane, thus restoring fluidity (Russell et al., 1995; Suutari and Laakso, 1994). 

The two major ways of achieving this modification are through de novo synthesis of unsaturated FAs (Suutari and Laakso, 1994) and desaturation of existing FAs (Los and Murata, 1998; Weber and Marahiel, 2003). In the latter case, the des gene is involved in the process. This gene encodes oxygen-dependent Δ5-desaturase, which is under the control of a cold-inducible TCS, DesK–DesR, which was identified first in Bacillus subtilis (Aguilar et al., 1998, 1999, 2001; Altabe et al., 2003). It is likely that the physicochemical state of the membrane is utilized as a thermosensor by cells. Therefore, a change in the lipid domains that surround the membrane-embedded DesK activates this protein, hence phosphorylating the cognate RR DesR. Subsequently, the phosphorylated DesR induces the expression of Δ5-desaturase (Aguilar et al., 2001). This system is oxygendependent and thus merely utilized by aerobic bacteria (Altabe et al., 2003). Nevertheless, membrane-mediated temperature sensing through TCS may also be used by C. botulinum. In fact, a cold-induced TCS, CBO0366–CBO0365, has been reported in C. botulinum ATCC 3502, and mutational analysis has indicated its importance in the cold shock response (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). In C. botulinum, which is strictly anaerobic, strong induction of FA biosynthesis genes has been reported after cold shock (Dahlsten et al., 2014), and increased levels of unsaturated FA have been observed when the growth temperature is lowered from $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ to $8 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Russell et al., 1995). In addition, temperature-dependent adjustment of the membrane lipid composition has been reported for Group II C. botulinum (Evans et al., 1998). However, the molecular basis underlying this modification is unknown. 

Another mechanism employed by bacteria to overcome cold stress–related disruption of RNA metabolism is bacterial DEAD-box RNA helicases that are involved in destabilization of cold-induced RNA secondary structures (Vakulskas et al., 2014; see Chapter 15.2). The importance of DEAD-box RNA helicases in cold stress has been documented in the Gram-positive model organism B. subtilis (Hunger et al., 2006) and in foodborne pathogens, such as Listeria monocytogenes (Markkula et al., 2012), Yersinia pseudotuberculosis (Palonen et al., 2012), and Bacillus cereus (Pandiani et al., 2010). In C. botulinum, a rapid induction of one of the DEAD-box RNA helicase–encoding genes (cbo2802) results in an over four-fold upregulation 1 h after a cold shock observed in a DNA microarray analysis (Dahlsten et al., 2014). 

An additional strategy of bacteria for overcoming cold stress is to accumulate low-molecular-weight molecules, such as polyols, polyamines, sugars, and amino acid derivatives. These molecules are called compatible solutes, and they protect cells from cold or hyperosmotic stress by preventing denaturation and aggregation of proteins (Shivaji and Prakash, 2010). In C. botulinum, compatible solute uptake mechanisms and putative choline–carnitine–betaine transporters are significantly induced after cold shock, whereas glutamic acid, arginine, and lysine have been speculated to be utilized as compatible solutes by C. botulinum ATCC 3502 under cold stress (Dahlsten et al., 2014). 

## 15.3.4 Cold stress–related two-component systems in C. botulinum

The genomes of human pathogen Group I C. botulinum ATCC 3502 and Group II Beluga strain contain 78 and 60 putative TCS-encoding genes, respectively (Sebaihia et al., 2007; Ulrich and Zhulin, 2010; see Section 3). This section summarizes the TCSs that have been shown to play a role in cold tolerance of C. botulinum. 

## 15.3.4.1 The role of the two-component system CBO0366–CBO0365 in cold tolerance of C. botulinum ATCC 3502

The two-component system CBO0366–CBO0365 of C. botulinum ATCC 3502 belonging to Group I has been shown to be significantly induced upon rapid temperature downshift from the optimal $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ to $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). In the cold-shocked culture, the relative messenger RNA (mRNA) levels of cbo0365 are 1.2 to 2.7-fold induced (p <0.05) at every measured time point (1 min, 30 min, 2 h, and 5 h) in relation to the expression level prior to cold shock, with the maximum level being measured 2 h after the shock (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). Similarly, 1.6 to 3.3-fold higher $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ relative mRNA levels were measured for cbo0366 in relation to the level prior to cold shock, with the maximum level again being detected 2 h after cold shock (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). Moreover, the genes encoding this TCS are the most strongly induced TCS-related genes upon cold shock in a global transcriptomic analysis of the ATCC 3502 cold shock response (Dahlsten et al., 2014). No such induction was observed for cbo0365 or cbo0366 in growth at optimal temperature; instead, both genes were strongly downregulated prior to entry into the stationary phase under these conditions (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). 

The increased expression of both of the CBO366–CBO365 TCS genes after cold shock suggests a need to produce more of the system components, possibly to ensure more effective function of the sensing machinery and subsequently increased capability to respond to cold-induced stress. The finding that the expression levels of both genes remained substantially elevated under cold conditions when compared with cultures at optimal temperature, under which condition both genes underwent strong downregulation much earlier, supports the theory that increased amounts of TCS components may be required for an optimal immediate response to occur. In addition, stabilization of mRNA under cold temperature might have an effect on the expression levels. 

## 15.3.4.2 Inactivation of either cbo0365 or cbo0366 impairs growth at 15 ◦C but not at 37 ◦C

To study the role of cbo0366–cbo0365 genes in the growth of C. botulinum ATCC 3502 at low temperature, the growth characteristics of cbo0365 or cbo0366 single insertional knockout mutants (Heap et al., 2007, 2010a,b) were compared to the wild-type ATCC 3502 strain at $3 7 { \ ^ { \circ } C } , 2 0 { \ ^ { \circ } C } ,$ , and $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). Both cbo0365 and cbo0366 knockout mutants exhibit a significantly slower growth rate and achieve a lower final optical density at both 15 and $2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ when compared to the wild-type culture (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). The most prominent differences between the wild-type strain and the two mutants have been observed at $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ which is near the minimum growth temperature of the ATCC 3502 strain (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). At ${ 3 7 } ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ both mutants show similar growth characteristics to the wild-type strain. These observations suggest a central role for the CBO0366–CBO0365 TCS in response and adaptation to low ambient temperature in Group I C. botulinum. 

## 15.3.4.3 Overexpression of cbo0366 enhances growth of C. botulinum ATCC 3502 wild type at low temperature

An overexpression vector pMTL82153 (Heap et al., 2009) harboring the coding sequence (CDS) of either cbo0365 or cbo0366 under the strong constitutive fdx promoter was introduced into the ATCC 3502 wild-type strain to investigate the effect of artificially increasing the amount of TCS components on cold tolerance of C. botulinum ATCC 3502 (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). The strain harboring pMTL82153–cbo0366 both grew more rapidly and achieved a higher optical density than a vector-only control strain at $1 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , suggesting that higher expression of cbo0366 may assist in growth at low temperature (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). However, introduction of pMTL82153–cbo0365 was not successful, suggesting that excess amounts of this regulatory protein may be toxic to the cell (Lindstrom¨ et al., 2012). 

## 15.3.4.4 Inactivation of cbo0365 markedly alters the C. botulinum ATCC 3502 transcriptome

The CDSs under putative regulation by the CBO0365 response regulator have been identified by comparing the transcriptomes of the wild-type ATCC 3502 and cbo0365 mutant strains during growth at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and 1 h after a temperature downshift to $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Dahlsten et al., 2014). The analysis was performed using DNA microarrays based on the ATCC 3502 genome (Sebaihia et al., 2007). Genes expressed significantly differently (false discovery rate (FDR) < 0.05) and markedly less or more (median log ratio of $< - 2 . 0 \ \mathrm { o r } > 2 . 0 )$ in the cbo0365 mutant versus the wild-type strain were considered to be under the regulation of cbo0365. 

${ \mathrm { A t } } 3 7 ^ { \circ } { \mathrm { C } } ,$ a total of 150 chromosomal CDSs displayed a significantly different expression pattern between the cbo0365 mutant and the wild-type strain, whereas 1 h after temperature downshift to $1 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ the number of differentially expressed chromosomal CDSs was 141 (Dahlsten et al., 2014). Whenever a CDS was markedly less or more expressed in the cbo0365 mutant than in the wild type at only one temperature, a similar but weaker change (median $\log _ { 2 }$ ratio between −2.0 and 2.0) was observed at the other (Dahlsten et al., 2014). 

The chromosomal CDSs less expressed in the cbo0365 mutant than in the wild type at both $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ were arranged in six loci, and included cbo0043 encoding a putative RNA polymerase sigma factor, cbo0344 encoding a metallopeptidase, cbo3197–cbo3199 and cbo3200–cbo3202 related to acetone–butanol–ethanol (ABE) fermentation, 16 CDSs related to prophage 1, and 49 CDSs related to prophage 2 (Dahlsten et al., 2014). The chromosomal CDSs significantly more highly expressed in the cbo0365 mutant than in the wild type were arranged in 32 loci. These included ones putatively encoding proteins with predicted functions in sporulation (cbo0069), multidrug resistance (cbo0072), pantothenate biosynthesis (cbo0422–cbo0425), fatty acid biosynthesis (cbo0502), arsenical resistance (cbo0753–cbo0756), glucoside uptake (cbo0880–cbo0883), motility (cbo2226 and cbo2227), phosphate binding and transport (cbo2521–cbo2525), and dehydrogenation (cbo2850–cbo2852) (Dahlsten et al., 2014). 

Mutational analysis of the identified genes and components of related metabolic pathways suggested novel roles for ABE fermentation, resistance to arsenic, and phosphate uptake-related genes in cold tolerance of C. botulinum (Dahlsten et $a l . ,$ 2014). Hypothetically, conversion of the central ABE fermentation acyl-CoA intermediates into structurally closely related branched-chain fatty acid (BCFA) primers could serve as means to initiate BCFA synthesis in clostridia. Thus, the cold-sensitive phenotype observed in the cbo3202 and cbo3199 mutants could be attributed to the lack of putative BCFA synthesis precursors (Dahlsten et al., 2014). 

To further characterize the role of the ABE fermentation pathway components in cold tolerance of C. botulinum ATCC 3502, mutants of cbo1407 and cbo2847 putatively encoding butanol dehydrogenase and CoA-transferase A, respectively, were constructed. Mutation of cbo1407 resulted in slower growth at $1 7 \ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , suggesting the importance of solvent formation at low temperature. The cbo2847 encoding CoA-transferase A is related to acid reassimilation after a switch to solventogenesis in C. acetobutylicum and coupled to acetone production with acetoacetate decarboxylase (Lehman et al., 2012). However, since the acetoacetate decarboxylase–encoding adc is missing from the genome of ATCC 3502, the functionality of the CoA-transferase A remains to be confirmed in C. botulinum (Figure 15.3.1). 

Nevertheless, inactivation of cbo2847 resulted in a coldsensitive phenotype similar to the cbo1407 mutant, suggesting a role for this enzyme in tolerance to low temperature in C. botulinum ATCC 3502 (Dahlsten et al., 2014). 

## 15.3.4.5 The two-component system CBO2306–CBO2307 is important for cold adaptation of C. botulinum ATCC 3502

During the growth of C. botulinum ATCC 3502 at optimal temperature $( 3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ , the relative expression of the TCS CBO2306– 2307 encoding genes cbo2306 and cbo2307 is significantly $\left( p < 0 . 0 1 \right)$ ) downregulated at the late logarithmic growth phase in relation to the early logarithmic growth phase (Derman et al., 2013). However, in the cold-shocked culture (rapid decrease of the culture temperature from 37 to $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in an ice-water bath), cbo2306 and cbo2307 are significantly $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ induced 30 min, 2 h, and 5 h after the cold shock (Derman et al., 2013). In addition, mutant strains with insertionally inactivated cbo2306 or cbo2307 (Heap et al., 2010a) with sense and antisense insertional orientations showed markedly impaired growth at $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in comparison to the parent ATCC 3502 strain (Derman et al., 2013). In contrast, no major differences in growth were observed between mutants and the parent ATCC 3502 strain at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/0d8657bb7c66e0c586f0b42dd87be3104790ec736e468739424fdd4757ab73e7.jpg)



Figure 15.3.1 Putative ABE fermentation pathway of C. botulinum ATCC 3502 and effect of inactivation of related components on cold tolerance. The genes putatively encoding the pathway components are inferred from homology to C. acetobutylicum ATCC 824. No homologs for C. acetobutylicum acetoacetate decarboxylase adc were discovered in the ATCC 3502 genome, suggesting inability of C. botulinum ATCC 3502 to produce acetone. Acidogenic reactions are presented with dashes, and solventogenic reactions with solid arrows; bold arrows represent the central reactions of the pathway. The existence of reactions (presented with red arrows) is uncertain in C. botulinum ATCC 3502 due to missing acetone production pathway genes. Insertional inactivation of genes crt, bcd, ctfA, and bdh (blue background) results in deteriorated growth at $1 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ .


The minimum growth temperatures of the mutant strains and wild-type strain were compared in growth on tryptone–peptone–glucose–yeast extract (TPGY) agar plates for 2 weeks (Hinderink et al., 2009; Korkeala et al., 1990). The minimum growth temperature boundaries of the mutants were significantly $( p < 0 . 0 1 )$ ) lower than those of the wild-type strain ATCC 3502, with the difference being $1 { - } 3 . 8 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Derman et al., 2013). 

Relative gene expression analysis by reverse transcription quantitative real-time polymerase chain reaction (RT-qPCR) showed significant induction of cbo2306 and cbo2307 after the cold shock in relation to nonshocked cultures, as opposed to downregulation at optimal growth temperature, suggesting that the increase in gene expression is a response to low temperature. The importance of the CBO2306–CBO2307 system was further demonstrated by impaired growth of insertional knockout mutant strains at $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ but not at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . 

## 15.3.4.6 Importance of TCS CLO3403–CLO3404 of C. botulinum Beluga E1 in cold shock response and growth at low temperatures

The expression of the CLO3403–CLO3404 TCS in the cold shock response of nonproteolytic C. botulinum Beluga E1 was studied by measuring the relative mRNA levels of clo3403 and clo3404 immediately before and 1 min, 30 min, 2 h, 5 h, and 24 h after a cold shock (rapid decrease of the culture temperature from $3 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ to $1 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in an ice-water bath) (Mascher et al., 2014). A nonshocked culture was used as a control. The RT-qPCR analysis indicated an induction of the clo3403 and clo3404 genes of 1.5- to 3.4-fold in the first 5 h after the cold shock relative to their expression at the mid-logarithmic growth phase (Mascher et al., 2014). At 24 h after the cold shock, the relative mRNA levels of both genes were significantly (p <0.05) downregulated relative to their levels at the mid-logarithmic growth phase. The nonshocked control culture showed downregulation of the TCS genes already at 2 h and 5 h in comparison to the mid-logarithmic growth phase (Mascher et al., 2014). 



Casino P, Rubio V, Marina A. 2010. The mechanism of signal transduction by two-component systems. Current Opinion in Structural Biology 20(6): 763–771. 





E botulism in France, September 2009. Euro Surveillance 14(45): 19394. 





Stumbo C, Purohit K, Ramakrishnan T. 1975. Thermal process lethality guide for low-acid foods in metal containers. Journal of Food Science 40(6): 1316–1323. 



In order to examine the role of the CLO3403–CLO3404 system in growth at low temperature $( 1 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ , insertional knockout mutants were constructed in sense and antisense insertional orientation for both clo3403 and clo3404 genes (Heap et al., 2007, 2010b). The TCS mutants showed impaired growth at $1 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ compared to the growth of the parent Beluga E1 strain. The sense and antisense insertional inactivation of clo3403 exhibited a prolonged lag phase, and the sense clo3403 mutant was unable to reach the wild-type level of cell density (Mascher et al., 2014). The clo3404 sense mutant also showed an extended lag phase, although this mutant was able to reach the wild-type level of cell density. The antisense clo3404 mutant showed slightly impaired growth compared to the wild-type Beluga E1 strain. Nevertheless, all four mutants showed significantly $( p < 0 . 0 1 )$ reduced maximum growth rates relative to the growth of the wild-type strain at $1 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ and these negative effects on growth were not observed at the optimum growth temperature of 30 ◦C (Mascher et al., 2014). In addition, the role of the CLO3403–CLO3404 system in growth at low temperature was further demonstrated by increased cell density and maximum growth rates by successful complementation of the mutations (Mascher et al., 2014).

The CBO0366–CBO0365 TCS plays a central role in the tolerance of C. botulinum ATCC 3502 to cold shock and its adaptation to low temperatures. This was demonstrated by the significantly impaired growth of both TCS mutants at $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Inactivation of cbo0365 results in marked changes in the transcriptome of C. botulinum ATCC 3502, at both optimal and low temperatures. The TCS exerts cold tolerance via direct CBO0365-mediated transcriptional control of metabolic pathways that are related to acetone–butanol–ethanol fermentation, arsenic tolerance, and phosphate uptake. The decreased cold tolerance of C. botulinum that results from the disruption of its ABE fermentation pathway could arise from impaired synthesis of precursors of branched-chain membrane lipids, or from blocked solvent formation that would hamper rapid solubilization of membrane lipids.

# Cold shock CspA protein production during periodic temperature cycling in Escherichia coli

David Stopar and Tina Ivancic 

University of Ljubljana, Biotechnical Faculty, Department of Food Science and Technology, Ljubljana, Slovenia 

## 15.4.1 Introduction

In the microbial environment, periods of constant conditions are few and far between. Escherichia coli may have a period of approximately constant thermal conditions when it is in the gut of its host organism. The conditions for E. coli, however, change dramatically when cells are exposed to the environment outside of the host. The temperature now changes on a regular diurnal or seasonal basis, as well as on less predictable time scales. 

To survive, the bacterial cell has to respond to temperature changes. The rate of response is critical. If the rate of response is very high, cells will faithfully track the environment changes, thereby spending a lot of energy. In a stochastically changing environment, this could be a waste of energy since the temperature may return to its original state within the response time. If, on the other hand, the response to environmental conditions is too slow, cells will miss the opportunity and will remain in a given physiological state even though it would be beneficial to respond (Thattai and van Oudenaarden, 2003). 

The ambient temperature in a microbial environment may vary periodically or stochastically. Theoretically, for growth in a perfectly periodic environment (i.e., constant period and amplitude), it would be beneficial for bacterial cells to have an internal oscillator that would be tuned to an external frequency (Strogatz, 1994). Cells once tuned to the period and amplitude of the external process would respond without a delay to environmental stimuli. Although this is costly as the internal oscillator is prone to intrinsic noise and requires several components, and the frequency bandwidth of the oscillator is necessarily limited, the benefits of such a device may outweigh the investment cost (Barkai and Leibler, 2000). This would allow each individual cell to respond to external change and vary its physiology accordingly. The dynamic heterogeneity is only beneficial if the rate of response to environment fluctuations is fine tuned. That is when cells are able to ignore brief variations in their local environments, but respond to persistent external changes. It is therefore proposed that, in a dynamically heterogeneous population, bacterial cells are able to enhance their net growth rates by anticipating and exploiting environmental changes, perpetuating cellular states that seem disadvantageous at one time but will prove advantageous at later times. 

The E. coli response to periodic temperature cycling seems to be an excellent model system to test the idea that bacteria can anticipate and exploit environmental changes. Both cold and heat shock responses with corresponding proteins and their functions have been described in detail (Chung et al., 2006; Yamanaka, 1999; see Section 13 and Chapter 15.4). The genetic regulatory network in response to temperature up- or downshift is known (Gualerzi et al., 2003; Schumann, 1996). Recently, the physiological response of bacterial cells to periodic temperature fluctuations has been experimentally tested (Ivancic et al., 2009). The results suggest that the population of E. coli cells is able to fine tune its physiological state in a perfectly periodic environment, with some unexpected outcomes. 

## 15.4.2 Materials and methods

E. coli strain K-12 was grown in lysogeny broth (LB) medium. Cells were first incubated for 1 h at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ , then they were transferred to $8 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 1 h, transferred back to ${ 3 7 } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ and so on to the end of the incubation time. The two temperatures represent the optimal temperature for growth $( 3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ and the minimal temperature that sustains growth $( 8 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ) \mathrm { o f } E .$ . coli (Shaw et al., 1971). During one temperature cycle, cells spent approximately 1 h at temperatures known to induce the cold shock response (Jones et al., 1987). 

The lipid composition of temperature-cycled cells was determined by gas chromatography on a Shimadzu GC-14A gas chromatograph equipped with a flame ionization detector (FID). Lyophilized cells were transesterified with HCl–methanol (Dionisi et al., 1999). Fatty acid methyl esters were separated on a capillary column with a nonpolar stationary phase (Equity-1, Supelco, 28046-U). A temperature gradient was set up at 4 ◦C/min starting at $1 5 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and ending at $2 5 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Standard fatty acid calibration mixtures were used for fatty acid identification. The ratios of total unsaturated fatty acids (UFAs) to saturated fatty acids (SFAs) were calculated. 

Total bacterial RNA was isolated using the DNase I, RNA Protect, and RNeasy kits (Qiagen, USA) according to the manufacturer’s instructions. For quantitative real-time polymerase chain reaction (PCR), RNA was reverse transcribed using random hexamers (Fermentas, Lithuania) by ImPromII reverse transcriptase (Promega, USA). The forward primer $5 ^ { \prime } -$ GGCTTCGGCTTCATCACTCCTG-3′ and the reverse primer 5′ -TACCAGCTGCCGGGCCTTTAG-3′ were constructed to quantify cspA transcript levels. PCR reactions were performed with the following parameters: $9 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 15 min, 40 cycles of denaturation at $9 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 s, primers annealing at $5 6 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 20 s, and extension at $6 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 25 s. The relative amounts of cspA transcripts were calculated by the $\Delta \mathrm { C } _ { \mathrm { q } }$ method (Pffafl, 2006). Messenger RNA (mRNA) transcript increase or suppression was expressed relative to the initial transcript levels at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ before the onset of temperature cycling. 

For Western blot, assays cells were lysed by sonication. Cell debris was removed by centrifugation. Proteins were separated by sodium dodecyl sulfate polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (SDS-PAGE) using a 12% polyacrylamide gel and were transferred onto a nitrocellulose membrane (Whatman, UK) using the wet method. Mouse anti-FLAG (Sigma-Aldrich, USA) was used as the primary antibody (1:1000), and horseradish peroxidase–conjugated goat antimouse IgG antibodies (Jackson ImmunoResearch, USA) were used as secondary antibodies (1:10000). An enhanced chemiluminescence detection kit (Pierce, USA) was used for protein detection on the membranes. The samples were scanned by a G BOX:HR CCD camera (Syngene, India) and analyzed with GeneTools (Syngene). Protein increases or suppression were expressed relative to the initial protein levels at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ before the onset of temperature cycling. 

## 15.4.3 Lipid response of E. coli to periodic temperature fluctuations

A generally accepted homeoviscous model states that the E. coli membrane consistently adapts to low or high temperature by changing the ratio of UFAs to SFAs (Sinensky, 1974). As cells are growing at increasing temperatures, there is an increasing tendency to incorporate longer and more SFAs into phospholipids. This enables synthesis of membranes whose lipids have a constant fluidity at the temperature of growth – a process dubbed homeoviscous adaptation. This model has been recently challenged when E. coli cells were periodically oscillated between 8 and $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Ivancic et al., 2009). 

As shown in Figure 15.4.1, during the first three temperature cycles, the ratio of UFAs to SFAs monotonically increased from 0.65 to 1.42. There was no decrease of the UFA–SFA ratio at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ as predicted by homeoviscous adaptation. The membrane fluidity of periodically cycled cells closely follows that of lowtemperature membrane for the first three temperature cycles. With a progressing number of temperature cycles $\left( n > 3 \right)$ , bacterial membrane reduced its fluidity with no significant effect of high or low temperature on the UFA–SFA ratio. The UFA– SFA ratio at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ at the end of incubation (1.05) was very different from that of cells that were grown constantly at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (0.18). The results may suggest that cell membrane has become conditioned not to respond to high temperatures during temperature fluctuations. To further test the idea, the incubation time at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ was increased after cells have completed three or five temperature cycles. As given in Figure 15.4.1, prolonged incubation at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ had no significant effect on cells in the stationary phase. The situation was slightly different in physiologically young cells. When cells completed three temperature cycles, prolonged incubation at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ decreased membrane fluidity, which was still significantly higher than in cells constantly incubated at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ This indicates that cell membranes become conditioned to periodic temperature cycling. It anticipates future periodic temperature fluctuations, ignoring an obvious change of local environment. Only if cells were incubated at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 2 h or more and were in the exponential growth phase did membrane fluidity decrease. Prolonged incubation at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ had no effect on membrane fluidity in the stationary growth phase.

Periodically incubated cells were incubated for a prolonged time at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ after they completed three temperature cycles (Δ), or after five temperature cycles (□).


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/6510382cf5dd56189681376e1870fa5ac7e158ff87282485dd87b0d904defdcb.jpg)


## 15.4.4 CspA response of E. coli to periodic temperature fluctuations

During periodic temperature fluctuations between 37 and $8 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ , a continuous physiological stress is generated. Several proteins are up- or downregulated during the thermo-cycling relative to cells incubated at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Ivancic et al., 2013). For example, the cold shock protein CspA was upregulated, whereas CspB was de novo synthesized (see Chapter 15.1). In addition, a significant upregulation of succinyl-CoA synthetase subunit alpha (SCS-α), periplasmic oligopeptide-binding protein (OppA), and maltosebinding periplasmic protein (MalE) was observed, whereas the electron transport flavodoxin-1 protein (FldA), phosphoserine aminotransferase (SerC), and porine protein (NmpC) were downregulated. It is interesting to note that only the regulation of CspA and CspB overlapped significantly with the known cold shock proteins during temperature fluctuation. Obviously, periodic temperature fluctuation has an effect on protein synthesis that is not a simple superposition of the effect seen during cold shock and at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Ivancic et al., 2013). 

The CspA protein, which is overexpressed during cold shock as well as during periodic temperature fluctuations, has an essential role in prolonged adaptation to low temperatures (Phadtare, 2004; see Chapter 12.1). CspA prevents formation of mRNA secondary structures that hinder mRNA translation and work as an RNA chaperone (Jiang et al., 1997). The response to cold shock is fast since CspA translation does not require coldadapted ribosomes (Jones and Inouye, 1996). Both transcriptional and posttranscriptional regulation have been implicated in cold shock (Brandi et al., 1996; Gualerezi, 2003). In the studies discussed here, cells were incubated at constant low temperature. The question, however, is if CspA regulation during the periodic temperature cycling is fundamentally different from regulation in cells that grow at constant temperature. 

The responses of cspA mRNA and CspA protein during periodic temperature cycling are presented in Figure 15.4.2. The transcript levels of cspA mRNA increased at low temperatures and decreased at high temperatures consistently during each temperature fluctuation period. This indicates a tight control of cspA mRNA level in cell regarding changes in environmental temperature as there was no delay to environmental temperature stimulus. It has been suggested that the stability of cspA mRNA at high and low temperatures is very different (Goldenberg et al., 1996; Mitta et al., 1997). The cspA mRNA has a 159 base long 5′ UTR region with a cleavage site that becomes more accessible to RNAse at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ The average lifetime of cspA mRNA decreases from 20 min at $1 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ to less than 12 s at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ The results suggest that as cells progress through the temperature cycle, increasingly higher temperatures monotonically decrease the amount of cspA mRNA. It is important to note that cspA mRNA is transcribed at high temperatures, but it is readily cleaved (Brandi et al., 1999). It has been suggested that cspA mRNA can act as a thermosensor during the cold shock based on functionally distinct mRNA secondary structure at low and high temperature (Giuliodori et al., 2010). The results of cspA mRNA fluctuations during periodic temperature cycling are consistent with different thermal mRNA stability. Hence, cspA mRNA enables the cell a reliable, fast response monitoring device of environmental temperature. 

It is therefore surprising that CspA protein, the product of cspA mRNA transcript, did not oscillate with temperature fluctuations (Figure 15.4.2b). After an initial small increase, the concentration of protein remained approximately constant through the experiment. This suggests temporal uncoupling of cspA mRNA production and protein biosynthesis. Such uncoupling has been observed in cells growing at ${ 3 7 } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ where cspA mRNA and its protein CspA are hardly detected even though cspA mRNA is transcribed (Brandi et al., 1999). The high levels of protein at high temperatures in the absence of cspA mRNA are therefore likely the result of a stable protein structure that is not quickly degraded at high temperatures (Hiller et al., 1998; Ivancic et al., 2013). As CspA negatively regulates its own synthesis (Bae et al., 1997), high levels of CspA also prevent biosynthesis at low temperatures when there is an abundance of mRNA. The high level of CspA during the whole temperature cycle could have an impact on cell physiology. For instance, the CspA act as a transcription regulator deactivating the H-NS global repressor (La Teana et al., 1991). 

Cells that reach 37 ◦C during the periodic temperature cycling have different CspA concentration compared to cells grown constantly at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . This may indicate that the regulation of CspA biosynthesis in periodic temperature cycle and constant temperature growth conditions is fundamentally different. However, the structural stability of cspA mRNA and its protein CspA determines the outcome in both cases. At very low frequency of periodic temperature oscillations, the temperature changes slowly, and cspA mRNA thermal stability dictates protein biosynthesis in a canonical translation–transcription cascade (i.e., dose-dependent translation at low temperatures, and no translation at high temperatures). On the other hand, at high frequency of periodic temperature oscillations, the negative feedback autoregulation and stability of CspA protein override the high-fidelity cspA mRNA internal oscillator signal and keep the protein concentration in the cell approximately constant. The results suggest that E. coli cells are able to ignore brief variations in temperature in their local environments, but respond to persistent external changes of temperature.

Figure 15.4.2 Relative increase or suppression of cspA mRNA during the periodic temperature cycling (a), and relative increase or decrease of CspA-FLAG protein concentration (b) during the periodic temperature cycling between 37 and $8 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ The data are normalized to the transcription and protein levels at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ before the onset of temperature cycling. Temperature in Celsius during the periodic temperature cycling is given on abscissa.


## 15.4.5 Conclusions

Periodic temperature cycling has an effect on E. coli physiology. Different proteins are expressed as compared to with either cold shock or $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Cells have different membrane composition. The response of cell fatty acids is not consistent with homeoviscous adaptation. The transcript levels of cspA mRNA increased at low temperatures and decreased at high temperatures consistently during each temperature cycle. There are no corresponding fluctuations in CspA protein levels. The results indicate that cspA mRNA is an internal thermosensor that responds without delay to temperature changes. Its effect on CspA synthesis depends on the amplitude and frequency of environmental temperature oscillations.

# Cold shock response in Escherichia coli: a model system to study posttranscriptional regulation

Anna Maria Giuliodori 

School of Biosciences and Veterinary Medicine, University of Camerino, Camerino, Italy 

## 15.5.1 Introduction

All living organisms, from bacteria to mammals, have evolved numerous mechanisms to respond rapidly to environmental fluctuations. A sudden temperature change is a type of stress that strongly affects both rates of enzymatic reactions and solute diffusion as well as the conformational dynamics and flexibility of macromolecules. Bacteria, which experience quite often thermal variations, have developed mechanisms to sense the temperature changes, to counteract the temperature-induced effects, and eventually to adapt to the new conditions. 

Temperature variations can produce different effects on the lifestyle of the bacterium. For instance, a temperature increase triggers the expression of virulence genes in many bacterial pathogens such as Shigella sp. or Yersinia sp. (Konkel and Tilly, 2000). However, both pathogenic and nonpathogenic enterobacteria can also experience sudden temperature downshifts due to their excretion from the intestinal habitat of animals. The physiological response to such an abrupt reduction in temperature is called the cold shock response (CSR). 

The CSR of the mesophilic bacterium Escherichia coli involves an acclimation period which lasts from one to several hours, depending on the extent of the thermal excursion. During this adaptive phase, cellular growth is interrupted and the rate of synthesis of the majority of the gene products is drastically reduced, with the exception of a select group of cold shock (CS) genes whose expression is transiently increased. Once the cells are adapted to the new temperature, bulk protein synthesis is restored, CS protein expression declines to a new basal level, and cellular growth resumes, albeit at a reduced rate (for reviews, see Barria et al., 2013; Gualerzi et al., 2003, 2011; Phadtare, 2004). 

The prevalence of nucleic acid–binding proteins in the list of the E. coli CS gene products (Table 15.5.1) suggests that the cold-induced formation or stabilization of unfavourable secondary structures in nucleic acids are the main enemy to fight against during the adaptive phase. Many of these CS nucleic acid–binding proteins are involved in DNA replication, supercoiling, and transcription (NusA, RecA, H-NS, GyrA, and DnaA); others are involved in RNA degradation and translation (CsdA, Pnpase, YmdB, initiation factors IF1, IF2, IF3, and PY); and some are implicated in the genesis of the ribosome under suboptimal conditions (RbfA and RNaseR). However, the nucleic acid–binding proteins that most characterize the CSR in E. coli (and many other bacteria) are those belonging to the csp gene family (Yamanaka et al., 1998), which is made up of nine paralogues (cspA–cspH). Among these proteins, CspA, CspB, CspE, CspG, and CspI are cold inducible, whereas CspC, CspD, CspF, and CspH are not (see also Chapter 15.1). Unfortunately, the assignment of a specific function to each paralogue has turned out to be a difficult task since the activity of many csp family members overlaps. In fact, in E. coli, four out of nine csp genes must be deleted to obtain a cold-sensitive strain, and this phenotype can be suppressed by overexpression of any member of the csp family, except for cspD (Xia et al., 2001). 

The list of the CS proteins also includes two protein chaperones, namely TF and Hsc66, two proteins involved in the synthesis of the protective sugar trehalose (OtsA and OtsB) and two enzymes implicated in lipid biosynthesis (palmitoleolyl transferase) and cellular metabolism (pyruvate dehydrogenase). The transient inductions of this type of proteins and enzymes suggest that the CSR is aimed also at (i) assisting protein folding, (ii) contrasting the cold-induced decrease in membrane fluidity, and (iii) accumulating sugars with a protective effect against the low temperature. This supposition is confirmed also by the results of the transcriptomic (Phadtare and Inouye, 2004) and proteomic (Kim et al., 2005) analyses carried out under CS conditions. In fact, the list presented in Table 15.5.1 refers only to the proteins whose CS induction was confirmed by a classical approach and does not show the entire set of genes found to be upregulated during CS in the genome-wide analyses. In addition to the proteins mentioned here, this set includes also proteins involved in flagellar biosynthesis and motility, other protein chaperones, and several enzymes implicated in various biosynthetic pathways or energy metabolism.

(2008)</td></tr></table>

As for the timing of the CS induction, some CS proteins tend to accumulate immediately after the temperature downshift (early CS proteins), whereas others are apparently induced after a lag (late CS proteins) (Weber and Marahiel, 2003). This observation has led to the hypothesis that the expression of the late CS genes might be dependent on the synthesis of the early proteins. 

In general, the extent of CS induction is inversely proportional to the pre-shock level of the CS proteins. Since many CS genes are highly expressed also at ${ } ^ { 3 7 } \ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ CS induction often results in only a modest increase (∼threefold to fourfold) of the CS protein levels (Gualerzi et al., 2003). However, when the $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ level of the CS protein is low, the extent of CS induction can be much more pronounced. For example, the relative CS increase of CspA is about 30-fold in the mid–late exponential phase, when the level of CspA at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ is small, and only threefold during the early exponential growth, when CspA is very abundant at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Brandi et al., 1999). Yet, regardless of the growth phase, the absolute level of CspA after CS reaches up to 2% of the total cellular proteins (Brandi et al., 1999). 

Many stress responses in bacteria are under the control of specific transcription factors (sigma factors), which coordinate the expression of the genes belonging to the same regulon (Neidhardt and Savageau, 1996). Conversely, the induction of CS genes is controlled neither by a sigma factor nor by a shared mechanism of regulation. In fact, CS gene induction relies on both transcriptional and posttranscriptional regulatory mechanisms, with a prevalence of posttranscriptional events capable of affecting the stability of the CS messenger RNAs (mRNAs) and/or their translation at low temperature, thereby ensuring a transient and rapid adaptation to the new condition. In this chapter, some of these regulatory mechanisms are reviewed, placing emphasis on cis-acting elements and trans-acting factors capable of influencing the translation of CS and non-coldshock (non-CS) mRNAs in E. coli. 

## 15.5.2 Cold shock gene induction mediated by changes in mRNA stability

The level of several CS mRNAs rises immediately after CS, mainly because of an immediate and dramatic increase in their stability. The chemical half-lives of many CS mRNAs measured before and various times after the temperature downshift (see Table 15.5.1 and references therein) indicate a transcript stabilization that lasts throughout the initial part of the cold adaptation phase and terminates when cell growth resumes. Under physiological conditions, the machinery that degrades RNA (the degradosome) in E. coli is composed of four components, namely the $3 ^ { \prime } { - } 5 ^ { \prime }$ exonuclease polynucleotide phosphorylase (PNPase), the enolase, the helicase RhlB, and the ribonuclease E (RNaseE). The sequential increase and decrease of RNA stability after CS can be explained by the transient inactivation of the degradosome followed by the activation of a new RNA decay pathway to prevent the accumulation of unnecessary CS mRNAs when cold adaptation is attained. Various experimental data concur to support this premise. Helicase CsdA, which accumulates during the cold acclimation phase, is capable of both replacing the resident RNA helicase of the RNA degradosome and interacting with RNaseE in the cold (Prud’homme-Gen´ ereux ´ et al., 2004). Second, PNPase of both E. coli and Yersinia enterocolitica participates in the selective degradation of csp mRNAs at the end of the acclimation phase, and the inactivation of the pnp gene resulted in cell growth retardation or arrest below $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Beran and Simons, 2001). 

Induction of cspA expression is largely independent of transcription (Brandi et al., 1996; Fang et al., 1997), being mainly controlled by posttranscriptional mechanisms that enhance both stability and translational efficiency of cspA mRNA (see also Chapter 15.1). The impressive increase in the cspA mRNA half-life after CS (≈12 s at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and 70 min at $1 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ is likely due not only to inactivation of the degradosome but also to cis-acting elements present in the mRNA itself. In this connection, it has been shown that the long (160 nucleotides $\left( \mathrm { n t s } \right) ) 5 ^ { \prime }$ untranslated region (UTR) of cspA mRNA confers instability to the transcript, since the lack of the first 80 nts of the $5 ^ { \prime }$ UTR increases 10-fold the half-life of the mRNA at both high and low temperature (Gualerzi et al., 2003). Furthermore, at ${ 3 7 } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ the $5 ^ { \prime }$ UTR of cspA confers an instability similar to that of cspA mRNA to cat mRNA, and overexpression of CsdA RNA helicase that unwinds double-stranded structures causes a net increase of the cspA mRNA half-life at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Brandi et al., 1999). 

To determine whether a particular structure of the $5 ^ { \prime }$ UTR could be responsible for the rapid degradation of cspA transcripts at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , the secondary structure of $c s p A$ mRNA was elucidated at both $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and CS temperatures by chemical and enzymatic probing (Giuliodori et al., 2010). This analysis showed that cspA mRNA is capable of adopting two different secondary structures at high and low temperature, respectively (Figure 15.5.1). 

At low temperature, the $5 ^ { \prime }$ UTR of cspA mRNA folds independently of the rest of the molecule and forms a pseudoknot structure, which is likely a folding intermediate stabilized by the low temperature. Conversely, at ${ 3 7 } ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ the majority of the $5 ^ { \prime }$ UTR and the proximal portion of the coding region of the cspA mRNA base-pair with each other and form an extended helix interrupted by several internal loops. Interestingly, the AUrich region upstream of the helix that bears the Shine–Dalgarno (SD) sequence is single stranded and contributes to the formation of a multihelix loop. In a previous study (Fang et al., 1997), this region, which was incorrectly predicted to form an internal loop flanked by two stable helices, has been proposed to be a potential site for RNaseE. However, this hypothesis has not been confirmed by subsequent experimental analyses (Hankins et al., 2007). On the other hand, RNaseE was found to cleave cspA mRNA about 20 nts downstream the termination codon at a single-stranded region that links two stable stem-loop structures existing at both high and low temperature (Hankins et al., 2007). However, RNAseE is not responsible for the rapid decay of cspA mRNA transcript at ${ 3 7 } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , as demonstrated by the fact that the shortened cspA transcript produced upon RNaseE cleavage is quite stable, at least in vitro, at all temperatures tested (Hankins et al., 2007). Since $\mathrm { H f q }$ binds the same region cleaved by RNaseE at the $3 ^ { \prime }$ end of cspA mRNA (Hankins et al., 2010), it has been hypothesized that the lability of cspA transcript could be due to its Hfq-dependent polyadenylation at ${ 3 7 } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ which in turn triggers the activity of 3′ -exonucleases. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/dae8bf0c3b0ad2aea6022b455638c7398f5f63624e6123590c0c2b14c34adca1.jpg)



Figure 15.5.1 Schematic representation of the secondary structure of cspA mRNA determined by chemical and enzymatic probing (Giuliodori et al., 2010) at the cold shock temperatures $( 1 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and $2 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ and at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . The regions spanning from nts ≈200 to ≈330 in the cold structure and from nts ≈230 to ≈340 in the $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ structure were omitted. The TIR elements are highlighted as follows: blue, initiation codon; red, Shine–Dalgarno sequence; green, AU-rich region, putative S1 binding site. The region cleaved in vitro by RNAse E at both high and low temperature (Hankins et al., 2007) is shown in magenta. Adapted from Giuliodori et al. (2010).


As for the $5 ^ { \prime }$ end of cspA mRNA, the deletion of the region comprised between nts G4 and C20 has been found to significantly destabilize the mRNA at low temperature and reduce the amount of cspA mRNA in cells during cold adaptation (Xia et al., 2002). The authors hypothesized that the aforementioned deletion could result in the removal of a $5 ^ { \prime }$ end stemloop structure, a spatial element that often protects mRNAs from exonucleolytic degradation (Mackie, 1998). However, this prediction is probably incorrect, because the probing experiments indicate that at low temperature these nucleotides do not form a stem-loop structure, being instead involved in alternative base pairings with downstream bases of the $5 ^ { \prime }$ UTR. Thus, this deletion likely causes cspA mRNA to assume a completely different conformation that is somewhat more susceptible to degradation at low temperature. In line with this interpretation, it has been found that the cold-induced structure of cspA mRNA has a half-life at low temperature longer than the $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ -structure (Giuliodori et al., 2010). 

In conclusion, the $5 ^ { \prime }$ UTR of cspA mRNA is one of the elements responsible for the temperature-dependent conformational change of cspA mRNA. The cold structure of cspA mRNA seems to protect this transcript from rapid degradation, whereas the conformation assumed at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ could have the opposite effect, thereby contributing to its rapid decay. Nevertheless, the possibility that the cspA mRNA half-life could be modulated through interactions with small RNAs or affected by changes in the number of translating ribosomes (Hankins et al., 2007) cannot be ruled out. 

## 15.5.3 Cold shock gene expression mediated by selective translation

Many E. coli CS-induced proteins were characterized during the 1980s and 1990s by a proteomic approach based on the 2D electrophoretic separation of 35S-labeled proteins synthesized before and after an abrupt temperature downshift (Etchegaray et al., 1996; Jones et al., 1987; Wang et al., 1999). Inspection and quantification of the resolved protein spots allowed the identification of three types of polypeptides (Jones et al., 1987): (i) proteins whose synthesis is interrupted during the initial hours following cold stress; (ii) proteins that continue to be synthesised in the cold at a rate either similar to or lower than that observed at $3 7 \ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ;$ and (iii) proteins whose synthesis increases upon CS. The proteins belonging to these three groups have been named non-CS, cold-tolerant (CT), and CS, respectively. 

Since CS leads to a temporary inactivation of the degradation machinery, as discussed here, all mRNAs present before the sudden temperature shift presumably remain in the cells for a while, even if their transcription is completely stopped by the stress. Nevertheless, the non-CS mRNAs apparently cannot be translated at the onset of the cold adaptation. Even an increase in mRNA abundance following CS does not lead per se to an increase in the corresponding gene product (Goldenberg et al., 1997). In fact, an interesting observation has been made when the in vivo translation of cspA mRNA was compared to that of chimeric mRNAs synthesized from transcriptional gene fusions between cspA and lacZ or cat reporter genes. The chimeric mRNAs accumulated rapidly during cold adaptation with a trend similar to that of cspA mRNA, but the level of the corresponding protein products increased only towards the end of the acclimation phase, unlike that of CspA and other CS proteins which mirrored exactly the level of their mRNAs (Figure 15.5.2). 

These results clearly demonstrate the existence of a CS translation bias whereby translation of the CS mRNAs is selectively favoured, whereas translation of the non-CS mRNAs is disfavoured or delayed. The translation bias lasts throughout the acclimation phase and ends before cell growth resumes (Goldenberg et al., 1997). 

The existence of this regulatory mechanism raises the question as to which elements cause the CS translational bias. To address this issue, the translation activity of a subset of CS, CT, and non-CS mRNAs was studied under conditions reproducing in vitro the CS translational bias occurring in vivo (Giuliodori et al., 2004). Through this reductionist approach, it was possible to establish that both cis-acting elements present on the transcripts, whose existence can be inferred also from other in vivo studies (Etchegaray and Inouye, 1999; Mitta et al., 1997; Yamanaka et al., 1999), and trans-acting factors, whose level increases during cold shock, concur to control protein expression after cold shock. 

## 15.5.4 Cis-acting elements

To determine which intrinsic structural property (cis-acting element) makes the mRNAs more or less suited for translation at low temperature, various types of mRNAs were compared

The transcription (+1) and the translation (+160) starts of the cspA gene are indicated. Both the wild-type E. coli cells and cells carrying the cspA fusions were grown at $3 7 \ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } \ \mathrm { i n }$ Luria-Bertani (LB) medium as described (Goldenberg et al., 1997). After temperature downshift to $1 { \dot { 5 } } { } ^ { \circ } \dot { \mathrm { C } } ,$ cell aliquots were taken at the indicated times and processed. The mRNA levels (closed circles) were determined by primer extension $( c s p A - l a c Z$ fusion) or Northern blotting (cspA and cspA–cat fusion). in translational assays carried out in vitro at 37 and $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in the absence of trans-acting factors induced by cold shock (Di Pietro et al., 2013, and unpublished data; Giuliodori et al., 2004). As typical CS transcripts, five mRNAs belonging to the E. coli csp gene family (cspA, cspB, cspE, cspG, and cspI), the hns mRNAs encoding the nucleoid-associated protein H-NS (La Teana et al., 1991), and P1infA mRNA originating from the P1 promoter of infA encoding initiation factor-1 (IF1) (Giangrossi et al., 2007) were used. As non-CS transcripts, hupA and cspD mRNAs, encoding the α-subunit of the nucleoid-associated protein HU (Giangrossi et al., 2002) and protein CspD, respectively, were chosen. Despite its homology to CspA (50% identical, 64% similar), CspD is expressed only in the stationary phase (Yamanaka and Inouye, 1997), has a different cellular localization compared to CspA (Giangrossi et al., 2001), and probably acts as a toxin (Yamanaka et al., 2001). In addition, 027-C mRNA, the transcript of a synthetic gene designed for high expression in cell-free systems at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Brandi et al., 2007), was also tested as a non-CS template. Finally, the coldtolerant transcripts tested were hupB mRNA, which encodes the β subunit of HU (Giangrossi et al., 2002); cspC mRNA, which is constitutively expressed at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Czapski and Trun, 2014; Yamanaka et al., 1998) but can be transcribed (Czapski and Trun, 2014, and unpublished results) and translated also at low temperature (Xia et al., 2001); and P2infA mRNA, the shorter transcript encoding IF1, whose transcription and translation are not stimulated by CS conditions (Giangrossi et al., 2007). The results of these translation experiments are summarized in Table 15.5.2. 

The various mRNAs can be classified as excellent (blue), good (cyano), sufficient (orange), or poor (red) templates according to the average level of translation observed in different tests. Inspection of these groups reveals that at ${ 3 7 } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ the CS, non-CS, or CT nature of the mRNAs does not have any influence on the translation activity. However, the nature of the various transcripts does affect protein synthesis at $1 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Indeed, at this temperature, the translation products of the non-CS hupA, cspD, and 027-C mRNAs are barely detectable above the background. The different behaviour of the various mRNAs becomes more appreciable when the maximum amount of protein synthesized at $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ is divided by the maximum amount synthesized at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with the same mRNA. This $1 5 / 3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ translational ratio, shown in Table 15.5.2 for each mRNA, is >0.5 for the majority of CS and CT mRNAs tested in this system, <0.5 for cspI mRNA and both infA transcripts, and ≪0.5 for the non-CS mRNAs. This suggests the presence of cis-acting element(s), which make(s) many CS and CT mRNAs suitable for translation at low temperature.

To understand the nature of the cis-acting elements, the translation initiation regions (TIRs) of the various transcripts were compared (Table 15.5.2). In the majority of the mRNAs tested, the lengths of both the SD sequence and spacer approach the values most commonly found in E. coli genes (Ma et al., 2002), so that none of the CS transcripts has a strong SD sequence that could justify high translation rates (Ma et al., 2002; Osterman et al., 2013). However, it may be argued that the SD–anti-SD interaction could attain a satisfactory strength at low temperature even with a reduced number of base pairing compared to $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Vimberg et al., 2007). In any event, similar TIRs in different mRNAs do not produce comparable rates of translation at low temperature (e.g., cspE and cspI). On the other hand, the presence of a suboptimal TIR in hns mRNA (weak SD sequence) could explain its scarce translation in the system at both 37 and $1 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . 

Interestingly, an AU-rich region upstream the SD sequence is present in 8 out of 13 mRNAs tested. It is well established that this type of sequence enhances translation, likely acting as a binding site for ribosomal protein S1 (Boni et al., 1991; Komarova et al., 2005). This protein ensures translation of structured mRNAs with weak SD sequences (Duval et al., 2013; Tzareva et al., 1994) and improves translation of mRNAs with strong SD sequences (Komarova et al., 2002). S1 protein is required also for the formation of the 30S initiation complex with cspA mRNA at both 37 and $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Giuliodori et al., 2010). Nevertheless, the presence of the putative S1 binding site is neither necessary nor sufficient for efficient translation at low temperature, as demonstrated by the fact that protein synthesis in the cold can be satisfactory (cspB and cspG mRNAs) or scarce (cspI, hupA, and 027-C mRNAs) regardless of the presence of the AU-rich region. 

Since structured TIRs are known to affect the translation initiation process (de Smit and van Duin, 2003; Marzi et al., 2007), the secondary structure of cspA mRNA was determined at 37, 20, and $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Giuliodori et al., 2010) and compared to that of cspD mRNA (manuscript in preparation). These two molecules were chosen because, despite belonging to the same gene family and having similar sizes, they are translated with the highest and lowest efficiencies at $1 5 ^ { \circ } C ,$ respectively. 

In the case of cspD mRNA, the SD sequence is exposed in an apical RNA loop, whereas the initiation triplet is found at the edge of a base-paired region. Furthermore, this structure is unaffected by temperature. The closed secondary structure of cspD TIR is probably responsible for the slow kinetics with which this mRNA forms the 30S initiation complex at low temperature (Giuliodori et al., 2007). 

On the contrary, cspA mRNA is present in two completely different conformations at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and at the cold shock temperature (Giuliodori et al., 2010), as already mentioned. More importantly, the elements of cspA mRNA regarded as relevant for translation initiation (i.e., initiation triplet, SD sequence, and presumptive S1 binding sites) are more accessible at low compared to high temperatures (Figure 15.5.3). In fact, at $2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and $1 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ the nucleotides of the AU-rich region and the SD itself fall into a single-stranded region, whereas the AUG initiation codon is part of a rather unstable helix. Conversely, at ${ 3 7 } \ { ^ { \circ } } \mathrm { C } ,$ , the TIR is fully double-stranded with both the AUG initiation codon and the SD sequence buried within a stable helix. The capacity of cspA mRNA to assume alternative structures at different temperatures has important functional consequences (Giuliodori et al., 2010), the cold-structure being more efficient than the $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } \cdot$ -structure in binding the 30S subunits, promoting transfer RNA–N-formylmethionine (fMet-tRNA) binding, and driving CspA synthesis at low temperature (Figure 15.5.3). 

As for the other mRNAs, a complete understanding of their secondary structure is still lacking. Yet temperature-dependent conformational changes of these transcripts were monitored by temperature gradient gel electrophoresis (TGGE). This analysis (unpublished results) confirmed that more than one mRNA displays nonlinear, temperature-dependent mobility variations, the most striking case being that of cspI mRNA, which is present in at least four different conformations below $2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . It is conceivable that this property could be partially responsible for the scarce translation of cspI mRNA at $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in vitro (Table 15.5.2 and Figure 15.5.5) and in vivo (Wang et al., 1999), but further experiments should be performed to understand the precise nature of the TGGE mobility variations of this and other transcripts (P2infA, cspC, and hns mRNAs) and the possible functional consequences. Finally, no clear correlation could be found between the presence of infrequently used codons and the level of protein synthesized at low temperature, at least in vitro. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/fbcfe0abe7797cf6e7861d960288c105ff8023f3357710583d840c875be95408.jpg)



Figure 15.5.3 ${ \mathrm { A t ~ } } 1 5 \ { } ^ { \circ } { \mathrm { C } } ,$ the cold shock conformation of cspA mRNA (blue) is more efficient than the $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ conformation (red) in promoting (i) its binding to the 30S ribosomal subunit; (ii) the binding of the fMet-tRNA to the 30S initiation complex, and (iii) its in vitro translation in the absence of cold shock trans-active factors. The TIR elements relevant for translational initiation are highlighted in different colours within the structure of the two cspA conformers. Adapted from Giuliodori et al. (2010).


In conclusion, not all mRNAs endowed with apparently standard TIRs are well translated in the cold by a non-CS translational apparatus, even if containing an AU-rich translational enhancer, and also the presence of the Downstream Box sequence seems to be irrelevant (La Teana et al., 2000). This and other observations suggest that translation at low temperature could be influenced in primis by the secondary structure of the TIR, which may affect the rate of initiation complex formation, in line with what was proposed for the translation process at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Kudla et al., 2009). 

## 15.5.5 Trans-acting factors

Among other effects, low temperature causes (i) the impairment of the translation initiation process (Broeze et al., 1978; Farewell and Neidhardt, 1998), (ii) the increase of the miscoding level (Friedman and Weinstein, 1964), (iii) the reduction of the elongation rate (Farewell and Neidhardt, 1998), and (iv) the increase of ribosomal subunits association (Zitomer and Flaks, 1972). The ability of the translational machinery to synthesize CS and CT proteins despite these unfavorable conditions is remarkable. Trans-acting factors which accumulate in the cells following cold shock play an important role in ensuring this ability (Gualerzi et al., 2011). These factors can affect translation by selectively favouring and/or disfavouring CS and non-CS mRNAs, respectively, and by assisting translation at low temperature without mRNA selectivity. 

One of the trans-acting factors involved in the translational bias is IF3, whose level increases during cold shock together with those of the two other initiation factors (Giuliodori et al., 2004). In fact, cold shock does not inactivate the regulatory mechanism existing at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ that preserves the equimolarity of the IFs (Howe and Hershey, 1983). However, cold shock does change the level of IFs relative to that of the ribosomes, leading to an increment of the IF–ribosome ratio from 0.15 at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ to 0.30 after 2 h at $1 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figure 15.5.4a). Hence, cold shock causes an imbalance of the above-mentioned ratio that is normally kept constant under physiological conditions by virtue of a coordinated regulation (Howe and Hershey, 1983). This imbalance likely results from the uncoupling of IF expression, which continues during cold shock due to de novo transcription and translation (Giangrossi et al., 2007; Giuliodori et al., 2007), and rRNA synthesis and maturation, whose rate is strongly reduced upon temperature downshift (manuscript in preparation). 

To understand the functional consequences of the higher IF– ribosome stoichiometric ratio, the CS, CT, and non-CS mRNAs were translated at 37 and $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in the presence of increasing amounts of each purified IF (Giuliodori et al., 2004). Under these conditions, no preferential stimulation of CS mRNA translation by IF3 was observed at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . However, at $1 5 ^ { \circ } C ,$ translation of CS mRNAs was increasingly enhanced by IF3, whereas that of the other transcripts was either moderately (CT) or scarcely (non-CS mRNAs) stimulated (see Figure 15.5.4b). Furthermore, at low temperature, IF3 was able to discriminate against the formation of functional 70S initiation complexes programmed with the non-CS cspD mRNA, through a mechanism that remains to be fully elucidated (Giuliodori et al., 2007). A plausible hypothesis is that the cold-stabilized closed structure of cspD mRNA could hinder the correct positioning of the mRNA and/or the fMet-tRNA on the 30S subunit, thus triggering the IF3 fidelity activity which prevents the locking of the 30S initiation complex (Wintermeyer and Gualerzi, 1983) and the subsequent joining of the 50S ribosomal subunit.
