![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/e211de0d28764eb7ede5b4b52fe93c5f5fd02db698a1f2a4053340f4c7e040af.jpg)



Figure 15.5.4 Effect of cold shock trans-acting factors on the translation at $1 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ (a) Increase in the level of IF1 (red), IF2 (grey), IF3 (green), and CspA (blue) with respect to the amount of ribosomes or total protein content (for CspA) present in cell extracts prepared from cells subjected to cold shock at $1 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for the indicated times. The values are normalized, setting the amount detected prior to the cold stress to 1. Figure modified from Giuliodori et al. (2004). (b) Effect of IF3, CspA, and IF1 on the translation of the indicated mRNAs at $1 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . The ordinate shows the average increment of translational stimulation obtained in vitro when the purified factors are added to the system within a range of concentration which mimics the cold shock increase observed in vivo (see (a)). For the IFs, this range corresponds to an increase in the IFs/70S stoichiometric ratio from 0.15 to 0.3, whereas for CspA the variation is from 20 to 50 μM. The average increment of translational stimulation (AI) is calculated from the data presented in Giuliodori et al. (2004) using the equation AI = $\left( \mathrm { T } 2 - \mathrm { T } 1 \right) / \left( \mathrm { C } 2 - \mathrm { C } 1 \right) ;$ , where C1 and C2 are the concentrations before and after the cold shock increase, respectively, and T2 and T1 the translation products observed at the two above-mentioned concentrations. Adapted from Giuliodori et al. (2004).


Another element that has a negative impact on the translation of non-CS mRNAs is protein PY (Agafonov et al., 2001; Maki et al., 2000), which is a conserved auxiliary ribosomal factor (Agafonov et al., 1999) expressed both at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ during the stationary phase (Agafonov et al., 1999, 2001; Maki et al., 2000) and throughout the cold acclimation period (Agafonov et al., 2001). PY binds the 30S subunit and the 70S ribosome (Agafonov et al., 1999; Di Pietro et al., 2013) in a position that overlaps the A and P sites, located near an inter-subunit bridge (Polikanov et al., 2012; Vila-Sanjurjo et al., 2004). For this reason, PY is able to stabilize the 70S ribosome against dissociation (Agafonov et al., 1999; Di Pietro et al., 2013; Vila-Sanjurjo et al., 2004) and to inhibit the binding of the initiator tRNA to the 70S ribosome at low temperature (Vila-Sanjurjo et al., 2004). However, PY cannot destabilize the fMet-tRNA once this is correctly positioned in the 70S initiation complex (Di Pietro et al., 2013). By virtue of these activities, PY was found to partially hamper translation at low temperature both in vivo and in vitro (Di Pietro et al., 2013). However, the extent of the inhibition is modest and does not support a central role of protein PY during cold stress. Since PY competes with the other ligands (tRNA, mRNA, and IFs) for the binding to the ribosome (Di Pietro et al., 2013), it is possible that the extent of PY inhibition could be inversely related to the rate and/or the efficiency by which initiation complexes are formed with the various mRNAs at low temperature. This hypothesis could explain why CS mRNAs, which tend to rapidly form initiation complexes at low temperature, are overall less inhibited by PY than non-CS mRNAs, which are less effective in promoting the rapid formation of initiation complexes. This holds true in particular for cspA mRNA, which is almost insensitive to PY inhibition (Figure 15.5.5). 

As for the factors that stimulate the translational activity at low temperature, it should be mentioned that both IF1 and CspA were found to stimulate the translation of all mRNAs tested with low specificity (Figure 15.5.4b). This stimulation may provide an additional advantage to those mRNAs that have a good translation capacity, thereby reinforcing the stimulation of both cis- and trans-acting elements such as IF3. Also, this stimulation may favour bulk translation (possibly in concert with other trans-acting factors) so as to support growth resumption after cold acclimatization. 

Interestingly, E. coli IF1 was found to complement the coldsensitive phenotype observed with a cspB cspC double deletion strain in Bacillus subtilis, suggesting that IF1 could perform an activity partly overlapping that of the Csp proteins (Weber et al., 2001). Taking into account the scarce binding selectivity of CspA (and its paralogues) (Jiang et al., 1997; Lopez and Makhatadze, 2000; Phadtare and Inouye, 1999), its low association constant for nucleic acids (Jiang et al., 1997; Phadtare and Inouye, 1999), and its predicted cellular concentration in the cold (Brandi et al., 1999), it has been calculated that CspA should be generically bound to cellular RNAs in several copies per molecule under cold shock conditions (Ermolenko and Makhatadze, 2002). This property could be exploited by the cell in order to control unfavourable secondary structures induced or stabilized by the cold, a hypothesis supported by various experimental data that demonstrate that CspA displays RNA chaperone activity in vitro (Jiang et al., 1997); facilitates melting of the secondary structure of MS2 mRNA, allowing its cleavage by Mycobacterium tuberculosis MazF (MazF-mt6) (Phadtare et al., 2009); and acts as transcriptional activator of hns and gyrA (Jones et al., 1992; La Teana et al., 1991). However, despite this experimental evidence, the mechanism of action of CspA and its protein homologues is still not well characterized. 

Another activity which improves the whole translation process at low temperature is exploited in concert by IF1 and IF3. In fact, the increased IF1+IF3/ribosome stoichiometric ratio is required to overcome the larger tendency of the ribosomal subunits to associate in the cold (Giuliodori et al., 2007), thus ensuring a sufficient pool of free 30S and 50S subunits for the translation initiation process. However, this activity seems to clash with that of the above-mentioned protein PY, which has been postulated to act as a “storing” factor that favours formation of idle 70S monomers when the cell translation capacity is reduced (Maki et al., 2000; Sharma et al., 2010). A model capable of explaining the coexistence of these apparently conflicting mechanisms is presented in Figure 15.5.6 

Following temperature downshift, the amount of active translating ribosomes (polysomes) diminishes considerably (Jones and Inouye, 1996; Uchida et al., 1970), possibly because of unfavourable mRNA secondary structures which impair initiation complex formation. The subunits resulting from polysome dissociation can either associate to form idle 70S ribosomes, stabilized and stored by protein PY, or remain free by virtue of the IF1 and IF3 anti-association activity, strengthened by the coldinduced increased level of these factors. In turn, free 30S subunits can be used to either initiate translation or be targeted by PY and subtracted from the pool. The fate of the 30S subunits would depend on the competition between PY and the ribosomal ligands (mRNA, Ifs, and initiator tRNA): The mRNAs which rapidly and efficiently form 30S initiation complexes, such as cspA mRNA, would likely compete more efficiently than the mRNAs less amenable to translation in the cold. Thus, the degree of translation inhibition caused by PY would depend on the translational capacity of the various mRNAs at low temperature.

# New insight into cold shock proteins: RNA-binding proteins involved in stress response and virulence

Charlotte Michaux1 and Jean-Christophe Giard2 

1Vogel laboratory, Institute for Molecular Infection Biology, University of Wurzburg, W ¨ urzburg, Germany ¨ 

2Unite de Recherche Risques Microbiens (U2RM), Equipe Antibio-r ´ esistance, Universit ´ e de Caen, Caen, France ´ 

## 15.6.1 Introduction

In the cold shock response (CSR) process, several proteins play predominant roles in mechanisms allowing bacteria to adapt and survive to temperature fluctuations. Among them, one family called cold shock proteins (Csps) is well studied, especially concerning their role as RNA chaperones (Jiang et al., 1997; see Chapters 15.1 and 15.5). Indeed, two main cold-induced changes have been recorded in nucleic acid structure: stabilization of DNA and RNA secondary structures and increased negative supercoiling of DNA (Mizushima et al., 1997; Wang and Syvanen, 1992). Certain Csps, such as CspA, the main cold shock protein described in Escherichia coli, but also several homologs, act as RNA chaperones, resolving the secondary structures in nucleic acids and facilitating transcription and translation at low temperature (Phadtare and Inouye, 2004; Phadtare et al., 2002) (see Chapters 15.1 and 15.5). CspA has been described as a small, highly cold shock–induced protein. First limited to the cold shock phase of life, it has been observed that CspA is very abundant within the normal growth range and during the early exponential phase (Brandi et al., 1999). Concerning the nine Csp paralogs in E. coli, it appears that their functions can differ even if some of them are induced by the same conditions as cold or starvation (Graumann and Marahiel, 1999; Graumann et al., 1997). Csp proteins appear involved in the CSR in all bacterial species examined so far (Giuliodori et al., 2007; Gualerzi et al., 2003; Schumann, 2009; Weber and Marahiel, 2002). In addition to being the most strongly inducible family at low temperature (Graumann and Marahiel, 1999), these proteins, which are between 80 and 130 amino acids in length, are present in Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria, and the two RNAbinding motifs found in their sequence, ribonucleoprotein-1 and -2 (RNP1 and RNP2), are highly conserved among prokaryotic species as well as in the eukaryotic kingdom (Bandziulis et al., 1989; Schroder ¨ et al., 1995). The Csp proteins are part of the Y-box family, an ancient family with a highly conserved nucleic acid domain (Schroder¨ et al., 1995; Wolffe, 1994). Ybox proteins have been named for their interaction with duplex DNA containing the Y-box (CAAT), but they also play an important role in binding to mRNA (Wolffe, 1994). The structure of these proteins consists of five antiparallel β-strands that form a β-barrel structure with two β-sheets. Two RNA-binding motifs are found between the β2 and β3 strands, and those aromatic RNP side chains are known to interact with RNA. 

It is now clear that in addition to the involvement of CspA and homologs in different levels of regulation of transcription, mRNA stability, and translation, Csp family members also act as RNA chaperones (Phadtare, 2004) (see Chapters 15.1 and 15.5). They are described as transcriptional antiterminators due to their ability to recognize and “melt” secondary structures in nucleic acids. Because Csps do not have a high degree of specificity for their substrates, they are able to bind an extensive range of RNA and DNA molecules, acting, for example, as transcriptional antiterminators during cold acclimation of cells (Jiang et al., 1997; Lopez et al., 2001; Phadtare and Inouye, 1999). The role of cold shock proteins in stress response and their ability to bind RNA have already been well studied in the main bacterial models, such as E. coli, Salmonella enterica serovar Typhimurium, and Bacillus subtilis. 

Csps are also involved in several other metabolic or stress response pathways. Among them, they play a role in the stationary phase (Balhesteros et al., 2010), nutrient starvation, antibiotic biosynthesis, UV sensitivity, regulation of protein expression in response to osmotic stress (see Section 11), oxidative stress (see Section 10), and tolerance to solvents (Chanda et al., 2009; Graumann and Marahiel, 1999; Jones et al., 1996; Schmid et al., 2009; Yamanaka et al., 1998). 

Our knowledge of the cellular regulatory system has further evolved since “noncoding” RNAs or small RNAs (sRNAs) (see Chapter 2.8 and Section 5) have recently emerged as a widespread new class of regulatory molecules in bacteria. Coupled to this new insight, RNA-binding proteins are required for noncoding RNA functions. Until recently, only a few proteins have been identified and characterized as having important roles in RNA-mediated gene regulation. Therefore, the identification of RNA-binding proteins is an important step to understand the lifestyle of many pathogens. Hfq and CsrA are the two most studied RNA-binding proteins, and their roles in sRNA regulation have been extensively studied, especially in Gramnegative bacteria (Chao and Vogel, 2010; Edwards et al., 2011; Romeo et al., 2013; Vogel and Luisi, 2011). However, homologs of global regulators such as Hfq are absent from the genomes of several Gram-positive pathogens, suggesting that other important RNA-binding proteins remain to be discovered. This is the case for Enterococcus faecalis, and the recent characterization of sRNAs in this Gram-positive opportunistic pathogen has motivated researchers to identify RNA-binding proteins (Shioya et al., 2011). 

E. faecalis is commonly found in the normal microflora of the human gut. However, this ubiquitous bacterium can switch from a commensal to a major opportunistic pathogen. It is the third most common nosocomial pathogen, especially when it infects extra-intestinal sites (Wisplinghoff et al., 2004). Twelve percent of all hospital infections, including urinary tract and wound infections, infective endocarditis, endophthalmitis, and peritonitis, are caused by enterococci, and 80–90% of infections are caused by E. faecalis (Jett et al., 1994; Johnson, 1994). Therefore, it is important to understand the mechanisms underlying enterococcal infections to prevent and combat the rising rates of nosocomial diseases. This “Janus-faced” bacterium is able to develop efficient adaptation processes that give it the ability to cope with several environmental changes (Ogier and Serror, 2008). About a dozen putative virulence genes have been reported in E. faecalis, including transcription factors (Hancock and Gilmore, 2002; Lebreton et al., 2009; Michaux et al., 2011; Qin et al., 2001; Shankar et al., 2001). However, only a few examples of RNA-dependent post-transcriptional systems have been described so far, such as ethanolamine riboswitch utilization, toxin–antitoxin (see Chapter 2.7), or the CRISPR–Cas system (see Chapter 2.11) (DebRoy et al., 2014; Palmer and Gilmore, 2010; Weaver, 2014). It has been tempting to speculate that RNAbinding proteins such as cold shock proteins are involved in the transcriptional and/or post-transcriptional regulation of mRNA in E. faecalis. 

For the first time, using an in vitro RNA-binding proteins capture system, we have identified and characterized a cold shock protein (named CspR for cold shock protein RNA binding) that plays roles in the CSR in E. faecalis, such as in long-term survival under nutrient starvation conditions (Michaux et al., 2012). Moreover, virulence assays performed with two different animal models and using mutant strains have shown for the first time that this protein is a virulence factor. In addition to the phenotypical characterization of this protein, experiments of cellular location have been performed. We have demonstrated that CspR, as expected, is present in the cytosol but also on the E. faecalis surface and may constitute an interesting immunotarget (Michaux et al., 2013). 

## 15.6.2 Material and methods

In this study, three methods are particularly noteworthy and are described because of their novelty and the results they support: RNA-binding proteins capture with poly(A) tails used to catch CspR, larvae of G. mellonella used as an infection model for E. faecalis, and experimental assays supporting the double location of CspR into cytoplasmic and surface parts of the E. faecalis cells. 

## 15.6.2.1 Identification of RNA-binding proteins

Inspired by the methods used for eukaryotic cells, we adapted the protocol for the identification of RNA-binding proteins in vitro by stringent capture of RNA–protein complexes (Castello et al., 2013). Sixty micrograms of total RNA (an equal mixture of RNA from exponential and stationary phase cells) were treated with Poly(A) polymerase (Epicentre, USA) at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min, and poly(A)-tailed RNA was mixed and annealed to Dynabeads Oligo (dT) 25 (Invitrogen, USA) by rotating continuously for 5 min at room temperature. The complex was washed three times with buffer 1 (10 mM Tris-HCl, 0.15 M LiCl, 1 mM EDTA, pH 7.5). One milligram of total proteins extract (an equal mixture of proteins from exponential and stationary phase cells) was added to the beads–RNA complex and annealed to RNA by rotating continuously for 10 min at room temperature. The beads–RNA– protein complex was washed twice with buffer 1 and buffer 2 (10 mM Tris-HCl, 0.5 M LiCl, 1 mM EDTA, pH 7.5). The complex was resuspended in 10 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.5), and the RNA– protein complex was removed from beads by heat treatment. Proteins were then analyzed and sequenced by mass spectrometry (MS). 

## 15.6.2.2 G. mellonella infection model of virulence

Infection of G. mellonella larvae with E. faecalis was carried out as described in Lebreton et al. (2009). Briefly, using a syringe pump (KD Scientific, USA), larvae (about 0.3 g and 3 cm in length) were infected subcutaneously with washed E. faecalis wild-type, mutant, or complemented strains from an overnight culture in GM17 (growth medium for lactic acid bacteria supplemented with glucose) (Terzaghi and Sandine, 1975). Around $6 { \times } 1 0 ^ { 6 } \pm 6 { \times } 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ colony-forming units (CFUs) per larvae were administered in 10 μl of sterile saline buffer. For each test, at least 15 insects were infected, and the experiments were repeated at least five times. This model was also used to determine the putative effects of anti-CspR polyclonal antibody: 0.0025 μl, $0 . 0 1 2 5 \mu \mathrm { l } ,$ and 0.025 μl of anti-CspR serum were mixed with the different bacterial cells preparations, then injected into the caterpillars. Larval killing was monitored at 18 h post, infection. 

## 15.6.2.3 Location of CspR: “surface protein shaving” experiment – immunolabeling and electron microscopy

New methods have been proposed to inventory the proteomic composition of a bacterial surface (Tjalsma et al., 2008). For this purpose, intact bacteria were incubated with trypsin beads (unable to enter the cells) to cleave surface proteins, after which these “shaved” proteins were identified by liquid chromatography–tandem MS (LC-MS/MS) analysis of trypsic peptides. As a control, the same experiments were performed with cells incubated without trypsin beads. Proteins identified in the “shaved fraction” by at least two peptides (not present in the control fraction) in two independent experiments were considered as surface-located proteins. 

The immunolocalization of CspR was a second approach performed to confirm a double location of this protein, notably on the cell surface. Thus, 100 nm ultrathin sections from blocks of EryS wild-type and ΔcspR mutant strains from exponential growing cells $\left( \mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 } \ 0 . 5 \right)$ were prepared according to Spehner and Cavalier (2008). The primary polyclonal antibody (anti-CspR antibody) was diluted in phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) (0.025 μl per 10 μl of PBS). Grids containing EryS strain or ΔcspR mutant strain were incubated for 1 h at $4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with the diluted anti-CspR antibody. The grids were then washed six times with PBS and incubated at room temperature for 1 h with 15 nm colloidal gold–labeled goat antirabbit immunoglobulin G (IgG) (Aurion, Germany) (diluted 1/40 in blocking buffer). A similar experiment was also performed with diluted nonimmune rabbit serum as a control. The cryosections and whole cells were observed with a JEOL 1011 transmission electron microscope at the Electron Microscopy Center of the University of Caen Basse-Normandie (France). 

## 15.6.3 Results

## 15.6.3.1 CspR: cold shock protein involved in stress response and virulence in E. faecalis

## Identification of a new RNA-binding protein

The recent identification and characterization of sRNAs in E. faecalis in the absence of Hfq-like proteins were the major arguments to look for RNA-binding proteins and evaluate their role in stress response and virulence (Michaux et al., 2014; Shioya et al., 2011). Using an in vitro method based on the coprecipitation of artificial poly(A) tailed RNAs and proteins (see Section 15.6.2.1), identification of RNA–protein complexes pulled down by magnetic d(T) beads revealed the presence of a cold shock domain–containing protein, CspR (Ef2925). This new in vitro screening method can be developed for the establishment of a future global in vivo method for the identification of RNA-binding proteins. CspR has attracted attention because it corresponds to a putative cold shock protein with two RNP domains. Note that this protein was retrieved among all the 58 available E. faecalis genomes. Interestingly, analysis of the regions adjacent to cspR suggested the possible importance of its neighboring genes: ef2924, shares strong homology with the RNase RnjA of B. subtilis, while ef2926 encodes RadC, known for its role in DNA repair and recombination (Saveson and Lovett, 1999). These observations suggest that the cold shock protein CspR could play a role as RNA-binding protein and/or RNA chaperone. Finally, from the E. faecalis V583 genome sequence, six putative cold shock proteins (including CspR) with RNP1 and RNP2 domains were identified. Among them, only one was annotated as a cold shock (Ef1991, CspC) because of its homology with CspC of E. coli and B. subtilis. However, no experimental evidence has been gathered so far to characterize the role and the involvement of Csps in the CSR and other stress responses in E. faecalis. 

## Involvement of CspR in the cold shock stress response and longterm starvation survival

In order to characterize the role of CspR, the corresponding mutant was constructed. Testing the behavior of the cspR deletion mutant strain subjected to a temperature downshift, a growth defect phenotype as compared to the wild-type strain was observed (Figure 15.6.1). In addition, we performed Western blot experiments that revealed an induction of the protein CspR when cells were incubated at $9 ~ ^ { \circ } C$ (Figures 15.6.1 and 15.6.2). 

As mentioned here, bacteria have several paralogs of csp genes in their genomes, and regulation of these last can be different, with some being induced by low temperature but some also during the stationary phase. CspB and cspC of B. subtilis, cspD of E. coli, and cspC and cspD of Caulobacter crescentus are examples of genes encoding cold shock proteins induced during the stationary phase (Balhesteros et al., 2010; Graumann and 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/1600e27f63ba134918896cce32f74a09b124a997d99eb388b652acd3502392fa.jpg)



Figure 15.6.1 Implication of CspR in cold shock stress response. Growth without shaking in GM17 medium at $9 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ during 72 h of E. faecalis wild-type strain (white diamonds) and ΔcspR mutant (black triangle) determined by optical density $\mathrm { ( O D _ { 6 0 0 } ) }$ ). Experiments have been repeated at least four times, and the results represent the mean ± standard deviation.


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/130d51b5d313eeaf244b812aed72bb93a4b8c59aa3cd28851ab4b476793fa895.jpg)



Figure 15.6.2 Detection of the CspR protein on the total, secreted, and surface protein by Western immunoblotting. Each lane contains 10 μg of the different type of proteins extracted from the wild-type strain harvested in exponential phase, stationary phase, or cold shock condition. Tot., total proteins; Sec., secreted proteins; Sur., surface proteins.


Marahiel, 1999; Lang and Marques, 2004; Yamanaka and Inouye, 1997; see Chapters 15.1 and 15.5). Two common features shared by both low temperature and stationary phase are rapid inactivation of ribosomal function and increased loss of translation fidelity, which are considered as the inducing signals (Graumann and Marahiel, 1999). Thus, it was of interest to test the effect of a cspR mutation on cellular survival during the stationary phase. While the growth of the wild-type and ΔcspR strains were similar in the exponential phase at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , the mutant strain entered the stationary phase at an $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ of 1.8 versus 2.4 for the wildtype. The other obvious phenotype observed was the difference in the ability to survive during long-term starvation. Indeed, 72 h after the onset of starvation, whereas the wild-type cells remained at about 40% survival, almost all the ΔcspR mutant cells were dead (Michaux et al., 2012). In addition to this observation highlighting the involvement of CspR in survival during the stationary phase, the induction of CspR at the protein level was clearly established during this growth period (Figure 15.6.2). Note that the involvement of a cold shock protein in both cold shock response and long-term survival has not been described until now. 

## Involvement of CspR in virulence of E. faecalis

Some RNA-binding proteins are known to modulate the transcription of virulence and/or stress-responsive genes. However, until recently, only vacB of Aeromonas hydrophila, an exoribonuclease required for growth at low temperature, was found as a cold shock protein and a virulence factor (Erova et al., 2008). In 2014, the role of CspA in the stress adaptation as well as in the virulence of Brucella melitensis has been demonstrated, and this became the third report, including the CspR study, of the implication of cold shock proteins in the bacterial virulence process (Michaux et al., 2012; Wang et al., 2014) 

To analyze the involvement of CspR in the pathogenicity of E. faecalis, a comparison between the abilities of the wild-type and the corresponding mutant strain to kill G. mellonella larvae was performed. This model is now a well-established animal model to identify and characterize bacterial virulence factors (Glavis-Bloom et al., 2012). There are three main ways by which Galleria combats bacterial infections: phagocytosis, melanization response (which is analogous to the formation of an abscess in mammals following an infection), and induction of antimicrobial immune effectors. From an experimental point of view, the infected larvae of Galleria were incubated at the human body temperature $( 3 7 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ) .$ , allowing E. faecalis to grow optimally (Tang, 2009). Moreover, the large size of the larvae allows infection with easily controlled concentration doses of bacterial cells (Glavis-Bloom et al., 2012). In addition, this is a costeffective technique that reveals a good correlation between the role of bacterial virulence factors in the Galleria and mice models (Jander et al., 2000). All together, these are solid arguments to use these larvae as a virulence model for enterococcal assays. The rate of killing was significantly lower for the larvae infected with the mutant cells than for those infected with the wild-type (Figure 15.6.3). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/c21d1f22aba4c8178b571be838826ea2da7107fbb7b08fbb5840ba0682eefa5a.jpg)



Figure 15.6.3 Effect of ΔcspR inactivation on virulence. Percent survival of G. mellonella larvae at 18 h after infection, with around $1 . 2 \times 1 0 ^ { 5 }$ CFUs of E. faecalis EryS, ΔcspR mutant, and ΔcspR complemented strain. Experiments were repeated at least five times, and the results represent the mean ± standard deviation for live larvae. The insert shows alive (two on the left) and dead (two on the right) larvae.


The importance of CspR in the infection process was also confirmed in a murine systemic infection model, where a better persistence of the wild-type compared to the mutant in tissue burdens in kidneys was observed (Michaux et al., 2012). 

Because it is well known that the phagocytic cells constitute an important part of the innate immunity against pathogens, the in vivo–in vitro macrophage infection model was used to determine whether the ΔcspR strain was affected at this stage of infection. Indeed, it was found that after 24 h post infection, the ΔcspR strain was significantly less present in macrophages than the wild-type (Michaux et al., 2012). 

Taken together, these results highlight the involvement of the cold shock RNA-binding protein CspR in the virulence and infection process of E. faecalis. This is the first report describing the implication of a cold shock protein in the virulence of Gram-positive bacteria. It may be related to the low survival during the stationary phase phenotype we observed, since the reduced virulence of the ΔcspR strain may be due to the decreased ability of this strain to survive during long-term starvation. 

## 15.6.3.2 CspR: both immunotarget and post-transcriptional regulator

Surprisingly, we observed that the addition of anti-CspR immune rabbit serum protected larvae against killing by E. faecalis. The wild-type cells were less virulent when anti-CspR antibody was added to larvae of G. mellonella. A graduated titration of the antibody was indeed observed (Michaux et al., 2013). 

Under these conditions, 70% of the ΔcspR mutant infected larvae survived. This protection of G. mellonella larvae against infection by E. faecalis was the first argument to suggest a possible location of CspR on the surface, even without leader peptide or putative transmembrane domain in the sequence. The protective effect of anti-CspR immune rabbit serum seems specific for enterococci, since no difference in protection by the antibody has been observed with Listeria monocytogenes (Michaux et al., 2013). It may be suggested that CspR could play a role in the bacterial recognition by phagocytic cells or by defense against antimicrobial agents present in the hemolymph of G. mellonella. Similar experiments using other animal models should be performed in order to evaluate the efficiency of the anti-CspR antibody. However, based on these data, CspR may be considered as an interesting immunotherapy target for the strategy to prevent or fight enterococcal infections. 

To investigate the possible and unexpected double location of CspR on the surface as well as intracellularly, different approaches were undertaken. Three different experiments were carried out: Western blot analysis on the total and surface protein fractions, identification of surface polypeptides by “proteolytic shaving” of intact bacterial cells followed by MS identification, and immunolocalization of CspR on E. faecalis using electron microscopy. 

Western blot analyses have shown a cross-reactive band corresponding to the molecular mass of CspR in the total and surface protein fractions (Figure 15.6.2). 

The presence of CspR on the surface of E. faecalis was also detected by trypsin shaving experiments among 70 proteins (Bøhle et al., 2011; Michaux et al., 2013). Both experiments were strong arguments for the presence of CspR protein on the cell surface. This was confirmed by electron microscopy after immunolocalization of CspR, marked by immunogoldlabeled polyclonal antibody (a secondary antibody that recognizes anti-CspR primary antibody). As shown in Figure 15.6.4, the immunogold markers were detected inside and on the surface of the bacteria. As a negative control, similar experiments were conducted with non-immune serum as well as with the ΔcspR mutant strain incubated with the gold-labeled anti-CspR antibody. 

A global proteomic approach has been carried out to identify proteins whose expression differed in the cspR mutant compared to the wild-type (Michaux et al., 2012). The aim was to obtain putative target candidates of this RNA-binding protein. In this study, 9 and 10 proteins exhibited increased and reduced levels in the mutant strain, respectively. Several of them are involved in the translation possess and the stress response. 

Interestingly, transcriptomic analysis of the corresponding genes revealed that the observed deregulations mainly operated at the post-transcriptional level. This is in accordance with the RNAbinding function of CspR, where it may participate in mRNA stability or in recognition by RNase. In this context, the interaction of CspR with the product of the ef2924 gene (a neighbor of cspR) that is homologous to RnjA, an RNase of B. subtilis, should be tested. 

## 15.6.4 Conclusion

Taken together, the results show for the first time that a cold shock RNA-binding protein can present a double location, both intracellularly and on the surface of the bacteria, and that anti-CspR antibody protects larvae against infection by E. faecalis. The diversity of the phenotypes observed with the cspR mutant points out the pleiotropic function of this protein. It is likely that the double location of CspR (in and on the surface of cells) is linked to different roles of this polypeptide, and that is the reason why it can be considered as a “moonlighting” protein. 

In E. faecalis, no global RNA-binding protein that could have a role in mRNA stabilization or interaction with sRNAs has been identified yet. The recent identification and characterization of sRNAs in E. faecalis have shown that some of them play a role in post-transcriptional regulation, stress response, and virulence (Michaux et al., 2014). This raises the hypothesis that these functions could involve RNA-binding proteins. E. faecalis is deficient in the well-studied RNA chaperone Hfq, which implies that more specific RNA-binding proteins may act as post-transcriptional regulators. We focused on the analysis of CspR because it was one of the candidates pulled down in the co-precipitation assay, and because some members of the cold shock protein family are known to have a role as RNA chaperones (Phadtare and Inouye, 1999). 

Like the known RNA-binding proteins Hfq, CsrA, ribosomal protein S1, H-NS, or RNaseE, whose pleiotropic roles have been shown (Dorman et al., 1999; Inouye et al., 1974; Sukhodolets and Garges, 2003; Wahba et al., 1974), it is likely that Csps have additional implications other than binding transiently and nonspecifically to RNA to resolve RNA folding problems that occur at low temperature (Byrgazov et al., 2013; Pichon and Felden, 2007; Vogel and Luisi, 2011). Several functions of Csps in connection with RNA have been already described as follows: binding to an mRNA polyA tail to prevent degradation (Feng et al., 2001), preventing internal cleavages by RNaseE, interacting with nascent RNA in a transcriptional complex (Hanna and Liu, 1998), inducing rpoS expression (Phadtare and Inouye, 2001), promoting antitermination mechanisms (Bae et al., 2000), or facilitating translation (Gualerzi et al., 2003). Moreover, a study described the interaction of Hfq with CspC, a cold shock protein, via mRNAs in E. coli (Cohen-Or et al., 2010). CspC is also able to interact with the 5′ -UTR of rpoS, and thus can complement the absence of the global chaperone Hfq. This study and the results concerning CspR provide new roles that could be attributed to Csps and pave the way for further characterization of a new class of global RNA-binding proteins in bacteria lacking an Hfq-like protein.

# Light regulation of cold stress responses in Synechocystis

Kirill S. Mironov and Dmitry A. Los 

Institute of Plant Physiology, Russian Academy of Sciences, Moscow, Russia 

## 15.7.1 Introduction

Poikilothermic organisms desaturate fatty acids (FAs) of their membrane lipids in response to a decrease in the ambient temperature. Insertion of the additional double bonds into the acyl chains of membrane lipids by fatty acid desaturases (FADs) compensates for a decrease in membrane fluidity caused by a drop in temperature (Los et al., 2013). Lipid unsaturation is one of the major contributors to adjustment of the cold-dependent acclimation of biological membranes (Los and Murata, 2004; Szalontai et al., 2000). Therefore, FADs are the important enzymes that participate in the acclimation of cells to cold stress (Los and Murata, 1998, 2004). 

Among 46 potential two-component regulatory systems (see Section 3) of Synechocystis (Los et al., 2010), the histidine kinase Hik33 (Suzuki et al., 2000) and the response regulator Rre26 (RpaB) (Kappell and van Waasbergen, 2007) have been identified as the components of the cold perception and transduction system. The genome-wide analysis of gene expression in Synechocystis with DNA microarrays revealed about 100 genes that are highly induced by cold (Suzuki et al., 2001). Only a part of these genes is controlled by the Hik33 and may be induced due to the changes in membrane fluidity (Murata and Los, 2006). Another part of cold-inducible genes is regulated by some other cold-sensing system that differs from the Hik33 (Los et al., 2010). 

A feedback link was found between the physical state of the cytoplasmic membrane of Synechocystis and the expression of the desA gene for the Δ12-desaturase by partial catalytic hydrogenation of the acyl chains of the plasma membrane lipids. The latter was induced both by cold and by catalytic hydrogenation of double bonds in fatty acyl chains at optimal growth temperature (Vigh et al., 1993). This means that transcription of a gene, which is involved in regulation of the membrane fluidity, is regulated by changes in membrane fluidity. Rigidification of the membrane causes induction of the cold-inducible desaturase gene(s), and, alternatively, fluidization of the membrane causes desaturase gene(s) silencing. A similar feedback link between the membrane fluidity and the expression of the desB gene for the ω3-desaturase was also demonstrated (Mironov et al., 2012a). It should be noted that cold-inducible desB gene is under partial control of Hik33 (Murata and Los, 2006), whereas coldinducible desA gene is not under control of Hik33 (Los et al., 2008). 

Here, we summarize our recent findings on cold-induced gene expression and cold-induced FA unsaturation in the cyanobacterium Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803, with the main focus on the light-dependent manner of regulation of these processes. 

## 15.7.2 Light-dependent and light-independent cold-induced changes in the fatty acid composition of membrane lipids

The optimal growth temperature of the cells of Synechocystis lies between 30 and $3 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . When temperature decreases, a threshold of $6 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ is enough to provoke the induction of transcription of genes for the FADs (Los et al., 1993, 1997). The following FADs are cold-inducible: desA (Δ12-desaturase), desB (ω3- or Δ15-desaturase) (Higashi and Murata, 1993), and desD (Δ6-desaturase). Following the induction of FAD gene expression, fatty acid unsaturation occurs in cells of Synechocystis (Mironov et al., 2012a,b; Wada and Murata, 1989, 1990). 

Cells of Synechocystis grown at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ under continuous light (optimal growth conditions) mainly accumulate 16:0, $1 6 { : } 1 \Delta ^ { 9 }$ , 18:1Δ9, 18:2Δ9,12, and $1 8 ; 3 \Delta ^ { 6 , 9 , 1 2 }$ with an unsaturation index of 0.8. Low-temperature treatment of these cells at $2 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in the light caused more than fourfold decrease in the amount of oleic acid (18:1Δ9), and 1.5-fold increase in the amount of γ-linolenic acid (Table 15.7.1). This is the result of the induction of the FA desaturase genes and of the enhanced FA unsaturation at low temperatures. Cold stress in the light caused accumulation of the ω3 FAs, namely, α-linolenic $( \alpha { - } 1 8 { : } 3 \Delta ^ { 9 , 1 2 , 1 5 } )$ and stearidonic (18:4Δ6,9,12,15) acids. These two FAs never have been detected at normal growth temperature (Mironov et al., 2012a,b; Wada and Murata, 1990). The unsaturation index of FAs in cold-stressed cells in the light increased up to 1.0.

Cells incubated at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 12 h in the dark did not demonstrate any significant changes in their fatty acid composition if compared with the illuminated cells (Table 15.7.1). Cold stress provided in the dark had no significant effect on composition of the major FAs of the membrane lipids. This observation suggests that light is necessary for the unsaturation of FAs at low temperatures in Synechocystis. 

The conversion of saturated stearic acid (18:0) and palmitic acid (16:0) into monounsaturated oleic (18:1Δ9) and palmitoleic (16:1Δ9) acid, respectively, is conducted by the Δ9 desaturase. Its expression in Synechocystis is constitutive and does not depend on temperature (Los et al., 1997) and/or light (Kis et al., 1998). 

The amount of linoleic acid $( 1 8 { : } 2 \Delta ^ { 9 , 1 2 } )$ slightly decreased in the cells at $2 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in the light. At the same time, the amount of oleic $( 1 8 { : } 1 \Delta ^ { 9 } )$ acid, which serves as a substrate for the Δ12 desaturase, decreased from 10 to 3%. It means that, in the light, oleic and linoleic acids were consumed in further steps of desaturation by the Δ6- and ω3-desaturases. Cold treatment of cells in the dark did not cause such dramatic changes in the amount of oleic acid, indicating that the desaturation at the $\Delta ^ { 1 2 }$ position is light dependent. 

Cold stress in the light, but not in the dark, caused an increase in the amount of γ-18:3 from 12 to 18%. Thus, desaturation at the $\Delta ^ { 6 }$ position of $\mathrm { C } _ { 1 8 }$ FAs might be also light dependent. 

The complete absence of the products of the ω3 desaturase (α-18:3 and 18:4) in cells, subjected to cold stress in the dark (Table 15.7.1), implies that light is obligatory for desaturation at the $\omega ^ { 3 }$ position of $\mathrm { C } _ { 1 8 }$ FAs in Synechocystis. 

## 15.7.3 Light-dependent and light-independent low-temperature-induced changes in cold-inducible gene expression

Cells grown at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ were treated for 30 min at designated temperatures with the interval of $2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (30, 28, 26, 24, 22, 20, or $1 8 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ in the light or in the dark. The changes in transcription of cold-induced genes were followed by reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction (RT-PCR) (Figure 15.7.1). 

A level of the messenger RNA (mRNA) for the desB gene gradually increased with the decrease in temperature from 32 to 18 $^ \circ \mathrm { C }$ in the light, but not in the dark. It is known that the desB gene is under partial control of the cold sensor Hik33, which may sense the cold-induced changes in the membrane fluidity (Murata and Los, 2006; Suzuki et al., 2000). A link between membrane fluidity and light regulation of the cold-induced desB gene was demonstrated with the use of the Δhik33 mutant defective in the Hik33 and the $d e s A ^ { - } - d e s D ^ { - }$ mutant, which was defective in Δ6- and Δ12-desaturases, and which could produce only monounsaturated FAs in the membrane lipids (Mironov et al., 2012a,b). 

The ndhD2 gene, which is under tight control of the cold sensor Hik33, behaved similarly to the desB gene. The ndhD2 was induced by cold only in the light, but not in the dark (Figure 15.7.1). Its induction depended on the presence of Hik33 in cells and on the membrane fluidity (Mironov et al., 2012b). Essentially, similar observations have been obtained with the cold inducibility of the hliB gene for the high-light-inducible protein (Figure 15.7.1), which is also tightly controlled by the Δ. Thus, cold-inducible genes that are regulated via Hik33 are also light dependent. 

Hik33 carries the PAS domain (Taylor and Zhulin, 1999). The latter is attributed to regulatory proteins that participate in regulation of the circadian clock in various organisms and in coordination of metabolism in photosynthetic cells (Ponting and Aravind, 1997). In Synechococcus, the homolog of Hik33, the histidine kinase NblS, also regulates light-dependent expression of the hli genes (van Waasbergen et al., 2002). Thus, light dependence of the induction of cold stress genes through Hik33 might be related to the PAS domain of this sensory kinase. 

## 15.7.4 Light-independent cold-induced expression of genes whose inducibility is not under control of Hik33

The genome-wide analysis of cold-inducible genes in Synechocystis revealed that about two-thirds of 100 highly cold-inducible genes are under control of Hik33, and another one-third is not (Murata and Los, 2006). For example, Hik33 does not control crhR for the cold-induced RNA helicase, and rbpA for the cold-inducible RNA-binding protein, which plays a role of the RNA chaperone under cold stress in cyanobacteria, similarly to the cspA and other csp genes of Escherichia coli or Bacillus subtilis (Sato, 1994). 


Figure 15.7.1 Light-dependent and light-independent cold-induced transcription of genes in Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803. Wild-type cells were at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ under continuous illumination and incubated at 30, 28, 26, 24, 22, 20, or $1 8 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min in the light or in darkness. The changes in the levels of the specific mRNAs were determined by the RT-PCR method. The desB, hliB, and ndhD2 genes represent Hik33-dependent cold-inducible genes. The crhR and rbpA genes represent Hik33-independent cold-inducible genes. The rnpB gene represents the constitutive gene for RNase P. (a) Gel electrophoresis of the specific amplified DNA fragments. (b) Quantification of the results of RT-PCR: Open bars represent samples exposed to white light, and dark bars represent samples incubated in the dark.

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/86ee52925570ef3ca60f1c53d387b3090710121482c90a803a64cbc3f366beb1.jpg)


As shown in Figure 15.7.1, cold induction of the rbpA does not depend on light, and this gene is equally induced by cold stress both in the light and in darkness. Its induction does not depend on the presence of the hik33 gene in the genome of Synechocystis (Mironov et al., 2012b). Very similar behavior was observed for the crhR gene. Its inducibility by cold was not regulated by light (Figure 15.7.1). Thus, the expression of genes, which are not controlled by the cold sensor Hik33, is not regulated by the membrane fluidity and/or light. Their cold inducibility was solely dependent on temperature. 

Light plays the crucial role in the regulation of various processes in plants and other photosynthesizing organisms (Nishihama and Kohchi, 2013). As shown in this chapter, transcription of a set of cold-induced genes, which are controlled by the cold sensor, Hik33, is regulated in a light-dependent manner (Mironov et al., 2012b). Light-regulated responses usually depend on the state of different photoreceptors, especially phytochromes (Kreslavski et al., 2012). 

In higher plants, phytochromes are usually transformed into their active form by absorption of red light with a maximum of 630–670 nm. Far-red light with a maximum of 730 nm transforms phytochromes back to their inactive form. Phytochromelike receptors with two photoreversible states also have been found in cyanobacteria (Batschauer et al., 2003). Cyanobacterial phytochrome-like proteins are highly homologous to phytochromes of higher plants, and they are represented by the sensor histidine kinases (Hughes et al., 1997). 

## 15.7.5 Cold-induced light-dependent gene expression depends on light quality

In Synechocystis, cold-induced genes that are under control of the sensory histidine kinase Hik33 are induced by cold only if light is provided, but never in the dark. In order to determine the light quality that triggers the cold-activated transcription of Hik33-dependent genes, we investigated the action spectra of the inducibility of genes. The interference light filters were used to provide light of 415, 466, 512, 578, 619, and 700 nm, equalized at 50 μmol photons m $^ { - 2 } \ s ^ { - 1 }$ , to the cells (Figure 15.7.2). Cells were grown at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and transferred to $2 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min. 

Cold-inducible genes differentially responded to different light quality. The hliB gene for the high-light-inducible protein, the ndhD2 gene for the NAD(P)H-quinone oxidoreductase, and slr1483 (for an unknown protein, which is strongly induced by cold) (Suzuki et al., 2001) were not induced by cold in the dark. These genes were strongly induced by cold under white, blue (415, 466 nm), green (512 nm), and red (700 nm) light. The desB gene for the ω3-desaturase was not induced in the dark, but it was strongly induced by cold under white and red (both 619 and 700 nm) light (Figure 15.7.2). The sigD gene for a type-II sigma subunit of the RNA polymerase does not seem to be much induced by cold. However, the level of its mRNA increases when cells were cold shocked under red (700 nm) light. 

The crhR for the cold-induced RNA helicase, which is not Hik dependent and whose induction by cold is not light dependent (Figure 15.7.1), was induced by cold in the dark, and at any provided light quality with an increase of the mRNA under red (700 nm) light (Figure 15.7.2). The rbpA gene for the RNAbinding protein was highly induced by cold with no discrimination toward quality of light (Figure 15.7.2).

These results show that cold-induced Hik33-dependent genes mainly require red light (∼700 nm) for cold induction. 

Hik33-independent genes are also light independent, and they are induced by cold in the darkness or in the light regardless of its wavelength. 

It should be noted that several genes examined were induced not only by red light but also by violet, blue, or green light. At this moment, the cross-talk between light-sensing systems (Hirose et al., 2008; Hitomi et al., 2000; Moon et al., 2010; Yeh et al., 1997; Zirak et al., 2007) that control gene expression in Synechocystis cannot be evaluated. Here, we will focus mainly on the desB gene for the ω3-desaturase and on the corresponding changes in red-light-dependent FA composition. 

## 15.7.6 Cold-induced desB and hliB gene expression is abolished in the Hik33-defecient mutant

The investigation of cold inducibility of the desB and hliB genes in the mutant of Synechocystis deficient in Hik33 revealed that their transcription was abolished in the mutant cells and did not respond to light or temperature (Figure 15.7.3). The Hik33-independent rbpA gene was induced by cold in the Hik33 mutant cells but did not respond to light, as previously detected (Figures 15.7.1 and 15.7.2). 

Red (635 nm) and far-red light (726 nm) in various combinations (5, 15, or 30 min of red light followed by 5, 15, or 30 min of far-red light exposure, etc.) did not reveal any notable changes in gene expression in wild-type cells and had no effect in the Hik33-deficient mutant (Figure 15.7.3). Disappearance of cold induction of desB and hliB genes in the Hik33-deficient mutant, which are strongly induced by cold under red light of 700 nm in wild-type cells, suggests that Hik33 may be involved in light regulation of transcription of these genes, and it might sense red light, as was previously suggested (Kreslavski et al., 2012). 

## 15.7.7 Light-quality-dependent low-temperature-induced changes in FA composition of membrane lipids in Synechocystis

Wild-type cells of Synechocystis grown at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ under white light accumulated 16:0, $1 6 { : } 1 \Delta ^ { 9 }$ , $1 8 { : } 1 \Delta ^ { 9 } ;$ , $1 8 { : } 2 \Delta ^ { 9 , 1 2 }$ , and $\gamma \mathrm { - } 1 8 { : } 3 .$ . Low-temperature treatment of these cells at $2 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ under white light caused a decrease in the amounts of γ-linoleic and oleic acids $( 1 8 { : } 1 \Delta ^ { 9 } )$ ). Cold stress under white light also caused accumulation of the $\omega ^ { 3 }$ FAs, namely, α-linolenic (18:3) and stearidonic (18:4) acids, which were not detected at normal growth temperature (Table 15.7.2). 

Wild-type cells incubated at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in the dark during 12 h did not demonstrate any significant changes in their fatty acid composition if compared with the illuminated cells. Cold stress in the dark had no effect on composition of the major FAs of the membrane lipids (Table 15.7.1). No accumulation of the $\omega ^ { 3 }$

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/f9daf887a5c603fc3bc900834dc49680266c79c21878e7851a2c049781d848f2.jpg)


FAs (α-18:3 and 18:4) has been observed under cold treatment of cells in the darkness (Tables 15.7.1 and 15.7.2). This observation confirms that light is necessary for the FA unsaturation at low temperatures in Synechocystis (Mironov et al., 2012b). 

Cold treatment of cells under blue (Table 15.7.2) or green (not shown) light did not cause any changes in FA composition if compared to cells grown at optimal temperature. Red light, however, provided with cold stress caused changes in FA composition comparable to white light resulting in the synthesis of ω3 FAs (Table 15.7.2). To investigate, whether red light has a triggering effect on cold-induced FA desaturation, cells were continuosly illuminated with red light for 12 h. Alternatively, cells were exposed at low temperature under red light for 30 min only, and then incubated in the darkness. These two types of treatments resulted in essentially similar changes in FA composition. This implies that short-term exposure of cells to red light (700 nm) triggers the $\omega ^ { 3 }$ FA desaturation, which continues even in the darkness.

Cold stress under blue light (466 nm) did not induce ω3 FA desaturation. Instead, exposure to red light after blue-light treatment resulted in the accumulation of the ω3 FAs after cold stress (Table 15.7.2). These results on the cold-induced ω3 FA desaturation triggered by red light are in agreement with the results on the red-light-mediated cold-induced transcription of the desB gene for the ω3-desaturase. 

Light regulation in Synechocystis operates through a number of histidine kinases – the receptors of blue (Moon et al., 2010; Zirak et al., 2007), green (Hirose et al., 2008), and red (Batschauer et al., 2003; Yeh et al., 1997) light. Green and red light receptors operate in a phytochrome-like mode, which is similar to higher plant phytochromes, implying red–far-red or similar switching cycles. We tried to determine whether redlight-dependent cold-induced gene expression or FA desaturation is regulated in a phytochrome-like mode or not. Interchanges of red (635 nm) and far-red light (726 nm) in various combinations (15 or 30 min of red light followed by 15 or 30 min of far-red light exposure, and vice versa) had no significant effect on the modes of FA desaturation (Table 15.7.3). 

Mutant cells of Synechocystis, defective in Hik33, displayed the products of ω3 desaturation even at normal growth temperature (Table 15.7.4). The analysis of FA composition reveals constitutive synthesis of the ω3-FAs in the ΔHik33 mutant cells. In these cells, the production of the ω3-FAs is not regulated by temperature and light. Neither white light nor red or far-red light regulated the appearance of α-linolenic and stearidonic acids in the mutant cells (Table 15.7.4). It seems that the mutation in Hik33 caused uncontrolled expression of the desB gene, which lost its cold inducibility under red light.

Histidine kinase Hik33 is a cold sensor that is also involved in light regulation of gene expression, and whose activity itself might be regulated by light (Hsiao et al., 2004; Tu et al., 2004). Hik33 was originally characterized as the DspA (drug sensory protein A), mutations in which resulted in cross-resistance to the herbicides difunon and diuron, as well as to the calmodulin antagonists chlorpromazine and trifluoperazine (Bartsevich and Shestakov, 1995), although no eukaryotic-type calmodulins have been reported in cyanobacteria so far. It turned out that Hik33 might play the role of a multisensory protein, which regulates numerous cellular systems, including those that are directly or indirectly associated with acclimation to environmental stresses, photosynthesis, light regulation, and so on (Murata and Los, 2006). Hik33 controls cold signal perception and transduction in the light-dependent manner (Mironov et al., 2012b, 2014), and it is also involved in regulation of genes that depend on light intensity (Hsiao et al., 2004; Tu et al., 2004).

Current data suggest that Hik33 is necessary for cold induction of genes in the light (Mironov et al., 2012b). Hik33 is involved in red-light regulation of cold responses in Synechocystis (Mironov et al., 2014). In the Hik33-mutant cells, transcription of the desB gene is not detectable at normal or low temperatures (Figure 15.7.3). The products of the ω3-FA desaturation are present in the mutant cells; however, this process is no longer regulated by temperature or light (including red light). This means that mutant cells retain some residual expression of the desB gene, whereas its temperature- and light-dependent regulation is completely lost. 

The histidine kinase Hik33 was first identified as a positive regulator of the desB promoter (Suzuki et al., 2000); however, it was suggested that the desB is under the partial control of Hik33 (Suzuki et al., 2001). Later, we observed some accumulation of the desB transcript in the ΔHik33 mutant cells exposed to cold shock (Mironov et al., 2012b). This accumulation, however, may be attributed to stabilization of the mRNA of the desB at low temperature, rather than to transcriptional control (Los et al., 1997). Some authors suggest a dual regulatory role of Hik33, which might act as positive and negative regulators, depending on environmental, especially light, conditions (Hsiao et al., 2004). It is clear now that the 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/dbef56e46b1557fdf3d99dbb3532d0cc4dfa3f29f3fc4a9051b3545612414617.jpg)



Figure 15.7.4 A scheme of light-dependent and light-independent regulation of cold-induced gene expression in Synechocystis.


Hik33-deficient mutant cannot induce the desB gene by cold stress and (red) light. Low-temperature light-dependent regulation of the ω3-FAs production is also lost in the mutant cells. Thus, the histidine kinase Hik33 might work as an additional red-light sensor, although its mode of operation semms to be different from that of the cyanobacterial and plant phytochrome system. The structural basis for light-dependent regulation throught Hik33 might be its PAS domain, which may coordinate light-dependent reactions. The mutation analysis is necessary to verify the importance of the PAS domain in red light regulation through Hik33. 

## 15.7.8 Conclusions

In Synechocystis, the expression of cold-induced genes thata are controlled by the cold sensory histidine kinase Hik33 (e.g., hliB and desB) is regulated by red light (Figure 15.7.4) with a maximum of ∼700 nm. The expression of cold-induced genes that are not controlled by Hik33 (e.g., crhR and rbpA) does not depend on light or light (spectrum) quality. The cold-induced accumulation of the ω3-polyunsaturated FAs in Synechocystis is also light dependent and triggered by red light of ∼700 nm. The sensory histidine kinase Hik33 is involved in the red-lightdependent cold regulation of gene expression and FA desaturation. Hik33 might phosphorylate the response regulator Rre26, which binds to promoter regions of the cold-inducible genes and activates their transcription (Kappell and van Waasbergen, 2007). The kinase activity of Hik33 is facilitated by a small protein, Ssl3451, a homolog of SipA protein of eubacteria (Sakayori et al., 2009). Thus, light-dependent regulation of cold-induced genes in Synechocystis involves at least three known components: Hik33, Rre26, and Ssl3451 (Figure 15.7.4). 

Previously, we have demonstrated that Hik33-independent cold-inducible genes (rbpA, crhR, etc.) (Figure 15.7.4) are regulated nonspecifically through cold-induced changes in genomic DNA supercoiling (Prakash et al., 2009). At present, there is no evidence regarding the proteins that would regulate lightindependent cold-induced gene expression in Synechocystis. 

Another open question is the regulatory mechanism(s) for the fatty acid desaturase genes, desA and desD. Their cold inducibility is partially regulated by changes in DNA supercoiling (Los, 2004; Prakash et al., 2009) and, as shown in Table 15.7.1, might be light dependent. 

The finding that cold-induced gene expression and FA desaturation depend on red light in the cyanobacterium Synechocystis may not be so important to understand the triggering mechanisms of stress responses in other, nonphotosynthesizing bacteria. However, this is important for understanding the mechanisms of cold stress tolerance in cyanobacteria, algae, and plants, the organisms that use sunlight as the main source of energy.
