As already discussed, an acidification of the cytosol may damage biological macromolecules such as proteins that might undergo partial or complete unfolding, leading to inactivation. Thus it is not surprising that, depending on the microorganism, the cytoplasmic DnaK and GroEL and/or periplasmic HdeA and HdeB chaperones play an important role (Lund et al., 2014; see Section 14). 

## 16.2.2.1 Distribution of protective systems in different Gram-positive and Gram-negative neutralophiles

Here and throughout the rest of this chapter, we provide a description of the best documented molecular responses to acidity in some selected microorganisms, especially considering their impact on human health. Readers interested in the occurrence of ATR or AR in other bacteria should refer to Table 16.2.1 for further details and specific references. 

## Escherichia coli

E. coli is a well-known enteric bacterium, extensively studied because many commensals and pathotypes are reported (Kaper et al., 2004). E. coli has an extremely plastic genome, and among commensal and pathogenic strains there can be more than one Megabase (Mb) difference in the genome size (Rasko et al., 2008), with diversity extending also to additional genetic material carried, for example, by virulence plasmids (Johnson and Nolan, 2009). However, both commensal and pathogenic strains are typically armed with very strong AR systems as well as ATR protective mechanisms. In this microorganism, the contribution of the amino acid decarboxylase–antiporter systems and the periplasmic chaperones play a prominent role that has been studied in great detail. Excellent reviews have been recently published on this topic (De Biase and Pennacchietti, 2012; Foster, 2004; Kanjee and Houry, 2013; Lund et al., 2014; Zhao and Houry, 2010). The efficiency and redundancy of these systems probably explain why the urease system is not present. 

The amino acid decarboxylase–antiporter systems fall into the category of AR systems; thus, they do not require pre-adaptation to a mildly acidic pH before an acid challenge. In order to be classified as AR, they also must meet the following requirements: (i) support survival in minimal medium at a pH of 2.0–2.5; and (ii) ensure survival (>10%, i.e., <1 log of bacteria loss) for at least 2 h (and up to 6 h) (Gorden and Small, 1993; Hersh et al., 1996; Lin et al., 1995, 1996; Riggins et al., 2013). Being armed with such systems, it is clear why E. coli (commensal and pathogenic strains) are characterized by an extremely low infection dose (<102 cells). 

E. coli possesses numerous AR systems that are inducible under different conditions: AR1, an oxidative glucose-repressed system that is active in anaerobic cells grown on rich medium at pH 5.5 before being exposed to an extreme acid shock (pH ≤2.5); and four amino acid–dependent AR systems, which require a supplementation (typically, 1 mM) of the name amino acid to function. Four amino acid–dependent systems have been extensively studied in terms of their regulation and mechanism of action (most of them are depicted in Figure 16.2.1): AR2, the glutamate-dependent system; AR3, the arginine-dependent system; AR4, the lysine-dependent system; and AR5, the ornithine-dependent system (reviewed by De Biase and Pennacchietti, 2012; Foster, 2004; Kanjee and Houry, 2013; Zhao and Houry, 2010). The recently discovered glutaminedependent mechanism, named AR2 Q because it shares part of the structural elements of the AR2 system, completes the list (Lu et al., 2013; Lund et al., 2014), though this system instead of using a decarboxylase uses an amidohydrolase, the glutaminase YbaS (Lu et al., 2013). 

The AR2 and AR3 systems have been studied intensively in the last 15 years, and we will mostly deal with AR2 in Sections 16.2.3.1 and 16.2.3.2. 

Recently, a novel ATR mechanism relaying the activities of YdfW (a formyl-CoA transferase) and YfdU (an oxalyl-CoA decarboxylase) has been reported to occur in E. coli (Fontenot et al., 2013). This is in line with a previous report showing that the corresponding genes are under EvgAS control (Masuda and Church, 2002; see Chapter 16.3). In addition, YhjX, a putative antiporter that shares 25% sequence identity with Oxalobacter formigenes oxalat $\mathrm { { e } _ { \mathrm { { i n } } } - \mathrm { { f o r m a t e } _ { \mathrm { { o u t } } } } }$ antiporter OxlT, is also EvgAS induced, though its deletion has no effect on oxalate-induced ATR (Fontenot et al., 2013). Notably, GadE, the major regulator of AR2 in E. coli, appears to play an important role in this oxalate-dependent ATR (Fontenot et al., 2013). 

Oxalate is a toxic byproduct of metabolism, and its accumulation in the body leads to several disorders such as hyperoxaluria, cardiomyopathy, calcium oxalate stones, and renal failure in mammals (Williams and Smith, 1968). Humans do not possess the enzymes required to metabolize dietary and endogenous oxalate, but gut bacteria such as Lactobacillus acidophilus, Oxalobacter formigenes, and Bifidobacterium animalis are pivotal in maintaining the intestinal oxalate balance through oxalate degradation (Abratt and Reid, 2010). 

## Salmonella enterica serovar Typhimurium

S. Typhimurium is a Gram-negative microorganism, well characterized but still regarded as a major health concern because of recurring outbreaks. It possesses a plethora of ATR and AR systems to cope with acidity in the gastric and macrophage phagosome compartments as well as in food matrices (i.e., food preservatives or acidic food). The active export of protons by $\mathrm { F _ { 1 } F _ { o } }$ ATPase is documented for this microorganism (Foster and Hall, 1990, 1991; Garcia-del Portillo et al., 1993). 

S. Typhimurium displays exponential and stationary phase ATR that is provided by inducible amino acid decarboxylases and more than 50 acid shock proteins (ASPs), the expression of which is controlled by RpoS (the stationary phase sigma factor of RNA polymerase) and OmpR in the stationary phase of growth. Fur and PhoPQ (a two-component system; see also Section 3; Chapter 16.3) trigger the expression of ASP in the exponential phase of growth by organic and inorganic acid stress, respectively (Bearson et al., 1997, 1998; Foster, 1993). In addition, in high-acid environments, PhoQ works in concert with PmrAB, another sensor protein, to sense low pH and phosphorylate PhoP (Audia et al., 2001; Bearson et al., 1998). 

Besides in S. Typhimurium, the two-component system PhoPQ (see Section 3) is essential for the virulence of Gramnegative bacteria such as S. flexneri and Yersinia pestis (Groisman, 2001; Monsieurs et al., 2005; Prost et al., 2007). PhoQ acts as a sensor for $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ and $\mathrm { C a } ^ { 2 + }$ , allowing the bacterium to sense the transition from the extracellular environment to intestinal host tissue (where antimicrobial peptides are) or the macrophage (where acidification occurs) (Vescovi et al., 1997). Nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) analysis has provided evidence that the periplasmic sensor domain of PhoQ is activated within acidified phagosomes, and intestinal tissue by the disruption of a network of hydrogen bonds (in which the residue His157 is crucial) is necessary to keep it in a repressed state. Thus, antimicrobial peptides and acidic pH activate PhoQ (Prost et al., 2007; Prost and Miller, 2008). 

In stationary phase cultures of S. Typhimurium, the levels of C17 and C19 CFAs, as measured by GC-MS, have been reported to increase, and the activity of the enzyme CFA synthase has been shown to participate in protection against acid stress. Indeed, mutants in the cfa gene have been shown to be sensitive to low pH (Kim et al., 2005). Also, acid adaptation has been shown to significantly increase the CFA level (Alvarez-Ordonez et al., 2009). More recently, cold stress (typically encountered in the food preservation chain; see Section 15) was shown to increase S. Typhimurium acid tolerance (Shah et al., 2013, 2014). In conclusion, abiotic stresses (acidic food and preservatives, refrigeration, and osmotic stress; see Section 11) enhance the ATR response of S. Typhimurium, a finding that should be always kept in mind (i.e., previous encounters) when considering the infective potential of a pathogen. 

S. Typhimurium also possesses the arginine and lysine decarboxylase–cognate antiporter protective systems (Figure 16.2.1), which play a prominent role in a wide range of pH (from 2.3 to 4.5) depending on oxygen availability and intracellular localization (Kieboom and Abee, 2006; Lee et al., 2007; Viala et al., 2011). S. Typhimurium CadC, a one-component system that acts as both sensor and transcriptional regulator, activates at mildly acidic pH the expression of cadA and cadB, coding for the lysine decarboxylase and the lysine–cadaverine antiporter, respectively (Watson et al., 1992). This occurs via a proteolytic cleavage of the N-terminal DNA-binding domain, which localizes in the inner membrane and activates the expression of the target genes (Lee YH et al., 2008). Similarly to PhoQ, low-pH detection in CadC is due to a change in the protonation state of some residues in the periplasmic sensor domain (i.e., under low pH, the relative orientation of two monomers in the active CadC dimer changes) (Haneburger et al., 2011), and the formation of a disulfide bridge between the residues Cys208 and Cys272 is not supported (though it can be replaced by a salt bridge when mutated into Asp208 and Lys272) (Tetsch et al., 2011). Notably, periplasmic cadaverine converts CadC into an inactive state (Haneburger et al., 2012), whereas lysine in the medium triggers the expression of the system but requires the lysine permease (LysP) to enter (Tetsch et al., 2008). 

## Helicobacter pylori

H. pylori is an exception among the neutralophiles for which acid protection mechanisms have been studied in detail (see Table 16.2.1). This Gram-negative bacterium, responsible for a wide range of gastric diseases (from gastritis to ulcer disease to gastric cancer), resides in the human stomach and relies on a potent cytoplasmic urease, which is produced in large amounts, and a periplasmic α-type carbonic anhydrase; the activity of both enzymes ensures that the periplasmic space is always kept at a pH close to neutral, thus never dampening the cytoplasmic pH. This mechanism of microenvironment neutralization is referred to as acid acclimation, and it not only ensures H. pylori survival but even supports growth under extreme acid conditions (Sachs et al., 2005). Based on the considerations made in Section 16.2.2 on the solubility $\mathrm { \ o f { C O _ { 2 } } }$ and $\mathrm { N H } _ { 3 } ,$ , the ability of H. pylori to keep periplasmic pH above 6.0 allows the formation of bicarbonate and the production of ammonium ions. Basically, urea (1–3 mM in gastric juice) passes through the inner membrane by a specific channel, UreI, and is then converted into $\mathrm { C O } _ { 2 }$ and ammonia by urease: the former spreads in the periplasm and buffers the pH as bicarbonate formed by the α-type carbonic anhydrase (Marcus et al., 2005; Scott et al., 2010), while the latter takes up protons and generates $\mathrm { N H _ { 4 } } ^ { + }$ in the cytoplasm and periplasm. UreI, an $\mathrm { H ^ { + } }$ -gated channel activated at acid pH, is required for the full development of urease activity (Weeks et al., 2000). It allows the passive and not-saturable transport of urea in a temperatureindependent fashion, and for this a histidine residue, His123, on the periplasmic side is required. 

The structure of urease has been solved (Ha et al., 2001): it is a 24mer, a tetrahedral complex of four UreA–UreB trimers. It contains 12 catalytic units. Thus, UreA and UreB are the structural subunits of urease, whereas UreE/G and UreE/F are required for nickel $( \mathrm { N i } ^ { 2 + } )$ insertion into preassembled urease to trigger urease activity. The transcription of the urease-encoding operon is mediated by ArsRS, a two-component histidine kinase system (Pflock et al., 2005; see Section 3), but also by NikR, an important regulator of $\mathrm { N i } ^ { 2 + }$ homeostasis (Li and Zamble, 2009). Notably, the ArsRS system operates only under mild acidity, whereas at a pH of 2.5 H. pylori makes use of the cytoplasmic histidine kinase FlgS and is able to recognize some important genes overlapping with ArsR (Wen et al., 2009). Also, in the case of ArsS, histidine residues play a role in the acid-driven activation: His94 was found to be critical for activation at pH 5.0, though in the presence of other mutations some other residues may take on this role (Muller et al., 2009). 

## Streptococcus

Among the Gram-positive bacteria, the members of the Streptococcus genus deserve major attention since they are the most abundant inhabitants of human oral plaque (Dewhirst et al., 2010). Whereas the decarboxylase–antiporter systems seem to be absent, all other protective systems are present but play different roles in different species of this genus (for specific references, see Table 16.2.1). For example, Streptococcus rattus, 

Streptococcus sanguis, and Streptococcus suis rely on the activity of the arginine deiminase (ADI) system, whereas the agmatine deiminase (AgDI) system is most important in Streptococcus mutans. On the other hand, Streptococcus salivaris copes with low pH through the activity of urease, and thus its survival is strongly dependent on the concentration of urea. In addition, in S. mutans, a conspicuous increase of long-chain monounsaturated fatty acids has been observed in response to acid stress. 

Notably, the proton-pumping activity of the $\mathrm { F _ { 1 } F _ { o } }$ ATPase, the expression of which is activated at low pH, is an ATR mechanism common to many species of this genus. 

## 16.2.3 The glutamate-dependent AR system (AR2)

Among the amino acid decarboxylase–antiporter systems, the glutamate-dependent system (AR2) is by far the most investigated because it is the most potent AR system in E. coli, though it is also operative in many other microorganisms, including S. flexneri, L. monocytogenes, Brucella microti, and newly described and atypical Brucella spp. (Damiano et al., 2015; De Biase and Pennacchietti, 2012). AR2 employs GadA and GadB, two PLP-dependent decarboxylases that catalyze the protonconsuming decarboxylation of glutamate (L-Glu) to generate γ- aminobutyrate (GABA) and GadC, the cognate antiporter performing (at the level of the inner membrane) the import of L-Glu and the export of GABA (Castanie-Cornet et al., 1999; De Biase et al., 1999; Hersh et al., 1996; Waterman and Small, 1996; see also Chapter 16.1). 

As shown in Figure 16.2.2a, the gene encoding GadA (gadA) is 2.1 Mb from the operon gadBC coding for GadB and GadC. Notably, gadA is also the rightmost gene in a genome region called the acid fitness island (AFI), which carries 13 additional genes coding for many regulators and for proteins involved at different levels in AR (De Biase and Pennacchietti, 2012; Foster, 2004; Zhao and Houry, 2010). 

The system is activated in cells grown to the exponential phase in minimal medium at pH 5.5 and in the stationary phase cells grown in rich medium, regardless of the pH. For the system to function, glutamate must be supplemented to the minimal medium at pH ≤2.5 that is used for the acid challenge (Castien-Cornet et al., 1999; De Biase et al., 1999; Lin et al., 1996). The AR2 becomes activated when a cell is exposed to an extremely acid environment (pH ≤2.5) because this leads to a sudden decrease of intracellular pH (≤5.0) due to the uncontrolled entry of protons inside the cell (Richard and Foster, 2004). At pH <3.0, the membrane antiporter GadC becomes activated, and the same happens intracellularly with the two decarboxylases GadA/B, the pH optimum of which is between 3.8 and 5.0 (Najjar and Fisher, 1954; Shukuya and Schwert, 1960). The presence of glutamate for AR2, as well as arginine for AR3, is pivotal in these systems because cells under acid stress keep their pH stable at 4.2±0.1, a value close to the optimum of pH of GadA and GadB (Foster, 2004). Furthermore, a unique feature of the AR2 system is that both glutamate and GABA have the γ-carboxyl group, whose $\mathrm { p K _ { a } }$ is close to pH under acidic conditions $\mathrm { ( p K _ { a } }$ of 4.1 and 4.0 for glutamate and GABA, respectively), so that they are able to buffer better than the amino acids of the other AR system (Lund et al., 2014).

(a) Location of the loci encoding the two glutamate decarboxylase isoforms GadA/B (blue), the glutamate–GABA antiporter GadC (red), additional membrane proteins (green), multidrug exporters (magenta), acid stress periplasmic chaperones (grey), LuxR-like transcriptional regulators (yellow), AraC-like transcriptional regulators (orange), and small regulatory RNAs (violet). The bent arrows show the transcripts’ starting points and the length of the operons. (b) Schematic model of E. coli GadB hexamer at acid (left) and neutral pH (right) showing the major conformational changes in the (i) 1–14 N-terminal region (red), (ii) 452–466 C-terminal tail (magenta), and (iii) 300–313 β-hairpin (light blue). PLP in the active site is shown in yellow.


Based on chemical considerations and on work carried out on GadC proteoliposomes (lipomomes reconstituted with correctly oriented GadC), it was possible to demonstrate that GadC recognizes and exchanges L-Glu or GABA by virtue of the protonation state of their carboxyl groups (De Biase and Pennacchietti, 2012; Ma et al., 2013; Tsai et al., 2013). In other words, GadC performs the antiport via a logic-gate mechanism by discriminating the two molecules according to their net charge: allowing only the entry of $\mathrm { G l u } ^ { 0 / - 1 }$ and the exit of $\mathrm { G A B A ^ { + 1 } }$ . In this way, GadC acts as an intelligent system in order to guarantee the export of intracellular protons and not permit proton influx (in the case of $\mathrm { G l u ^ { + 1 } { - } G A B A ^ { 0 } ) }$ or neutral antiport $( \mathrm { G l u ^ { 0 } { - } G A B A ^ { 0 } }$ or $\mathrm { G l u ^ { + 1 } { - } G A B A ^ { + 1 } ) }$ (Ma et al., 2013; Tsai et al., 2013). The substrate selectivity of GadC is also proven by experiments with glutamine (Gln), a natural amino acid chemically similar to Glu but missing the γ-carboxyl group: Thus, Gln can only exist as $\operatorname { G l n } ^ { 0 }$ or $\mathrm { G l n ^ { + 1 } }$ , depending on the protonation state of the α-carboxyl group at acidic pH. The only species that is transported by GadC is Gln0, which is the one not protonated on the α-carboxyl group (Ma et al., 2013; Tsai et al., 2013). This is an important finding because it also establishes a link between the AR2 and the AR2 Q systems, taking into account that Gln is much more abundant as the free amino acid than L-Glu (Lu et al., 2013). 

In addition, the GadC-mediated $\mathrm { G l u ^ { 0 } { - } G A B A ^ { + 1 } }$ electrogenic antiport may also be of help in maintaining the proton motive force, thereby supporting the synthesis of ATP even in conditions in which the electron transport chain is not working (Shepherd et al., 2010; Small and Waterman, 1998). 

## 16.2.3.1 Structural and biochemical features of E. coli GadB and GadC

The crystal structure of E. coli GadB at acidic (active form) and neutral (inactive form) pH (4.6 and 7.6, respectively) allowed for the first time an understanding of the structural basis for the pHdependent regulation of this enzyme and what kind of amino acids are critical for this (Capitani et al., 2003). 

E. coli GadB has two key features that make this enzyme very different from the other bacterial amino acid–dependent decarboxylases: first, GadB is a homohexamer of 318 kDa that can be described as a trimer of dimers and organized in two layers, where each monomer is located; and, second, in the pH range of 4.6–7.6, the hexameric structure is unaffected, unlike arginine and lysine decarboxylases, participating in AR3 and AR4, respectively, which dissociate into dimers when exposed to a less acidic environment (Andrell et al., 2009; Kanjee et al., 2011). 

However, like many other PLP-dependent enzymes, the GadB dimer is the structural unit because each active site is composed by amino acids coming from both subunits in the functional dimer (Capitani et al., 2003). The entire structure is stabilized by the N-terminal domain (residues 1–57) of each monomer, which acts like a hook and departs perpendicularly from the surface of every subunit to reach the opposite layer, where it establishes crucial contacts with the counterpart of the functional dimer and with a monomer belonging to a neighboring dimer. 

Surprisingly, the exposure to acid pH triggers critical structural changes affecting the N- and C-terminal tails, and it repositions a β-hairpin (Figure 16.2.2). 

Residues 3–15 of the N-terminal domain are disordered in the structure at neutral pH but ordered at pH 4.6 because this region is rich in negatively charged amino acids, which at neutral pH are deprotonated and therefore repel each other; the protonation state of these same residues at acid pH leads to the acquisition of an ordered helical structure and gives rise to two triple-helix bundles, one for each layer (Figure 16.2.2b). Studies on an Nterminal Δ1–14 mutant suggest that acquiring an ordered helical structure is fundamental for the membrane localization of GadB, where the high concentration of protons and the proximity with GadC enhance the enzymatic activity and the efficiency of the system (Capitani et al., 2003). Besides, the N-terminal domain is further stabilized by the binding of halides at allosteric sites underneath each α-helix in the two bundles. Halides, especially chloride (abundant in gastric secretions), by stabilizing the active form, act as positive allosteric modulators (Gut et al., 2006; Iyer et al., 2002). 

As schematically depicted in Figure 16.2.2b, exclusively at neutral pH the residues 452–466 in the C-terminal tail are finely structured and point toward the active site where the penultimate residue in the sequence, His465, establishes a reversible covalent bond with the PLP–Lys Schiff base and closes the site funnel by physical hindrance (Gut et al., 2006), a role confirmed by studying the site-specific GadBHis465Ala and GadBΔHis465Thr466 mutants. On the contrary, at acidic pH, the C-terminal tail exits from the active site, and its movement is accompanied by the shift of a β-hairpin (residues 300–313) toward the center of the active site entry. Thus, at acid pH, the enzyme becomes active because the active site becomes accessible to substrate and solvent (Capitani et al., 2003; Gut et al., 2006). 

The multiple sequence alignment of 20 bacterial Gad indicates that 84 amino acid residues are conserved (18% of the GadB sequence), some of which can be considered as characteristic residues of all bacterial Gad (De Biase and Pennacchietti, 2012). These include the following amino acids (numbering refers to E. coli GadB): (i) Asp86, which in GadB active form points toward the γ-carboxyl group of the substrate L-Glu; (ii) Glu89, which is close to Asp86 at acidic pH; (iii) Gln163, which establishes a “stacking” contact with the cofactor PLP; (iv) Thr62 and Phe63, which contact the γ-carboxyl group of L-Glu; (v) His275, which is critical for the positioning of the cofactor PLP; (vi) Tyr305, which during the catalytic cycle might act as a proton donor; and (vii) His465, which is responsible for closing the active site at pH ≥6.0. Finally, Cys64, Cys130, and Cys165, which are in the active site, might be involved in disulfide bridge formation, although the crystalline structure does not support this hypothesis (Capitani et al., 2003). 

Recently, structural and biochemical studies on E. coli GadC have provided insight into the functioning of this protein (Ma et al., 2012, 2013; Tsai et al., 2013). The crystal structure, solved at pH 8.5, indicates that at this pH GadC is in the inactive conformation, and that to assay its activity in proteoliposomes it is necessary to decrease the pH to ≤5.5 (Ma et al., 2013), similarly to the cognate GadB decarboxylase (Gut et al., 2006; Pennacchietti et al., 2009). Notably, at pH 8.5, GadC adopts a mechanism, resembling that of GadB, to control its activity: the C-terminal fragment (residues 477–511), named the C-plug, is visible and points toward the substrates transport channel, thereby locking it. It was demonstrated that the protonation state of histidine residues is crucial to achieve this (Ma et al., 2012). 

The finding that GadC is able to recognize and exchange L-Gln correlates with the upregulation of the E. coli ybaS gene, coding for a glutaminase, under the same stress condition in which the operon gadBC and AFI genes are upregulated (Hayes et al., 2006; Shepherd et al., 2010; Tucker et al., 2002, 2003; Weber et al., 2005). 

## 16.2.3.2 The AR2 system in other bacteria

To date, the AR2 system is reported to occur in many other bacteria, such as S. flexneri, L. monocytogenes, Brucella microti, and the lactic acid bacterium Lactococcus lactis. In general, the proximity of gadB and gadC genes in many enteric bacteria (pathogenic or not) suggests a pivotal role for their survival during their transit of the GIT. Indeed, in several Grampositive and Gram-negative bacteria, these genes are next to or near each other, and such arrangement likely guarantees a coordinated and thus more efficient expression (De Biase and Pennacchietti, 2012). In particular, the Gram-negative bacterium B. microti and many newly described and atypical Brucellae (Damiano et al., 2015; Occhialini et al., 2012) possess functional gadB and gadC genes, unlike many other pathogenic species of the same genus whose gad genes are interrupted by frameshift mutations or stop codons. In the case of B. microti, the functional AR2 system was shown to improve survival in a mouse model following oral infection. 

The foodborne pathogen L. monocytogenes has five genes linked to the GAD system: gadT1 and gadT2, encoding the antiporters, and gadD1, gadD2, and gadD3, coding for the decarboxylases, which are in three distinct genomic loci (gadD1T1, gadD2T2, and gadD3). The first is responsible for the growth under mild acidity, and the second under strong acid stress (Cotter et al., 2001a,b, 2005). Data suggest that the gadT2 gene likely codes for the principal exchanger L-Glu-GABA under extreme acidity (Karatzas et al., 2010). 

Like L. monocytogenes, many other bacteria possess two or more genes coding for the glutamate decarboxylase, and at the moment the most known of the group are Lactobacillus brevis and Edwardsiella tarda (the only species of the Edwardsiella genus that is pathogenic for humans). A bioinformatics search assigns a high score to gadC when searching for gadB in Yersinia enterocolitica, Lawsonia intracellularis, and E. tarda, suggesting also that gadB and gadC are organized in operons in these microorganisms (De Biase and Pennacchietti, 2012). Besides, in the non-enteric bacteria Salinospora tropica (Gram-positive) and Bordetella avium (Gram-negative), gadB and gadC genes lie next to each other and are divergent. Similarly, in other bacteria, gadB and gadC genes are spaced by one or more functionally linked genes. This is the case for Bacteroides thetaiotaomicron and Clostridium perfrigens, where the spacing genes are important for the biosynthesis of glutamate in the former, or for the export of GABA in the latter (Nguyen and Sparks-Thissen, 2012). 

Last but not least, among Bifidobacterium the only species which possess GAD genes are Bifidobacterium dentium, Bifidobacterium adolescentis L2-32, and Bifidobacterium angulatum DSM 20098. In particular, in B. dentium, an opportunistic pathogen for the human oral cavity, the presence of gadB and gadC genes, which are upregulated up to 90 and 51-fold respectively in an acid environment, is likely linked to its fitness and competitiveness in the oral niche (Ventura et al., 2009). 

Polymerase chain reaction (PCR) on the gadBC operon and GAD activity assays suggest that Escherichia albertii and Citrobacter braakii exhibit a glutamate-dependent acid resistance (Park and Diez-Gonzalez, 2004).

In many bacteria, as reported in the examples in this chapter, the gadBC system is closely related to the ability to survive in acidic conditions outside or inside a host, thus improving the fitness trait to pathogenic species. However, recent findings reported that counteracting an acid stress is only one of the functions of such genes. Indeed, an interesting and fascinating hypothesis suggests that the decarboxylation of Glu combined with the electrogenic antiport (Glu0–GABA+1) generates the proton moving force closely responsible for the production of ATP (Higuchi et al., 1997; Sanders et al., 1998), an extremely successful strategy not only for anaerobes like acid lactic bacteria (LAB) but also for some facultative anaerobes, such as E. coli and S. flexneri. In fact, in E. coli, the expression of gadB and gadC genes increases under respiratory stress, confirming their role in ATP synthesis under $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -limiting conditions (Shepherd et al., 2010).

The production of GABA by a decarboxylase–antiporter system finds a very important application role in the fermentation of protein-rich food containing high levels of protein-derived glutamate and glutamine (Su et al., 2011). Indeed, the ability to produce and release GABA in LAB is directly linked to both survival during the passage through the GIT and additional physiological conditions. GABA, apart from acting as an inhibitory neurotransmitter in higher animals, is reported to be a diuretic, tranquillizer, and hypotensive (Wong et al., 2003) with strong insulin-secreting properties, to promote the healing process of cutaneous wounds and improve the survival of fibroblasts exposed to oxidative stress (Adeghate and Ponery, 2002; Han et al., 2007; Ito et al., 2007). In addition, GABA possesses positive properties for chronic alcohol-related symptoms (Wong et al., 2003), and it is currently under investigation for the production of new cosmetic formulations because it may induce the expression of human genes involved in skin protection, such as filaggrin, hyaluronan synthase, and beta-defensin-2 (Di Cagno et al., 2010).

# Two-component systems in sensing and adapting to acid stress in Escherichia coli

Yoko Eguchi and Ryutaro Utsumi 

Department of Bioscience, Graduate School of Agriculture, Kinki University, Nakamachi, Nara, Japan 

## 16.3.1 Introduction

Bacteria respond to the environmental stresses they encounter by controlling their gene expression via factors such as twocomponent signal transduction systems (TCSs), sigma factors, and transcription factors (see Sections 3, 4). These factors are usually coordinated to form cascades and networks of regulation to adapt themselves to environmental changes. The acid response network in Escherichia coli is a good example: At least 14 gene products directly participate in the biochemistry of acid resistance (AR), and at least 15 regulators govern the expression of one or more of the 14 AR genes (Foster, 2004). 

In E. coli, AR helps cells pass through the extremely acidic barrier of the stomach to reach the intestine of the host. E. coli cells employ at least four AR systems for extreme AR (allowing cells to survive at levels too acidic (pH ≤2.5) to permit growth) to combat the acid barrier (Lund et al., 2014). AR system 1 is induced under oxidative growth conditions (see Section 10), repressed by glucose, dependent on RpoS, and operative during pH 2.5 challenges in minimal medium without the addition of amino acids (Castanie-Cornet et al., 1999; Lin et al., 1995). The other three AR systems require the addition of glutamic acid (AR system 2), arginine (AR system 3), or lysine (AR system 4). These systems use the amino acid–dependent decarboxylase– antiporter systems (Foster, 2004). AR system 2 is the most effective system of the four, and it relies on the two glutamic acid decarboxylase isozymes GadA and GadB, as well as the glutamate–GABA (γ-amino butyric acid) antiporter GadC (Castanie-Cornet et al., 1999; Hersh et al., 1996; Smith et al., 1992) (see also Chapter 16.2). The arginine decarboxylase AdiA and the arginine–agmatine antiporter AdiC are the components of AR system 3, and the lysine decarboxylase CadA and the lysine–cadaverine antiporter CadB are the components of AR system 4. AR systems 3 and 4 are induced by low pH, by anaerobic conditions, and in the presence of the corresponding amino acid (Gong et al., 2003; Meng and Bennett, 1992). AR system 2 is under extremely complicated control, with at least 10 regulatory proteins controlling the gadA and gadBC loci (Ma et al., 2004), but at the top of the regulation network sits the EvgS–EvgA TCS (Itou et al., 2009; Ma et al., 2004; Masuda and Church, 2002). Activation of EvgS–EvgA initiates a transcriptional cascade of regulators EvgA, YdeO, and GadE. These regulators induce the expression of a number of AR genes, including the genes involved in AR system 2. In addition to the transcriptional cascade, EvgS–EvgA activates another TCS, PhoQ–PhoP (see also Chapter 16.2), via a small connector protein, SafA. This is a 65-amino-acid membrane protein that is directly induced by EvgA and directly interacts with the sensor PhoQ at the inner membrane (Eguchi et al., 2007, 2012). Several other small proteins that connect TCSs have been reported, implying the existence of a signal transduction network in E. coli. 

In this chapter, we will first describe the signal transduction cascade of the TCSs, from the EvgS–EvgA to PhoQ–PhoP TCSs and then to RssB (the response regulator of a TCS system) connected by small connector proteins, that contribute to AR (Eguchi et al., 2011). Next, signals that activate the EvgS sensor and switches in the signal transduction cascade will be shown. Finally, the domains in the sensor EvgS involved in sensing the signals will be discussed (Eguchi and Utsumi, 2014). 

## 16.3.2 Materials and methods

Bacterial strains and plasmids: The E. coli strains and plasmids used in this study are listed in Table 16.3.1. The strains were aerobically grown at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in Luria-Bertani (LB) medium (1% (w/v) Bacto tryptone (BD Diagnostics, USA), 0.5% (w/v) Bacto yeast extract (BD Diagnostics), 1% (w/v) NaCl, pH 7.5), M9 medium (pH 5.5 or 7.5 with the addition of 0.2% glucose, 0.0001% vitamin $\mathrm { B } _ { 1 }$ , and 0.004% casamino acids (w/v)) (Miller, 1992), or minimal A medium (pH 5.5 or 7.5 with the addition of 0.2% glucose, 0.0005% vitamin $\mathrm { B } _ { 1 } ,$ , and 0.004% casamino acids (w/v)) (Miller, 1992). When necessary, selective antibiotics (100 μg/mL ampicillin, 25 μg/mL kanamycin, 25 μg/mL chloramphenicol, or 12.5 μg/mL tetracycline) were added to the medium. Arabinose was added to the culture at a final concentration of 0.002% or 0.2% (w/v) when cells were transformed with plasmids carrying an arabinose promoter.

AR assay: AR was determined by the method of Masuda and Church (2002). Cells were grown to the mid-exponential phase, and a 50 μL cell culture was transferred to 1.95 mL pre-warmed LB medium (adjusted to pH 2.5 with HCl) and incubated at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 1 h. Viable cells in the acidified culture were counted by plating serial dilutions onto LB plates. The percentage of acid survival was calculated as the number of viable cells remaining after acid treatment divided by the viable counts before acid treatment. Each experiment was repeated at least three times, and the means and standard errors were determined. 

β-Galactosidase assay: A single colony of an E. coli strain was inoculated in 5 mL LB medium containing appropriate antibiotics and grown overnight with aeration at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Ten milliliters of minimal medium with antibiotics and arabinose for induction were inoculated with 0.1 mL overnight culture and grown at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with aeration. When the culture reached a cell optical density at 600 nm $\mathrm { ( O D _ { 6 0 0 } ) }$ of 0.2 to 0.3, cells were subjected to a β-galactosidase assay in duplicate, and the results were expressed as Miller units (Miller, 1992). The data shown are means and standard deviations from at least three individual cultures. Statistical analysis was performed by unpaired t-tests using Prism® software (GraphPad, USA). 

Detection of EvgS variants and chimera sensors in the membrane fraction: Cells were grown in 10 mL of supplemented minimal medium as described for the β-galactosidase assay to $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 } = 0 . 2 – 0 . 3$ . From the cultures, 0.4 mL was sampled for the β-galactosidase assay, and the remaining 9.6 mL was centrifuged for 10 min at 2300×g and $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . The pellets were suspended in 500 μL lysis buffer (100 mM NaCl, 50 mM Tris-HCl, pH 8) and sonicated for cell disruption. The cell lysates were centrifuged for 10 min at 2300×g and $4 ^ { \circ } C ,$ and the supernatants were further centrifuged for 30 min at 17,800×g and $4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ to collect the membrane fractions. The pellets were suspended in 20 μL lysis buffer and 5 μL of 5× sample buffer for sodium dodecyl sulfate–polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (SDS-PAGE), and 10 μL of each sample was subjected to SDS-PAGE. The electrophoresed proteins were then transferred to a polyvinylidene difluoride membrane (Immobilon-P transfer membranes; Millipore, USA) and probed with anti-EvgS or anti-PhoQ antiserum. Detection was carried out with enhanced chemiluminescence (ECL) peroxidase-labeled antirabbit antibody (GE Healthcare Bio-Science, USA) and LuminataTM Forte Western horseradish peroxidase substrate (Millipore). Signals were acquired using an Image-Quant Imager 400 system (GE Healthcare Bio-Science). 

Construction of EvgS variants and EvgS–PhoQ chimeras: Site-directed mutagenesis at L152 of EvgS was performed using the QuikChange site-directed mutagenesis kit (Stratagene) as described in Eguchi and Utsumi (2014). pBADevgSdeltaSD was constructed using the evgS531- F (5′ -ctatatagcgagcaattttatattg-3′ ) and evgS29-R (5′ - acgatattcgatgtaatcttcg-3′ ) primers and pBADevgS as a template for polymerase chain reaction (PCR). The PCR product was treated with DpnI (Toyobo, Japan) to degrade the template plasmid, phosphorylated at its 5′ end with Polynucleotide Kinase (Toyobo) and self-ligated. EvgS– PhoQ chimeras were constructed by blunt ligating DNA fragments encoding $\mathrm { E v g S _ { 1 - 5 5 8 } }$ and $\mathrm { E v g S _ { 1 - 7 1 0 } }$ to a plasmid with an arabinose-inducible phoQ encoding $\mathrm { P h o Q } _ { 2 1 5 - 4 8 6 }$ and $\mathrm { P h o Q } _ { 2 6 7 - 4 8 6 }$ (Eguchi and Utsumi, 2014). All constructed plasmids were confirmed by DNA sequencing. 

## 16.3.3 Results and discussion

## 16.3.3.1 Connectors of TCSs contribute to AR

When the EvgS–EvgA TCS in E. coli was activated by inserting a mutation in the sensor EvgS (evgS1) (Kato, 2000) or overexpressing the response regulator EvgA, cells in the exponential phase showed increased survival after extreme acid treatment (pH 2.5, 1 h) (Eguchi et al., 2011; Masuda and Church, 2002). EvgS–EvgA switches on the transcriptional cascade and induces the expression of AR genes such as gadA, gadBC, hdeA, hdeD, and ydeP (Itou et al., 2009; Masuda and Church, 2002, 2003), conferring AR to the cells. EvgS–EvgA also turns on a signal transduction cascade to PhoQ–PhoP via the small connector protein SafA (Eguchi et al., 2007). The PhoQ–PhoP system is repressed by a high concentration of divalent cations, but it is activated by cationic antimicrobial peptides and low pH (Bader et al., 2005; Garcia Vescovi et al., 1996; Prost et al., 2007). PhoQ– PhoP regulates the expression of some AR genes (Zwir et al. 2005). For this reason, we hypothesized that the signal transduction cascade from EvgS–EvgA to PhoQ–PhoP (see Chapter 16.2) may contribute to the AR conferred by EvgS activation. 

First, we deleted safA from an evgS1 strain to see how it contributed to the AR phenotype. We also deleted phoP and phoQ. As a result, the absence of safA, phoP, or phoQ rendered the evgS1 cells acid sensitive. Complementation of the evgS1 safA double mutant with a safA-expressing plasmid rescued the safA phenotype and increased the survival rate, but it did not rescue the evgS1 phoP or evgS1 phoQ mutants (Table 16.3.2). 


Table 16.3.2 Connector SafA contributes to acid resistance conferred by EvgS–EvgA activation.


<table><tr><td>Strain</td><td>Survival rate (%)</td></tr><tr><td>Wild / vector*</td><td>0.004 ± 0.007</td></tr><tr><td>evgS1 / vector</td><td>1.38 ± 0.14</td></tr><tr><td>evgS1 safA / vector</td><td>0.029 ± 0.024</td></tr><tr><td>evgS1 safA / pBADsafA</td><td>2.06 ± 0.74</td></tr><tr><td>evgS1 phoP / pBADsafA</td><td>0.018 ± 0.011</td></tr><tr><td>evgS1 phoQ / pBADsafA</td><td>0.042 ± 0.024</td></tr></table>


∗Vector: pBAD18. Survival rates are expressed as mean ± SD from three individual experiments. 


These results indicate that the connector SafA contributed to the AR phenotype via the PhoQ–PhoP system. Complementation of evgS1 phoP and evgS1 phoQ mutants with a low-copy plasmid that included the phoPQ operon partially restored the acid-resistant phenotype (Eguchi et al., 2011). The reason why the survival rates did not recover to the level of evgS1 mutant may be due to the enhanced expression of phoP and phoQ from the plasmid. 

In E. coli, cells are usually more acid resistant in the stationary phase than in the exponential phase. In our former transcriptome analysis, comparing evgS1 mutants to wild-type E. coli in the exponential phase (Eguchi et al., 2003), expression of as many as 25 RpoS-upregulated genes (including katE, appA, dps, ficA, and osmY) was increased in the evgS1 mutant. We measured and compared the cellular RpoS levels of the wildtype strain and the evgS1 mutant, and we found that RpoS actually accumulated in the evgS1 mutant. Chloramphenicol chase experiments further showed that the accumulation of RpoS was due to stabilization of RpoS (Eguchi et al., 2011). 

The response regulator RssB is one of the various factors that control RpoS stability (Muffler et al., 1996). RssB binds to RpoS and delivers it to the ClpXP protease for degradation (Zhou et al., 2001). Small-sized anti-adaptors (IraP, IraD, and IraM) bind to RssB and prevent it from binding to RpoS, increasing RpoS stability (Bougdour et al., 2008). IraM is an anti-adaptor that is 107 amino acids long and is induced by the PhoQ–PhoP system. Thus, IraM can also be called a connector of TCSs because it connects the PhoQ–PhoP system to the response regulator RssB (Mitrophanov and Groisman, 2008). Expression of iraM was actually induced by EvgS–EvgA activation via PhoQ–PhoP, and IraM was involved in the accumulation of RpoS induced by EvgS activation during the exponential phase (Eguchi et al., 2011). IraM also contributed to the AR conferred by EvgS–EvgA. 

From our results, we propose a signal transduction cascade that regulates AR genes. Activation of the EvgS–EvgA system directly induces the connector SafA, SafA directly activates the PhoQ–PhoP system, the PhoQ–PhoP system directly induces connector IraM, and IraM directly binds to the response regulator RssB and stabilizes RpoS (Figure 16.3.1). An increase in the cellular RpoS level enhances gadE and gadX expression, leading to the induction of many AR genes, such as gadA, gadBC, and hdeA (see also Chapter 16.2). In addition to the stabilization of RpoS, activation of PhoQ–PhoP also induces expression of the AR genes gadW and hdeA. This explains why deletion of safA, phoP, or phoQ from an evgS1 mutant resulted in a larger reduction of survival rate than deletion of iraM. Thus, a network of AR gene regulation initiated by the EvgS–EvgA system is proposed. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/2da6cd7372c3e1473b4ac90dd72bb3c2b732f479c22a2776c9419f5f95637a15.jpg)



Figure 16.3.1 Model of the regulation network of acid resistance (AR) genes initiated by the EvgS–EvgA system. EvgS–EvgA induces the EvgA–YdeO–GadE cascade of AR gene regulation, and a branched pathway via SafA activates the PhoQ–PhoP system, leading to an enhanced level of cellular RpoS via IraM. RpoS induces gadE, the central regulator of AR genes, enforcing the acid-resistant phenotype.


## 16.3.3.2 Signals required for EvgS activation

What stimuli activate the sensor EvgS? EvgS is a hybrid histidine kinase composed of a histidine kinase (HisKA and HATPase c), a receiver, and Hpt transmitter domains (see Section 3). The phosphate at the initial histidine kinase domain subsequently transferred to an aspartate residue in the receiver domain and is relayed to the second histidine residue in the Hpt domain before being transferred to the aspartate residue in EvgA (Perraud et al., 

1998). EvgS also has a large periplasmic region consisting of two tandem PBPb (bacterial periplasmic solute-binding protein) domains that are ubiquitous in all kingdoms of life; the plasticity of these domains offers several possible modes of action. Because a signal peptide is predicted at the N-terminal end, EvgS is predicted to have a single transmembrane domain with the Nterminal end protruding into the periplasmic space. 

It has been shown previously that E. coli cells growing in mild acidic minimal medium (pH 5.5) induce AR by activating the EvgS–EvgA system (Burton et al., 2010; Ma et al., 2004). Therefore, we first checked whether this condition activated the EvgS sensor. An E. coli strain with a chromosomal ydeP–lacZ operon fusion was used to analyze the activity of the EvgS–EvgA system. Expression of ydeP is directly regulated by EvgA. When this strain was grown in minimal A medium (pH 5.5), ydeP expression was induced. However, no induction was observed in M9 medium (pH 5.5) (Eguchi and Utsumi, 2014). We compared the compositions of the two minimal media and noticed that M9 medium had higher [Na+] and lower [K+]. In order to check the effect of [K+] against EvgS activation, KCl was added to M9 medium at different concentrations. As shown in Table 16.3.3, ydeP expression was induced by the addition of KCl at final concentrations of 100 mM at pH 5.5 but not at pH 7.5. To our surprise, the addition of NaCl and LiCl to M9 medium at pH 5.5 also induced ydeP expression, but the addition of MgCl did not. These results strongly suggested that, in addition to low pH, high concentrations of alkali metals such as Na+ and K+ are required for EvgS activation. This effect of a high concentration of alkali metals was not a consequence of high osmolality, and it was dependent on EvgS. When measured by our reporter system, the pH range for EvgS activation was from pH 5.0 to 6.0 (Eguchi and Utsumi, 2014).

## 16.3.3.3 The periplasmic region of EvgS is involved in activation

Strain KMY1 is another reporter strain of the EvgS–EvgA system that was constructed in our laboratory (Kato et al., 2000). This strain is an MC4100 derivative with a lacZ gene under the control of an emrK promoter. Expression of the emrK gene is directly regulated by EvgA (Kato et al., 2000). KMY1 was grown in KCl-supplemented M9 medium (pH 5.5), and to our surprise, EvgS–EvgA was not activated. When another reporter strain measuring the expression of the emrK gene but derived from a different strain (MG1655 emrKY–lacZ) was used, EvgS– EvgA was activated by KCl-supplemented M9 medium (pH 5.5). Thus, we compared the evgS sequences of the two strains, KMY1 and MG1655 emrKY–lacZ, and found that “CTT” encoding Leu152 in MG1655 emrKY–lacZ was mutated to “TTT” encoding Phe in KMY1. This residue is located in the first PBPb domain in the periplasmic region of EvgS. In order to confirm that this mutation led to the desensitization of EvgS, plasmids to express MG1655-type EvgS (pBADevgS) and KMY1-type EvgS (pBADevgSL152F) were transformed into MG1655 evgS ydeP– lacZ and assayed in KCl-supplemented M9 medium (pH 5.5). As shown in Table 16.3.4, EvgS activation was observed only by complementation with pBADevgS, confirming that L152F led to the desensitization of EvgS in strain KMY1. Furthermore, EvgS L152 in pBADevgS was changed to Ala, Arg, Glu, Ile, and Tyr and analyzed in MG1655 evgS ydeP–lacZ. As a result, EvgS L152E and L152I showed strong activation by KClsupplemented M9 medium (pH 5.5), L152A and L152R showed moderate activation, but L152Y showed no activation. Because the L152F and L152Y mutations caused desensitization of EvgS, the aromatic ring inserted at the L152 position may have hindered the conformational change evoked by the signals; alternatively, other factors such as changes in protein folding may have been responsible. It should be noted that EvgS L152 is quite highly conserved among E. coli and Shigella strains. To the best of our knowledge, the EvgS L152F mutation is found in only two E. coli strains, MC4100 and DH1. 


Table 16.3.4 L152 in the periplasmic region is involved in EvgS activation.

From three individual experiments.</td></tr></table>

To examine whether the periplasmic sensor domain $( \mathrm { E v g S } _ { 3 0 - 5 3 0 } )$ was necessary for signal recognition, pBADevgSΔSD, expressing EvgS without the periplasmic sensor domain, was transformed into MG1655 evgS ydeP–lacZ and assayed. As a result, no activation was observed in KClsupplemented M9 medium (pH 5.5) (Table 16.3.5), showing that the periplasmic region of EvgS was necessary for activation by the signals. Similar results showing that the periplasmic region of EvgS has a role in EvgS activation have been shown by Johnson et al. (2014). 

## 16.3.3.4 The linker region is also required for acid recognition

We further examined whether the periplasmic region of EvgS was sufficient for signal perception. The EvgS sensor domain was fused to the cytoplasmic region of PhoQ to examine whether it responded to the signals. Two chimeric sensors, shown in Figure 16.3.2, were constructed. PvgS-A has residues 1–558 of EvgS fused to the cytoplasmic region of PhoQ comprising the HAMP, HisKA, and HATPase c domains (residues 215–486), and PvgS-B has residues 1–710 of EvgS fused to the HisKA and HATPase c domains of PhoQ (residues 267–486). The difference between the two chimeras is that PvgS-B has the PAS domain-like linker region of EvgS, and PvgS-A has the HAMP domain of PhoQ instead of the EvgS linker. The activity of these chimera sensors was measured with a reporter strain of the PhoQ–PhoP system because the HisKA and HATPase c domains were derived from PhoQ. Plasmids expressing these chimeras were transformed into the MC4100 phoQ $\mathrm { P } _ { m g t A - l a c Z }$ strain (MG1607) for the assay. This reporter strain was derived from MC4100 and has a desensitized EvgS L152F.

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/f4af86095a9e35258e7f891b2ba6ab94af95f7581b2b058668b711592023c178.jpg)



Figure 16.3.2 Construction of the chimeric sensors of EvgS and PhoQ. Diagram of EvgSΔSD, PvgS-A, and PvgS-B. Residues 30–530 were deleted in EvgSΔSD, residues 1–558 of EvgS were fused to residues 215–486 of PhoQ in chimera PvgS-A, and residues 1–710 of EvgS were fused to residues 267–486 of PhoQ in the chimera PvgS-B.


These transformants were grown in the presence of arabinose to induce the chimeras and were assayed (Eguchi and Utsumi, 2014). PvgS-B was activated in the presence of both a high concentration of KCl and acidic pH, but the wild-type PhoQ was not activated. This result indicated that the activated conformation of the EvgS periplasmic and linker domains caused by the signals was transduced to the PhoQ HisKA and HATPase c domains for their activation. As for PvgS-A, this chimeric sensor did not respond to acidic pH. However, a weak but significantly higher activity was observed when KCl was added to the medium at both pH 7.5 and 5.5. The conformational change of the periplasmic and/or transmembrane regions of EvgS was transduced to the PhoQ HisKA and HATPase c domains via the PhoQ HAMP domain independent of the acidic signal. These results suggest that K+ was perceived at the periplasmic region of EvgS, whereas the cytoplasmic linker domain of EvgS was required in addition to the periplasmic region for low-pH perception. 

Because fluctuations in pH are among the most frequently encountered stresses to which bacteria are exposed, various systems that sense the acid stress and transduce the information inside the cell have been reported. In E. coli, CadC and PhoQ are among the sensors that are known to respond to mildly acidic pH. CadC is a membrane-integrated transcriptional regulator that combines sensory, signal transduction, and DNA-binding activities within a single polypeptide. Activation of this system induces the cadAB operon, which encodes the lysine decarboxylase and lysine–cadaverine transporter of AR system 4 (Meng and Bennett, 1992; Watson et al., 1992). CadC is activated at pH <6.6 in the presence of lysine. Lysine is not directly sensed by CadC, but low pH is sensed by the periplasmic domain of CadC (Tetsch et al., 2011). PhoQ is a histidine kinase sensor of the PhoQ–PhoP TCS (see also Chapter 16.2); it detects signals such as divalent cations, antimicrobial peptides, and low pH. These signals are also sensed at the periplasmic domain, which has been shown by reconstitution of Salmonella PhoQ in vesicles with the signals inside (Prost et al., 2007). 

In the present study, analysis of the EvgS–PhoQ chimeras showed that the activated conformation of the EvgS periplasmic and linker domains can be transduced to the PhoQ histidine kinase domain. Changes in [K+] were sensed at the periplasmic domain, but both the periplasmic and linker domains were necessary to transduce the low-pH signal to the histidine kinase domain. Low pH may be perceived at the periplasmic domains, as is the case for CadC and PhoQ, because deletion of the periplasmic domain of EvgS or changing it to the PhoQ periplasmic domain made the sensor nonresponsive to low pH. In this case, the linker domain may serve as the transmitter of the periplasmic conformational change caused by low pH. However, because activation by [K+] can be transduced to the PhoQ HAMP domain and that by low pH cannot, we prefer to assume that the EvgS linker itself is related to low pH sensing. 

How can the cytoplasmic linker domain sense low pH? The cytoplasmic pH (pHi) in E. coli is maintained at around 7.5 over a range of extracellular pH values from 5.0 to 9.0 (Slonczewski, 1981). When HCl was added to change the external pH from 

7.5 to 5.5, the cytoplasmic pH fell within 10–20 s to pH 5.6– 6.5 but recovered rapidly to around pH 7 within 30 s after HCl addition, followed by slower recovery over the next 5 min to a pH close to 7.5 (Wilks and Slonczewski, 2007). HCl was used to adjust M9 medium to pH 5.5 in this study, so the pHi was assumed to be in a neutral pH range. Adding weak acids to the medium disrupts the strong pHi homeostasis. When acetic acid was used to adjust the M9 medium to pH 6.0 (cells did not grow at pH 5.5), no EvgS activation was observed by the addition of KCl (data not shown). Considering that the pHi of these cells was nearly pH 6.0, and the pHi of cells in HCl-supplemented M9 medium (pH 6.0) was in the neutral range, EvgS may have been activated by the pH gradient at the inner membrane. This may be a novel mechanism of pH perception. The mechanisms responsible for bacteria detecting pH changes are only beginning to be understood. We believe that our findings will contribute to the understanding of pH sensing.

# Slr1909, a novel two-component response regulator involved in acid tolerance in Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803

Lei Chen, Qiang Ren, Jiangxin Wang, and Weiwen Zhang 

Laboratory of Synthetic Microbiology, School of Chemical Engineering and Technology, Tianjin University, Tianjin, PR China 

## 16.4.1 Introduction

Photosynthetic cyanobacteria, as important primary producers and significant contributors of fixed carbon budgets, have been found to be present in a wide range of ecological habits on Earth (Giordano et al., 2005; Partensky et al., 1999). Besides their important roles in global carbon fixation, cyanobacteria have been recently used as a “microbial cell factory” to produce renewable biofuels and fine chemicals due to their capability to utilize solar energy and CO as their sole energy and carbon sources (Wang et al., 2013). However, it remains challenging to design and construct a robust cyanobacterial chassis that possesses beneficial properties such as resistance to various environmental stresses, including temperature, light intensity, salinity, limited water supply, and so on (Jin et al., 2014; Tahara et al., 1817). Among these common stresses in natural environments, pH change, especially decreased pH from acid rain or ocean acidification due to recent increased global industrialization, has been recognized as one major environmental stress for cyanobacteria (Ren et al., 2014; Shi et al., 2012; Tahara et al., 2012; Wen et al., 2011). 

It has been reported that cyanobacterial cells usually exhibit optimal growth and photosynthesis at pH values ranging from 7.5 to 11, and they are rarely found in habitats with pH values below 5 (Tahara et al., 2012). Some studies have already been conducted to reveal the resistance mechanism of cyanobacteria to acid stress. In the model cyanobacterium Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803 (hereafter, Synechocystis), by screening deletion mutants of adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-binding cassette (ABC) transporter genes with unknown substrates for acid stress sensitivity in low-pH medium, Tahara et al. (2012) identified slr1045, which encodes an ABC transporter, as an essential gene for survival under acid stress. Further studies showed that the phosphatidylglycerol (PG) content in the Δslr1045 mutant was much lower than the wild type, and that PG addition to the medium compensated for the growth deficiency of the Δslr1045 mutant, suggesting a role of Slr1045 in the stabilization of cell membranes under acidic conditions. In another study, genes expressed in response to low-pH stress were identified in Synechocystis using DNA microarrays, and 32 genes were found to be upregulated by more than threefold, while 29 genes were found to be downregulated by at least threefold after the acid treatment. Among these upregulated genes, expressions of two genes, slr0967 and sll0939, constantly increased throughout 4 h acid stress conditions, indicating the important roles of these two genes in the acid acclimation process (Ohta et al., 2005). Further studies showed that deletion of the response regulator encoding gene, sphR, affected the growth rate under acid stress conditions, and the downregulation of the acid-responsive genes slr0967 and sll0939 occurred upon the deletion of sphR, suggesting that induction of Slr0967 and Sll0939 by SphR may be essential for growth under acid stress conditions (Uchiyama et al., 2012). Moreover, overexpression of slr0967 and sll0939 induces the cells to become tolerant to acid stress (Uchiyama et al., 2014). It has also been shown that the expression of sll0939 was significantly repressed in the slr0967 deletion mutant, suggesting that sll0939 is directly involved in the low-pH tolerance of Synechocystis and that slr0967 may be essential for the induction of acid stress–responsive genes (Uchiyama et al., 2014). 

Two-component signal transduction systems (TCSTSs) are considered as one of the predominant signaling mechanisms for global regulation of the cellular response to various environmental changes (Hoch, 2000; see Section 3). It has been reported that Synechocystis contains a large number of TCSTS coding genes, including at least 27 histidine kinases, 20 hybrid-type histidine kinases, and 44 response regulators that are involved in regulating gene expression under a wide range of different stress conditions (Ashby and Houmard, 2006; Liu et al., 2015; Los et al., 2010; Zhang and Shi, 2005). As compared to other environmental stress conditions (Liu et al., 2015; Los et al., 2010), currently very little is known concerning the roles of TCSTSs in regulating tolerance to acid stress. Uchiyama et al. (2012) recently identified a response regulator sphR gene (slr0081) involved in acid tolerance by screening deletion mutants of selected response regulator genes in a low-pH medium. However, currently the knowledge about the acid response network and its regulation in cyanobacteria is still very limited. In this study, to further explore the regulatory network related to acid stress in cyanobacteria, a library of gene knockout mutants for all 44 putative response regulator–encoding genes in Synechocystis was conducted and screened under acid stress. An orphan TCSTS response regulator, Slr1909, was identified to be involved in acid tolerance in Synechocystis. Employing a combination of a quantitative iTRAQ-LC-MS/MS (isobaric tag for relative and absolute quantitation, and liquid chromatography– tandem mass spectrometry) proteomic approach coupled with gas chromatography–mass spectrometry (GC-MS)-based metabolomics, the possible regulatory network mediated by regulator Slr1909 in Synechocystis was further determined. 

## 16.4.2 Materials and methods

This is a summary of the materials and methods. For full details, please see the original publication (Ren et al., 2014). 

Bacterial growth conditions: Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803 and the knockout mutants constructed in this study were grown in BG11 medium (pH 7.5 or low pH, as stated in the text) under a light intensity of approximately 50 μmol photons $\mathrm { m } ^ { - 2 } \mathsf { s } ^ { - 1 }$ in an illuminating incubator of 130 rpm at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Tian et al., 2013). Cell density was measured using a UV-1750 spectrophotometer at $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 3 0 }$ . 

Construction of knockout mutants: A fusion polymerase chain reaction (PCR)-based method was employed for the construction of gene knockout fragments (Wang et al., 2002). The linear fused PCR amplicon, containing the chloramphenicol resistance cassette with two flanking arms of DNA upstream and downstream of the target gene, was used directly for natural transformation into Synechocystis, and the chloramphenicol-resistant transformants were obtained and confirmed by PCR and sequencing analysis. 

iTRAQ-LC-MS/MS proteomics analysis: (i) Protein preparation and digestion: For each sample, 10 mg of cells was washed with phosphate buffer (pH 7.2) and broken by the liquid nitrogen grounding method. The supernatant was mixed well with ice-cold acetone. For digestion, the protein pellet from the previous step was resuspended in digestion buffer (100 mM triethylammonium bicarbonate (TEAB), 0.05% w/v sodium dodecyl sulfate (SDS)) to a final concentration of 1 mg/mL. Equal aliquots (500 μg) from each lysate were then digested with trypsin. (ii) iTRAQ labeling: The iTRAQ labeling of peptide samples derived from the wild-type control and the gene knockout mutant samples was performed using an iTRAQ Reagent 8-plex kit (Applied Biosystems, USA) according to the manufacturer’s protocol. Four samples (two biological replicates for the wild-type control and two biological replicates for the mutant) were iTRAQ labeled. The peptides were labeled with respective isobaric tags, incubated for 2 h, and vacuum centrifuged to dryness. The labeled control and the mutant replicate samples were pooled and fractionated using a PolySULFOETHYL ATM SCX column (200 × 4.6 mm, 5 μm particle size, 200 A pore size) using ˚ a high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) system (Shimadzu, Japan) at a flow rate of 1.0 ml $\mathrm { m i n ^ { - 1 } }$ . (iii) LC-MS/MS proteomic analysis: The mass spectroscopy analysis was performed using an AB SCIEX TripleTOFTM 5600 mass spectrometer (AB SCIEX, USA), coupled with an online microflow HPLC system (Shimadzu, Japan), as described in Tian et al. (2013). The MS data were processed using Proteome Discoverer software, version 1.2.0.208 (Thermo Scientific, USA), to generate a peak list. The default parameters of Proteome Discoverer were used. The data acquisition was performed with Analyst QS 2.0 software (Applied Biosystems/MDS SCIEX). Protein identification and quantification were performed using Mascot 2.3.02 (Matrix Science, UK) (Tian et al., 2013). Genome sequence and annotation information of Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803 were downloaded from NCBI and CyanoBase (Kaneko et al., 2003). Proteins with 1.5-fold or more change between the mutant and the control samples and p-values of statistical evaluation less than 0.05 were determined as differentially expressed proteins. 

GC-MS-based metabolomics analysis: For metabolomics analysis, cells of the wild type and the Δslr1909 mutant were collected from normal and acid-stressed cultures at 24, 48, and 72 h. The same amount of cells for each sample was collected, immediately frozen in liquid nitrogen, and stored at $- 8 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ before use. The metabolomics analysis protocol included: (i) Metabolome extraction: The cells were resuspended in 1.0 mL cold 10:3:1 (v/v/v) methanol–chloroform– $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O }$ solution (MCW), frozen in liquid nitrogen, and thawed five times. 100 μL of supernatant was then added to an internal standard (IS) solution (100 μg/mL U-13C-sorbitol, 10 μL) before it was dried by vacuum centrifugation to normalize variation across samples. (ii) Sample derivatization: Derivatization was conducted according to the two-stage technique described before (Stein, 1999). (iii) GC-MS analysis: Analysis was performed on an Agilent 7890A Gas Chromatograph (GC) coupled to an Agilent MSD 5975 system equipped with a HP-5MS capillary column (30 m × 250 mm id). 2 μL derivatized sample was injected in a splitless mode at $2 3 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ injector temperature. The GC was operated at constant flow of 1.0 mL/min helium. The range of mass scan was m/z 38–650. (iv) Data processing and statistical analysis: The mass fragmentation spectrum was analyzed using the Automated Mass 

Spectral Deconvolution & Identification System (AMDIS) (Roessner et al., 2001) to identify the compounds by matching the data with the Fiehn Library (Fiehn, 2002) and the mass spectral library of the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). Peak areas of all identified metabolites were normalized against the IS, and the acquired relative abundances for each identified metabolite were used for future data analysis. All metabolomics profile data were first normalized by the internal control and the cell numbers of the samples, and then subjected to principal component analysis using the software SIMCA-P 11.5 (Laiakis et al., 2010). 

## 16.4.3 Results and discussion

## 16.4.3.1 Identification of Slr1909 involved in acid tolerance in Synechocystis

A library of deletion mutants for all 44 response regulator– encoding genes in Synechocystis was constructed (Chen et al., 2014). For the identification of acid-sensitive mutants, the growth phenotypes of these mutants and the wild-type strain were examined under acid conditions ranging from 6.0 to 6.5. The comparative analysis allowed the identification of Δslr1909, which was more sensitive to acid stress (Figure 16.4.1). In the normal BG11 medium of pH 7.5, the Δslr1909 mutant grew as well as the wild-type control, indicating that the knockout of the slr1909 gene didn’t affect cell growth under the normal pH condition. While in the medium of low pH (pH 6.0–6.5), although growth of both the wild-type strain and the Δslr1909 mutant was reduced, growth of the Δslr1909 mutant was reduced more significantly, suggesting the slr1909 gene was involved in acid tolerance in Synechocystis. In addition, the results demonstrated that the acid sensitivity of the Δslr1909 mutant was pH dependent, as an increased growth reduction was observed in the medium of pH 6.1 compared to the pH 6.5 medium (Figure 16.4.1a–c). The sensitivity of the Δslr1909 mutant was also confirmed in BG11 solid plates (Figure 16.4.1d). 

Sequence analysis showed that the slr1909 gene encoded an orphan response regulator (see Section 3). Protein domain analysis indicated that the Slr1909 protein contains a REC CheY-homologous receiver domain at its N-terminus (E-value, $4 . 0 2 \mathrm { e } ^ { - 4 2 } )$ and a HTH LUXR domain close to the C-terminus that could be involved in a wide variety of activities in various biological processes (E-value, $1 . 1 2 \mathrm { e } ^ { - 2 4 } )$ . The slr1909 gene was also annotated as a NarL subfamily protein Rre9 response regulator in the NCBI database (Ren et al., 2014). 

## 16.4.3.2 Comparative iTRAQ-LC-MS/MS proteomics analysis

To determine the possible regulatory network mediated by Slr1909, a quantitative iTRAQ-LC-MS/MS proteomics approach was employed. For proteomic analysis, the wild-type control and the Δslr1909 mutant were grown in BG11 media of pH 7.5 (control) and pH 6.2 (acid condition), and cells were collected at the exponential phase (i.e., at 48 h) (Figure 16.4.1b). For each set of samples, two biological replicates were independently generated, and proteins were prepared and pooled into one sample before being subjected to the iTRAQ-LC-MS/MS proteomic analysis (Tian et al., 2013). The final qualified spectra were matched to 2043 proteins, representing approximately 57.25% of the 3569 predicted proteins in the Synechocystis genome (Kaneko et al., 2003).

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/cd425809eb6214e56b16cc00d2370291673e81eebf3450a122e5d5c0d42202f1.jpg)



Figure 16.4.1 Growth of the wild type and the mutant Δslr1909 in BG11 media under acid stress conditions: (a) liquid BG11 cultivation at pH 6.1; (b) liquid BG11 cultivation at pH 6.2; (c) liquid BG11 cultivation at pH 6.5; (d) solid BG11 plate at pH 6.2. Five microliters of cells at $\mathrm { { O D } } _ { 7 3 0 } = 0 . 1$ 1 and its serial dilutions were spotted onto BG-11 plates at pH 6.2 and cultured for 7 days. Ren et al. (2014). Reprinted with permission of Elsevier.


A proteomic approach has recently been employed to determine the acid stress responses in several bacteria (Martinez et al., 2010; Roxas and Li, 2009) and has been demonstrated as an effective tool in uncovering the global cellular response under acid stress. In cyanobacteria, a comparative proteomic analysis was previously performed in Synechocystis to identify proteins involved in responses to acid stress, and certain proteins known for their role in pH homeostasis including oxalate decarboxylase and carbonic anhydrase were identified (Kurian et al., 2006). In this study, to further decipher cellular responses to acid stress in Synechocystis, two quantitative proteomic comparisons – the wild type at pH 6.2 versus the wild type at pH 7.5, and the Δslr1909 mutant at pH 6.2 versus the Δslr1909 mutant at pH 7.5 – were first carried out to reveal proteins responsive to acid stress. Using fold changes of >1.5 and a statistical significance of <0.05 as cutoffs, it was determined that 131 and 195 proteins were differentially regulated in the two comparisons described here, among which 92 proteins were overlapped, indicating that a large portion of the responsive proteins were found in both the wild type and the mutant under acid stress. The upregulated proteins under acid stress with known functions are listed in Table 16.4.1. Among them, several proteins with putative roles during pH stress were identified in our study. For example, Slr1035 encoding a glutaredoxin-like protein, Sll1520 encoding a DNA repair protein RecN, and Slr1924 encoding a D-alanyl-D-alanine carboxypeptidase were found upregulated in the wild type or the Δslr1909 mutant under acidic condition (Table 16.4.1). They were previously found to be involved in stress adaptation (Sanchez-Riego ´ et al., 2013), geraniol tolerance (Shah et al., 2013), and ionic and hyperosmotic stress (see Section 11) (Pr´ıncipe et al., 2009), respectively. Moreover, it worth noting that seven proteins involved in transport functions were upregulated in both the wild type and the Δslr1909 mutant by acid stress, including four proteins (i.e., Slr0040, Slr0041, Slr0043, and Slr0044) of a low-CO -induced cmpABCD operon encoding a high-affinity bicarbonate transporter (Omata et al., 2001), suggesting that transporters are important for tolerance to acid stress in Synechocystis. 

On the other hand, consistent with the slower growth of the wild type and the Δslr1909 mutant under acid stress (Figure 16.4.1a–c), proteins involved in protein synthesis (e.g., ribosomal proteins and translation initiation factor IF2), central intermediary metabolism (i.e., Sll0220, Sll1079, Sll1432, and Slr1167), DNA metabolism (i.e., Sll0569, Sll0766, Sll1045, and 

Slr0083), and energy metabolism (i.e., Sll1220, Slr0897, Slr1367, Slr1648, Slr1739, Slr1743, and Slr2033) were found downregulated by acid stress (Ren et al., 2014). 

Since Slr1909 was involved in acid stress (Figure 16.4.1), to identify the proteins responsible for the increased acid sensitivity upon the knockout of slr1909 and the corresponding acid response network mediated by Slr1909, a third comparative proteomic analysis, the Δslr1909 mutant at pH 6.2 versus the wild type at pH 6.2, was established. The results showed that 10 and 24 proteins were down- and upregulated, respectively (Table 16.4.2). Among the downregulated proteins, Slr1452 of a sulfate transport system substrate-binding protein was downregulated 1.55-fold upon the knockout of slr1909. In a previous transcriptomic analysis of Shewanella oneidensis under alkaline pH 10, upregulation of sulfate transport and sulfur metabolism genes was also observed (Leaphart et al., 2006). It is interesting to note that three proteins, Slr1259, Slr1260, and Slr1261, of which the corresponding genes seem to be located in an operon, were all downregulated upon the knockout of slr1909 and are worth further investigation of their roles in the regulatory network mediated by Slr1909. Among the upregulated proteins in the knockout mutant of slr1909, Slr1512 and Slr1513 were previously found involved in inorganic carbon uptake under inorganic carbon limitation condition (Wang et al., 2004), and Slr0897 of a beta-1,4-glucanase-like protein (SsGlc) was found to be involved in salt stress (Tamoi et al., 2007). Four proteins involved in signal transduction – Sll1330, Slr1037, Sll1334, and Sll1689 – were upregulated upon the knockout of the slr1909 gene under acidic condition (Table 16.4.2), and their roles in acid response in the Δslr1909 mutant may be worth further investigation. The study provided a proteomic description of the acid response network mediated by the orphan regulator Slr1909 in Synechocystis. 

## 16.4.3.3 Comparative metabolomic analysis of acid response network mediated by Slr1909

Recent studies have demonstrated that metabolomics could be a powerful complement to other “omics” approaches to fully decipher the metabolic networks (Yoshikawa et al., 2013). In this study, to further elucidate the metabolic responses to acid stress regulated by Slr1909, comparative metabolomic analysis was performed following the optimized protocol established by Krall et al. (2009). Triplicate cells of the wild-type control and the Δslr1909 mutant grown in BG11 media of pH 7.5 (control) and pH 6.2 (acid condition) at three time points (i.e., 24, 48, and 72 h) were collected, and the metabolites were isolated and analyzed by GC-MS. Under optimized analytical conditions, the metabolomic analysis allowed the detection of a total of 52 metabolites from Synechocystis, including various amino acids, sugars, and organic acids, detected in almost all replicate samples (Ren et al., 2014). 

The time-series metabolomic data were used to generate principal component analysis (PCA) score plots in two corresponding comparative analyses: (i) the wild type at pH 6.2 versus the wild type at pH 7.5, and (ii) the Δslr1909 mutant at pH 6.2 versus the Δslr1909 mutant at pH 7.5 (Figure 16.4.2). As revealed by the score plots, obvious growth time effects of both the wild type and the Δslr1909 mutant under different pH levels (i.e., 6.2 vs. 7.5) were observed, since a clear separation and clustering for samples of each time point were found. At the time point of 48 h, the difference between pH 6.2 and pH 7.5 was greater for the Δslr1909 mutant when compared with the wild type (Figure 16.4.2), suggesting greater metabolic changes in the Δslr1909 mutant occurring at this time point, which is consistent with the growth time course in Figure 16.4.1.

To further determine variation of individual metabolites between the Δslr1909 mutant and the wild type under acid stress, a loading plot was generated using metabolomic profiles of 48 h since a greater difference was observed between the mutant and wild type at that time point (Figure 16.4.3). 

The result showed that more than a dozen potential biomarker metabolites were important for the discrimination of the Δslr1909 mutant and the wild type under acid stress, including D-mannose (which was the most significantly changed alloinositol), D-allose, methyl stearate, methyl palmitate, talose, pyruvic acid, D-(+) galactose, succinic acid, D-ribose-5- phosphate, D-glucose-6-phosphate, 4-guanidinobutyric acid, adenosine, phosphoric acid, palmitic acid, linoleic acid, and stearic acid. Among these metabolites, several monosaccharide and fatty acids previously known to be involved in other environmental stresses in various microbes were included, such as mannose, galactose, palmitic acid, linoleic acid, and stearic acid (Empadinhas and da Costa, 2011; Kumar et al., 2012; Moreno et al., 2000; Ruenwai et al., 2011). The study provided new insight into the metabolomic network regulated directly or indirectly by the Slr1909 response regulator involved in the low-pH response.

As a common environmental stress condition, acid can cause significant stress to cyanobacteria; it is thus important to obtain a better understanding of cellular responses to acid stress and the related regulatory mechanism. The study provided the identification, proteomic, and metabolomic characterization of a novel response regulator, Slr1909, involved in low-pH response in Synechocystis. In addition, the possible regulatory mechanism that Slr1909 may mediate was also investigated.
