# Comparative mass spectrometry–based proteomics to elucidate the acid stress response in Lactobacillus plantarum

Tiaan Heunis∗, Shelly Deane, and Leon M.T. Dicks 

Department of Microbiology, Stellenbosch University, Stellenbosch, South Africa 

∗Current address: DST/NRF Centre of Excellence for Biomedical Tuberculosis Research, SAMRC Centre for Tuberculosis Research, Division of Molecular Biology and Human Genetics, Faculty of Medicine and Health Sciences, Stellenbosch University, Cape Town, South Africa 

## 16.5.1 Introduction

Lactobacillus plantarum, amongst other lactic acid bacteria, is generally regarded as safe, with certain strains routinely marketed as probiotics (live organisms that, when administered in adequate amounts, confer a health benefit on the host) (Ducrotte´ et al., 2012; Sanders, 2008). Probiotics encounter various stresses during transit through the gastrointestinal tract (GIT), which include osmotic and oxidative stress (see Sections 10 and 11), acidic conditions, bile salts, and hydrolytic enzymes (Bron et al., 2011). Probiotic organisms have to survive these harsh conditions to exert a beneficial effect on the host. However, little is known about the effect of acid stress on global protein expression in L. plantarum, and only a few studies have attempted to address this (Hamon et al., 2014; Heunis et al., 2014). 

Mechanisms involved in acid resistance and tolerance are the focus of intense research to understand survival strategies and to identify biomarkers for acid resistance in lactobacilli. Unbiased, global approaches are thus essential to unravel the complex mechanisms involved in acid tolerance and survival (see also Chapters 16.1–16.4). 

Next-generation sequencing technologies have greatly facilitated whole-genome sequencing and produced a plethora of sequences that are now publicly available, including that of L. plantarum (Siezen et al., 2012). This enables prediction of all possible gene products and thus facilitates systems-level investigation of perturbations in an organism of interest. Advances in proteomics, especially mass spectrometry (MS)-based proteomics, have catapulted the technology from merely cataloguing a small number of proteins to the detection and quantification of thousands of proteins within a few hours (Mann et al., 2013). Furthermore, global analysis of posttranslational modifications, protein localization and turnover, and identification of protein–protein interactions and protein complexes is also now possible with current proteomics methods (Altelaar et al., 2013; Mallick and Kuster, 2011). This chapter highlights the first steps to elucidate the acid stress response of L. plantarum on a systems level by using comparative proteomics. 

## 16.5.2 Materials and methods

Bacterial culture and growth conditions: L. plantarum 423 (van Reenen et al., 1998) was grown in MRS (de Man, Rogosa, and Sharpe) broth or cultured on MRS agar, as described by Heunis et al. (2014). Three colonies were inoculated, separately, into 5 mL MRS broth and incubated at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 24 h. Pre-cultures were inoculated (1%, v/v) into 200 mL MRS broth and incubated for 12 h to reach the onset of the stationary phase $\left( \mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 \mathrm { n m } } \approx 8 . 0 \right)$ . The cells were harvested (8000 g, 15 min, $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ and resuspended in 10 mL prewarmed MRS broth. Five millilitres of the cell suspension were subsequently inoculated into either 100 mL MRS broth (pH 6.4), or 100 mL MRS broth adjusted to pH 2.5 with HCl. All media were pre-warmed to $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ before inoculation. The cultures were incubated at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 1 h, harvested (8000 g, 15 min, $4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ) _ { : }$ , and washed twice with sterile saline (0.85% w/v NaCl, prepared with double-distilled water). Cell pellets were stored $\mathrm { a t } - 8 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ until further use. 

Sample preparation and protein extraction: Cell pellets were suspended in lysis buffer (7 M urea, 2 M thiourea, 50 mM DTT, and 4% w/v CHAPS) containing a bacterial protease inhibitor cocktail (Sigma-Aldrich, USA). Cells were disrupted with glass beads (0.5 mm) for 7 min using a vortex on maximum speed, kept on ice for 1 min, and vortexed for another 7 min. The lysate was centrifuged (12,000 g, 15 min, $2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ) _ { : }$ , then the supernatant was collected and centrifuged again for 15 min (12,000 $\mathrm { g } , 2 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ to remove cellular debris. The protein concentration in the cell-free lysate was determined using a 2D Quant kit (GE Healthcare Life Sciences, USA), according to the instructions provided by the manufacturer. 

Filter-aided sample preparation (FASP): Five microliters of protein (10 μg) were added to 45 μL SDT lysis buffer (4% w/v SDS, 100 mM DTT, and 100 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.6, added just before use). The sample was mixed with 150 μL of UA buffer (8 M urea in 100 mM Tris-HCl, pH 8.5) and filtered using a Amicon ultra-centrifugal filter (MWCO 30 kDa; Millipore, USA) at $1 4 { , } 0 0 0 \ \mathrm { g }$ for 15 min. An additional 200 μL of UA was added to the filter and centrifuged again. The proteins were alkylated by addition of 100 μL of a 50 mM iodoacetamide solution in UA buffer and kept in the dark for 20 min. Samples were centrifuged (14,000 g, 10 min) to remove iodoacetamide and washed twice with 100 μL UB buffer (8 M urea and 100 mM Tris-HCl, pH 8.0). Samples were subsequently washed twice with 100 μL 50 mM ammonium bicarbonate (14,000 g, 10 min). Proteins were digested with 40 μL trypsin (100 ng/μL) for 17 h at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in a wet chamber. The filter was placed in a clean microcentrifuge tube the following day and centrifuged (14,000 g, 10 min), followed by the addition of 40 μL 0.5 M NaCl and further centrifugation. The solution was acidified by adding 4 μL formic acid. The filtrate was desalted using C18 StageTips, dried under vacuum, and stored at $- 2 0 { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . Peptides were dissolved in 5% (v/v) acetonitrile containing 0.1% (v/v) formic acid, and 10 μL injections were made for nano–liquid chromatography (LC). 

Mass spectrometry: Methods and data analyses were as described by Heunis et al. (2014). Briefly, all experiments were performed on a Thermo Scientific EASY-nLC II connected to a LTQ Orbitrap Velos mass spectrometer (Thermo Scientific), equipped with a nano-electrospray source. Separation was performed on an EASY-Column (2 cm, ID 100 μm, 5 μm, C18) pre-column, followed by separation on a XBridge BEH130 NanoEase column (15 cm, ID 75 μm, 3.5 μm, C18) with a flow rate of 300 nL/min. Solvent A was 100% water in 0.1% formic acid, and solvent B was 100% acetonitrile in 0.1% formic acid. The following gradients were used: 5 to 8% B in 5 min, 8 to 40% B in 270 min, 40 to 60% B in 10 min, 60 to 80% B in 5 min, and 80% B for 10 min. The mass spectrometer was operated in data-dependent mode to automatically switch between Orbitrap-MS and LTQ–tandem MS (MS/MS) acquisition. Data were acquired using the Xcalibur software package. The precursor ion scan MS spectra (m/z 400−2000) were acquired in the Orbitrap with resolution $\mathrm { R } = 6 0 { , } 0 0 0$ and $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 6 }$ accumulated ions. The 20 most intense ions were isolated and fragmented in the linear ion trap (number of accumulated ions, $1 . 5 { \times } 1 0 ^ { 4 } )$ using collision-induced dissociation (CID). The lock mass option enabled accurate mass measurement in both the MS and MS/MS modes (polydimethylcyclosiloxane, $\mathrm { ~ \textit ~ { ~ m ~ } ~ } { } z =$ 445.120025). In data-dependent LC−MS/MS experiments, a CID energy of 35% and a 60 s dynamic exclusion duration were used. Mass spectrometry conditions were 1.8 kV, and a capillary temperature o $\cdot 2 5 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ with no sheath and auxiliary gas flow. The ion selection threshold used in MS/MS was 500 counts at an activation Q-value of 0.25 in 10 ms. 

Data analysis: An automated database search of tandem mass spectra was performed, using MaxQuant 1.2.2.5 (Cox and Mann, 2008), to identify proteins using the L. plantarum JDM1 database (CP001617, downloaded on 5 December 2012 with 2948 entries). Carbamidomethyl cysteine was set as fixed modification. Oxidized methionine, N-acetylation, deamidation (NQ), Pyro-Gln, and Pyro-Glu were set as variable modifications. The precursor mass tolerance was set to 20 ppm, and fragment mass tolerance to 0.8 Da. Two missed tryptic cleavages were allowed. Proteins were considered positively identified when they were identified with at least one unique tryptic peptide per protein and had a protein and peptide false discovery rate (FDR) of less than 0.01. A label-free approach was followed during data analysis, and the LFQ values assigned were used for statistical analysis using Perseus, as part of MaxQuant. Data were transformed (log 2) and imputated. Two-sample t-tests were performed using a p-value with a threshold of 0.05. Differentially abundant proteins were only considered if they were detected in all three replicates $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ ), had a fold regulation of at least two times, and had a coefficient of variation (%CV) lower than 5%. A minimum of two unique peptides per protein (with an average of seven peptides per protein) was used for quantification. Stringent criteria of $\mathrm { P E P } < 0 . 0 1$ and $\mathrm { F D R } < 0 . 0 1$ were implemented. The Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes (KEGG) (Kanehisa and Goto, 2000) was used to determine the position of the identified proteins in respective pathways. Gene ontology (GO) enrichment was performed using the Gene Ontology Enrichment Analysis Software Toolkit (GOEAST) (Zheng and Wang, 2008), and overrepresented GO terms were analysed with REVIGO (Supek et al., 2011). Cytoscape (Shannon et al., 2003) was used for network visualizations. 

## 16.5.3 Results and discussion

In total, 6734 peptides, corresponding to 992 proteins (926 proteins with more than two unique peptides), were identified. Linear correlation (Figure 16.5.1) and clustering of the replicates indicate low biological variance as well as robustness of the workflow used. 

A total of 294 proteins were identified as differentially abundant $( \geq 2 . 0 \mathrm { - f o l d } , p < 0 . 0 5 , \mathrm { F D R } < 0 . 0 1 )$ , with 97 proteins identified as more abundant. Twelve proteins were solely detected when cells were exposed to pH 2.5 (Heunis et al., 2014). In total, 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/5d1967c4bfde43dd6a27f96cef02364947f6c44cca969322d7cd2c6d0a3e57b3.jpg)



Figure 16.5.1 Multi-scatterplot of L. plantarum 423 replicates with Pearson correlation coefficient.


149 proteins were detected as less abundant in acid-stressed cells, whereas 36 proteins were solely detected in control L. plantarum 423 at pH 6.4. For the differentially abundant proteins, 255 of the 289 proteins had three or more peptides associated with the quantified protein (Figure 16.5.2). Sixty-three of these proteins had more than 10 unique peptides per protein (Figure 16.5.2). 

Differentially abundant proteins are involved in all major metabolic processes and may influence the physiological state of the cell, as determined by GO enrichment (Figure 16.5.3). 

The proteomic data suggest that acid-stressed cells of L. plantarum 423 are not growing as actively as nonstressed (control) cells, as would be expected under such hostile conditions (Figure 16.5.4). Reduced growth in acidic conditions was also reported for Staphylococcus aureus (Bore et al., 2007) and Lactobacillus casei (Broadbent et al., 2010). Lower abundance of proteins, or proteins solely detected in control L. plantarum 423, included those that play a role in extracellular polysaccharide biosynthesis (Pgm, GalU, and RfbA), cell envelope biogenesis (TagH), lipid metabolism (FabZ1, FabZ2, FabH2, FabG1, FabD, AccA2, AccD2, AccB2, and AcpA2), peptidoglycan biosynthesis and rearrangement (Ddl, MurE1, GlmU, MurA2, JDM1 0269, JDM1 0268, JDM1 2724, and JDM1 2414), cell shape determination (MreB1, MreB2, and RodA2), purine and pyrimidine biosynthesis (including, but not limited to, Prs1, PyrB, PyrG, PyrH, PurH, PurR, GuaA, Apt, Udk, and CarB), transcription (including, but not limited to, CspC, CspP, PurR, Rrp1, and RpoA-D), translation (including, but not limited to, PrfA, MetS, Tuf, GlyQ, LysS, RplW, PheT, RpsJ, PrfC, and ArgS), amino acid metabolism (Gdh and GlnA), and peptide and protein degradation (ClpC, ClpL, ClpB, ClpX, PepC1, PepN, PepD2, and PepF1). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/9133e34f9304695cecea03b03e07aaa99244978ee9e8c98bc1a63263b8a92ad2.jpg)



ected as more abundant in acid-stressed L. plant Figure 16.5.2 Illustrative networks showing unique peptides mapped to representative proteins indicated by gene name. Blue nodes indicate proteins detected as less abundant, and red nodes indicate


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/ee1aa60fe9006058fd4320121c5a375b7958d039180d30dc0fcedc728a820804.jpg)



Figure 16.5.3 Network of enriched GO terms in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423. Darker colours indicate GO terms that are highly enriched in the dataset.


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/02f950e7c9cc196991db947f9964d4c6d8cbeaf8ba82dfabb64bd0ca1e7bf936.jpg)



Figure 16.5.4 Summary of the major responses observed in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423. The illustration represents the plasma membrane and intracellular milieu of L. plantarum, as whole cell lysates were used to study stress responses. Responses in red and blue were associated with proteins detected as more abundant and less abundant in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423, respectively.


Proteins more abundant, or solely detected in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423, included those that play a role in ion transport (JDM1 0884, JDM1 2502, and CutC). Ion homeostasis has previously been linked to the acid stress response of Lactobacillus bulgaricus, Listeria monocytogenes, Streptococcus mutans, and Escherichia coli (Dashper and Reynolds, 1992; Iyer et al., 2002; Penaud et al., 2006; Stasiewicz et al., 2011). Very few proteins that play a role in purine and pyrimidine metabolism, as well as protein biosynthesis, were detected as more abundant in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423. However, it seems that the proteins detected as more abundant have a role in, amongst others, detoxification (Xtp1), possibly to supply the cells with fermentable sugars from the hydrolysis of purine and pyrimidine nucleosides (JDM1 0325) and ensure proper protein folding and stabilization (PpiB). Interestingly, a large number of proteins involved in lysine biosynthesis were detected as more abundant in acid-stressed cells of L. plantarum 423, including Asd2, DapA1, DapB, and DapD. The role of increased lysine biosynthesis in the acid response of L. plantarum is not yet clear and warrants further investigation. Since intermediates in lysine biosynthesis are used for peptidoglycan biosynthesis, the phenomenon could be a consequence of decreased peptidoglycan biosynthesis. Branched-chain amino acid (BCAA) biosynthesis has previously been linked to the acid tolerance of S. mutans (Len et al., 2004; Santiago et al., 2012). We detected a BCAA aminotransferase (BcaT) as more abundant in acid-stressed cells of L. plantarum 423. 

Proteins playing a role in the general stress response were also significantly more abundant in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423. These included DnaK, GrpE, GroEL, GroES (see Section 14), heat shock proteins (Hsp1 and 3; see Section 13), universal stress proteins (JDM1 1005, JDM1 1432, JDM1 2311, and JDM1 1469), and alkaline shock proteins (Asp1 and Asp2). Furthermore, a strong oxidative stress response was observed with increased abundance of GshR2, GshR4, Gpo, MsrA2, and Tpx. Oxidative stress response proteins have also been detected in acid-stressed Bacillus cereus and acid-stressed Lactococcus lactis (Budin-Verneuil et al., 2005; Frees et al., 2003; Mols et al., 2010) (see Section 10). Interestingly, we observed lower abundance of the gamma chain of the $\mathrm { F _ { 0 } F _ { 1 } }$ –adenosine triphosphate (ATP) synthase (AtpG). None of the subunits of this system was identified as acid resistance determinants in L. plantarum (Hamon et al., 2014). $\mathrm { F } _ { 0 } \mathrm { F } _ { 1 } { \cdot } \mathrm { A T P }$ synthases are believed to play an important role in the acid stress response of fermentative bacteria (Cotter and Hill, 2003). However, this system does not seem to play an important role in the acid stress response of L. plantarum. 

Significant changes in abundance were observed in the profile of proteins involved in carbohydrate utilization of acid-stressed L. plantarum 423. Proteins playing a role in sugar transport (Pts9C, Pts20A, and Pts20B), fructose and mannose utilization (GutB, MtlD, Pmi, SacK1, and FruK), starch and sucrose metabolism (PgmB2), glycolysis (Fba), pyruvate metabolism (EutD, Lox, AcdH, LdhL, PdhA, Adh1, HicD3, and AccB3), the pentose phosphate pathway (JDM1 1856), and amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism (NanE, NagA, and Gnp) were detected to be more abundant, or solely detected, in acidstressed L. plantarum 423. The hiCD3 gene is one of eight genes positively correlated with survival of L. plantarum in the GIT (van Bokhorst-van de Veen et al., 2012). On the other hand, proteins with lower abundance in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423, or solely detected in control cells, included those involved in sugar transport (Pts16ABC), starch and sucrose metabolism (Map3 and Map2), starch and glycogen metabolism (Amy2), the pentose phosphate pathway (Gnd2 and Gpd), pyruvate metabolism (PycA, Ack1, and Ack3), and glycolysis (Pbg4). The identified proteins indicate the utilization of various carbon sources even in a glucose-rich environment. This coincides with the lower abundance of the catabolite control protein A (CcpA), indicating that the acid-stressed cells are relieved from catabolite repression. Interestingly, NanE, NagA, and Gnp were detected as more abundant in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423. This indicates that acid-stressed L. plantarum 423 cells can utilize cell wall components, such as N-acetyl-D-mannosamine 6- phosphate and N-acetyl-D-glucosamine 6-phosphate, as alternative carbon sources during severe acid stress. Furthermore, the release of ammonia during N-acetyl-D-glucosamine utilization could possibly play a role in alleviating acid stress in L. plantarum 423. Altered pyruvate metabolism was observed in L. plantarum 423 upon exposure to acid stress. Lactate dehydrogenases (HicD3 and LdhL1) and a lactate oxidase (Lox) were also more abundant. This indicates that lactate, or at least a percentage thereof, may be converted back to pyruvate and $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ . This could explain in part the oxidative stress response observed. Acetate kinase 1 was less abundant in acid-stressed L. plantarum 423, and acetate kinase 3 was only detected in control cells. These enzymes are responsible for the reversible conversion of acetyl phosphate to acetate. This was rather unexpected, as ATP would have been generated by the dephosphorylation of acetyl phosphate to acetate via the acetate kinases. Furthermore, an acetaldehyde dehydrogenase (AcdH) and the alpha subunit of the E1 component of the pyruvate dehydrogenase (PdhA) were more abundant. The phosphotransacetylase, EutD, was also more abundant and is responsible for the conversion of acetyl-CoA to acetyl phosphate. This could possibly lead to an increase in the acetyl phosphate pool during acid stress in L. plantarum 423. It thus seems that acetyl phosphate is preferentially converted to ethanol via Adh1 or that it may serve as a phosphate donor under acidic conditions. Acetyl phosphate is a global signalling molecule in E. coli (Klein et al., 2007), and it phosphorylates intracellular glucose in certain streptococci (Keevil et al., 1984). However, this role of acetyl phosphate needs to be confirmed in L. plantarum. Taken together, these shifts seem to enable L. plantarum 423 to utilize diverse carbon sources and, combined with altered pyruvate metabolism, lead to production of more basic compounds that might help ease acid stress, increase pH, and enhance survival.

Heunis T, Deane S, Smit S, Dicks LMT. 2014. Proteomic profiling of the acid stress response in Lactobacillus plantarum 423. Journal of Proteome Research 13(9): 4028–4039. 



We observed some overlap between proteins detected in our study and those reported in a previous study on acid resistance markers in L. plantarum (Hamon et al., 2014), as shown in Figure 16.5.5. 



Iyer R, Iverson TM, Accardi A, Miller C. 2002. A biological role for prokaryotic ClC chloride channels. Nature 419(6908): 715–718. 



Even though only eight proteins were shared between the two studies, other proteins were detected in similar pathways for six of the nine proteins reported in a study conducted by Hamon et al. (2014), as shown in Table 16.5.1. This suggests some level of concordance between the responses of different L. plantarum strains when exposed to acidic environments. However, increased abundance of only one protein implicated in acid resistance of L. plantarum by Hamon et al. (2014), GrpE, was also detected in our study. The methionine synthase and the 30S ribosomal protein S2, also identified by Hamon and coworkers (2014) as putative determinants of acid resistance, did not show a significant difference in abundance in our study. 



Kanehisa M, Goto S. 2000. KEGG: Kyoto encyclopedia of genes and genomes. Nucleic Acids Research 28(1): 27–30. 



In conclusion, the expression of a number of proteins is altered during acid stress in L. plantarum to ensure survival. Mechanisms that seem to play a role in acid stress include increased ion homeostasis, general stress response, detoxification of reactive oxygen species (see Section 10) and nucleotide damage, BCAA biosynthesis, and altered carbohydrate metabolism (including catabolism of cell wall components) to produce basic compounds. As expected, reduced growth in acidic conditions is associated with downregulation of processes such as extracellular polysaccharide biosynthesis, cell envelope biogenesis, lipid metabolism, peptidoglycan biosynthesis and rearrangement, cell shape determination, purine and pyrimidine biosynthesis, transcription, translation, amino acid metabolism, and peptide and protein degradation. Furthermore, a variety of uncharacterized proteins were differentially influenced during acid stress. These proteins of unknown function could play an important role in the acid stress response of L. plantarum (Heunis et al., 2014) and warrant further investigation.

## Adaptation to nitrosative stress

# Transcriptional regulation by thiol-based sensors of oxidative and nitrosative stress

Timothy Tapscott1‡, Matthew A. Crawford2‡, and Andres V ´ azquez-Torres ´ 1–3 

1Molecular Biology Program, University of Colorado School of Medicine, Aurora, Colorado, USA 

2Department of Immunology and Microbiology, University of Colorado School of Medicine, Aurora, Colorado, USA 

3Veterans Affairs Eastern Colorado Health Care System, Denver, Colorado, USA 

‡Equal contribution 

## 17.1.1 Introduction

During the course of infection, pathogenic bacteria encounter a myriad of microenvironments and host defense mechanisms. Oxidative (see Section 10) and nitrosative stress is prominent among the antimicrobial pressures imposed by innate host defense (Nathan and Shiloh, 2000). Phagocytes are primary sources of oxidative and nitrosative stress, owing to their ability to generate superoxide $( \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ^ { \bullet - } )$ and nitric oxide (∙ NO) through the enzymatic activities of NADPH oxidase and inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS), respectively (Babior et al., 2002; Stuehr, 1999). Superoxide and nitric oxide are precursors of reactive oxygen species (ROS) and reactive nitrogen species (RNS), including, among others, hydrogen peroxide $\left( \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } \right)$ , hydroxyl radical (HO∙ ), nitrogen dioxide $( \cdot _ { \mathrm { N O } _ { 2 } } ) _ { : }$ , and peroxynitrite (ONOO−) (Fang, 2004; Nathan and Shiloh, 2000). ROS and RNS are strong electron acceptors with the capacity to react with diverse biomolecules, causing oxidative and nitrosative damage (Avery, 2011; Fang, 2004; Farr and Kogoma, 1991). The high levels of ROS and RNS associated with inflammation have the potential to overwhelm a bacterium’s ability to maintain redox balance, a situation that can disrupt central metabolism, DNA integrity, and protein synthesis, all of which are essential for life (Avery, 2011; Grisham et al., 1999). 

In order to minimize the deleterious effects caused by ROS and RNS, bacteria rely upon highly regulated stress response systems that coordinate changes in bacterial defense and metabolic programs (Fang, 2004; Henard and Vazquez-Torres, 2011). ´ These adaptive changes help protect the cell from endogenously produced and host-derived reactive species (Imlay, 2008). Broadly, bacterial antioxidant and antinitrosative strategies include avoidance of ROS and RNS (Vazquez-Torres´ et al., 2000), suppression of reactive species production (Crawford et al., 2006), direct removal through scavenging and enzymatic detoxification systems (McCord and Fridovich, 1968; Song et al., 2013), repair of oxidative and nitrosative damage suffered by macromolecules (Imlay and Linn, 1986), and changes in cellular metabolism that not only increase antioxidant buffering but also generate precursors required for biomolecule repair (Henard et al., 2010; Singh et al., 2007; see also Section 10). Redox-active transcriptional regulators are central to the ability of bacteria to rapidly sense oxidative and nitrosative stress and subsequently control the diverse cellular processes that defend against ROS- and RNS-mediated insults. These regulatory sensors typically contain a redox-active center (e.g., heme groups, iron–sulfur clusters, or cysteine thiols) that, upon oxidation or nitrosylation, triggers structural changes that transduce the redox signal into a regulatory output associated with the control of gene expression (Green and Paget, 2004; Vazquez-Torres, ´ 2012). Notably, the sensitivities, specificities, sensing mechanisms, and regulatory outputs vary widely among individual redox-active regulators, thereby allowing the cell to orchestrate uniquely tailored adaptive responses to a dynamic array of ROS and RNS. 

The goal of this chapter is to review the known effector mechanisms by which thiol-based sensors of oxidative and nitrosative stress mediate transcriptional regulation. Specifically, we will consider thiol-based regulators that work by binding to (i) promoter regions of target genes, (ii) alternative sigma factors (see Section 4), or (iii) the bacterial RNA polymerase (RNAP) (see Chapter 2.5). The regulatory breadth of each effector mechanism is also discussed, with special emphasis given to the novel value of transcriptional regulation at the level of RNAP and its potential to integrate oxidative, nitrosative, and nutritional stress responses into a coordinated regulatory output important in bacterial pathogenesis. 

## 17.1.2 Redox modification of thiol-based sensors

The amino acid cysteine is capable of imparting proteins with distinct features important in structural determination, metal coordination, enzymatic catalysis, and regulatory control (Collet and Bardwell, 2002; Giles et al., 2003). The ability of cysteine residues to influence these interdependent determinants of protein function relies predominantly on the redox state of the sulfhydryl group (R-SH). The sulfur atom of cysteine can exist in a number of different oxidation states, and thereby serves as a sensitive site for a spectrum of posttranslational redox modifications that convey particular biochemical properties (Antelmann and Helmann, 2011). The ability of thiol groups to undergo reversible oxidation and nitrosylation, which directly alters protein function, provides the foundation for thiol-based sensing of oxidative and nitrosative stress (Vazquez-Torres, 2012; see ´ Chapter 10). 

In general, cysteine thiols are weak acids with $\mathsf { p } K _ { a }$ values near 8.5 (Benesch et al., 1955); thus, the relatively unreactive protonated form is favored at physiological pH. In contrast, crucial cysteine residues of redox-active proteins are frequently stabilized in the unprotonated thiolate form (R-S−), a strong nucleophile that reacts readily with ROS and RNS (Netto et al., 2007). Ionization of the cysteine side chain and its stabilization in the absence of oxidative and nitrosative signals are promoted by specific elements of the protein microenvironment. Interactions of thiolates with basic amino acid residues (notably, lysine and arginine), metal cofactors, α-helix dipoles, and hydrogen bond networks are excellent examples by which the negative charge of thiolates is stabilized in a protein (Ferrer-Sueta et al., 2011; Netto et al., 2007). Interactions within a cationic protein microenvironment also stabilize the anionic charge of the redox transition state, considerably increasing the rate of reaction (Ferrer-Sueta et al., 2011; Hall et al., 2010). This notion intimates that, as the oxidation reaction proceeds, stabilizing interactions are capable of subtle rearrangement, first stabilizing the thiolate, then the transition state, and finally the leaving group (Ferrer-Sueta et al., 2011). 

The array of ROS and RNS encountered in biological systems is vast and accompanied by an enormous variety of exceptional chemistries (Paulsen and Carroll, 2013). Here, we will briefly describe biologically relevant redox modifications of bacterial thiol-based sensors engendered by $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } , \mathrm { \cdot N O } ,$ , and ONOO−. 

## 17.1.2.1 Hydrogen peroxide

Direct oxidation of redox-active cysteine thiols by $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ results in the formation of sulfenic acid (R-SOH) (Gupta and Carroll, 2014). While sulfenylation alone can trigger functional changes in thiol-based sensors (Kim et al., 2002; Lo Conte and Carroll, 2013), in most instances sulfenic acids are unstable and react with a neighboring thiol, resulting in the formation of an intraor intermolecular disulfide bond (R-SS-R). This condensation reaction can also produce mixed disulfides between the sulfenylated cysteine and low-molecular-weight thiols such as glutathione, mycothiol, and bacillithiol (Fahey, 2013). Additionally, in the absence of a nearby thiol group, sulfenic acid can react with primary and secondary amines, including amide groups of the protein backbone, to yield sulfenamides (R-SN-R) (Lee et al., 2007). In contrast to these reversible modifications, sulfenic acid can also be further oxidized to sulfinic $\left( \mathrm { R - S O } _ { 2 } \mathrm { H } \right)$ and sulfonic $\left( \mathrm { R - S O } _ { 3 } \mathrm { H } \right)$ derivatives that are considered to be irreversible modifications in bacterial cells (Paulsen and Carroll, 2013). 

## 17.1.2.2 Nitric oxide

S-nitrosylation involves covalent attachment of an NO group to a redox-active cysteine thiol to form an S-nitrosothiol (R-SNO), a reversible modification that can affect protein function (Hess et al., 2005; Kim et al., 2002). Despite being a free radical, ∙ NO is relatively stable and lacks direct reactivity toward most thiol groups (Fukuto et al., 2012). Although the predominant nitrosylating agent(s) in vivo is unknown, several possible mechanisms for ∙ NO-mediated protein nitrosylation have been proposed (Ridnour et al., 2004; Smith and Marletta, 2012). Perhaps the most critical of these processes is transnitrosation of redoxactive cysteine thiols, whereby the nitrosonium cation $\left( \mathrm { N O ^ { + } } \right)$ is transferred to a thiolate. Low-molecular-weight S-nitrosothiols $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , S-nitrosoglutathione (GSNO) and S-nitrosocysteine (CysNO)), dinitrosyl iron complexes (DNICs), and dinitrogen trioxide $( \mathrm { N } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 3 } )$ are each potentially biologically relevant donors of $\mathrm { N O ^ { + } }$ (Bosworth et al., 2009; Hogg, 2002; Wink et al., 1994). Alternatively, in the presence of molecular oxygen, ∙ NO can undergo auto-oxidation to form $\mathbf { \nabla } \cdot \mathrm { N O } _ { 2 } .$ . This RNS can subsequently remove an electron from thiol groups, yielding a thiyl radical (R-S∙ ) that can resolve through disulfide bond formation or react with ∙ NO to generate an S-nitrosothiol (Madej et al., 2008). The nitroxyl anion (NO- ) has also been found to mediate thiol-dependent biological activities (Hughes, 1999). 

## 17.1.2.3 Peroxynitrite

While ROS and RNS are distinct families of molecules, their generation, chemistry, and biological activity are often closely linked. Peroxynitrite is an informative example of this intersecting relationship. Peroxynitrite is formed by the diffusioncontrolled reaction between $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ^ { \bullet - }$ and ∙ NO (Blough and Zafiriou, 1985). Importantly, this limits ONOO− formation to sites where both radicals are available concurrently and at close to equimolar amounts. Peroxynitrite and its conjugate acid, peroxynitrous acid (ONOOH, $\mathsf { p } K _ { a } \mathsf { \Gamma } ^ { } 6 . 5 \mathrm { - } 6 . 8 )$ , are strong biological oxidants capable of modifying redox-active cysteine thiols via two principal mechanisms (Quijano et al., 1997; Radi, 2013). The first involves direct oxidation by ONOOH and results in the formation of R-SOH, an intermediate of disulfide bond formation and other redox modifications as discussed in this chapter. The second involves homolytic cleavage of the labile peroxo (O–O) bond, thereby generating HO∙ and $\mathbf { \cdot } _ { \mathrm { N O } _ { 2 } }$ (Goldstein and Merenyi, 2008). These radicals mediate consequent pro- ´ tein modifications that include formation of the thiyl radical, an important intermediate in disulfide bond formation and Snitrosylation. 

## 17.1.3 Sensing of reactive species by transcription factors

Direct interaction with DNA is the most common mechanism by which bacterial thiol-based sensors mediate transcriptional regulation. Oxidative and nitrosative modifications of redox-active transcription factors trigger conformational changes that fundamentally affect binding to cognate DNA sequences (Antelmann and Helmann, 2011; Marshall et al., 2000). As these protein–DNA interactions directly influence the recruitment of RNAP to specific loci, target gene expression can be differentially controlled by reduced and oxidized forms of the regulator. Thiol-based, redox-active transcription factors that both sense and transduce oxidative and nitrosative signals typically control a defined subset of genes whose products participate in the direct detoxification of the triggering reactive species (Vazquez- ´ Torres, 2012). Accordingly, oxidation or nitrosylation of key cysteine residues in thiol-based sensors usually enhances bacterial resistance to oxidative and nitrosative stress. The following are several examples of thiol-based redox sensors that coordinate gene expression in response to ROS and RNS through direct interaction with DNA. 

## 17.1.3.1 OxyR

OxyR is a thiol-based sensor that responds primarily to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } .$ - mediated oxidative stress (Christman et al., 1985) (see Section 10). Upon oxidation, OxyR promotes the transcriptional activation of approximately 30 genes involved in bacterial resistance to biological peroxides (Storz et al., 1990). Positively regulated gene products of the OxyR regulon in Escherichia coli and Salmonella enterica serovar Typhimurium include the catalase KatG and the alkylhydroperoxide reductase AhpCF that detoxify $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Storz and Tartaglia, 1992); the glutaredoxin Grx1 and the glutathione oxidoreductase GorA that help restore redox balance and protect against disulfide stress (Fernandes and Holmgren, 2004); and the DNA-binding ferritin-like protein Dps and the ferric homeostasis regulator Fur that limit the damaging effects of iron-dependent Fenton chemistry (Winterbourn, 1995). 

OxyR and its widely conserved orthologs in Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria are members of the LysR family of transcriptional regulators that use a helix-turn-helix motif to bind DNA (Maddocks and Oyston, 2008). Reduced OxyR acts as a transcriptional repressor, binding as a pair of dimers near the promoter region of target genes via two sets of adjacent major DNA grooves separated by one helical turn (Toledano et al., 1994). Upon exposure to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ the thiolate of OxyR $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 9 9 } }$ is oxidized to sulfenic acid, a species that rapidly condenses with the thiol group of $\mathrm { \dot { C } y s ^ { 2 0 8 } }$ to form an intramolecular disulfide bond (Choi et al., 2001; Zheng et al., 1998). Disulfide bond formation results in structural rearrangement at the oligomeric interface, characterized by a 30◦ rotation between subunits (Choi et al., 2001). Rotation among the OxyR subunits allows oxidized, tetrameric OxyR to bind four adjacent major DNA grooves (Toledano et al., 1994). This alteration in DNAbinding specificity is reversible and promotes productive interactions with RNAP leading to the activation of gene transcription (Antelmann and Helmann, 2011). 

While structural, mutational, and in vivo studies have demonstrated OxyR to form an intramolecular disulfide bond between $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 9 9 } }$ and $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 2 0 8 }$ in response to peroxide (Tao, 1999; Zheng et al., 1998), other redox modifications occurring exclusively at $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 9 9 } }$ have also been found capable of mediating transcriptional activation. Indeed, sulfenylation, S-nitrosylation, and Sglutathionylation of OxyR at $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 1 9 9 }$ each activates transcription in vitro; however, OxyR structure, DNA-binding affinity, cooperativity of binding, and promoter activity differ depending on the specific modification (Kim et al., 2002). This supports the novel notion that, rather than being a simple “on-off ” thiol switch, thiol-based sensors can distinguish among, and differentially regulate gene transcription in response to, individual ROS and RNS according to the specific redox modifications incurred (Kim et al., 2002; Lu et al., 2005). Further support for the notion of a “smart” thiol switch comes from a recent study that demonstrated S-nitrosylated OxyR to induce an antinitrosative regulon that is distinct from that of oxidized OxyR in vivo (Seth et al., 2012). 

Consistent with the prevalence of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ in host defense, OxyR has been found to be important in bacterial pathogenesis. More specifically, OxyR promotes Pseudomonas aeruginosa virulence in murine models of acute pneumonia and burn sepsis (Lau et al., 2005), enhances Yersinia pestis survival in an insect model of infection (Erickson et al., 2011), and supports gastrointestinal and urinary tract colonization by Klebsiella pneumoniae and E. coli, respectively, in mice (Hennequin and Forestier, 2009; Johnson et al., 2006). Interestingly, OxyR does not appear to play a significant role in the resistance of S. Typhimurium to oxidative killing by host phagocytes (Papp-Szabo` et al., 1994), suggesting that functional redundancy exists. In this sense, a second LysR-type transcriptional regulator, Hrg, has been shown to enhance S. Typhimurium resistance to H O produced intracellularly by activated macrophages (Lahiri et al., 2008). Hrg also contributes to Salmonella virulence in a murine model of infection (Lahiri et al., 2008). Although Salmonella Hrg contains two cysteine residues, potential sensory functions have not been examined. 

## 17.1.3.2 OhrR

OhrR, a member of the MarR family of transcriptional regulators (Wilkinson and Grove, 2006), promotes bacterial defense against organic hydroperoxides formed by free radical attack on cellular fatty acids (Girotti, 1998; see Section 10). As with most MarR-type regulators, reduced OhrR dimers bind a pair of inverted-repeat DNA sequences that overlap the −10 and −35 promoter elements necessary for efficient recognition by RNAP (Fuangthong et al., 2001). Consequently, in the absence of peroxide stress, OhrR acts as a transcriptional repressor of target genes by encumbering the binding of RNAP to promoter regions (Wilkinson and Grove, 2006). Oxidation of OhrR results in conformational rearrangement and rotation of the winged helixturn-helix DNA-binding domains to a position unable to bind DNA (Newberry et al., 2007). Dissociation of OhrR unmasks the −10 and −35 promoter elements allowing RNAP binding and transcriptional activation. In this manner, OhrR controls ohrA, a fundamental organic hydroperoxide resistance determinant that encodes a peroxidase capable of reducing peroxidated fatty acids to their corresponding alcohols (Fuangthong et al., 2001). 

OhrR orthologs can be divided into two classes depending on whether one or two cysteine residues participate in the sensing of reactive species (Antelmann and Helmann, 2011). The $1 { - } \mathrm { C y s }$ class of orthologs is typified by Bacillus subtilis OhrR that contains a single redox-sensitive cysteine residue, $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 1 5 }$ (Fuangthong and Helmann, 2002). Structural examination and mutagenesis studies have demonstrated that the negative charge of the reactive $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 1 5 }$ thiolate is stabilized through several interactions dependent on the protein microenvironment, including a helix dipole and hydrogen bonding with the side chains of $\mathrm { T y r } ^ { 2 9 }$ and $\mathrm { T y r } ^ { 4 0 }$ located in an adjacent helix (Hong et al., 2005). Structural analysis has also identified a continuous strip of solvent-exposed aromatic and nonpolar residues thought to stabilize the binding of lipophilic oxidants (Hong et al., 2005). This unique structural feature is highly conserved among all OhrR orthologs and likely accounts for the specificity of OhrR for organic hydroperoxides. Oxidation of $\mathrm { \bar { C } y s ^ { 1 5 } }$ by peroxides, such as cumene hydroperoxide, yields a sulfenic acid modification that is necessary but insufficient for structural rearrangement and translational derepression (Fuangthong and Helmann, 2002). Further reaction of this intermediate with low-molecular-weight thiols, including free cysteine and bacillithiol in vivo (Lee et al., 2007), generates a mixed disulfide that triggers structural changes, thereby negating DNA binding and target gene repression. Sulfenylated $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 1 5 }$ can also react with the neighboring backbone amide group of $\mathrm { P h e } ^ { 1 6 }$ to form a sulfenamide or undergo further oxidation to sulfinic or sulfonic derivatives, as is the case upon exposure to linoleic acid hydroperoxide (Lee et al., 2007; Soonsanga et al., 2008). While each of these modifications abolishes DNA binding and relieves transcriptional repression, sulfinylation and sulfonylation are not known to be reversible in bacteria. 

The 2-Cys class of OhrR orthologs is best illustrated by Xanthomonas campestris OhrR (Mongkolsuk et al., 1998). Analogous to the 1-Cys mechanism of peroxide sensing, X. campestris OhrR contains an N-terminal sensory cysteine, $\mathrm { C y } \mathrm { s } ^ { 2 2 }$ , that reacts with organic hydroperoxides to yield a sulfenic intermediate (Panmanee et al., 2006). Sulfenylation of $\mathrm { C y } \mathrm { \mathrm { { s } } } ^ { 2 2 }$ allows for the formation of an intersubunit disulfide bond with $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 1 2 7 }$ (Newberry et al., 2007). Disulfide bond formation elicits reorientation of the DNA-binding domains, thus disrupting the ability of OhrR to remain bound to operator DNA and relieving OhrR-dependent transcriptional repression. 

Though other OhrR-like regulators share the 1-Cys or 2- Cys mechanism of peroxide sensing and transcriptional derepression, several orthologs have been found to mediate greatly expanded regulatory roles. Staphylococcus aureus MgrA, SarZ, and SarA are each examples of MarR–OhrR-type transcription factors that sense oxidative stress using a lone cysteine residue (Ballal and Manna, 2010; Chen et al., 2006; Poor et al., 2009). These regulators control genes that are not only important in antioxidant defense $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , SarZ controls the ohr peroxidase, and SarA controls the sodA superoxide dismutase and trxB thioredoxin reductase), but also maintain regulatory roles over many other genes involved in diverse cellular processes including virulence, antibiotic resistance, and a variety of metabolic processes (Chen et al., 2009; Luong et al., 2006). It is important to note that, in addition to oxidation of the critical cysteine residue, MgrA, SarZ, and SarA have each been shown to be modified via phosphorylation, suggesting that these proteins are more broadly responsive to bacterial physiology (Sun et al., 2012). Similarly, the OspR and MexR transcriptional regulators of P. aeruginosa, which harbor two redox-active cysteines, mediate antioxidant defense and also participate in coordinating antibiotic resistance, quorum sensing (see Section 21), and cellular metabolism (Chen et al., 2008; Lan et al., 2010). 

## 17.1.3.3 NmlR

NmlR (Neisseria merR-like regulator) describes a wellconserved subfamily of MerR-type transcriptional regulators involved in bacterial resistance to oxidative and nitrosative stress. NmlR contains four cysteine residues thought to coordinate $Z \mathrm { n } ^ { 2 + }$ and mediate redox-responsive transcriptional control (Kidd et al., 2005). In response to oxidative stress, NmlR positively regulates a small regulon in Neisseria gonorrheae that encodes the copper export ATPase CopA and the thioredoxin reductase TrxB (Kidd et al., 2005; Potter et al., 2009). NmlR enhances the resistance of gonococcus to antimicrobial killing by organic hydroperoxides and diamide-mediated depletion of reduced thiol pools (Kidd et al., 2005). Moreover, the positively regulated effector TrxB, which catalyzes the NADPH-dependent reduction of thioredoxin important in cellular redox buffering, protects N. gonorrheae against $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ^ { \bullet - }$ and ∙ NO (Potter et al., 2009). TrxB also promotes the survival of gonococci within primary human cervical epithelial cells (Potter et al., 2009). Similarly, the NmlR ortholog of Streptococcus pneumoniae induces the transcription of adhC, encoding an alcohol dehydrogenase that corrects imbalances in thiol-disulfide homeostasis through the reduction of GSNO (Stroeher et al., 2007). This induction is essential in pneumococcal defense against RNS and, while dispensable for colonization of the nasopharynx, is important for systemic infection in a murine model of infection (Stroeher et al., 2007). 

MerR-type regulators characteristically mediate both transcriptional repression and activation while bound at the same site on DNA, an extended spacer region between the −10 and −35 elements of target promoters (Brown et al., 2003). In the absence of signal, bound DNA is distorted as such to obscure the −10 element and repress transcription; in the presence of signal, structural changes occur in the regulator, thereby unwinding the operator DNA, straightening the helix backbone, and productively reorienting the −10 and −35 elements to allow interaction with RNAP and transcriptional activation (Brown et al., 2003). Analogous to other MerR-type regulators, NmlR binds between the −10 and −35 elements of target promoters, indicating a similar mechanism of transcriptional control (Kidd et al., 2005). As the four NmlR cysteine residues are necessary for transcriptional activation, it has been proposed that redox modification and consequent disulfide bond formation and/or $Z \mathrm { n } ^ { 2 + }$ release may trigger structural rearrangement and the induction of gene expression (Kidd et al., 2005). 

## 17.1.3.4 SsrB

The response regulator SsrB is the principal transcriptional activator of Salmonella pathogenicity island 2 (SPI2), a 25 kb gene cluster induced inside host cells and required for intracellular survival and proliferation (Hensel, 2000). Classical activation of SsrB occurs following phosphorylation of $\mathrm { A s p } ^ { 5 6 }$ , located in the N-terminal receiver domain, by the cognate sensor kinase SsrA (Feng et al., 2004; Hensel, 2000; see Section 3). Phosphorylation drives conformational changes that relieve inhibitory contacts between the receiver domain and the C-terminal DNAbinding domain of SsrB, thereby exposing the DNA recognition helices and dimerization interface (Carroll et al., 2009; Feng et al., 2004). SsrB dimerization occurs upon binding degenerate, AT-rich DNA targets, thereby allowing both direct transcriptional activation and relief from H-NS gene silencing (Walthers et al., 2007). RNS-mediated modification of SsrB $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 2 0 3 }$ , which is located in the dimerization interface of SsrB and conserved in both typhoidal and nontyphoidal strains of Salmonella, abolishes the DNA-binding activity of SsrB, an event that prevents the activation of SsrB-controlled target genes (Husain et al., 2010). This transcriptional outcome represents a novel departure from traditional redox-active transcription factors that characteristically induce genes whose products are directly involved in antioxidative and antinitrosative defense via detoxification and repair programs. 

Upon exposure of Salmonella to acidified nitrite, a primary source of nitrosative stress in the gastric lumen and within the phagosomes of macrophages, $\mathrm { C y } \mathrm { \bar { s } } ^ { 2 0 3 }$ of SsrB has been shown to be S-nitrosylated (Husain et al., 2010). $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 2 0 3 }$ has also been found to participate in intermolecular disulfide bond formation between SsrB monomers and to be directly modified by ONOO− and other RNS (Carroll et al., 2009; Husain et $a l .$ , 2010). More specifically, ONOO− oxidizes $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 2 0 3 }$ to a mixture of sulfenic, sulfinic, and sulfonic acid derivatives. While sulfinylation and sulfonylation are not common in bacterial redox signaling, substitutional mimicry of the negative charge possessed by these modifications (C203D and C203E) also blocks SsrB dimerization and transcriptional activity, suggesting that a charge-based mechanism of structural change and transcriptional control may exist (Carroll et al., 2009; Vazquez-Torres, ´ 2012). Interestingly, $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ does not diminish the capacity of SsrB to bind DNA, indicating that SsrB has an inherent measure of specificity toward RNS (Husain et al., 2010). While the significance of individual redox modifications remains to be defined, the ability of SsrB to respond to RNS through $\mathrm { C y s } ^ { 2 0 3 }$ plays an important role in Salmonella pathogenesis (Husain et al., 2010; McCollister et al., 2005). This is perhaps best exemplified by the observation that sustained SPI2 expression by Salmonella expressing the RNS-resistant SsrB C203S variant markedly decreases Salmonella dissemination and virulence in an $\mathrm { N R A M P 1 ^ { R } }$ C3H/HeN murine model of oral infection as compared to wild-type bacteria carrying the RNS-sensitive allele (Husain et al., 2010). 

## 17.1.4 Sensing of reactive species by antisigma factors

The multi-subunit bacterial RNAP consists of a catalytic core $( \alpha _ { 2 } \beta \beta ^ { \prime } \omega )$ and a dissociable sigma (σ) subunit that is responsible for promoter recognition (Darst, 2001; see Chapter 2.4 and 2.5). In actively growing cells, promoter specificity is typically directed by a single, principal σ subunit that is subject to the influence of DNA-binding proteins that repress or activate transcription at specific loci (Paget and Helmann, 2003). However, bacteria also encode a number of alternative σ subunits that can bind core RNAP and function in place of the dominant σ subunit (Helmann, 2002; Kazmierczak et al., 2005) (see Chapter 2.4 and 2.5). As alternative σ factors have distinct promoter selectivities, they can redirect RNAP to a particular subset of genes and thereby activate transcription from these loci. Transcriptional regulation via σ switching mechanisms is a well-described event in response to stress that allows a cell to simultaneously regulate a large number of genes whose products typically function in similar physiological processes (Kazmierczak et al., 2005). 

## 17.1.4.1 ??R and RsrA

The extracytoplasmic function alternative sigma subunit $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ (see Chapter 2.6) is critical for Streptomyces coelicolor resistance against oxidative stress elicited by diamide and the $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ^ { \bullet - }$ generators plumbagin and menadione (Paget et al., 1998). Microarray analysis has identified 77 ${ \mathfrak { o } } ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ -dependent gene targets activated in response to diamide, 37 of which have been validated by nuclease mapping (Kallifidas et al., 2010). Positively regulated genes encode products that function in maintaining cysteine and methionine in their reduced state; these include TrxA and TrxC thioredoxins, TrxB thioredoxin reductase, MsrA and MsrB methionine sulfoxide reductases, a glutaredoxin-like enzyme, and PhdD ferredoxin reductase (Kallifidas et al., 2010; Paget et al., 1998). The biosynthetic genes cysM, important in cysteine biosynthesis, and mshA, necessary for production of the low-molecular-weight thiol mycothiol, are also upregulated in a $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } } \mathrm { - d e p e n d e n t }$ manner, as are the clp and lon encoded ATPdependent proteases thought to be important in degrading protein aggregates formed during oxidative stress (Bender et al., 2010; Kallifidas et al., 2010) (see Section 10). Moreover, in response to oxidative stress, ${ \mathfrak { o } } ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ also induces the expression of cellular effectors and enzymes that independently mediate transcriptional regulation and thereby further increase the regulatory breadth of ${ \mathfrak { o } } ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ . These include the alternative sigma subunit $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { H r d D } }$ and the guanosine tetraphosphate synthase RelA (Kallifidas et al., 2010). 

The availability of $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ to associate with RNAP and mediate transcriptional activation of antioxidant defenses is controlled by RsrA, a cytoplasmic antisigma factor that acts as a specific negative regulator of $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ (Kang et al., 1999) (see Chapter 10.5). RsrA is a zinc-binding antisigma (ZAS) factor that contains seven cysteine residues. $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 1 } , C y s ^ { 4 1 } }$ , and $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 4 4 } }$ are essential for $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ sequestration and RsrA sensory function in vivo (Paget et al., 2001). While currently unresolved, zinc coordination is most likely facilitated by $\mathrm { C y } \mathrm { \dot { s } } ^ { 1 1 }$ together with $\mathrm { H i s } ^ { 3 7 } , \mathrm { C y s } ^ { 4 1 }$ , and $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 4 4 } }$ that form the conserved Hx $\mathrm { \mathrm { C x } } _ { 2 } \mathrm { C }$ motif held by other ZAS family regulators (Zdanowski et al., 2006). Interestingly, while zinc coordination is not required for $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ binding by RsrA, metal ligation is important in stabilizing the structure of reduced RsrA and limiting thiolate reactivity in order to prevent inappropriate disulfide bond formation among the critical cysteine residues in the absence of oxidative stress (Jung et al., 2011). In its reduced, zinc-bound form, RsrA associates with $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ , thereby precluding its interaction with RNAP (Kang et al., 1999). In response to oxidation, disulfide bond formation occurs between $\mathrm { \bar { C } y s ^ { 1 1 } }$ and either $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 4 1 } }$ or $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 4 4 } }$ , with the latter being the primary resolving cysteine (Bae et al., 2004). Disulfide bond formation releases the bound zinc ion and also causes increased α-helical structuring incompatible with binding to $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ (Li et al., 2003). The consequent release of $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { R } }$ allows its association with RNAP and transcriptional activation of antioxidant defenses. 

## 17.1.5 Sensing of reactive species by RNAP regulators

During the course of infection, many microbial pathogens encounter high amounts of reactive species produced as part of the innate host response. Iron–sulfur clusters of dehydratases are particularly susceptible to oxidative damage, and thus ROS and RNS can induce functional auxotrophies associated with cytotoxicity. Bacterial pathogens associated with inflammation must rapidly sense and respond to the nutritional deprivation associated with oxidative and nitrosative stress. Coordinating multiple stress responses to promote resistance to high levels of oxidative and nitrosative stress requires a global transcription program capable of integrating redox balance with the metabolic demands of the cell. Transcriptional regulation at the level of RNAP can coordinate these responses to rapidly and reversibly redirect the transcriptome in accordance with environmental stresses encountered by the cell. One notable example is the broadly conserved stringent response (see Section 7). 

First reported in E. coli experiencing nutritional starvation, the stringent response is characterized by the transcriptional activation of amino acid biosynthetic genes and repression of translational machinery in order to balance protein production with nutrient availability (Cashel and Kalbacher, 1970). The stringent response has been observed in almost all bacteria and is regulated by the small protein DksA and the nucleotide guanosine tetraphosphate (ppGpp) that is enzymatically synthesized by RelA and SpoT in response to shortages in amino acids, phosphate, fatty acids, nitrogen, carbon, or iron (Cashel and Kalbacher, 1970; Dalebroux and Swanson, 2012; Silverman and Atherly, 1979) (see Section 7). In addition to the classical coordination of transcription in the stringent response, DksA was recently shown to respond to oxidative and nitrosative stress (Henard et al., 2010, 2014; Henard and Vazquez-Torres,´ 2012). DksA is highly conserved among Gram-negative bacteria, suggesting that the novel ability of Salmonella DksA to sense and respond to ROS and RNS is likely generalizable to other microorganisms. 

## 17.1.5.1 DksA

Rather than binding DNA promoters directly, as is the case with most regulatory proteins, DksA regulates gene transcription by interacting with RNAP (Paul et al., 2004; Perederina et al., 2004). DksA contains two conserved, acidic amino acid residues at the tip of the coiled-coil domain that are thought to be critical for the interaction of this regulatory protein with RNAP (Lee et al., 2012; Lennon et $a l .$ , 2012). The interaction between DksA and free RNAP occurs with an apparent dissociation constant of 100 nM, whereas its interaction with DNA-bound RNAP is approximately 10 times weaker (Lennon et al., 2009). Although the mechanism(s) of DksA-dependent transcriptional regulation is not fully elucidated, it is thought that DksA destabilizes RNAP–promoter interactions and consequently reduces the open complex lifetime (Paul et al., 2004; Rutherford et al., 2009). Transcriptional regulation by DksA is promoter specific, and DksA can act as either an activator or a repressor depending on the kinetics of transcriptional initiation (Lennon et al., 2012). Importantly, since DksA levels within the cell do not fluctuate during nutritional stress and activation of the stringent response (Paul et al., 2005), DksA regulatory activity is likely adjusted posttranslationally. 

All DksA orthologs contain at least one conserved cysteine residue, corresponding to $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 1 4 } }$ of Salmonella DksA. Analysis of the DksA consensus sequence and structure suggests that the DksA microenvironment is conducive to sensing reactive species (Henard et al., 2014). A number of conserved basic and hydrophobic residues may promote the formation of a thiolate at $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 1 4 } }$ . Specifically, the strongly basic guanidino group of $\mathrm { A r g } ^ { 1 2 5 }$ is located about 7 A from˚ $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 1 4 } }$ , potentially lowering the $\mathsf { p } K _ { a }$ and promoting thiolate formation necessary for nucleophilic attack on biologically relevant peroxides, such as $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and ONOO- . Additionally, the conserved residues $\mathrm { I l e } ^ { 1 2 1 }$ , Leu126, and ${ \mathrm { L e u } } ^ { 1 3 4 }$ form a hydrophobic pocket flanking $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 1 4 } }$ that may act to exclude water and thus affect thiolate reactivity. Alternately, these hydrophobic residues may force the $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 1 4 } }$ side chain away from the protein core, thereby increasing thiolate accessibility to ROS or RNS. 

While some DksA orthologs contain a second conserved cysteine residue, the largest group contains four cysteine residues integral to a zinc-binding motif. Mutation of any cysteine in the four-cysteine zinc-binding motif equates to a dksA null phenotype, demonstrating that all four cysteine residues coordinating the zinc are required for DksA to regulate gene transcription (Henard and Vazquez-Torres, 2012). Zinc coordina-´ tion may lower the $\mathsf { p } K _ { a }$ of the thiol to promote thiolate formation, thereby contributing to sensory function (Jung et al., 2011). Alternatively, thiolate stabilization by a zinc cation can lower the basal state energy, thereby increasing the transition state energy barrier to form an oxidation product (Ferrer-Sueta et al., 2011). Low reactivity is critical to prevent DksA from reacting with endogenous levels of ROS and RNS. This ensures that the transcriptional changes mediated by DksA in response to oxidative and nitrosative stress are restricted to high amounts of reactive species engendered during inflammation. Taken together, these determinants are markedly similar to those reported to influence thiolate formation in redox-active transcriptional regulators and are consistent with a thiol-based sensory function. 

## 17.1.5.2 DksA cysteine thiols are modified by ROS and RNS

Recent studies by our laboratory have shown that DksA is capable of forming disulfide bonds in response to oxidation with $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and ONOO− (Henard et al., 2014). Although the distances between the individual cysteine residues of DksA are nearly equal, disulfide bonding is not uniform. Disulfide bonds preferentially involve the conserved $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 1 4 } }$ residue, which is consistent with the microenvironment of DksA promoting $\mathrm { C y s ^ { 1 1 4 } }$ thiolate formation (Henard et al., 2014). DksA has also been shown to be S-nitrosylated by biologically active nitrogen oxides generated from the acidification of nitrite, a biologically relevant source of nitrosative stress encountered in the host (Henard et al., 2014). Posttranslational modification of DksA by reactive species results in functional changes that affect the activity of this RNAP transcriptional regulator. 

Oxidation and S-nitrosylation of cysteine thiols results in zinc release from DksA (Henard et al., 2014), which may serve a regulatory role for DksA activity by inducing changes in secondary structure. Indeed, DksA can lose approximately 30% of its α-helical secondary structure in response to ROS and RNS (Henard et al., 2014). Changes in α-helicity that affect the binding of DksA to the secondary channel of RNAP may be an important determinant of regulation. In fact, DksA oxidized by 

ONOO− binds RNAP 30% less than reduced DksA (Henard et al., 2014), potentially increasing the abundance of DksA-free RNAP to inhibit the activation of DksA-dependent gene transcription (Henard et al., 2014). These observations support a model whereby posttranslational modification of DksA by high levels of ROS and RNS directly affects its regulatory activity during oxidative and nitrosative stress. It is not currently known if DksA mediates similar regulatory roles in response to low amounts of ROS and RNS. 

While the literature describing the role of DksA in the stringent response is substantial (reviewed in Potrykus and Cashel, 2008; see Section 7), little is known about the role of oxidized DksA in regulating gene transcription. During the stringent response, DksA preferentially activates amino acid biosynthesis and transport genes, such as livJ and hisG, which encode a component of the high-affinity branched-chain amino acid transport system and a phosphoribosyl phosphotransferase involved in histidine biosynthesis, respectively (Paul et al., 2005). Transcriptional analysis shows genes activated by DksA are repressed in cells experiencing oxidative and/or nitrosative stress (Henard et al., 2014). This is in contrast to its role as a transcriptional activator during nutritional starvation. The repression of gene transcription by oxidized DksA appears to be independent from its previously described role in the stringent response. Interestingly, the role of DksA in repressing translational machinery, a hallmark of the stringent response, is maintained during oxidative and nitrosative stress, demonstrating DksA is not inactivated by posttranslational modifications. In vitro transcription reactions have revealed that DksA-dependent gene repression is direct, promoter specific, and reversible (Henard et al., 2014). The novel role of DksA as a sensor of ROS and RNS may help regulate transcriptional responses of nutritional programs to overcome the metabolic challenges imposed by oxidative and nitrosative stress. 

## 17.1.5.3 Role of DksA in bacterial pathogenesis

DksA has been shown to contribute to the virulence of many microbial pathogens, including Shigella flexneri (Mogull et al., 2001), P. aeruginosa (Jude et al., 2003), and Salmonella (Henard and Vazquez-Torres, 2012). DksA-´ deficient Salmonella are attenuated in an acute murine model of salmonellosis (Henard et al., 2010, 2014; Henard and Vazquez-´ Torres, 2012). Importantly, dksA-deficient Salmonella regain virulence in NADPH phagocyte oxidase or iNOS-deficient mice, which are unable to produce $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ^ { \bullet - }$ or ∙ NO, respectively (Henard et al., 2010; Henard and Vazquez-Torres, 2012). These´ data demonstrate the importance of DksA in the antioxidant and antinitrosative defenses required for Salmonella pathogenesis. DksA also coordinates antioxidant defenses of S. flexneri (Mogull et al., 2001). 

The role of DksA in promoting antioxidant defense in Salmonella experiencing oxidative stress has been found to be multifactorial. First, it has been observed that the role of DksA in resistance to oxidative stress is greatest in stationary phase cells, demonstrating the importance of DksA in regulating antioxidant defenses during nutritional starvation (Henard et al., 2010). Second, DksA helps coordinate the expression of glutathione biosynthetic genes necessary for the synthesis of the low-molecular-weight antioxidant glutathione (Henard et al., 2010). Third, DksA regulates critical steps in the pentose phosphate pathway and tricarboxylic acid cycle associated with the generation of NADPH and NADH (Henard et al., 2010). Low levels of NADPH and NADH could contribute to the hypersusceptibility of DksA-deficient Salmonella to the bactericidal effects of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Henard et al., 2010). Although these reduced pyridine nucleotides contribute to cellular reducing power, tight regulation of NAD(P)H levels is critical because too much reducing power can stimulate the reduction of free flavins driving Fenton chemistry and DNA oxidative damage (Woodmansee and Imlay, 2003). Taken together, DksA promotes survival during oxidative stress by balancing metabolic limitations imposed by reactive species with reducing power that drives central metabolism and resistance to oxidative stress. 

DksA-deficient Salmonella are hypersusceptible to the bacteriostatic effects of ∙ NO (Henard and Vazquez-Torres, 2012).´ Supplementation with amino acids abrogates most of this hypersusceptibility, demonstrating that metabolic limitations imposed by ∙ NO are minimized by the regulatory activity of DksA (Henard and Vazquez-Torres, 2012). With over 200 ´ [Fe-S] proteins identified, the single [4Fe-4S] enzyme IlvD, a dihydroxy-acid dehydratase involved in branched-chain amino acid (BCAA) biosynthesis, has been found to be a critical target of ∙ NO-mediated cytotoxicity (Hyduke et al., 2007). Disruption of BCAA biosynthesis by ∙ NO can result in BCAA functional auxotrophies and bacteriostasis during nitrosative stress (Hyduke et al., 2007). Depletion of BCAA pools can also lead to perturbations in gene regulation and the inappropriate activation of starvation signals (Hyduke et al., 2007). Indeed, it is possible that oxidized DksA fosters resistance to RNS by repressing inappropriate or unnecessary gene transcription during high fluxes of nitrosative stress. 

## 17.1.6 Conclusion

Pathogenic bacteria experience numerous ROS and RNS generated by the innate immune response. Redox-active transcriptional regulators coordinate antioxidant and antinitrosative defense programs to facilitate survival during association with the host. Importantly, the levels of oxidative and nitrosative stress experienced by pathogenic bacteria vary during disease progression. While not absolute, transcriptional regulation by thiol-based sensors observes a hierarchy whereby increasing levels of ROS and RNS progressively enhance the breadth of antioxidant and antinitrosative defense programs (Figure 17.1.1). Coordinating gene transcription in response to oxidative and nitrosative stress through a hierarchy of thiol-based sensors allows the cell to differentiate low levels of endogenous ROS and RNS produced as byproducts of normal cellular metabolism from high fluxes of reactive species generated by phagocytes. During low levels of oxidative and nitrosative stress, highly reactive thiol-based transcription factors (e.g., OxyR) promote the transcriptional activation of specific, yet limited, sets of target genes to promote detoxification of ROS and RNS. Increasing levels of ROS and RNS require a more robust transcriptional response that can be achieved by utilizing σ switching to redirect RNAP to a particular, expanded subset of genes. As the levels of ROS and RNS begin to overwhelm frontline cellular defenses and approach bactericidal and bacteriostatic levels, global transcriptional redirection is required. Since DksA regulates over 10% of the transcriptome, thiol-based regulation of RNAP by DksA offers considerable breadth of gene regulation, thereby allowing coordination of gene transcription in accordance with high levels of oxidative and nitrosative stress. In this context, appropriate transcriptional regulation in response to varying levels of oxidative and nitrosative stress is a fundamental feature in the antioxidant and antinitrosative defense programs of bacteria. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/099d9bc64099a835d239b97a8ebf6cad8dafb1de676cbd94532d45fef575acfa.jpg)



Figure 17.1.1 Regulation hierarchy among thiol-based sensors. Thiol-based transcriptional regulators allow microbial pathogens the ability to orchestrate an appropriate response to varying levels of reactive species in a hierarchal fashion. Highly responsive thiol-based sensors such as OxyR allow the cell to mount a targeted response to ROS and RNS by activating specific genes whose products are necessary to maintain redox balance during moderate levels of oxidative and nitrosative stress. When a greater transcriptional response is required, sigma switching can redirect RNAP to a particular subset of genes, thus expanding the antioxidant and antinitrosative response. At high levels of oxidative and nitrosative stress, redox-active regulation of RNAP by DksA redirects global transcription, integrating redox balance with the metabolic demands of the cell.
