## C H A P T E R 17.2

# Haemoglobins of Mycobacterium tuberculosis and their involvement in management of environmental stress

Kanak L. Dikshit 

Council of Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR) Institute of Microbial Technology, Chandigarh, India 

## 17.2.1 Introduction

Mycobacterium tuberculosis (Mtb) is one of the most dreaded human pathogens; it infects nearly one-third of the human population, causing 1.5 million deaths per year (World Health Organization, 2013). Amongst the infected population, 90% harbour the bacterium but do not display any symptom of disease. One of the major contributing factors to the success of Mtb as a human pathogen is its ability to remain in the dormant state in humans for decades, with subsequent reactivation later in life (Bloom and Murray, 1992). Mtb encounters different levels of oxygen availability during its intracellular life cycle, ranging from oxygen-rich conditions during lung infection to severe hypoxia within the macrophages and calcified granuloma where tubercle bacilli primarily reside (Boshoff and Barry, 2005; Starck et al., 2004). Although Mtb has an obligate requirement of oxygen for its cellular metabolism (Berney and Cook, 2010; Park et al., 1992), paradoxically, it is able to grow and adapt successfully under an oxygen-limiting environment (Voskuil et al., 2004) and has an excellent ability to adjust its aerobic metabolism in response to varying levels of oxygen. Unravelling of the first mycobacterial genome data in 1998 (Cole et al., 1998) from Mtb revealed that it carries two genomic loci, glbN and glbO, that may encode haemoglobin (Hb)-like proteins, HbN and HbO, respectively. More recently, a two-domain Hb, having unusual features within the haem and the flavin adenine dinucleotide (FAD)-binding domains, has been identified in Mtb (Gupta et al., 2011, 2012). Subsequently, accumulating microbial genome data have revealed that Hb-encoding genes are ubiquitous amongst mycobacteria and that different classes of Hbs usually coexist in many virulent and avirulent species of mycobacteria (Gupta et al., 2011), suggesting that these haem proteins may be playing diverse functions in their cellular metabolism. This chapter provides updates on singleand two-domain haemoglobins of Mtb and addresses their role in cellular metabolism, stress management, and pathogenicity of tuberculosis. 

## 17.2.2 Truncated haemoglobins of Mtb

Truncated haemoglobins (trHbs) constitute a class of small oxygen-binding proteins that deviate drastically from conventional Hbs in their structural organization and host the haem in a two-over-two (2/2) alpha-helical sandwich (Milani et al., 2001, 2003). Three distinct classes of trHbs – Type I, Type II, and Type III – have been identified based on functional residues of their haem-binding pockets and the presence of a tunnel system within the protein matrix that provides convenient access for the entry of gaseous ligands from the outer surface (Wittenberg et al., 2002). Thus, these trHbs lack the conventional mechanism of entry and exit for the gaseous ligands which is operative in classical Hbs. The HbN and the HbO of Mtb share only 18% sequence homology and are expressed at different growth phases of Mtb, suggesting that they may play different physiological functions in their native host. Within the macrophages and avascular calcified granuloma, where Mtb primarily resides during infection, copious amounts of reactive oxygen and nitrogen species are generated, and the availability of oxygen remains extremely low (Chan et al., 1992; Rich et al., 1997). It is anticipated that, by virtue of their ability to bind oxygen with high affinity, HbN and HbO may be able to sequester oxygen under oxygen limitation and may support the aerobic metabolism of its host under the hypoxic environment of the tuberculous granuloma. Additionally, the ability of these trHbs to avidly bind oxygen and interact with reactive oxygen and nitrogen species may protect survival of Mtb under the hazardous environments of its pathogenic life cycle. 

## 17.2.2.1 Truncated haemoglobin, HbN

The HbN from Mtb belongs to group I of the trHb family. Its tertiary structure is based on a 2/2 alpha-helical sandwich, instead of the three-over-three (3/3) helical arrangement present in eukaryotic globins (Milani et al., 2001). Despite deviating from conventional globin folds, the HbN of Mtb shares the conservation of proximal HisF8 and distal PheCD1 with conventional Hbs, but its E7 position, which is always occupied by a histidine or glutamine residue in classical globins, and contributes to the stabilization of bound oxygen, is replaced by a leucine residue and may not be involved in modulating its oxygenbinding properties. The most striking structural features of the HbN that have emerged after unravelling of its X-ray structure are its 12-residue-long, highly polar N-terminal Pre-A region that protrudes out of its compact trHb fold, and the presence of a well-defined tunnel system, with a long and a short branch, that connects the active site of the protein with the external environment (Figure 17.2.1). These hydrophobic tunnels have been proposed to play pivotal roles in accession, diffusion, and storage of ligands for the protein function (Bidon-Chanal et al., 2006; Milani et al., 2001). A phenylalanine residue (PheE15), which is located in the long tunnel and appeared in two different conformations in the crystal structure, has been proposed to act as a gate to the haem (Oliveira et al., 2012) for the access of ligands from the external environment. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/15d8a82ce76f030077c7119356fc545c7faa8c4d2ccd9fbb98b9f4c8f798d397.jpg)



Figure 17.2.1 Structural organization of haemoglobin, N. of Mycobacterium tuberculosis showing the Pre-A motif, protruding out of a compact truncated globin fold. Organization of tunnel branches within the protein matrix is shown in blue, and position of haem is marked. Movement of ligands via tunnel branches is shown by an arrow.


The HbN encoding gene, glbN, of Mtb has been cloned and expressed in E. coli as a 14.4 kDa dimeric protein, displaying reversible oxygen binding (Couture et al., 1999). Measurements of ligand-binding properties of the HbN, as shown in Table 17.2.1, revealed that its oxygen affinity is extremely high, which may aid Mtb in trapping oxygen under the hypoxic environments of macrophages and granuloma. The oxygenbound HbN reacts readily with NO and shows a stoichiometric formation of nitrate (Ouellet et al., 2002; Pathania et al., 2002a) by catalysing rapid oxidation of NO via NO dioxygenation with a second-order rate constant $( \mathrm { k _ { N O D } 7 4 5 \ \mu M ^ { - 1 } \ { s ^ { - 1 } } ) }$ , which is several-fold higher than that of horse heart myoglobin $( \mathrm { k _ { N O D } 4 5 \ \mu M ^ { - 1 } \ { s ^ { - 1 } } } )$ . These results are surprising since, despite lacking an integrated redox partner, NO dioxygenase activity of the HbN appeared quite efficient, very similar to two-domain flavoHbs, where a globin module is linked with a reductase domain. Raman spectra of HbN revealed that its distal haem pocket is distinct from those of other globins and carries an unstrained haem Fe-proximal coordination that is ideally suited for oxygen and NO interactions (Yeh et al., 2000). The cellular level of the HbN remains low during the exponential phase of aerobically growing Mtb (unpublished observation), but its cellular content increases many-fold in Mtb and Mycobacterium bovis (Couture et al., 1999) when cells enter the stationary phase, where nutrient and oxygen levels drop substantially, corresponding to the environment encountered in macrophages and granuloma. Transcriptional activity of the glbN gene is elevated under hypoxia and nitrosative stress in vitro and 48 h of macrophage infection (Pawaria et al., 2008). Thus, the regulation mechanism of the glbN gene correlates well with the functionality of its encoded protein. The HbN detoxifies NO efficiently and, therefore, needs to be available during intracellular infection of Mtb, where the levels of NO and reactive nitrogen increase substantially in response to microbial infection. 

## 17.2.2.2 Truncated haemoglobin, HbO

Most of the studies on mycobacterial Hbs have centred on the HbN of Mtb, and less is known about its molecular cousin, HbO, which belongs to the type II class of trHbs and displays only 18% sequence homology with the HbN. The HbO attains a typical 2/2 globin fold, having TyrB10, PheE14, and HisF8 that are conserved amongst the trHb family, but the CD1 position, which is occupied by a phenylalanine residue in other globins, is replaced by a tyrosine residue in the HbO of Mtb (Milani et al., 2003). At variance with the HbN of Mtb, the HbO carries a highly charged 16-residue-long EF loop region which is conserved amongst type II trHbs and may be functionally relevant. The crystal structure of the HbO shows that the protein exists as a compact dodecamer (Figure 17.2.2a) with a covalent bond linking TyrB10 and TyrCD1 within the distal haem pocket (Milani et al., 2003). Structural analysis of the HbO dimer revealed that its dimeric assembly might be weak due to small inter-subunit contacts, indicating that its oligomeric state may change under certain physiological conditions. Resonance Raman spectroscopy of the HbO further suggested that it carries a unique distal pocket which is rigid due to a covalent bond linking the TyrB10 and TyrCD1 that are involved in stabilizing the bound ligand in concert with the TrpG8 residue (Figure 17.2.2b). It has been suggested that these hydrogen bonds may restrict the movement of ligands to and away from the haem and may confer high affinity to this protein (Mukai et al., 2002). The protein matrix of the HbO also displays a tunnel system with long and short branches which are more restricted than the HbN due to the orientation of the G and H helices and the placement of TrpG8 that blocks these tunnels, turning them into small cavities. Unlike HbN, accessibility of gaseous ligands to the distal site of the HbO is favoured by a small apolar AlaE7 which does not obstruct the entry of ligands into the haem cavity (Milani et al.,

2003). After oxygen binding to the haem, TrpG8 is displaced which leads to the opening of these tunnels and may act like a docking station for ligands via an unknown dynamic behaviour of the protein. 

The glbO gene, encoding HbO of Mtb, has been expressed in E. coli as a 14.5 kDa protein and has initially been purified as a dimer (Pathania et al., 2002b). However, it has been found to exist in different oligomeric states, as both a monomer and dimer, depending on the ionic strength of the medium (Mukai et al., 2002), suggesting that it may have weak inter-subunit contacts. When expressed in E. coli, recombinant HbO appears to be strongly associated with the cell membrane and facilitates oxygen uptake in conjunction with terminal oxidases (Liu et al., 2004; Pathania et al., 2002b), leading to speculation that the HbO may be involved in enhancing the cellular respiration of its host by employing a new mechanism. However, purified HbO exhibits a high oxygen-binding affinity and low $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ dissociation rate constants (Table 17.2.1) (Ouellet et al., 2003), which argue against its role in oxygen delivery. However, changes in its oxygen affinity in vivo after interacting with the components of the cell membrane, as have been observed in other cases (Rinaldi et al., 2006), cannot be ruled out.

A low but consistent expression of the glbO gene has been found throughout the growth cycle of aerobically growing culture of M. bovis (Pathania et al., 2002b), but its transcriptional activity increases significantly under different stress conditions, including hypoxia, oxidative, and nitrosative stress (Pawaria et al., 2008), which indicates its functional diversity and involvement under general stress conditions. 

## 17.2.3 Flavohaemoglobin of Mtb

In silico analysis of microbial genome data suggests that more than one flavoHb encoding genes may be present in many mycobacterial species (Gupta et al., 2011); however, Mtb carries only one flavoHb (MtbFHb), which displays several differences within its haem- and flavin-binding sites that are functionally conserved amongst conventional flavoHbs (Figure 17.2.3). Although structural features, required for adopting a classical globin fold and functional residues PheCDI, GlnE7, and HisF8, are preserved in MtbFHb, it carries a hexa-coordinated haem domain. The most prominent change in MtbFHb is the lack of hydrogen-bonding interactions between HisF8, TyrG5, and Glu23 within the proximal site of its globin domain that is conserved in conventional flavoHbs and regulates the redox properties of their haem domain. Thus, the peroxidase-like imidazole character present in a typical flavoHbs (Mukai et al., 2001) is absent in MtbFHb. Similarly, its reductase domain is also modified and devoid of critical residues needed for the binding of cofactors FAD and NADH (Gupta et al., 2011, 2012), but instead it carries another site that resembles a FAD-binding motif of respiratory d-lactate dehydrogenase (d-LDH). These unusual features suggest that the structural and functional properties of MtbFHb are distinct from those of conventional flavo-Hbs. Based on these unusual characteristics, MtbFHb has been classified as a type II class of flavoHbs that is restricted to actinomycetes. 

A lot less is known about MtbFHb at present. Only one report on the primary characteristics of this flavoHb has been published (Gupta et al., 2012), which has shown that it is a monomeric protein of 43 kDa that remains closely associated with the cell membrane and bound with lipids. At variance with conventional flavoHbs, MtbFHb does not have any significant NOD activity, but it confers resistance against oxidative stress when expressed in Escherichia coli or Mycobacterium smegmatis (Gupta et al., 2012) through a novel electron transfer mechanism via consumption of d-lactate that may accumulate during lipid peroxidation under oxidative stress due to the absence of a d-lactate-metabolizing gene in Mtb (Pinchuk et al., 2009). Transcriptional activity of the MtbFHb-encoding gene (Rv0385) increases under oxidative stress and displays a nearly threefold increase 24 h post infection of macrophages (Gupta et al., 2012). This suggests increased biosynthesis of MtbFHb under oxidative stress and during intracellular infection of Mtb. However, further studies are required to understand the precise role of MtbFHb in electron transfer and oxidative stress responses of mycobacteria. 

## 17.2.4 Role of mycobacterial haemoglobins and flavohaemoglobins in stress management of Mtb

Mtb has evolved excellent protective mechanisms to encounter the hostile environment of its intracellular niche. According to conventional wisdom, Mtb is an obligate aerobe, but during its intracellular regime, it experiences severe paucity of oxygen and toxicity of reactive oxygen and nitrogen species that are produced in copious amounts as part of a major host defence mechanism against bacterial infection (Zahrt and Deretic, 2002). The ability of Mtb to persist in this unfavourable environment to cause active disease or undergo dormancy is of great relevance to its overall pathogenesis. Studies conducted since the 2000s have suggested that trHbs and flavoHbs are important proteins for Mtb during its intracellular regime, and their vital role in stress management has emerged recently. Some of the proposed functions of these Hbs are shown in Table 17.2.2. 

## 17.2.4.1 Scavenging of reactive nitrogen and protection from nitrosative stress

The HbN deviates from the orthodox function of haemoglobins (i.e., as an oxygen storehouse and transporter). The proposition that the Mtb HbN actively participates in NO-related chemistry has been substantiated via several rounds of experiments. It has been demonstrated that the HbN of Mtb carries potent NOD activity and protects cellular respiration and survival of heterologous hosts, such as E. coli, from the toxicity of gaseous NO and nitrosative stress (Pathania et al., 2002a). Disruption of the glbN gene in M. bovis, which is identical to the glbN gene of Mtb, causes a drastic reduction in the NO consumption activity of stationary phase cells, resulting in a marked NO-induced inhibition of aerobic respiration (Ouellet et al., 2002). Acquisition of the HbN in an NO-sensitive mutant of an intracellular pathogen, Salmonella enterica Typhimurium, also allowed it to replicate and survive in both na¨ıve and activated macrophages (Pawaria et al., 2007). These complementary studies thus support the involvement of the HbN in relieving the toxicity of NO and nitrosative stress that might be vital for the pathogenicity of Mtb during disease development. 

## 17.2.4.2 Molecular mechanism of HbN function

Compared to horse heart myoglobin, the NOD reaction catalysed by Mtb HbN is ∼15-fold faster. In contrast, the NOD activity of the HbN present in a non-pathogenic mycobacterium,

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/41859cfd6217c85d1e4d8ef837fff509c1aa97429186313959dc6ac6ada9943c.jpg)



Figure 17.2.3 (Continued)


M. smegmatis, is extremely low, despite having more than 80% sequence homology with Mtb HbN (Lama et al., 2006). This suggests that Mtb HbN is more evolved to resist NO toxicity and nitrosative stress. The HbN-mediated $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 } \mathrm { - N O }$ chemistry for NO dioxygenation is modulated by its two unusual structural features: the Pre-A region, which is highly polar and flexible and modulates dynamic motion of the HbN for its optimal function (Lama et al., 2009), and the protein tunnel system that facilitates ligand entry and diffusion towards the protein active site (Oliviera et al., 2012). Deletion of the Pre-A results in drastic reduction in the NO-scavenging function of the HbN, and extended MD simulations have pointed out that the lack of Pre-A changes the dynamic motion of the protein backbone and conformational state of the long tunnel gate that may restrict the passage of NO towards the haem cavity (Lama et al., 2009). A novel dual-path mechanism for ligand access via a protein tunnel has been proposed for the HbN function (Bidon-Chanel et al., 2006, 2007), where entry of $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ via a short tunnel gate and binding to the haem trigger dynamic alterations in the protein backbone that regulate the opening of the long branch via a phenylalanine gate (PheE15), thereby facilitating the access of NO to the $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -bound haem. High NOD activity of the HbN may be regulated by a redox partner that can efficiently donate electrons to keep it in the functional ferrous state for rapid oxygen binding. It has been shown that NADH–ferredoxin reductase or related proteins may interact with the HbN via its CD loop to facilitate electron transfer through a novel mechanism, where the Pre-A motif regulates backbone motion of the protein and movement of the CD loop (Singh et al., 2014). 

## 17.2.4.3 Scavenging of reactive oxygen and protection from oxidative stress

Physiological functions of the HbO and MtbFHb are not very obvious at present; however, primary studies have indicated that both of these haem proteins may be involved in oxidative stress management of $M t b ,$ albeit in different ways. It has been shown that HbO can catalyse $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -mediated oxidation of ABTS (1350 $\mathbf { M } ^ { - 1 } \ s ^ { - 1 } )$ , and its turnover rate for the ABTS peroxidation is threefold higher than that of horse heart Mb (Ouellet et al., 2007). This study suggests that the active site of the HbO may have a peroxidase activity and may be participating in relieving oxidative stress. Such a function is supported by the fact that the HbO of M. leprae, which is more than 80% homologous to the HbO of Mtb, scavenges $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and relieves oxidative stress when expressed in recombinant E. coli (Ascenzi et al., 2008). The expression profiles of the $g l b \mathrm { O }$ gene have been studied by constructing transcriptional fusion with green fluorescent protein and monitoring the promoter activity in vitro and in vivo during macrophage infection (Pawaria et al., 2008). These studies indicate that $g l b \mathrm { O }$ expression lasts for a longer time under low levels of oxidative and nitrosative stresses as well as under hypoxia, suggesting the requirement of the HbO under these conditions. This is supported by the observation that the promoter activity of the $g l b \mathrm { O }$ gene increases significantly during macrophage infection (Joseph et al., 2012; Pawaria et al., 2008), where Mtb gets exposed to cumulative effects of multiple stresses. Additionally, the presence of elevated levels of the HbO imparts increased survival to M. smegmatis in THP-1 macrophages (Joseph et al., 2012). These studies suggested that HbO of Mtb may be involved in regulating the cellular metabolism of tubercle bacilli under general environmental stresses apart from oxidative stress. The molecular mechanism by which HbO can do so remains to be investigated. 


Table 17.2.2 Cellular localization and functional properties of truncated haemoglobin and flavohaemoglobin of M. tuberculosis.


<table><tr><td>Proteins</td><td>Cellular localization</td><td>Post-translational modification</td><td>Proposed functions</td></tr><tr><td>HbN</td><td>Cell membrane and cell wall</td><td>Glycosylation</td><td>NO-detoxification and nitrosative stress relief; host-pathogen interactions</td></tr><tr><td>HbO</td><td>Cell membrane</td><td>?</td><td>Oxygen sequestration and respiration; redox reactions; peroxidase activity</td></tr><tr><td>FHb</td><td>Cell membrane</td><td>?</td><td>Relief from oxidative stress and lipid peroxidation; redox balance</td></tr></table>

Involvement of MtbFHb in alleviation of oxidative stress has also been proposed, based on the observation that it is able to provide protection from reactive oxygen when expressed in E. coli and M. smegmatis (Gupta et al., 2012). MtbFHb does not display any alkyl hydroperoxide reductase activity, which suggests that its protective effect under oxidative stress may not be due to any peroxidase activity. MtbFHb is a peripheral membrane-associated protein of Mtb and remains associated with complex lipids like cyclopropanated and unsaturated fatty acids, and lipid peroxidation is reduced during expression of MtbFHb in E. coli and M. smegmatis (Gupta et al., 2012). This observation has led to the speculation that its functions may be related to protection of membrane integrity during infection, when Mtb gets exposed to high levels of oxidative stress. MtbFHb lacks a d-lactate dehydrogenase gene, thus, under oxidative stress, an accumulation of d-lactate may occur due to increased lipid peroxidation and may be toxic to the cells. MtbFHb catalyses the oxidation of D-lactate into pyruvate in a FAD-dependent manner, similar to the respiratory (NADindependent) D-LDHs. It has been proposed that removal of accumulated d-lactate and lowering of lipid peroxidation in the presence of MtbFHb may change the redox balance of the cell and provide a stabilizing-rigidifying effect on the membrane. Although the precise mechanism of MtbFHb function in electron transfer and the oxidative stress response requires further exploration, increased transcriptional activity of Rv0385 (MtbFHb-encoding gene) during oxidative stress and within the macrophages suggests its critical role in cellular metabolism and pathogenicity of Mtb. This is also supported by recent proteomic and microarray data showing significant induction of Rv0385 in Mtb within the infected dendritic cells in response to oxidative stress (Tailleux et al., 2008). 

## 17.2.4.4 Oxygen metabolism and stress management under hypoxia

Oxygen affinity of the HbN and the HbO of Mtb is exceptionally high (Couture et al., 1999; Mukai et al., 2002); thus, release of bound oxygen from the protein is very slow, which argues against their role in oxygen transfer. However, the high oxygen affinity of these trHbs may ensure the availability of a critical level of oxygen under a hypoxic environment of macrophages, where tubercle bacilli primarily reside, and within the granuloma when the bacilli enter the latent phase. Due to its membrane association properties and its interactions with the components of the respiratory apparatus of the cell during expression in E. coli, the role of the HbO has been implicated in $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ metabolism (Liu et al., 2004; Pathania et al., 2002b). The HbO-mediated increase in $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ uptake rates has been observed in the E. coli $c y d ^ { - }$ (cytochrome $d ^ { - } )$ mutant, but not in the case of the cyo− (cytochrome O) mutant, suggesting an interaction of the HbO with the respiratory components of the cell that may contribute to increased growth, perhaps by more efficient aerobic respiration. The presence of a low level of the HbO throughout the growth cycle of M. bovis (Mukai et al., 2002; Pathania et al., 2002b) suggests its vital requirement for growth and viability. Additionally, owing to its high oxygen affinity, Hbo may function as an $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ collector or reservoir to support respiration of Mtb under hypoxia. 

## 17.2.4.5 Redox homeostasis and stress management

Many haem proteins, including haemoglobins, use the haem– iron complex for sensing gaseous ligands, including oxygen, for signal transduction (Vinogradov and Moens, 2008). Amino acid residues and structural organization of the distal site play a vital role in modulating the interaction of haem–Fe-bound ligands for catalysing specific redox reactions and signal transduction. The distal site of the HbO acquires a unique structural organization due to hydrogen bonding between its aromatic residues (Milani et al., 2003; Mukai et al., 2002). It has been shown that the HbO may be prone to redox reactions due to the presence of oxidizable residues within its active site (Ouellet et al., 2007). It has been observed that the Mtb HbO reacts with hydrogen peroxide and forms an intermediate containing ferric haem iron and oxidizing equivalents, similar to the product of the reaction of peroxidases, indicating that it may have a peroxidase activity and may be involved during oxidative stress. This is supported by the fact that the HbO of M. leprae also displays $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -scavenging activity and similar oxidation–reduction reactions (Ascenzi et al., 2006, 2008). However, to understand the molecular mechanism of function and ascertain the physiological role(s) of the HbO, more studies are required. 

## 17.2.5 Conclusion and future directions

Occurrence of multiple haemoglobins in Mtb, which has a unique structural organization, is interesting as well as intriguing. The presence of two distinct trHbs, with simplified 2/2 globin folds, reflect on their biological functions that are different from oxygen storage and transport. The combination of a closed distal pocket and elongated hydrophobic protein matrix tunnel indicates that the mechanism of ligand entry-exit in trHbs of Mtb is very distinct from classical eukaryotic Hbs, and they may be involved in diverse cellular functions. Similarly, a drastic deviation in structural organization and function of MtbFHb from conventional flavoHbs has been recognized. Although the role of the HbN in NO scavenging and the involvement of HbO in $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ chemistry and redox reactions have been proposed, their precise physiological functions still need to be investigated in their native host, especially due to the fact that these Hbs may undergo some post-translational modifications in Mtb, as has been reported in the case of HbN (Arya et al., 2013). This study has unravelled a new role of HbN in host–pathogen interactions. Not much work has been done on HbO and flavoHb of Mtb and their involvement in redox homeostasis and stress management requires further exploration. In particular, there have not been any studies carried out on the HbO involving its native host, and not much is known about flavoHb of Mtb either. Detailed studies on structure and function of both truncated and flavohaemoglobin of Mtb should provide further insight into their physiological roles and the stress responses of Mtb during pathogenesis of tuberculosis.

# What is it about NO that you don’t understand? The role of heme and HcpR in Porphyromonas gingivalis’s response to nitrate $( \mathbb { N O } _ { 3 } )$ , nitrite $( \mathbb { N } \mathbb { O } _ { 2 } )$ , and nitric oxide (NO)

Janina P. Lewis1–3 and Benjamin R. Belvin1,3 

1Philips Institute for Oral Health Research, Virginia Commonwealth University, Richmond, Virginia, USA 

2Department of Microbiology and Immunology, Virginia Commonwealth University, Richmond, Virginia, USA 

3Department of Biochemistry, Virginia Commonwealth University, Richmond, Virginia, USA 

## 17.3.1 Introduction

For a long time, nitrite has been of interest mostly to toxicologists due to its potential to be converted into nitrosamines shown to be associated with malignancies (Bartsch et al., 1988; Tannenbaum and Correa, 1985). However, a dramatic turn took place when several lines of evidence led to the discovery that our bodies do produce nitrogen oxide from nitrite that is then used for central physiological aspects, such as blood flow, peripheral nerve transmission, brain function, gut mobility, metabolism, and immunity (Moncada et al., 1991). The dramatic turn in our understanding of the role of nitrite is especially relevant to the oral environment. Nitrite is mainly derived from dietary nitrate. Following intake of nitrate-rich foods, nitrite concentrations increase dramatically due to oral nitrate-reducing bacteria such as Lactobacilli through the action of nitrate reductase (NaR) (Hammes, 2012; Lundberg et al., 2004; Xia et al., 2006; Xu and Verstraete, 2001). These bacteria require nitrate to respire and thus thrive in the absence of oxygen. Both nitrate and nitrite are actively taken up and concentrated by salivary glands, and thus the oral cavity has extremely high concentrations of those compounds, reaching up to 1 mM (Lundberg et al., 2004). Considering such fortuitous circumstances, exploring the ways to take advantage of the naturally occurring nitrite seems irresistible. To take advantage of the already high levels of nitrite in the oral cavity, two considerations are in order. First, oral bacteria are required for generation of the bioactive nitrite from dietary nitrate, and thus ways to maintain levels of those beneficial bacteria must be considered. Second, ways to exploit nitrite to reduce the growth of pathogenic bacteria require knowledge of the molecular basis of nitrogen metabolism and nitrosative stress protection in these organisms. Knowledge of such mechanisms would enable design of therapeutics that would specifically target pathogens. 

With regard to microorganisms, most of the studies have concentrated on the role of nitrite as a nutrient for microbial metabolism. Alternatively, other studies have focused on the role of microbial response mechanisms to the toxic nitric oxide generated by host cells in response to infection. However, recent studies took advantage of the toxic effects of nitrite to treat infections. It is well known that nitrite is used in meat preservatives to inhibit growth of pathogens, mainly Clostridium botulinum (Reddy et al., 1983). Also, recent studies show the potential to use acidified nitrite to treat other microbial infections (Carlsson et al., 2003, 2005; Weller et al., 2001; Yoon et al., 2006). Importantly, nitrite’s effect on oral bacteria also has been investigated, and as much as 0.2 mM of nitrite at acidity levels below pH 5 has antimicrobial effect against Fusobacterium nucleatum, Eikenella corrodens, and Porphyromonas gingivalis (Allaker et al., 2001). However, considering that periodontal pockets usually have a basic pH, nitrite concentrations may need to be higher to reduce microbial growth. Thus, alteration of microbial protective mechanisms may be needed to render the bacteria more susceptible to nitrite concentrations normally present in the oral cavity. 

Porphyromonas gingivalis is a Gram-negative anaerobic bacterium that has been demonstrated to play a role in the development and progression of chronic adult periodontitis (Ezzo and Cutler, 2003; Lamont and Jenkinson, 1998). It also is a microorganism that rapidly induces changes in microbial composition even when present in low numbers, and it triggers inflammatory changes (Hajishengallis et al., 2011). P. gingivalis is also found in other parts of the body, such as the cardiovascular system and umbilical cord (Beck et al., 1998; Dorn et al., 2000, 2001). The main habitat of this bacterium, the oral cavity, has been reported to have nitrite concentrations ranging from 10 μM to greater than 1 mM (Lundberg et al., 2004). Furthermore, following food intake, especially foods rich in sucrose, the pH in the oral cavity drops drastically due to acid production from the sugar by oral microbiota such as streptococci and lactobacilli. These conditions are favorable for the chemical generation of nitric oxide from nitrite (Palmerini et al., 2003). In addition, nitric oxide may be generated from nitrite by oral microbiota during metabolism via the respiratory nitrite reductase system, NrfHA (Spiro, 2006). 

Another source of nitrite and nitric oxide in human hosts are eukaryotic cells, which respond to microbial infection by producing nitric oxide and $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -related species (Fang, 1997). This response is part of the innate immune response and is mediated by inducible nitric oxide synthases (iNOS) by both immune cells and nonimmune ones (MacMicking et al., 1995; Nathan, 1995). Indeed, host cells have been shown to increase nitric oxide production when challenged with P. gingivalis (Ghosh et al., 2008; Sun et al., 2010) or even P. gingivalis vesicles (Imayoshi et al., 2011). Also, mice lacking iNOS have a reduced ability to clear P. gingivalis, compared to the C57BL/6 wild-type murine line (Gyurko et al., 2003), thus verifying the antimicrobial role of nitric oxide with respect to the periodontopathogen. However, iNOS also is required to mediate P. gingivalis–induced alveolar bone loss (Gyurko et al., 2005). In agreement with these findings, the severity of periodontal diseases is increased with increased nitric oxide production (Batista et al., 2002), indicating that iNOS plays an important role in disease progression. However, these data also show that oral periodontal pathogens are capable of withstanding high nitric oxide concentrations and triggering disease. Of note, the interactions between nitrite and superoxide or peroxide lead to the generation of peroxynitrite, a highly toxic agent for bacterial cells (Brunelli et al., 1995). 

Despite the high concentrations of nitrite and nitric oxide in environments inhabited by P. gingivalis, the mechanisms of nitrite and nitric oxide detoxification in P. gingivalis are not known. However, knowledge of the genomic sequence of this bacterium has allowed us to predict possible mechanisms involved in such processes (Nelson et al., 2003). Not only the effector molecules involved in the protection of P. gingivalis from nitrosative stress but also regulatory mechanisms allowing for adaptation to the stress have yet to be determined in P. gingivalis. Response to nitrate and nitrite stress has been shown to be regulated by fumarate–nitrate regulator (Fnr)-like proteins in other bacteria (Rodionov et al., 2005; Spiro, 2006). This is a broad-spectrum family of regulators that have been shown to mediate adaptation to a variety of stimuli, including lack of oxygen (Fnr), toxin products, and exposure to nitric oxide (Dnr) (Kiley and Beinert, 1998; Mazoch and Kucera, 2002; Rinaldo et al., 2006). Also, in silico analysis has shown that a novel Fnrlike regulator, designated HcpR, regulates the expression of Hcp and other genes involved in response to nitrite (Cadby et al., 2011; Rodionov et al., 2005). However, so far the roles of HcpR in the regulation of hcp expression and the response to nitrite have not been verified experimentally. 

In our studies, bioinformatics analyses and examinations of P. gingivalis transcriptional response to nitrite and nitric oxide were performed (Lewis et al., 2012). In addition, the protein involved in the adaptation of P. gingivalis to nitrosative stress was identified. Our data show that it is indispensable for adaptation of the bacterium to nitrite and nitric oxide, and this adaptation is mediated by its ability to activate expression of hcp. We thus designate the regulator HcpR. We further characterize the mechanism of the regulator and show that it is hemin dependent. This work gives insight into the molecular mechanisms of protection against nitrosative stress in P. gingivalis. It also is a starting point for further, more comprehensive studies aiming to determine the mechanisms of P. gingivalis adaptation to nitrosative stress. Such knowledge is expected to provide targets for future therapeutic approaches. 

## 17.3.2 Materials and methods

## 17.3.2.1 Bacterial strains and growth conditions

In this study, P. gingivalis strain W83 and its derivatives were used. The P. gingivalis strain W83 is a virulent, encapsulated strain (Singh et al., 2011). The bacteria were maintained in an anaerobic chamber (Coy Manufacturing, USA) filled with atmosphere consisting of 80% $\mathrm { N } _ { 2 } \mathrm { : }$ , 10% $\operatorname { H } _ { 2 } ,$ and 10% $\mathrm { C O } _ { 2 }$ at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Blood agar plates (TSA II, 5% sheep blood; BBL, USA) were used to maintain the bacteria. Growth of P. gingivalis was achieved using brain heart infusion (BHI) broth containing hemin (5 μg/ml; Sigma-Aldrich, USA). Nitrosative exposure studies were carried out in mycoplasma medium (BD, USA). Briefly, bacteria grown on blood agar plates served to inoculate 3 ml of mycoplasma medium (BD) broth. The cultures were grown for 48 h under anaerobic conditions, and then diluted 1:10 with fresh mycoplasma broth. Following 18 h of incubation under anaerobic conditions, these cultures were used for our growth studies. 

Genetic constructs were transformed into One Shot TOP10 Chemically Competent Escherichia coli cells (Invitrogen, Life Technologies, USA). E. coli BL21 (DE3 pLys) (Novagen, EMD4Biosciences, Merck, Germany) cells were used to prepare recombinant HcpR protein. Kanamycin (50 μg/ml) and erythromycin (300 μg/ml) were used for the selection of transformants. P. gingivalis mutants were selected using clindamycin (0.5 μg/ml in BHI agar media). 

## 17.3.2.2 Generation of P. gingivalis mutant strains

A polymerase chain reaction (PCR)-amplified fragment of P. gingivalis W83 genomic DNA containing the hcpR gene (PG1053) was mutagenized by insertion of an ermF–ermAM cassette into the SspI site of the gene. This fragment was then used to replace the wild-type gene copy in the P. gingivalis W83 genome (Fletcher et al., 1995). Transformants were selected on BHI agar plates supplemented with 0.5 μg/ml of clindamycin. A mutant with the expected insertion of the ermF–ermAM cassette in the genome was designated as V2807. The mutation in V2807 was then reverted as described in He et al. (2006). Briefly, the DNA fragment containing an intact copy of the hcpR gene was electroporated into the V2807 strain, and revertants were selected by plating on mycoplasma agar containing 500 nM nitrite. The reversion of the hcpR mutation was then verified by PCR followed by sequencing, as well as the absence of growth on BHI plates supplemented with clindamycin (0.5 μg/ml). The revertant strain was designated as V2835. An oxyR (PG0270) mutation was generated using the strategy described here; the ermF–ermAM cassette was inserted into the HincII site (556 bp of the gene). 

## 17.3.2.3 Quantitative reverse transcription (qRT)-PCR analysis of hcp expression

Primers for hcp and the 16S (used to normalize the amount of input RNA) were used. The sequences were as follows: PG0893F AAAGCTGTCATCGTCCTGCT and PG0893R CGATCAGCGTCCGAATATCT (for hcp), and PG16F AGGCGGAATTCGTGGTGTAG and PG16R TTTGAT-ACCCACGCCTTCGT (for 16S). RNA was isolated from bacteria grown with and without nitrosative stress using the RNeasy mini-kit (Qiagen, USA), according to the manufacturer’s protocol. DNA was removed from the RNA preparations using the DNA-free kit (Ambion, USA). Following complementary DNA (cDNA) preparation with the ArrayScript (Ambion) and 10 ng of total RNA (for 16S rRNA, 0.02 ng of total RNA was used), quantitative PCR (qPCR) was carried out using the SYBR Green qPCR mix (Applied Biosystems, USA). 

## 17.3.2.4 Expression of HcpR in E. coli

For the electrophoretic mobility gel shift assays, the coding sequence of the hcpR gene was PCR amplified from the chromosomal DNA of P. gingivalis W83 and cloned into the pCR2.1 vector. The cloned fragment was then subcloned into the pET30a vector (Novagen, EMD4Biosciences, Merck), which encodes a tail of six histidines at both the $5 ^ { \prime }$ and $3 ^ { \prime }$ ends of the gene. Following transformation of the pET30-hcpR construct into E. coli BL21 (DE3) cells Novagen protein expression was achieved using bacteria grown in lysogeny broth (LB) supplemented with 50 μg/ml of kanamycin until a mid-log phase $( \mathrm { O D } _ { 6 6 0 \mathrm { n m } } =$ 0.6) and then induced with 1 mM of isopropyl beta-D-1- thiogalactopyranoside (IPTG). 

For analysis of the heme-binding capacity of the HcpR, a recombinant form of the protein without the His tag was prepared. Thus, the P. gingivalis hcpR gene (PG1053) was amplified from genomic DNA using sequence-specific primers. The primers m-PET-hcpR-Forward and m-PET-hcpR-Reverse were engineered to add BamHI and XhoI sites on the $5 ^ { \prime }$ end and 

$3 ^ { \prime }$ end, respectively. The hcpR PCR product and pET21-d vector were digested and gel purified. The digested fragments were ligated with T4 DNA ligase (Life Technologies) to create the plasmid pET21-hcpR and transformed in TOP10 E. coli (Life Technologies) for antibiotic screening. A successful clone was identified through sequencing and transformed into BL21 (DE3) $E .$ coli for expression. The clone was engineered to have a tail of six histidines attached to the amino terminus of the gene product. The modified pET21-d sequence places a TEV protease cleavage site between the end of the hcpR gene and the 6× His tag; this cleavage site was used to remove the tag after purification. 

Transformed BL21 (DE3) containing the pET21-hcpR plasmid was grown in self-induction media with shaking (225 rpm) overnight at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ After overnight growth, the cultures were spun down at 12,000 RCF (relative centrifugal force) and washed in phosphate-buffered saline (PBS). 

Recombinant HcpR (rHcpR) was purified under native conditions using the Nickel NTA-Agarose (Qiagen), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. Cell pellets were suspended in binding buffer $( 5 0 \mathrm { m M N a H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { P O } _ { 4 } ,$ 300 mM NaCl, 10 mM imidazole, pH 8), and 100 mg of lysozyme was added to the lysis mixture and incubated for 30 min. After incubation, cells were sonicated to fully lyse cells. Cell lysates were centrifuged to pellet cell debris. Lysates were passed through a column containing Ni– NTA resin (Qiagen) equilibrated with binding buffer. The column was washed with 150 mL of wash buffer (50 mM ${ \mathrm { N a H } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { P O } } _ { 4 }$ , 300 mM NaCl, 20mM imidazole, pH 8) and eluted with elution buffer (50 mM ${ \mathrm { N a H } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { P O } } _ { 4 } ,$ , 300 mM NaCl, 250 mM imidazole, pH 8). Sodium dodecyl sulfate–polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (SDS-PAGE) was used to verify the purity of rHcpR, and the protein concentration was assessed with the Bradford assay (Bio-Rad, USA). 

## 17.3.2.5 Heme-binding assay (TMBZ assay)

HcpR was reconstituted by simply adding heme to the protein mixture. Apo-HcpR and heme-reconstituted HcpR were run on a native polyacrylamide gel. Excess heme was added to bovine serum albumin (BSA) and P. gingivalis OxyR and run as positive and negative controls, respectively. After running, the gel was soaked in tetramethylbenzidine (TMBZ) for 30 min. The TMBZ was removed, and the gel was washed with deionized water. Hydrogen peroxide (3%) was then applied to the gel for staining. The presence of heme was marked by a blue-green stain on the gel. 

## 17.3.2.6 UV-Vis studies

Apo-HcpR was dialyzed into sample buffer (25 mM $\mathrm { N a H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { P O } _ { 4 } ,$ , 100 mMNaCl, 1 mM TCEP) and reconstituted with heme anaerobically. To the heme protein, 10 mM dithionite was added to create the ferric derivative. Spectra of the hemereconstituted HcpR (≈10 μm) was recorded on a Duo UV/Vis Spectrophotometer (Biodrop, UK) using a gastight 1 cm quartz cuvette. 

## 17.3.2.7 Electrophoretic mobility gel shift assay (EMSA)

Recombinant HcpR DNA binding was examined using a 200 bp DNA fragment that was PCR amplified from a genomic region upstream of the hcp. Following labeling with biotin using the Biotin 3′ End DNA Labeling Kit (Thermo Scientific, USA), the DNA was used in an EMSA assay carried out with the LightShift Chemiluminescent EMSA kit (Thermo Scientific), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. Lysates from E. coli containing the pET30-hcpR construct as well as purified rHcpR were used in our assays. 

## 17.3.2.8 Bioinformatics analysis

The basic local alignment search tool (BLAST) was used to find protein sequences similar to HcpR. The sequences were examined and analyzed using the US National Center for Biotechnology Information (NCBI) search tools and the European Molecular Biology Laboratory’s European Bioinformatics Institute (EMBL-EBI) services. The HcpR three-dimensional homology model was constructed using the Phyre2 protein-folding server using a Pseudomonas aeruginosa DNR crystal structure as a template (PDB ID 3dkw). According to the model the active-form HcpR is a chimeric protein. The active-form HcpR is a chimeric protein. The first 130 residues (the hemebinding domain) were modeled on the DNR crystal structure, and the last 98 are modeled on the structure of cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) receptor protein (PDB ID 1run). The potential heme-binding sites were found using AutoDock Vina to scan for potential ligand-binding sites. 

## 17.3.3 Results

## 17.3.3.1 P. gingivalis can grow with high nitrite concentrations

The oral cavity is home to a number of stress-inducing agents, and chief among them is nitrosative stress. Nitrite concentrations in the oral cavity can exceed 1 mM and after meals can reach levels as high as 4 mM. To determine the susceptibility of P. g ing ivalis for nitrite, the wild-type P. g ing ivalis W83 strain was grown in the presence of various concentrations of nitrite (Figure 17.3.1). We found that the concentrations of 1 mM had little effect on the growth of the wild-type strain. At 4 mM nitrite, we found that the bacterium was still able to grow, albeit at a somewhat reduced rate. It was not until an 8 mM concentration of nitrite that the growth of HcpR was fully suppressed. 

## 17.3.3.2 Nitrosative stress protection mechanisms in P. gingivalis

Despite the high concentrations of nitrite and nitric oxide in an environment inhabited by P. gingivalis, the mechanisms of nitrite and nitric oxide detoxification in P. gingivalis are not known. However, knowledge of the genomic sequence of this bacterium allowed us to predict possible mechanisms involved in such processes (Figure 17.3.2) (Nelson et al., 2003). P. gingivalis is an anaerobic bacterium, and, like other anaerobic bacteria, it encodes putative genes that play a role in respiration using alternative electron acceptors such as in nitrite ammonification. Thus, the cytochrome c nitrite reductase system, NrfAH, which converts nitrite into ammonia, was identified in the genome of the bacterium (PG1820–1821) (Meuric et al., 2010). A small protein coding for a putative nitrite reductase was also encoded by PG2213. Furthermore, a hybrid cluster protein (Hcp) encoded by PG0893 was found in P. gingivalis. This redox enzyme has been shown in anaerobic bacteria (Desulfovibrio spp.) and facultative anaerobic bacteria (Escherichia coli, Salmonella typhimurium, Acidothiobacillus ferrooxidans, Rhodobacter capsulatus, and Shewanella oneidensis) (reviewed in Rodionov et al., 2005). Hcp has been shown to be induced by nitrite, indicating that it has a role in nitrogen metabolism. The proposed role of Hcp is to reduce hydroxylamine generated from nitrite or nitric oxide into ammonia and water (Cabello et al., 2004; Rodionov et al., 2005; Wolfe et al., 2002). As such, it plays a significant role in the detoxification of toxic nitrogen compounds. Indeed, recent studies have shown that Hcp plays a role in the protection of P. gingivalis from exposure to nitric oxide (Boutrin et al., 2012). However, the mechanisms leading to the generation of hydroxylamine and the molecular mechanism of P. gingivalis Hcp remain to be determined. Also, the role of nitrite in metabolism of the bacterium coding for the respiratory ammonification mechanism is yet to be determined (Nelson et al., 2003). 


Figure 17.3.1 Growth of P. gingivalis under nitrosative stress. Wild-type P. gingivalis W83 was grown in the presence and absence of nitrite to the mid-logarithmic phase in mycoplasma broth without hemin. Bacterial cultures were passaged twice in the mycoplasma broth before being diluted to an OD of 0.1. To individual cultures, 1 mM, 4 mM, or 8 mM final concentration of nitrite was added. Cultures without nitrite served as controls. Means from three experiments performed on separate days are shown.

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/241cd527797e3e490acfa1852c94dab3750dfdb7c64acc35b912f52b26ecf047.jpg)



Figure 17.3.2 Putative nitrosative stress protection mechanisms in P. gingivalis. Predicted nitrosative stress protection mechanisms and their upregulation in bacteria grown under nitrite (green) and nitric oxide (red) exposure.


We then compared the transcriptional profile of P. gingivalis genes using RNA derived from BHI with and without nitrite (Lewis et al., 2012), as well as with and without the GSNO (nitric oxide–producing compound). Our data show that nitrite exposure resulted in upregulation of hcp (PG0893), PG0616 coding for thioredoxin, PG1227 encoding a LysR-type regulator, PG0776 and PG0900 coding for oxidoreductases, PG1318 encoding an extracytoplasmic function (ECF) sigma protein, PG1129 encoding ribonucleotide reductase, and PG1321 encoding a formate–tetrahydrofolate ligase. Several genes coding for hypothetical putative proteins were also upregulated (Figure 17.3.2). Nitric oxide–induced expression of upregulated genes included PG1551 encoding the HmuY protein, which has been shown to mediate hemin uptake in P. gingivalis (Lewis et al., 2006; Olczak et al., 2005; Smalley et al., 2007), and PG2213 encoding a putative nitrite reductase. Also upregulated were oxidative stress response mechanisms such as PG0195 coding for rubrerythrin, PG0275 and PG0616 encoding putative thioredoxins, and PG0090 coding for Dps (Figure 17.3.2). In addition, genes coding for regulators, such as PG1203, PG1205, PG0173, PG1431-2, PG0162, PG0121, PG0465, and PG1240, were upregulated. Finally, a large portion of the upregulated genes included ones coding for hypothetical proteins, indicating that novel aspects may be detected when considering nitric oxide protection mechanisms in P. g ing ivalis. 

## 17.3.3.3 Regulation of nitrosative stress protection in P. gingivalis

Having confirmed the induction of the hcp gene by nitrosative stress, we then searched for the molecular mechanisms of regulation of gene expression. We examined several candidates predicted to play a role in nitrosative stress protection (Figure 17.3.2), including the OxyR homolog encoded by PG0270 (as it was shown to play a role in hcp regulation in E. coli) (Seth et al., 2012) and the Fnr-like regulator encoded by PG1053. Mutation of PG0270 had no effect on growth of P. gingivalis in the presence of nitrite (results not shown). We then examined the role of the protein encoded by PG1053, initially predicted to play a role in oxygen tolerance in P. gingivalis. Growth of the mutant was comparable to that of the parental strain under both anaerobic and aerobic (6% oxygen) conditions (results not shown). However, it is noteworthy that a significant reduction in growth of the V2807 strain (PG1053 – insertional mutant) was observed in bacteria grown under nitrosative stress when compared to the parental W83 strain (Figure 17.3.3). Although the V2807 strain grew in the presence of 40 mM nitrate (Figure 17.3.3b), significant growth reduction was observed when 0.2 mM nitrite was used $( P < 0 . 0 5$ , W83 vs. V2807 under 0.2 mM nitrite, Student’s t-test). Furthermore, V2807 did not grow in the presence of 1 mM nitrite $( \ast p < 0 . 0 0 2$ , W83 vs. V2807 under 1 mM nitrite, Student’s t-test) (Figure 17.3.3b). These results were further verified using an hcpR mutant revertant strain, V2835. This strain grew similarly to the parental strain in media supplemented with nitrite, thus further demonstrating that the growth inhibition in V2807 was due to mutation in the hcpR gene. 

We also tested the sensitivity of V2807 to exposure to nitric oxide (using the nitric oxide generator, GSNO). As shown in Figure 17.3.3c,d, the V2807 strain is more susceptible not only to nitrite but also to GSNO $( \ast p < 0 . 0 0 1$ , W83 vs. V2807 under 30 nM GSNO, Student’s t-test) when compared to the parental W83 strain (Lewis et al., 2012). These data further verify the role of HcpR in nitrosative stress protection in P. gingivalis.

## 17.3.3.4 PG1053 (hcpR) regulates hcp expression

The major gene upregulated by both nitrite and nitric oxide is hcp (Boutrin et al., 2012; Lewis et al., 2012). qRT-PCR analysis showed that hcp was drastically upregulated by nitrite and GSNO exposure; however, no such upregulation was detected in the HcpR-deficient V2807 strain (Figure 17.3.4a). The induction of expression of hcp was also observed in the HcpR revertant strain, V2835, thus verifying that the reduced expression of hcp in V2807 is indeed due to the mutation in the hcpR gene. Finally, we also tested the effect of the OxyR mutation using strain V2798 on expression of hcp; again, nitrite exposure resulted in significant overexpression of the gene, indicating that OxyR plays no role in regulation of hcp in P. gingivalis. These results indicate that HcpR upregulates hcp expression upon exposure to nitrite. To verify the direct involvement of HcpR in regulating hcp expression, we examined binding of the protein to the promoter region of the gene. Analysis of the genomic locus of hcp has shown that the gene is flanked by intergenic sequences (IGSs) and thus is not part of an operon (Lewis et al., 2012). This was further verified by determination of hcp’s transcriptional start site using high-throughput sequencing of the P. gingivalis transcriptome (Lewis et al., 2012). We identified the hcp’s transcriptional start site to be located 29 bp upstream of the translational start site. Furthermore, upstream of the transcriptional start site, we found a direct-repeat sequence (TGTCGCnnnnGCGACA) (Lewis et al., 2012). As reported previously, to verify binding of HcpR to the predicted DNA sequence, we performed an electrophoretic mobility shift assay with both E. coli cell lysates as well as purified rHcpR (Lewis et al., 2012). As shown in Figure 17.3.4b, reduced migration of a DNA fragment containing the HcpR binding site was observed in E. coli cell lysates expressing HcpR (lane 2). No reduced mobility was observed in the lane containing E. coli lysate grown in the absence of IPTG (Figure 17.3.4b, lane 3). Furthermore, the purified rHcpR bound the hcp promoter DNA. The binding was only observed when rHcpR reconstituted with hemin was used (lanes 6–9). Also, the binding was reduced by unlabeled hcp promoter DNA (lanes 7 and 8), indicating that the binding is specific. These results indicate that HcpR binds to the hcp promoter sequence and directly regulates hcp expression.

## 17.3.3.5 Sequence and structure analysis of P. gingivalis HcpR

The 684 bp hcpR gene codes for a 228 amino acid protein that is calculated to be 24 kDa in size. InterProScan (EBI) was utilized to search the protein sequence for conserved motifs and different protein signatures. The protein is composed of three different domains: residues 1–120 comprise an N-terminal ligandbinding domain, which shares similarities to the ligand-binding domains of the cAMP receptor protein (Crp) family of proteins; residues 120–179 form a helical domain, which we designate as a dimerization helix; and residues 180–228 form a C-terminal helix-turn-helix DNA-binding motif found in the Crp family of regulators. Furthermore, HcpR was predicted to be a member of the Fnr–Crp superfamily of transcriptional regulators (Figure 17.3.5d). 

A three-dimensional model of the full-length HcpR protein was constructed using the Phyre2 protein-folding server and the crystal structure of Dnr (PDB ID 3dkw) as a template (Figure 17.3.5a,c). Dnr is a transcriptional regulator in Pseudomonas aeruginosa and shares a sequence similarity with HcpR. Every functional member of the Fnr–Crp superfamily forms homodimers; as such, a dimeric homology model of HcpR was constructed (Figure 17.3.5b). The large helical domain that composes that dimerization helix contains a number of hydrophobic residues on one surface of its face. This creates a large region of hydrophobic interactions between the two subunits, stabilizing the homodimer oligomer. 

Of the entire protein structure, the most conserved region of the protein is the C-terminal helix-turn-helix DNA-binding motif. Between HcpR and Dnr, there is an 80% sequence similarity between the two domains (Figure 17.3.5c). This is an expected result, as the helix-turn-helix motif is well conserved across many branches of phylogeny.

Figure 17.3.5 Bioinformatics analysis of HcpR. (a) Structural homology model and domain organization of HcpR developed using the Phyre2 protein-folding server. The Pseudomonas aeruginosa DNR was used as the template to build the HcpR model. The monomer consists of three domains: an N-terminal heme-binding domain, a dimerization helix, and a C-terminal helix-turn-helix DNA-binding domain. (b) Structural homology model of the HcpR dimer. (c) Sequence alignment and comparison of the Porphyromonas gingivalis HcpR (HcpR) gene and the P. aeruginosa DNR gene (DNR). A star (∗) denotes a position has a single fully conserved residue. Among the many redox-sensing regulators, the only cofactors identified are iron–sulfur clusters (as seen in Fnr, an oxygen sensor in E. coli) and heme (as seen in Dnr). 

## 17.3.3.6 Heme-binding properties of HcpR

To characterize the heme-binding properties of HcpR, we took advantage of the pseudo-peroxidase activity of heme. Recombinant HcpR was expressed and purified in E. coli, and the 6× His tag was removed from the protein to ensure that it does not interfere with heme binding. After reconstitution with heme, HcpR was run on a native polyacrylamide gel for separation and subsequently stained for peroxidase activity. The stained gel reveals what appears to be an association between HcpR and heme under native conditions (Figure 17.3.6a). The spectral properties of heme–HcpR were also characterized (Figure 17.3.6b). Under anaerobic conditions, dithionite was added to create the ferric derivative of heme. Under these circumstances, a very large Soret peak appears in the 410–420 nm region of the spectrum in the HcpR–heme reconstituted sample. This Soret peak is a characteristic of heme, is found in the 400 nm region of the spectrum, and is seen in heme-binding protein. It is not uncommon to see a shift in the Soret peak when heme is bound to the protein. In the context of HcpR, there is a very noticeable shift and change of shape in the Soret peak when heme binds. 

## 17.3.4 Discussion

The nitrite concentrations in the oral cavity are the highest in the human body; they are usually 1 mM and can reach levels as high as 4 mM after consumption of a meal rich in nitrates such as spinach, beetroot, lettuce, or fruits (Lundberg et al., 2004). In addition to nitrite, the oral cavity also contains nitric oxide derived from nitrite or released by host cells as part of its innate immune response. Therefore, bacteria inhabiting such environments must be able to protect themselves against such high concentrations of nitrite and nitric oxide. Our studies show that the oral pathogen, P. gingivalis, is capable of withstanding concentrations of nitrite as high as 4 mM and thus must have mechanisms that will allow it to detoxify the nitrosative stress compounds. Identification of such protective mechanisms and learning the molecular mechanisms of their action could in turn be exploited for the development of therapeutic approaches aimed at inhibiting action of those mechanisms and consequently reducing the ability of P. gingivalis to survive the high concentrations of nitrosative stress encountered in the oral cavity.

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/0fa0024a6c707857b3d490b53c0f67d25b67f7de0bf3d3618a58561f078d8c57.jpg)



Figure 17.3.6 Heme-binding properties of HcpR. (a) Pseudo-peroxidase activity of heme-bound HcpR as detected by a TMBZ (tetramethylbenzidine) assay staining on a native gel. The negative control (P. gingivalis OxyR; lane 1), HcpR (lane 2), and positive control (BSA) were all reconstituted with heme for 2 h. All samples were run on a native polyacrylamide gel. Gels were soaked with TMBZ for 30 min. After soaking, the gels were washed and stained with 3% hydrogen peroxide. (b) Spectra of 1 mg/mL of purified recombinant HcpR (20.8 μM) (thin line) and 1 mg/mL of HcpR reconstituted with 1 μg of heme. The spectrum of Fe2+ derivative of heme is also shown. The addition of heme yields a species with a very large Soret band at 420 nm, which is typical of a heme-binding protein.


Analysis of the genomic sequence of the P. gingivalis W83 genome revealed several candidates that possibly play a role in nitrosative stress protection. These data were further reinforced using microarray analysis data derived from comparison of gene expression in bacteria grown with and without nitrosative stress (Lewis et al., 2012). The most upregulated gene was hcp, indicating that the gene product is indispensable for growth of the bacterium under nitrosative stress. Such data are in agreement with other reports demonstrating that the hcp gene product is required for P. gingivalis’s growth with nitric oxide (Boutrin et al., 2012). The upregulation of hcp is also in agreement with other reports (Haveman et al., 2004; Rodionov et al., 2005; Seth et al., 2012; van den Berg et al., 2000) demonstrating upregulation of hcp in a variety of anaerobic bacteria, including D. vulgaris, in which hcp2 was shown to be upregulated 254-fold upon exposure to nitrite (Haveman et al., 2004), and anaerobically grown E. coli that also upregulated hcp when exposed to nitrite (Seth et al., 2012). 

Furthermore, our microarray analysis implicated hemin as an important player in defense against nitrosative stress. The major hemin uptake–encoding protein hmuY (PG1551) (Lewis et al., 2006; Smalley et al., 2011) was significantly upregulated upon exposure to nitric oxide–generating agent GSNO (Figure 17.3.2). Hemin, by itself, has been shown to play an antioxidative role in P. gingivalis in the form of a mu-oxo-dimer; a dimer of iron protoporphyrin IX bridged by oxygen, on the surface of microbial cells (Smalley et al., 2000). Furthermore, internalized hemin would also have a role in nitrosative stress protection as it is required as a cofactor for a variety of hemoproteins that include the nitrite reductase system NrfHA (Figure 17.3.2). This is a multiheme protein system composed of a periplasmic pentaheme cytochrome c nitrite reductase (NrfA) that can reduce nitrite, nitric oxide, or hydroxylamine to ammonia; and membrane-anchored NrfH, which is a tetraheme cytochrome c menaquinol dehydrogenase (Figure 17.3.2) (Meuric et al., 2010). Other reports show that this system plays a crucial role in defense against nitrite, nitric oxide, and hydroxylamine stress in Wolinella succinogenes (Kern et al., 2011; Meuric et al., 2010), and we postulate that it also plays a similar role in P. gingivalis. Since NrfHA is active when complexed with several molecules of heme, it would not be able to mature to active form under hemin-depleted conditions. Thus, the observed upregulation of the hemin uptake–encoding genes may be a way to ensure provision of hemin that will allow the Nrf system to remain functional. 

Both bioinformatics and functional analysis have implicated rubrerythrin to play a role in the nitrosative stress response (Mydel et al., 2006; Sztukowska et al., 2002). Our microarray analysis also shows that rbr expression is elevated in the presence of nitric oxide, and it is thus consistent with the protective role of rubrerythrin against reactive nitrogen species (Mydel et al., 2006; Sztukowska et al., 2002). Finally, upon exposure of P. gingivalis to GSNO, genes coding for reactive oxygen detoxification proteins, such as rbr and dps, were upregulated (Lewis et al., 2012; Sztukowska et al., 2002; Ueshima et al., 2003). 

The upregulation of hcp as quantified using qRT-PCR was shown to be greater than 150-fold higher upon exposure to nitrosative stress (Figure 17.3.3). This is also in agreement with upregulation of the gene in the presence of nitrosative stress in other bacteria, as mentioned in this chapter. However, despite the significant upregulation of hcp in the variety of bacteria in response to nitrite, thus indicating that the gene product plays a major role in nitrosative stress protection, the mechanisms of the gene regulation remains fairly unknown. Two repressors, NsrR and NorR, were implicated in regulating hcp expression in facultative anaerobic bacteria, such as enterobacteria, β- and α-proteobacteria (Filenko et al., 2007), and Vibrionales, respectively (Rodionov et al., 2005). It is noteworthy that the regulation of hcp in E. coli was also reported to be mediated by OxyR (Seth et al., 2012). 

Our bioinformatics analysis predicted several candidates that possibly play a role in the response of P. gingivalis to nitrosative stress. That included the OxyR-like regulator (PG0270) and an Fnr-like protein (PG1053) (Figure 17.3.2). To assess their contributions to nitrosative stress protection, we generated mutants deficient in two of the candidates, OxyR (V2835) and the Fnrlike regulator (V2807). Our data show that the Fnr-like regulator, and not OxyR, plays a role in nitrosative stress protection. This is clear evidence that the mechanisms of nitrosative stress protection in anaerobic bacteria belonging to the Bacteroidaceae family differ from those encountered in gamma-proteobacteria, and thus novel findings are expected from pursuing investigation of the mechanisms involved. 

Data included in this section show that survival of the mutant V2807 in the presence of nitrite and GSNO is reduced relative to the parental strain. As host cells are the source of nitric oxide, we also hypothesized that survival of the V2807 strain will be compromised when in the presence of host cells. P. gingivalis can adhere, invade, survive, and multiply in a variety of host cells (Dorn et al., 2000; Yilmaz et al., 2006), and thus it is expected that it would be exposed to nitrosative stress released as part of the immune defense against invading pathogens. Indeed, in our work reported by Lewis et al. (2012), the V2807 strain cannot survive with host cells, thus further underscoring the biological relevance of the HcpR regulator and warranting more detailed molecular analysis. 

Consistent with its biological role in nitrosative stress protection, we demonstrate here that HcpR activates hcp expression in the anaerobic bacterium P. gingivalis (hence, it is designated HcpR). However, we also observe that the P. gingivalis OxyR plays no role in regulation of the gene. Such results would be in agreement with a previous report of Fnr playing a role in regulation of hcp in anaerobically grown E. coli (Filenko et al., 2007) but would contradict the findings that OxyR regulates hcp expression (Seth et al., 2012). Our data further support the report implicating HcpR-like regulators in bacterial sensitivity to nitrosative stress (Cadby et al., 2011). Given the biological significance as well as the presence of HcpR-encoding genes in other bacteria belonging to the Bacteroidetes phylum, such as Prevotella intermedia 17, Bacteroides fragilis, and Bacteroides thetaiotamicron, it is imperative that the mode of action of the protein be elucidated. Of note is the fact that this protein is also encoded on the genome of Thermotoga maritima, an ancient anaerobic Gram-negative bacterium (Huber et al., 1996), indicating that hcpR may have been derived from Archaea (Nelson et al., 1999). 

Our studies show that HcpR requires heme for binding to DNA. This is analogous to binding of Dnr to DNA where hemin is also required to convert the regulator into its active DNAbinding form (Castiglione et al., 2009). However, the molecular mechanism of Dnr and the role of heme still remain unclear, and thus elucidation of the mechanism of HcpR may shed light on the action of both regulators. Among the many redox sensors, the only identified cofactors are iron–sulfur clusters and heme. The iron–sulfur proteins have been well characterized in many different bacterial species; however, there are very few bacterial heme sensor proteins that have been studied. A look at the Nterminal ligand-binding domain of HcpR reveals that it is lacking the typically conserved cysteine residues that are necessary to form the iron–sulfur domain (in all, there is only one cysteine residue in HcpR). Thus, we hypothesize that HcpR utilizes the heme cofactor. Furthermore, there are a number of hydrophobic residues that line the inside of the ligand-binding pocket. The protoporphyrin IX ring of heme B is very hydrophobic, and many other heme-binding proteins typically bind their cofactor through extensive hydrophobic interactions between the protein side chains and the ring. 

Binding of heme to the protein is a necessary step in the full activation of HcpR and is required for proper functioning of the protein. The exact mechanism by which HcpR binds heme is not known; however, it is possible that it binds free intracellular heme. Our studies have shown that recombinant HcpR is capable of binding heme, and the spectral properties indicate that it binds in a specific manner. Furthermore, heme binding is required for HcpR to bind to DNA. However, the reconstitution of purified protein utilizes levels of heme that are much higher than physiological levels of P. gingivalis. Furthermore, from our studies it appears that HcpR does not bind heme efficiently under aerobic conditions. Therefore, it is possible that HcpR utilizes a lyase or chaperone to coordinate heme binding in vivo. As for the in vitro system, this lack of help is simply overcome by adding an excess of heme. 

We have constructed an active model of HcpR (Figure 17.3.7). We postulate that the heme-binding pocket is located in the Nterminal domain. The binding pocket encompasses a number of hydrophobic residues that interact with the largely hydrophobic protoporphryin IX ring of heme. The presence of heme allows HcpR to bind nitric oxide. In principle, heme-based sensor proteins act as bistable switch molecules. The switching from one state to another is initiated by the binding of the gaseous XO ligand to, or dissociation from, the heme. We hypothesize that nitric oxide binding to heme induces a conformational change to the ON state. The switch in that state allows HcpR to modulate gene expression by recruitment of RNA polymerase.

Figure 17.3.7 Model of a chimeric HcpR protein in the ON state. Protein bound to DNA (designated in green) is shown. The chimera is composed of the N-terminal domain of the HcpR homology model (the first 130 residues). The DNA-binding domain (last 98 residues) is modeled after the cAMP receptor protein (PDB ID 1RUN). Each monomer is depicted in red and blue, respectively. Heme is shown in yellow.
