# Di-iron RICs: players in nitrosative-oxidative stress defences

L´ıgia S. Nobre and L´ıgia M. Saraiva 

Instituto de Tecnologia Qu´ımica e Biologica Ant ´ onio Xavier, Universidade Nova de Lisboa, Oeiras, Portugal ´ 

## 17.4.1 Introduction

In eukaryotes, cells of the innate immune system produce nitric oxide (NO) enzymatically by triggering the expression of the inducible NO synthase (iNOS) as a defence mechanism against pathogens (Bogdan, 2001; Kidd, 2011). The microbial cytoxicity of NO is intimately associated with iron and oxygen, through the generation of reactive nitrogen and oxygen species (RNS and ROS (see Section 10), respectively). The main biological targets of RNS are (i) iron centres, leading to the formation of iron nitrosyl complexes (haem, iron–sulphur, and other iron proteins); (ii) thiolates, with the formation of S-nitrosothiols; and (iii) radical species. These reactions lead to inhibition of several enzymes that have supporting life roles, thus having multiple deleterious biological effects (Pacher et al., 2007). Therefore, microbial cells have mechanisms to sense and respond to these aggressions by activating transcription regulators and expressing genes encoding several proteins and enzymes for detoxification and repair, which altogether protect against nitrosative and oxidative stresses (Green et al., 2014; Spiro et al., 2012). 

Particularly prone to ROS and RNS-induced damage are the iron–sulphur (FeS)-containing proteins, which participate in general cellular processes, such as amino acid synthesis, sensing, gene regulation, and cellular respiration (Johnson et al., 2005). Iron–sulphur clusters are by their nature rapidly destroyed by ROS and RNS. Disintegration of the centres is observed, in particular, for solvent-exposed clusters, such as the [4Fe–4S]2+/1+ family of dehydratases, that include key enzymes of the tricarboxylic acid cycle (e.g., aconitase and fumarase). Reaction of FeS centres with NO generates stable dinitrosyl–iron complexes (DNIC) that may remain bound to the protein, whereas other RNS and ROS can cause oxidation of the clusters to unstable forms that result in partial or total disintegration of the centres (Drapier, 1997; Imlay, 2006). 

Several microbial systems have been shown to be involved in the de novo assembly of FeS clusters, such as the ISC (iron– sulphur cluster) and the SUF (sulphur assimilation) machineries (Roche et al., 2013). On the contrary, the systems in charge of recovering partially dismantled FeS clusters remain largely unknown. So far, microbial RICs (repairs of iron centres) represent one of the few examples of a family of proteins proposed to be involved in the repair of damaged FeS clusters, and current knowledge of them is described in the remainder of this chapter. 

## 17.4.2 Identification of RIC in Escherichia coli

The identification of a protein involved in protection of FeScontaining enzymes was first carried out in E. coli during the analysis of the role of the ytfE encoded protein (Justino et al., 2007). These studies were triggered by several transcriptomic experiments that consistently revealed the high induction of the ytfE gene in cells exposed to nitrosative stress conditions (Constantinidou et al., 2006; Justino et al., 2005; Mukhopadhyay et al., 2004). Purification and characterization of E. coli YtfE showed that it is a protein with a di-iron centre of the histidine–carboxylate family. Mutation of the ytfE gene yields a strain with higher susceptibility to NO and decreased activity of several FeS enzymes (Justino et al., 2006, 2007). Later studies of orthologues from Staphylococcus aureus and Neisseria gonorrhoeae strengthened the notion that these proteins protect and repair FeS clusters damaged by exposure to NO and hydrogen peroxide, which led to the name of Repair of Iron Centres for this family of proteins (Overton et al., 2008). 

## 17.4.3 Biochemical and spectroscopic properties of RICs

Recombinant RIC from E. coli and S. aureus is a ∼25 kDa protein that forms homodimeric complexes and contains approximately two iron atoms per monomer. For the dimeric Ralstonia eutropha RIC homologue, reduction with dithiothreitol caused the disappearance of the dimer, suggesting that it is related to the formation of disulphide bridges (Strube et al., 2007). As with the majority of bacterial RICs, R. eutropha protein contains at the N-terminal domain a highly conserved motif DfCCgG, with two cysteinyl residues that are likely candidates to be involved in disulphide bridging. Nevertheless, the functional relevance of the RIC dimer remains to be established as studies with N. gonorrhoeae RIC, which lacks the cysteine residues containing the N-terminal region, show that it retains the ability to promote repair of damaged FeS clusters (Overton et al., 2008). 

In RICs, the two iron atoms have been found to form a non-haem binuclear iron centre by a comprehensive biochemical characterization that included UV-visible, electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR), resonance Raman, Mossbauer, and¨ extended X-ray absorption fine structure (EXAFS) spectroscopies (Justino et al., 2006; Nobre et al., 2014; Todorovic et al., 2008). 

UV-visible spectra of E. coli and S. aureus RICs exhibit a broad band at 360 nm in the oxidized form that bleaches upon reduction. EPR spectra, optimally detected at circa 15 K, present all g-values at ∼2 (E. coli RIC: g-values of 1.96, 1.92, and 1.88) (Justino et al., 2006; Overton et al., 2008), which are typical of di-iron centres in the mixed-valence $\mathrm { F e } ^ { 2 + } { - \mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + } }$ state. The Mossbauer spectrum of the¨ E. coli 57Fe-RIC also shows that the di-iron centre is in a mixture of two forms, the oxidized $( \mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + } -$ $\mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + } )$ and the mixed-valence one $( \mathrm { F e } ^ { 2 + } \mathrm { - } \mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + } )$ . Reduction potentials of the di-iron centre of $\mathrm { E } = + 2 6 0$ and +110 mV have been determined for the E. coli RIC by means of an EPR-monitored redox titration (Justino et al., 2006; Nobre et al., 2014). Resonance Raman spectrum of the oxidized E. coli RIC displays a weak absorption band at 350 nm, resulting from a ligand-toiron charge transfer transition, and a band at 490 cm−1 indicative of a μ-oxo bridged di-iron centre. In the oxidized enzyme, the presence of a μ-oxo group bridging two $\mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + }$ atoms has been revealed by isotopic exchange with 18O-labeled water as it induced a downshift of the band at $4 9 0 ~ \mathrm { c m ^ { - 1 } }$ to $4 7 8 \ \mathrm { c m ^ { - 1 } }$ . However, the μ-oxo bridge seems to be absent in the reduced form (Todorovic et al., 2008). The Mossbauer spectrum of the¨ E. coli 57Fe-RIC showed that the two iron ions are antiferromagnetically coupled, with parameters that are also consistent with the presence of a μ-oxo bridge involving the diferric centre. Furthermore, Mossbauer studies have indicated that the RIC¨ iron ions are more labile when the centre is in the mixed-valence redox state as compared with the (μ-oxo) diferric one (Nobre et al., 2014). The EXAFS spectrum of E. coli RIC is dominated by a single oscillation frequency, implying the presence of light ligands, such as O and N. Based on the EXAFS analysis for the oxidized RIC, a model with an average iron coordination by two histidine residues, two oxygen ligands at an average distance of ${ \sim } 2 \mathrm { \ A } .$ , and an iron–iron distance of ∼3.6 A has been proposed. ˚ The EXAFS data also indicate that the Fe–Fe distance does not vary significantly between the resting ferric and fully reduced (diferrous) state. Moreover, the diferrous state is considered to have on average one more ligand per iron atom, as inferred by the presence of an extra ligand in the reduced protein. EXAFS of E. coli RIC incubated with NO showed the formation of a single {FeNO} species, and that binding of NO does not induce alteration in either metal–metal distance or type and number of iron ligands (Todorovic et al., 2008). 

Amino acid sequence analysis of E. coli RIC indicates that it contains a HHE–hemerythrin cation binding motif similar to that present in hemerythrin-like proteins. These proteins are non-heme di-iron proteins involved in oxygen transport and host antibacterial defence (Bailly et al., 2008). 

E. coli RIC homologues are widely distributed in bacterial phyla, including in proteobacteria, bacteroidetes, firmicutes, actinobacteria, acidobacteria, and in the eukaryotic pathogens Trichomonas vaginalis and Cryptococcus neoformans. Alignment of the amino acid sequences of more than 100 RIC homologues shows a high conservation of several histidine and carboxylate residues, including H84, H105, H129, E133, H160, and H204 (numbering refers to residues in E. coli RIC). These residues are located in conserved α-helix regions of the secondary structure that is predicted to have a four-helix-bundle fold, as is generally observed for di-iron proteins (Kurtz, 1997). Therefore, these residues have been speculated to be involved in the ligand sphere of the di-iron centre. Recently, site-directed mutagenesis studies of E. coli RIC showed that H84, H129, H160, H204, E133, and E208 are indeed the ligands of the centre, with E133 and E208 being crucial for its formation (Nobre et al., 2015). 

Altogether, the data so far available indicate that RICs have all structural features typical of di-iron proteins, with the two iron atoms, separated by ${ \leq } 4 \ \mathrm { \AA } ,$ , bound by terminal carboxylate and/or histidine ligands, and bridged by an oxo, hydroxo, or aqua ligand, as depicted in a structural predicted model for the RIC centre (Figure 17.4.1). Also in these proteins, the binuclear iron centre is considered the active centre, as the E. coli apo-RIC is unable to promote repair of oxidatively damaged FeS clusters (Justino et al., 2007). 

## 17.4.4 RIC is a protective protein that donates iron to FeS proteins

In vitro studies showed that deletion of the ric gene generates an E. coli strain more sensitive to nitrosative and oxidative stresses, and with decreased enzymatic activity of FeS proteins, such as aconitase and fumarase (Justino et al., 2005, 2007; Vine et al., 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/1b88cf5010cbdd61dbec6dbba17cd48f974cf39bb69c808ba2df079e0034a929.jpg)



Figure 17.4.1 Putative model for RIC di-iron centre. In as-isolated RIC, the di-iron centre is in two main oxidation states: diferric $( \mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + } { - } \mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + } )$ and mixed valence $( \mathrm { F e } ^ { 2 + } \mathrm { - } \mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + } )$ . The two iron atoms (orange circles) are coordinated by four histidine residues and the two μ-carboxo bridges are provided by glutamate residues. A μ-oxo-bridge is also predicted to exist in the as-isolated RIC, which upon reduction is no longer detected. Red, oxygen atoms; blue, nitrogen atoms.


2010). It has also been observed that the intracellular formation of oxidatively damaged FeS species (i.e., [3Fe-4S]+ centres and DNIC) occurs at higher rates and to a larger extent in cells lacking the ric gene. While loss of the enzymatic activities is more pronounced in the E. coli ric mutant strain and no repair is detected, addition of RIC promotes the regaining of activity to levels similar to the wild type (Justino et al., 2007). Likewise, supplementation with purified S. aureus RIC of cell lysates of E. coli Δric expressing fumarase A and exposed to hydrogen peroxide or NO also restores the fumarase activity to levels similar to those observed before damage (Overton et al., 2008). It has therefore been concluded that RICs promote the repair of FeScontaining proteins that are exposed to oxidative or nitrosative stresses (Justino et al., 2007). More recently, insight into the E. coli RIC repair-associated mechanism started to be gained by experiments proving the RIC’s ability to deliver iron for the ab initio formation of an iron–sulphur cluster (Nobre et al., 2014). In these assays, E. coli RIC was shown to donate iron to the formation of a FeS centre into the apo-form of the spinach ferredoxin and into E. coli IscU, when in the presence of the sulphidedonating system IscS and L-cysteine. 

Although it is well established that cysteine desulphurases, such as IscS, are the enzymes responsible for providing sulphur for the formation of the clusters, the iron source is an ongoing debate. In bacteria, proteins such as E. coli RIC, CyaY, IscA, SufA, and Salmonella enterica YggX have been reported as iron donors for the in vitro maturation of FeS clusters (Ding and Clark, 2004; Ding et al., 2004; Layer et al., 2006; Lu et al., 2008; Skovran et al., 2004). For E. coli RIC, dissociation constants for the ferric and ferrous iron of $1 0 ^ { - 2 7 }$ M and $1 0 ^ { - 1 3 }$ M (Table 17.4.1), respectively, have been determined (Nobre et al., 2014). Iron dissociation constants of the other potential iron donors vary from a strong ferric iron affinity in E. coli IscA and CyaY to a weak ferrous iron binding in S. enteric YggX and E. coli IscA– SufA and CyaY (Table 17.4.1). 

E. coli CyaY, the bacterial homologue of human frataxin, has been proposed to participate in the biogenesis of FeS centres as CyaY binds ferrous and ferric iron, albeit with different affinities (Table 17.4.1) and interacts with the E. coli IscS–IscU complex (Adinolfi et al., 2009; Layer et al., 2006). However, and in contrast with the Saccharomyces cerevisiae homologue, depletion of CyaY does not lower the activity of FeS enzymes, and the iron binding to CyaY is weaker than to human ferritins (Bou-Abdallah et al., 2004; Nair et al., 2004). Moreover, CyaY has been reported to be an inhibitor of FeS cluster assembling by slowing down the activity of the cysteine desulphurase IscS (Adinolfi et al., 2009). Additionally, CyaY only binds iron in the presence of hydrogen peroxide and not under reducing conditions. Although these contradictory results have prevented the establishment of its function in FeS biogenesis, CyaY is currently considered an iron chaperone (Ding et al., 2007). 

E. coli IscA, the A-type FeS cluster biogenesis protein of the ISC system, has been reported to bind iron strongly (Table 17.4.1), and to provide it to IscU for the formation of a FeS cluster, under conditions of limited availability of cellular free iron, simulated by the addition of the iron chelator citrate, and in the presence of the thioredoxin system (Ding and Clark, 2004; Ding et al., 2004, 2007). Despite the strong affinity of IscA to ferric iron (Mapolelo et al., 2012), the protein has been isolated in the apo-form or with very low iron content. Furthermore, it is still unclear whether IscA binds transiently to a FeS cluster instead of iron (Bilder et al., 2004; Mapolelo et al., 2012). Therefore, the function of IscA is not fully confirmed.

Figure 17.4.2 Regulation of ric in bacteria. (a) E. coli ric is negatively regulated by NsrR and possibly indirectly controlled by the general regulators FNR and Fur. Under nitrosative stress conditions, NsrR loses its DNA binding ability and ric repression is lifted. (b) P. stutzeri ric is shown to be under the positive control of DnrD in response to NO. (c) In S. aureus, the induction of ric by RNS is dependent on the two component system SrrAB. SarA and MgrA are also proposed to inhibit ric expression and SarV to activate ric transcription. Induction of S. aureus ric transcription by ROS may occur via MgrA. (d) R. eutropha ric (norA) expression is upregulated by RNS, a process that is positively controlled by NorR. Regulatory schemes represented in this figure are based on data reported in the references mentioned in the text.


In S. enterica, deletion of yggX causes defects in the intracellular FeS metabolism and lowers the resistance of the strain to oxidative stress (Gralnick et al., 2001, 2003). Considering that S. enterica YggX also binds iron (Table 17.4.1), the protein has been implicated in iron trafficking; however, this function has been called into question as the E. coli homologue is not able to bind iron (Osborne et al., 2005; Skovran et al., 2004). 

Hence, among the proteins so far proposed to donate iron to FeS centre assembling, RIC presents the more favourable ferrous iron dissociation constant $( \sim 1 0 ^ { - 1 3 }$ M), since all other proteins exhibit very labile $\mathrm { F e } ^ { 2 + }$ that is expected to be dissociated under physiological conditions (Table 17.4.1). 

## 17.4.5 Transcriptional regulation of ric expression

In E. coli, the transcription of the ric gene is significantly elevated in cells exposed to nitrosative stress (Flatley et al., 2005; Justino et al., 2005; Mukhopadhyay et al., 2004; Pullan et al., 2007), and in cells cultured anaerobically in the presence of nitrate and nitrite (Constantinidou et al., 2006; Overton et al., 2006). Upregulation of ric genes by nitrosative stress conditions has been observed for several other organisms, namely S. enterica, S. aureus, N. gonorrhoeae, N. meningitidis, R. eutropha, Pseudomonas stutzeri, and the pathogenic fungi C. neoformans (Chow et al., 2007; Heurlier et al., 2008; Karlinsey et al., 2012; Overton et al., 2006; Pohlmann et al., 2000; Richardson et al., 2006; Vollack et al., 2001). E. coli ric is also induced by heat shock, a condition that causes protein denaturation (Richmond et al., 1999; see Section 13), and S. aureus ric is upregulated by oxidative stress (Chang et al., 2006; see Section 10). In S. enterica and Yersinia pestis, ric genes are more expressed in cells infecting macrophages and rats, respectively (Eriksson et al., 2003; Sebbane et al., 2006). 

The genomic organization of the ric genes has a high degree of variation among microorganisms, and different regulators have been reported to control ric transcription (Justino et al., 2009). 

In E. coli, N. gonorrhoeae, and N. meningitides, ric expression is negatively regulated by NsrR (Figure 17.4.2a) (Bodenmiller and Spiro, 2006; Heurlier et al., 2008; Overton et al., 2008). This transcription factor is a member of the Rrf2 family and contains a FeS cluster that senses NO. Nitrosylation of the cluster leads to the loss of NsrR DNA-binding activity and consequent de-repression of the target genes (Tucker et al., 2010). 

In P. stutzeri, induction of ric (also named dnrN) in response to NO depends on the DnrD regulator. DnrD is an activator of the fumarate nitrate regulator (FNR) family, and a putative FNR box has been identified in the promoter region of the coding sequence for P. stutzeri RIC (Figure 17.4.2b) (Vollack et al., 2001). 

In E. coli, ric was also shown to be de-repressed in cells lacking the transcription factors FNR and Fur (ferric uptake regulator) (Figure 17.4.2a) (Justino et al., 2006, 2009). However, regulation by FNR and Fur may be due to indirect effects as no canonical Fur−FNR binding sites are apparently present in the promoter region of the E. coli ric gene (Justino et al., 2006, 2009). 

In S. aureus, transcription of the ric gene (previously dubbed scdA) is under a complex regulation (Figure 17.4.2c). The two-component system, SrrAB, whose regulon is activated by hypoxia and nitrosative stresses, activates the S. aureus ric expression in the presence of nitric oxide (Kinkel et al., 2013). MgrA is a member of the MarR family of regulators and is proposed to be an oxidative stress sensor (Chen et al., 2006) that negatively controls ric, a repression that is lifted in the post– exponential growth phase, possible due to the maximal activity of the TCA cycle activity and electron transport and the higher iron demand that occurs at this stage (Ingavale et al., 2003). It is plausible that the ROS induction of S. aureus ric may occur via MgrA (Figure 17.4.2c). In S. aureus, SarA represses ric as increased levels of ric were measured in the sarA mutant. SarV positively controls the ric transcription (Manna et al., 2004), which may result from an indirect effect as both MrgA and SarA negatively control SarV. Nevertheless, it remains to be elucidated whether MrgA, SarA, and SarV can directly bind to the ric promoter. 

In R. eutropha, ric (known as norA) forms an operon with the NO reductase encoding gene (norB), and the two genes are under the positive control of the NO sensor NorR (Figure 17.4.2d). NorR is a $\sigma ^ { 5 4 }$ (RpoN)-dependent activator whose encoding gene is located upstream and divergently transcribed from the norA–norB operon (Pohlmann et al., 2000). 

## 17.4.6 Role of RICs in pathogenic organisms

RIC homologues are widespread in nature, including in pathogenic microorganisms (Figure 17.4.3), and the proteins have been implicated in the in vivo survival of several pathogens. 

Salmonella is one of the most common foodborne pathogens that may cause from small outbreaks in the general population to large outbreaks in larger facilities, particularly in hospitals and in institutions housing children, elderly, and AIDS patients (Jones et al., 2006; Laufer et al., 2014). A Salmonella ric mutant was attenuated for survival in the liver and spleens of mice in comparison with the parental strain. Moreover, RIC has been shown to promote Salmonella growth during systemic infection of mice, thereby contributing to the virulence of the pathogen (Karlinsey et al., 2012). 

S. aureus is a pathogen that causes serious health-related concerns due to the high incidence of antibiotic-resistant strains in hospitals and the outside community, which are responsible for an increasing number of deaths per year worldwide (van Hal et al., 2012). S. aureus synthesizes a single aconitase, a dehydratase of the tricarboxylic acid cycle, containing a [4Fe-4S]+2/+1 cluster susceptible to be damaged by NO and oxidants such as hydrogen peroxide (Gaupp et al., 2012). Under these stress conditions, the S. aureus ric mutant strain has lower aconitase activity and undergoes faster decrease of its activity than the parental strain, therefore indicating that RIC contributes to protect aconitase from oxidative stress (Overton et al., 2008). 

Gonococci such as N. gonorrhoeae are pathogens acquired by sexual contact with infected persons. Although N. gonorrhoeae usually affects the mucous membranes of the urethra in males and the endocervix and urethra in females, the infection may disseminate to a variety of tissues, and these infections have high prevalence and low mortality (Criss and Seifert, 2012). The role of RIC in this bacterium has been shown in two different ways: (i) inactivation of the N. gonorrhoeae ric gene leads to a strain more sensitive to NO than its parental strain; and (ii) RIC is implicated in oxidative stress protection as the ric mutant has increased sensitivity to hydrogen peroxide (Overton et al., 2008). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/53bee5ee-cd07-4a05-866a-713143f64838/d4fe9c813cebbe04057a7fe66941e54f8bec37ca7d35b5a309d8d181e01eb05c.jpg)



Figure 17.4.3 Dendrogram of RIC family of proteins. Dendrogram, generated with Dendroscope (Huson et al., 2012), highlights the organisms for which RICs are implicated in oxidative and nitrosative stress resistance and/or FeS metabolism. Adapted from Overton et al. (2008).


C. neoformans causes a systemic fungal infection, namely meningitis, which complicates several immunosuppressive diseases, including HIV. The symptoms of cryptococcosis can be suppressed by treatment with antifungal agents, but often the infection is not eradicated and may require a lifelong treatment (Brizendine et al., 2013; Perfect, 2014). The gene encoding the CNA2870 protein, which contains a HHE–hemerythrin cation binding motif similar to that present in E. coli RIC, is induced by NO and protects cells from NO killing, pointing to a function similar to that of RICs. However, the conditions under which C. neoformans CNA2870 contributes to virulence have not been established (Chow et al., 2007). 

Haemophilus influenzae is a Gram-negative pathogen that colonizes the human nasopharynx of healthy individuals, causing primarily asymptomatic infections. Nevertheless, spreading of bacteria to specific anatomical sites leads to otitis media, meningitis, septicemia, and respiratory infections (Dominguez et al., 2004; Murphy et al., 2009). The H. influenza ric mutant exhibits increased sensitivity to NO donors and reduced survival following macrophages infection, a phenotype that is abrogated by treatment of macrophages with an inhibitor of the mammalian-inducible NO synthase enzyme. These results implicate H. influenzae RIC in a NO-protective pathway that contributes to the resistance of the pathogen to host defences (Harrington et al., 2009). 



Bogdan C. 2001. Nitric oxide and the immune response. Nature Immunology 2(10): 907–916. 





resistance to nitrosative stress and hypoxia. MBio 4(6): e00696– 00613. 



RIC homologues are also found in Trichomonas vaginalis. This protozoan is a flagellated trophozoide living in the urogenital tract and responsible for the most common nonviral sexually transmitted diseases, namely vaginitis in females and, occasionally, urethritis in males. T. vaginalis has also been associated with several complications, such as cervical cancer, infertility, atypical pelvic inflammatory disease, and progression of HIV (Edwards et al., 2014). T. vaginalis is highly dependent on iron and on two essential FeS-containing proteins, namely the pyruvate–ferredoxin oxidoreductase and ferredoxin. Our recent published results indicate that, following oxidative damage, the T. vaginalis RIC1 is able to reactivate the activity of the E. coli FeS–fumarase (i.e., it may protect the protozoan from stress conditions). Interestingly, the second copy of the gene is a DNA binding protein (Nobre et al., 2016).

## 17.4.7 Final remarks



Brizendine KD, Baddley JW, Pappas PG. 2013. Predictors of mortality and differences in clinical features among patients with Cryptococcosis according to immune status. PLoS One 8(3): e60431. 





Laufer AS, Grass J, Holt K, Whichard JM, Griffin PM, et al. 2014. Outbreaks of Salmonella infections attributed to beef – United States, 1973–2011. Epidemiology and Infection 1–11. 



The role of RICs in microbial protection is now supported by several studies carried out at the biochemical and physiological levels and for several microorganisms, including both Grampositive and Gram-negative pathogens. 



Chang W, Small DA, Toghrol F, Bentley WE. 2006. Global transcriptome analysis of Staphylococcus aureus response to hydrogen peroxide. Journal of Bacteriology 188(4): 1648–1659. 





Layer G, Ollagnier-de Choudens S, Sanakis Y, Fontecave M. 2006. Ironsulfur cluster biosynthesis: characterization of Escherichia coli CYaY as an iron donor for the assembly of [2Fe-2S] clusters in the scaffold IscU. Journal of Biological Chemistry 281(24): 16256–16263. 



The liability of FeS clusters to oxidative stress results in the release of iron ions, and reaction with RNS converts the clusters into an unstable DNIC form, leading exposure to the two stresses to partial or total disintegration of the FeS centre. While reconstitution of a completely disaggregated cluster requires reinsertion of both iron and sulphur, the partial disruption of a cluster may only need reintroduction of iron. Yet, in both cases, the repair mechanism involves reinsertion of iron. Although sulphur is provided by the activity of proteins of the Isc or Suf iron–sulphur assembly systems, neither of these systems can apparently compensate for the lack of RIC (Overton et al., 2008). Experiments showing that RICs can participate in the formation of a new cluster by donating iron (Nobre et al., 2014) have reinforced the proposal of RICs promoting iron insertion in partially damaged and/or fully dissembled FeS clusters, together with cysteine and cysteine desulphurase IscS (Figure 17.4.4), by a mechanism that still remains to be fully elucidated.

# The Vibrio cholerae stress response: an elaborate system geared toward overcoming host defenses during infection

Karl-Gustav Rueggeberg and Jun Zhu 

Department of Microbiology, Perelman School of Medicine, University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, USA 

## 17.5.1 Introduction

Enteric pathogenic bacteria have evolved to exploit their target hosts, acquiring nutrients and proliferating in the apparently hostile environment of the upper and lower intestinal tract, and they must overcome various factors within the host, such as the extreme acidity of the stomach, secreted digestive enzymes and bile, competition from established gut flora, and components of the innate and adaptive immune systems. These factors all play a critical role in suppressing rampant pathogen replication within the host, yet highly successful pathogens can still quickly reproduce at significant cost to the host. A prime example of such an organism is Vibrio cholerae. The bacteria have adapted to all of the aforementioned stressors through tightly controlled regulation of a network of stress response genes. The expression of these genes is highly specific to the stressor, allowing the bacteria to fine-tune its response to both increase its survival and decrease energy expenditure. V. cholerae is the cause of diarrheal cholera throughout the world, and much of its virulence is derived from two main virulence factors: cholera toxin (CT) and toxin–co-regulated pili (TCP) (Dirita et al., 1991; Faruque et al., 1998; Weber and Klose, 2011). CT is a two-component adenosine diphosphate (ADP)-ribosylating toxin, encoded by the CTX genes located on a lysogenic filamentous bacteriophage, CTXΦ (Guidolin and Manning, 1987; Waldor and Mekalanos, 1996); whereas TCP is encoded by genes found on a separate chromosomal location called the Vibrio pathogenicity island (VPI) (Karaolis et al., 1998, 2001; Taylor et al., 1986). Expression of these two components is directly controlled by the transcription factor, ToxT, which also happens to be encoded by the VPI (Dirita et al., 1991; Prouty et al., 2005; Weber and Klose, 2011). Although the genes coding for CT and TCP are indispensable for disease progression, other genes have been shown to be essential for bacterial survival and dissemination throughout the small intestine (Nelson et al., 2009; Skorupski and Taylor, 1997; Weber and Klose, 2011). Expression of these genes in response to damaging agents (e.g., acid and bile) and motilityimpairing agents (e.g., mucin) enables V. cholerae to effectively colonize the entire length of the intestine. In addition, genes involved in resisting reactive oxygen species (ROS) (see Section 10) produced by various cells of the innate and acquired immune systems are also critical for V. cholerae colonization and persistence within the intestines. It has been documented that both the acid and ROS stress responses in V. cholerae have been linked to activation of virulence pathways, including CT expression, which suggests that a high degree of crosstalk is occurring between the stress response and virulence pathways during infection. In this review, we will discuss how V. cholerae senses these host-derived factors, as well as competing gut microbiota, and in turn activates the corresponding stress response pathway to overcome them. 

## 17.5.2 Overcoming acid stress

Compared to other enteropathogens such as Escherichia coli, Salmonella, and Shigella, V. cholerae is the most acid sensitive, requiring a higher oral infectious dose (Foster, 2001). Regardless, V. cholerae is still a successful pathogen of the gastrointestinal tract. V. cholerae gains access to its preferred colonization site within the small intestine after the host ingests the bacteria and it passes through the stomach. In order to survive the extreme acidity of the stomach, the pathogen induces expression of a series of genes that mitigate the damaging effects, collectively called the acid tolerance response (ATR) (Merrell and Camilli, 1999; see Section 16). The V. cholerae ATR can be divided into two pathways: inorganic, which is stimulated by low pH and organic acids (Merrell and Camilli, 1999, 2000a,b). 

Both inorganic and organic ATR contribute to bacterial survival during passage through the stomach, where V. cholerae encounters a low-pH solution with varying degrees of organic material depending on the abundance of food ingested by the host at the time of infection (Nalin et al., 2014). While the two ATR pathways share several bacterial factors, certain proteins are unique to each one (Merrell and Camilli, 1999). For example, both ATR pathways are largely dependent on expression of a lysine decarboxylase, CadA, which directly increases the intracellular pH through the conversion of the amino acid lysine into cadaverine (Merrell and Camilli, 1999; Neely and Olson, 1996). cadA expression is directly controlled by the transcriptional regulator cadC, which is highly similar to the wellcharacterized ortholog from E. coli. Works published at the turn of the century underscored the importance of cadC in upregulating expression of cadA in order to promote V. cholerae survival during acid challenge (Merrell and Camilli, 1999, 2000b). Though both ATR pathways stimulate induction of shared downstream components, they are activated through different upstream response regulators. Currently, the upstream factors that mediate the inorganic ATR are unknown. In contrast, activating factors of the organic ATR have been elucidated. ToxR, an inner membrane transcriptional regulator bearing significant similarity to the OmpR family (Miller et al., 1987), is essential for organic ATR (Merrell et al., 2001). Despite its role in modulating the ToxT regulon, it appears that ToxR does not act through ToxT to stimulate the organic ATR (Merrell and Camilli, 1999). Therefore, a peripheral ToxR-regulated pathway must be transducing the acid stress signal to cadC (Merrell et al., 2001). The toxT-independent pathway of the ToxR regulon encompasses OmpU and OmpT, two outer membrane porins involved in solute transport across the membrane. OmpU expression is positively upregulated, whereas OmpT expression is repressed by ToxR (Crawford et al., 1998; Li et al., 2000). These two porins are believed to form homotrimeric complexes, held together by hydrophobic interactions, which surround a water-filled channel (Chakrabarti et al., 1996). Deletion of ompU blocks the ToxR-mediated organic ATR, highlighting the importance of this porin to an effective acid stress response. In addition, ATR-deficient toxR strains can be rescued by ectopic overexpression of OmpU (Merrell et al., 2001). Merrell and colleagues propose two models for the function of OmpU in organic ATR: (i) OmpU prevents influx of excessive organic acid, since it is preferentially expressed versus the leakier OmpT; and (ii) OmpU directly contributes to the integrity of the outer membrane and lowers overall permeability through the bilayer. Together, OmpU and CadA function synergistically during the ATR, where the former limits organic acid entry into the cell and the latter de-acidifies the intracellular environment as organic acids accumulate within the cell (Figure 17.5.1). 

Despite the importance of ToxR-mediated signaling in the organic ATR, additional genes outside the ToxR regulon are

Figure 17.5.1 Acid stress: Vibrio cholerae enters the stomach as it is ingested and immediately encounters highly acidic conditions. Acid exposure induces the acid tolerance response (ATR) in the bacteria, enhancing its survival (see Section 16). The presence of acid stimulates ToxR to simultaneously upregulate OmpU and downregulate OmpT expression to block entry of acidic molecules. Intracellular acid accumulation activates CadC to increase expression of CadA and increase intracellular pH. Blue arrows indicate up- or downregulation.
